Sing Me Something Soft (Sing Me Anything)

by thetalee

Summary

Things have been looking up for Buck and Eddie. They have a new house, with plenty of space to expand their family. Except the road to happily ever after comes with speed bumps and pot holes.

Notes

Title from "Existentialism on Prom Night" by Straylight Run

See the end of the work for more notes

Chapter 1: Oh, The Humanity

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

November

“Who is it?” Eddie spat the question out, glaring at Buck from his spot by the fireplace and the altar with the ofrendas that none of them had the heart to dismantle just yet. He was falling back on old habits, toxic habits. He was falling back on the old safety of anger to cover up the hurt.

He knew he was doing it, but the alternative was to break, and he could not do that. Not now. Not when he already had to figure out how to clean the mess of yet another soon-to-be-broken marriage. Not when he’d have to help keep Chris together.

“Who’s what?” his husband asked, and had the audacity to try and look confused, which just pissed Eddie off even more. Buck had gotten home just a minute earlier, greeting him happily and chatting up a storm as he took off his shoes, just as he always did. He’d claimed to have been at Maddie’s for some sibling bonding time, except…

Except Eddie had called Maddie, and she’d had utterly no idea what he was talking about when he asked if Buck was there.

Except that it wasn’t the only time in recent weeks that Buck had claimed to be somewhere, only for it to be a lie.

“The person you’ve been seeing,” he snarled, doing his best to keep from just screaming the words. He should not have been doing this tonight, with Chris in his room and Abuela watching her shows in hers. But Eddie was just so… Hurt.  “The person you’ve been fucking behind my back.”

And Buck just stared at him.

In utter silence.

August

The new house, and the living arrangements, was an adjustment. Somehow, they managed to move in both themselves and Abuela over just a couple days in the middle of July. Admittedly, they’d had ample help from everyone at the station, because their coworkers would probably help dispose of multiple bodies if the payment was Bobby’s and Abuela’s food. Even the ones from B and C shifts.

Weeks later, and they were still unpacking boxes. Like the first time Eddie had moved, Chris’ room had been finished and furnished before literally anything else. His walls had new paint and the ceiling had glow-in-the-dark stars stuck to it. So did the master bedroom - the bedroom with the ensuite - and the spare one, actually. The spare one because there would, hopefully, be another child living in it before too long. The master bedroom because, despite their best efforts, Abuela had managed to bully them into taking it through sheer force of personality, and Buck had extra stars left over.

The rest of the house was a work in progress. It was kinda fun, in a way, to meld their things together with Abuela’s. The furniture was a mish-match of both of theirs - Abuela’s favorite chair, for example, was in the living room with Eddie’s slightly newer couch - while the kitchen cabinets were simply overflowing with pots and pans and what-have-you.  And Eddie and Buck kept coming home from shifts to find that more things had found places on the walls.

They, through unspoken and unanimous agreement, left Abuela to the decorating. Neither of them had truly realized how lonely and bored the woman must have been, living by herself, until they noticed how much happier she seemed to be now.  Thankfully, after getting her on their insurance, it was a simple matter for Carla to take her on as a client as well, since the aide was already going to be there for Christopher.

A simple matter, and a weight off Eddie’s shoulders, who hadn’t quite managed to ward off his fears of another broken hip after his father brought it up during the trip to Texas months before.

It was mid-August now. Chris was going back to school, and both Buck and Eddie had promised to be free labor to help May move into her dorm later in the week, and things were good. And Buck was certain that he hadn’t stopped smiling since the first Sunday that they managed to properly host everyone for dinner.

Things were good.

That isn’t to say that things were uneventful, though.

Buck had seen many extraordinary things that were now seared into his memory. A bus sticking out of the side of a building; a skyscraper leaning over the street; the ocean itself raising up to smash them like Neptune’s fist. A blimp resting precariously upon the light fixtures of a sports stadium didn’t quite manage to rival anything but the bus, but it was still quite the sight. It was already starting to deflate, and something was on fire and putting out smoke, either the lights or the blimp’s gondola.

“If this is what we get in August, I hate to see what this year’s September Disaster is going to be,” Buck told the others as he pulled the truck into the parking lot, peering through the windshield at it. Ortiz, who normally drove the ladder truck, had called in, and so Buck was the next qualified person to drive.

“September Disaster?” Albert echoed from behind him.

“Yeah, y’know. Every September, there’s a disaster,” Buck explained as he pulled them to a stop. “Earthquake, tsunami, earthquakes and a mudslide. Cyberterrorism. Every September except my first one, actually.”

“You were the disaster that September,” Bobby quipped from the captain’s seat, ignoring Buck’s offended squawk as everyone hopped out. Immediately, he started issuing their marching orders. “Buck, Hen, kill that light tower. Eddie, when the ladder’s in position, run point with Albert.”

Nodding, Buck and Eddie bumped wrists before separating to grab the equipment they needed. Before long, Buck was hurrying beside Hen, Halligan in hand. The electrical box for the lights was, of course, directly beneath the drooping dirigible. Even as he brute forced the box open, he couldn’t help but shoot anxious looks at what was looming above them, and caught Hen doing the same.

There was no finesse, and no time for it. The moment the panel door swung open, Hen was reaching in with her own tool and started cutting through wires. There was a small snap and crackle and a minor burst of sparks, and then the strange sensation of an absence of a sound - the electric hum - that Buck hadn’t even realized he was hearing.

“Power’s out,” Hen relayed into the radio. “It’s safe to approach.”

There wasn’t an immediate acknowledgement, but as they headed back for the trucks, Buck saw Bobby climb up to stand beside the person operating the ladder controls.

“Eddie, Albert, ladder’s moving into position. Let’s get that fire out.”

Buck lost track of where Hen went after they stowed away the tools. He went to hover around the ladder truck, just below where Bobby was standing, and craned his neck to watch as Eddie and Albert climbed up with another Halligan and a fire extinguisher. He hated, absolutely hated, when he wasn’t there to literally have his husband’s back, when he had to stand by and watch and hope as someone else had it. But he’d have to get used to it, considering that when Bobby went on his honeymoon, Buck would be spending two weeks in the same position.

So this was good practice, even if he hated it.

“Fire’s out, Cap,” Eddie radioed after several minutes of smothering the fire. “Swing us over to the cab.”

“No sure how long you’ll have,” Bobby radioed back as the ladder was adjusted to allow them access to the cabin door. “Blimp's getting heavier as it deflates.”

There wasn’t an immediate reply. From below, Buck saw Eddy switch the extinguisher for the Halligan and bust the door open. And then his heart leaped into his throat as the whole thing rocked ominously the moment his husband stepped a foot inside. Albert, bless him, immediately grabbed the back of Eddie’s coat.

Buck couldn’t see what was happening from his vantage point. All he knew was that Eddie disappeared inside, leaving Albert alone at the end of the ladder. Someone, one of the pilots, appeared at the entry way and the paramedic helped her out.

And then the entire thing rocked again.

“Buck.” Hen’s voice had him leaping in his skin, and he tore his eyes away from the tableau above to find her holding a c-collar out to him. “For the pilot with the head injury.”

“Yes ma’am,” Buck said, snagging it out of her hand and clambered up, slipping the collar onto his arm for safe keeping..

“That’s it, Al, nice and easy. Let’s get her down here,” Bobby was saying into the radio as Buck passed, and the two nodded to each other in acknowledgement. “Eddie, Buck’s on his way up to help evacuate the co-pilot.”

He hurried up the ladder, pausing to give Albert a warning of “on your left” before squeezing around him and the patient he was escorting down. The moment he got to the open entryway, spotting Eddie hunched over the unmoving co-pilot.

“Babe, hey,” he called, getting his husband's attention and stretching his arm out to hand him the collar. The other man took it with a smile and started putting it around the patient’s neck, who in turn must have done something that Buck couldn’t see, because Eddie was grabbing his radio and saying,

“Cap, pilot’s coming to.”

“Alright,” Bobby replied. “You two, move fast, move safe.”

“Sir, can you feel the pressure I’m applying?” he heard Eddie ask, and could see him rubbing at the patient’s chest. He couldn’t hear the reply, but there must have been one, because Eddie let out a relieved, “Good.”

His husband made quick work of the straps still holding the pilot in place, and with a short countdown, got the man to his feet. And it would have been a perfectly easy and simple task to get the man to Buck, except they were essentially on a giant seesaw of death. The moment the two stepped away from the controls, the entire thing rocked dangerously. Gravity was not their friend today.

Which, actually…

“Cap!” Buck hollered down, bypassing the radio in favor of well developed lungs. “Lower me three feet!”

“Copy that.”

He held on as the hydraulics worked to move the ladder beneath him, lowering it so it was just beneath the bottom lip of the entryway. There was a bit more ominous teetering that followed, as Eddie and the co-pilot gingerly made their way to him and the ladder. The moment the patient was in reach, Buck reached out and got a firm hold of him, helping him to cross the small gulf of open air.

The captain had said to move safe, but he’d also said to move fast, and so Buck felt no guilt about perhaps hurrying the man along. He kept glancing between the patient, the bottom of the ladder, and back to the blimp above. Bobby’s somewhat worried urgings to Eddie over the radio was not helping Buck’s anxiety any, either.

And then there was a jolt, and when Buck looked up, he saw Eddie sliding down the ladder a few rungs before he managed to grab hold of the railing and stop himself.

It was easier, after that, to climb down the rest of the way.

“Did you know that the Hindenburg disaster literally only took less than a minute?” Buck asked the others, looking up from his phone.

It was downtime between calls, and they had gathered in the den area, waiting for the clock to run out and free them to head home. He was curled up against Eddie’s side as his husband was soundly trounced by Hen at Mario Kart. Albert was perched in one of the comfy chairs, his own phone in hand.

Probably texting Ravi, since he was stuck helping cover on another shift for the week.

“Oh, the humanity,” Hen said, and then cheered as she managed to hit Eddie with a shell, sending him spinning off course.

“And they still don’t know what caused it to catch fire,” Buck continued. “But there’s been theories that it was anything from static electricity to sabotage to-”

“Buck.” Bobby’s voice cut him off. “I need to talk to you in my office.”

There was something about the captain’s face, when Buck glanced up, that had his stomach curdling. He wasn’t sure what it was, exactly. The frown, perhaps. Or the pinched look of his eyes. Whatever it was, Buck didn’t like it.

“Yeah, uh, sure,” he said, reluctantly pushing himself off the couch and away from his husband. “Now?”

“Please,” Bobby said, and then turned to walk away. Buck shared confused looks with the others, before shrugging and following. 

He had long ago stopped feeling like he’d been called to the principal whenever he stepped foot in the captain’s office, but the ghost of that feeling crept up on him now as he stepped through the door. He didn’t close it behind him, and Bobby didn’t mention it, so he didn’t think he was in trouble.

Not that he’d done anything to be in trouble for, but still.

“Is this more impromptu captain’s lessons?” he asked, walking forward to lean against one of the chairs facing the desk, as Bobby sat behind it with a sigh. “Y’know, I was thinking about signing up for those leadership courses you mentioned, the ones the department offers. And I’ve been uh, working through the books they suggest.”

“That’s good,” Bobby said, nodding. “But that’s… That’s not really why I called you in here.”

“Okay,” Buck said, drawing the word out. This was doing absolutely nothing to help with his building anxiety. “Am- Am I in trouble? Did I do something…?”

“No, no, you- No,” the captain told him, shaking his head. “You’ve been exemplary on the field, as always.”

Normally, the praise would have Buck preening, but this time…

“So then what’s going on?”

“I just got off the phone with the battalion chief,” Bobby explained, and the man wasn’t quite meeting Buck’s eyes. “They wanted to talk about who would be in charge while I was away.”

“Well, I mean, you’ve been pretty insistent on it being me for months now,” Buck said, trying to keep his tone light. “So I don’t see what the problem is.”

“That is the problem, apparently,” the captain said.

“What’s the problem?”

“I was told that the brass…” Bobby paused, hesitated. “I was told that at this time, they don’t think you’re the best person to be left in charge of A-shift in my absence.”

“What?” he blurted out. “Why? Did- Did I do something?”

“No, Buck, you-”

“Because I don’t understand, pops, they had no problem with me being put in charge back in… Back in May.” He trailed off, realization washing over him like ice water. Back in May, before Jonah Greenway. Back in May, before… “Taylor. This is about Taylor’s story, isn’t it? They’re still angry about that?”

He remembered how angry the chief had been, right afterwards. The way he’d been raked over the coals as they sought for a scapegoat to blame for their embarrassment, and as Taylor’s friend, he’d nearly been the perfect candidate. If one ignored the fact that he was busy nearly being murdered by the subject of her story. He had thought that he managed to dodge anything more than that temporary discomfort of being grilled for anything and everything he might have told the reporter, but clearly he had not.

He and Taylor had barely even spoken in months, and yet here they were.

“I’m being punished,” Buck said, feeling oddly hollow. He’d been dreading being left in charge, but now that he was being told that he couldn’t do it… “I’m being punished for something that I didn’t even do.”

“I’m sorry, Buck,” Bobby said, finally looking him in the eye. And Buck wanted to tell him exactly where to stick that ‘sorry,’ but he knew it wasn’t the captain’s fault. “Maybe… Maybe when there’s been more time to put the whole thing behind them.”

“Yeah, maybe,” he agreed, in a voice devoid of emotions. “I’m just gonna…”

He did not slam the door behind him, and considered it a win.

Chapter End Notes

Thought process: "I really liked Captain Hen. I should make sure Captain Hen is a thing, like in canon. But I already have Buck primed and ready for being captain, what to do..."

Chapter 2: Ring Through

Chapter Summary

In which Eddie is trying his best to support his husband.

There was something wrong with Eddie’s husband.

Okay, there were several things wrong with his husband, but Eddie loved him despite and because of all of them. He loved him when he was loud and obnoxious, he loved him when his humor was juvenile and inappropriate. He even loved him when he was being moody and obstinate, like he was regressing to his teenage years. Except, at the moment, he wasn’t being any of those things, and that was… Wrong.

Buck was being quiet. Not silent, but quiet. Eddie spent the entire time he was playing Boggle with Christopher at the breakfast bar - and soundly beating him, because life lessons could be found everywhere - keeping an eye on his husband as he fixed dinner with Abuela. And he was talking, but only when Abuela spoke to him. Answering questions, commenting where expected, but he wasn’t chatting away the entire time like he usually did while they worked.

Ever since they had moved into their new home, the task of cooking was done to the soundtrack of Buck and Abuela’s overlapping chatter. Constant and flowing, and Eddie was fairly certain they managed to have two or three conversations at once with just each other. 

But not tonight.

Earlier that day, towards the end of their shift, Bobby had taken Buck to the captain’s office to talk about something. And the younger man had walked out of the office dimmer, somehow. They’d asked, of course, what Bobby had wanted to talk to him about. Or, well, Eddie had asked after Hen and Albert looked at him expectantly.

“He was telling me I wasn’t going to be captain while he’s gone,” was all Buck had told them. And then nothing else, no matter how much everyone pressed for more details.

It was only marginally better when dinner was served and everyone was eating, because Buck made a point of asking Chris about his first day back to school. But even then, he clearly sat back and let their son dominate the conversation. And Abuela had clearly noticed, too, because she very pointedly asked Eddie if anything had happened at work that day. The fact that her question didn’t have Buck jumping in with a story only made things even more obvious.

There was something wrong with Eddie’s husband.

After dinner, after the table had been cleared and Chris had run to his room, Eddie grabbed Buck by the elbow and led him through the laundry room and into the backyard. His husband didn’t even make a token effort of resistance, and he was soon making Buck sit in one of the patio chairs that had migrated with Abuela.

“Alright, I’ve let you stew,” Eddie began, standing in front of him with his arms crossed, staring him down. “What’s going on? You’ve been off ever since you talked to Bobby today. And what did you mean, you won’t be captain while he’s gone?”

“Exactly what I said,” Buck answered, and normally there would be a careless shrug thrown in. Normally, but not now. Right now, he was just slumped in the chair. “I’m not going to be the substitute captain.”

“Why?”

“Because I’m tainted goods,” his husband answered. When Eddie simply raised his eyebrows, waiting for more, the man let out a sigh. “The brass decided they didn’t want me as captain. Because of Taylor’s story.”

“Taylor’s story,” Eddie repeated, voice coming out far calmer than he was feeling.

“That’s what they told pops,” Buck said, more animated than he was before, more agitated. He got to his feet and started pacing. “But I bet it’s also the lawsuit, and all the stupid things I did as a probie, too. I- I bet there’s also, y’know, I’m the guy who hid the fact I was fucking my coworker for half a year.”

“We hid that,” Eddie tried, but his husband wasn’t listening.

“I mean, I stole a truck for a hook-up. I sued the department. Of course I’m tainted goods, of course the brass took one look at pops’ suggestion and shot it down,” he continued to rant. “I don’t know why he ever thought I’d be captain material. I don’t know why I ever thought it. There was never any way that they’d-”

Eddie interrupted his rambling, and his pacing, by grabbing his arm and pulling him into a hug.

“That’s enough of that, mi sol,” he told him. “You know I don’t like it when you talk about yourself like this.”

“Why?” Buck asked. “Nothing I said was wrong. I’m not going to be captain, I’ll be lucky to get any kind of promotion because I’ve screwed everything up with my stupid choices-”

Once again, Eddie interrupted him, this time by landing a sharp smack on his denim covered rear. Buck jumped and yelped and tried to pull away, but Eddie didn’t let him go.

“Seriously, babe?”

“You were doing it again,” he said, shrugging. “I’m not going to let someone talk about my husband like that.”

“My hero,” Buck drawled, and though Eddie couldn’t see, he was willing to bet the words were accompanied by an eyeroll. “You can’t do that to the brass, though.”

“Watch me,” Eddie retorted, and achieved his goal of getting his husband to laugh. It was barely more than a snort, but it was something. “You’re catastrophizing.”

“Big word.”

“Shut up. Did Bobby say anything about anything else?” he asked. “Or did he just mention Taylor’s story?”

And oh, he was definitely tempted to call the reporter up and tear her a new one. Later, though. When his husband was asleep and less likely to catch him in the act.

“Just the story about Jonah,” Buck confirmed.

“Then everything else is an assumption,” Eddie told him. “What else did Bobby say?”

“He just said something about more time to put it behind them.”

Well that was utterly unhelpful, but probably the best any of them could do.

“Listen, this isn’t on you,” Eddie said, drawing back to look him in the eye. When his husband tried to interrupt, he stopped him with a finger over his lips. “No, I said to listen. If this was anything else besides that stupid story, they would have raised a fuss the first two shifts you were in charge.”

“You don’t-”

“But they didn’t,” he continued, talking over him. “Because this isn’t on you. This is barely about you. This is someone holding a grudge over something Taylor did. Bobby’s right. With more time, they’ll get over it.”

“Maybe,” Buck sighed, and being as it wasn’t an outright denial, Eddie decided to take it as a win. “I just wish…”

“I know.”

Later, after they’d gone to bed, Eddie slipped out of the bedroom with his phone. The call went to voicemail, which was to be expected at this time of night. But that was fine. It was better than fine.

It wasn’t going to be a conversation, anyway.

Buck’s phone started ringing on the way to the station the next morning, and he frowned when he saw Taylor’s name flashing on the screen. The reporter hadn’t called him in months. The two of them hadn’t talked since he told her, point blank, how disappointed he was with her actions. So he was a bit hesitant when he donned his bluetooth and answered with an awkward,

“Hello?”

“Why did I wake up to five voicemails from your husband, half of it in Spanish, calling me a - and I quote - ‘vapid insufferable bitch’ and that I should - again, quoting - ‘drown myself in Nair for the betterment of mankind’?”

“I’m sorry.” Taking advantage of being stopped at a red light, Buck turned his head to stare down Eddie, who was trying and failing to look innocent where he sat in the passenger seat. “My husband did what?”

Despite his amusement derived from Eddie’s actions - “I cannot believe you called up Taylor just to yell at her, babe.” - Eddie could tell that Buck was only somewhat in a better mood during their next shift. He was still far more subdued compared to how he typically was, the usual pep in his step missing as they walked into the station. And Eddie watched as he shrunk in on himself even more when they passed the captain’s office and heard Mayfair’s voice. A short, maternal looking woman, the C-shift captain seemed to always be smiling and could, Eddie was certain, knock him flat on his ass.

“-I thought that was the point of your nepotism?” she was saying, sounding very displeased. “So that you had someone to cover for you, and Tim and I didn’t have to double up our shifts anymore!”

“Nepotism,” Buck muttered bitterly. “Maybe that’s all it ever- Hey!” He jumped and glared at Eddie. “Is this going to me a thing, now? Are you going to spank me every time I say something you don’t like?”

“Maybe,” Eddie said. “Is it working?”

“If you’re trying to tempt me into breaking our ‘no quickies at work’ rule, then yes.”

“I will get a hose,” Hen warned them, rolling her eyes as she passed.

The beginning of the shift passed quickly, after Mayfair left and Bobby came out of his office looking like he was nursing a headache. Buck stayed subdued, although he was at least refraining from badmouthing himself within Eddie’s hearing. Overall, the shift started okay and continued as such.

And then they got a call for a reported gunshot wound.

It wasn’t the first GSW they’d responded to, because while the city wasn’t an Escape From New York hellscape, it was still a densely populated urban environment, and people got stupid and violent when in close proximity. Sometimes, not even necessarily violent, just stupid. But every time they had to respond to one since the sniper, the others would turn and look at both him and Buck. Every time, without fail.

It wasn’t as blatant as it used to be, right after he returned to work, but it was still noticeable.

Eddie wasn’t certain why he and Buck got singled out for the Looks, since both Bobby and Hen had both also been shot. Though, considering Buck’s minor breakdown following the prison riot, after he briefly thought Eddie had been shot again, maybe he could understand things a little.

Anyways, they were sent off to a reported GSW on a golf course, which. That was new. Eddie hadn’t heard of people being shot over that particular type of driving. Then again, he’d been a first responder long enough that he really oughtn’t be surprised.

“Dispatch, One Eighteen is at the golf course, victim is in sight,” he heard Bobby relay into the radio while they hopped out of the engine. They could see the presumed vic laying on his back, a younger man - the caller - crouched over him, and an officer he didn’t recognize that came up to them. He saw Bobby pause, consider something for a moment, and then glance at Buck. “Buck, do you want to…?”

“What?” Buck looked at the captain, and then at the cop. “Oh, uh. No, you’ve got this Cap. Obviously. I’ll just, uh…”

He gestured vaguely before heading for the vic, Eddie and the others following behind. Behind them, Bobby let out a dejected sigh. When they got to them, Buck stayed back and simply watched. Which wasn’t unusual for a medical call, but it was still troubling that he didn’t comment at all.

“I’ll look at your hand,” Albert said, and Eddie hadn’t even realized that the caller’s hand was injured. “Over here, so they have space.”

Eddie knelt down beside the vic with Hen while Albert led the other one some paces away. He put on his stethoscope and got the blood pressure cuff on the guy’s arm as Hen cut open his shirt.

“Keyhole thoracic entry wound, right sternum,” the senior paramedic described. “Steady bleeding, but no spurting, so it looks like it missed the aorta.”

“Heart rate’s One Sixty-five BPM, systolic BP is Ninety-eight,” Eddie told her, scowling down at the wound. He was unfortunately intimately familiar with vitals like that, because, “that indicates he was likely struck by a large-caliber round.”

“What was your dad shot with?” Hen asked the caller.

“My tungsten wedding ring,” he answered, peering around Albert to look at them.

“Never saw that in combat,” Eddie quipped, and then glanced over his shoulder at Buck. “Hey, that reminds me. We still need to get those silicone wedding rings like we’ve been talking.”

“Whenever we have the time, babe.”

“Oh my god, are you two married?” the caller asked. “That’s so sweet.”

“How exactly did this happen?” Bobby asked as Eddie packed the wound and Hen ran morphine and fluids.

“I was trying to fish a ball out of the wood chipper-” Ay dios, please don’t say- “-but my hand got sucked into the blade.” Why were people idiots? “My ring jammed it long enough for my hand to slide out, but then the ring just went through.”

“Despite reaching into a wood chipper, you still have all your fingers, and none of them look broken,” Albert announced, and Eddie wondered if he was finding it difficult to not call the guy an idiot, or if that was just him.

“Doctors should be able to remove the ring at the hospital,” Hen told the vic, after they got him on a gurney and headed for the ambulance.

“I’m gonna call Mom and tell her to meet us there,” the vic’s son told him, walking along beside.

“Oh, and tell Joey I’m not gonna make it to the driving range tonight,” the vic requested.

“You’re seriously still playing golf with my ex-boyfriend?”

Oof.

“He’s like the son I never had. The one who can play golf.”

Double oof.

“Hey, Buck?” Eddie started as they climbed back into the engine’s cab.

“Yeah, babe?”

“If for some impossible reason we break-up-”

“I’ve told you,” Buck interrupted, settling into his seat. “We’re never getting a divorce.”

“Still. If we do, please don’t play golf with my father.”

His husband made a face.

“I don’t even like golf.”

Chapter 3: Support

Chapter Summary

In which Eddie continues to be a supportive husband.

Chapter Notes

Merry Christmas, and Happy Hanukkah.

I am currently surviving Winter-geddon, so I hope all of you are safe and warm.

Wednesday is move-in day for the University of Southern California, and Buck and Eddie both found themselves keeping their promise to help. It was a bit concerning how much stuff May planned on bringing with her. A year living on her own in her own apartment had led to the accumulation of many possessions, most of it currently kept in her old bedroom at the Grant-Nash house.

Personally, Buck didn’t see her staying in the dorms past her freshman year.

Anyways, with Chris at school and Abuela spending the day with Maddie, Buck and Eddie showed up at the Grant-Nash residence for lunch and then to help load up boxes before heading for the residence hall that May would be spending the next several months at. When they got there and parked beside Bobby’s truck and May’s car, Buck spent several moments just gawking at the others moving in.

“Are some of these kids even old enough to be in college?” he asked as he unbuckled and got out. “See, like that one. He doesn’t look any older than Chris does!”

“Mi sol, I’m very sorry to tell you this,” Eddie said, joining him at the back of the jeep. “But I think saying that officially makes you old.”

“The fact that we went to bed last night at nine-thirty and I didn’t complain about it makes me old,” Buck retorted and grabbed a couple of boxes, one stacked on top of the other. “May! Where do we carry these to?”

“The front entrance is over there,” she told him, pointing. He couldn’t help but notice that she just had a bag hanging off her elbow, while Bobby behind her carried a box that could probably fit Chris and his crutches. Athena, meanwhile, wasn’t carrying anything. “I have to get my key from the desk, first.”

It was an awkward few minutes, standing around while May got that sorted out. And then they had to dig out their IDs to leave with the bored looking young woman working the desk, because apparently the school had a Thing about letting unknown people just waltz in and out of the building. Afterwards, they all filed into the far-too-small elevator, riding it up to May’s floor.

Between the three of them - Bobby and Buck and Eddie - they managed to unload all of the boxes and carry them up. The first of May’s roommates finally showed up as they were helping unpack the last of her things, and it was… Awkward, to say the least.

“And that’s Buck and Eddie,” May told her, and Buck gave a little wave. The roommate was staring at him, and at his husband, and attention that he used to very much enjoy had his skin crawling instead. “My brother and his husband.”

“Your brother, huh?”

“And his husband,” May repeated, the emphasis making it clear that she had noticed the interest in the other woman’s voice.

“I can still look,” she said, barely making an effort to be quiet about it. “Oh, to be a fly on the wall…”

“Okay!” Buck clapped his hands together as Bobby choked out a laugh. “I think that’s everything from the jeep, right?”

“Pretty sure,” Eddie agreed, looking far too amused for someone who was also being stared at like a particularly juicy piece of meat.

“Okay, right then. Pops, ma, we’re off!” he said. “May, if you need anything - ever - just give me a call, okay?”

“What if I need anything?” her roommate asked, batting her eyelashes.

“Uh- Um- Call Nine-One-One!” Buck said, before grabbing Eddie’s hand and fleeing from the building.

Eddie’s faith in humanity was pretty heavily dented these days, so he was not surprised when they were called to situations that tried to limbo his already low expectations. The shift was already not the best: Hen was off, and so Eddie was stuck assisting Albert instead of partnering with Buck. At this rate, perhaps he should follow Buck’s suggestion and look into actually getting paramedic certified, with how often he was forced into the role.

They were called to a car lot, with reports that ‘a game had gone awry.’ They found a frantic salesman with two people, a man and a woman, clinging to a new car and a crowd of people just gawking. The woman looked to be in distress, while the man was radiating smugness.

“How long have these people been standing out here?” he heard Bobby ask as he walked past, making his way to the woman, who answered with,

“Twenty-eight hours.”

“Is that even legal?” Buck asked.

“Let’s take a listen,” Eddie said, putting on his stethoscope and pressing it against her back. She was sunburnt and cringing in pain, and when he pinched at the skin on her hand, it stayed tented up for longer than he was comfortable with. “When’s the last time you had any water?”

“Two days,” she answered, and it took everything he had to not roll his eyes. “Been trying not to drink that much because water equals pee, and pee equals losing. And I need this car.”

“Well, based on your lower back pain, and what sounded like fluid in your lungs, I’m guessing you’re suffering from acute kidney failure,” he told her. “Not to mention, you probably damaged your bladder.”

“I need to keep one hand on this car at all times,” she said. “Can’t you run an IV in the other arm or something?”

Well that was another dent in his faith in humanity.

“How are you feeling, sir?” Albert asked his guy, on the other side of the car. “You’ve been out in the sun for nearly two days now, right? Can’t be easy.”

“I’m doing fine,” the other contestant said, shooting a smug grin at Eddie's patient. “I could go a whole ‘nother day.”

“Me, too,” the woman spat back.

With that exchange, Eddie is left to shake his head and despair over the human race as he goes back to where Bobby and Buck are standing just a few feet away, Albert joining him.

“We’ve got to do something, Cap,” he told them. “She’s refusing to leave.”

“So’s my guy,” Albert adds.

“How about this competition gets paused so they can get treatment?” Bobby suggested, turning to the salesman. “You can pick it up in a day or two. Maybe inside, so they’re out of the elements?”

“Sorry, but no can do,” the salesman answered, not looking the least bit sorry, in Eddie’s opinion.

“What do you think people are gonna say when they find out a woman died at your dealership?” Bobby asked the man, who did not look particularly moved. “I am trying to give you an out, here.”

“Rules are rules.”

“And the rules are that they have to keep one hand on one part of the vehicle at all times, right?”

“That’s right.”

“Buck?”

“On it, Cap.” Buck was grinning, Eddie could tell, as he hurried for the truck. “Saws and jaws.”

“Saws?” the salesman repeated, finally looking a bit worried. “You’re not gonna cut that lady’s arm off, are you?”

“I don’t need to. She just needs to keep her hand on some part of the vehicle at all times,” Bobby explained to the man as Buck came back, showing off the power saw like he was a model on the Price Is Right. “You didn’t say that part had to remain attached to the vehicle.”

“You can’t just come in here and carve up my inventory like that!” the salesman protested.

“Rules are rules,” Bobby shot back.

“Look, reporters,” Eddie announced as someone from the local news and her cameraman showed up. He was just thankful it wasn’t Taylor. “This is gonna play great on the morning news.”

Buck revved up the saw and teased the car with it, repeatedly stopping and drawing back just before it could bite into the metal. He was grinning the entire time, and Eddie was reminded that he was in love with the biggest dork ever. But at least he was acting more like himself than he had been since the brass decided to be stupid.

“Well?” Bobby asked, looking at the salesman expectedly.

(Both contestants won a car.)

“He’s going to ask Hen,” Buck said.

It wasn’t quite the middle of the night, just past ten, but they were curled up in bed together, Eddie’s head on his chest with Buck playing with his hair. Bobby had pulled him into his office before they left for home to tell him, presumably in an effort to keep him from being blindsided later. An effort to appease his battered ego and to show that the captain was still relying on his input, or whatever Bobby’s logic had been.

It didn’t matter: he was tainted goods as far as the brass was concerned.

“To fill-in for him?” Eddie asked, for clarification’s sake. Buck nodded. “And how do we feel about that?”

Buck let out a snort and tugged at his hair.

“She’s our friend,” he said. “She’ll make a great captain. We feel happy for her.”

“Yeah, okay,” Eddie said, pushing himself up to stare down at him. “And what else do you feel?”

“She’s my friend,” Buck repeated. “I’m happy for her.”

“Buck,” Eddie sighed. “What would Copeland say, right now?”

He rolled his eyes and let out a sigh of his own, but dutifully recited,

“My feelings are valid.”

“Right, so: how do we feel?”

“Pretty fucking pissed,” Buck blurted out, surprising even himself with his tone. “Like, I know she deserves it, and she’ll be good at it, but I feel like she’s getting it because it’s been, I don’t know. Stolen from me. Like the rug has been pulled from under me. Which is stupid, because she was always his first choice-”

“Was she?” Eddie interrupted to ask.

“I mean, yeah?” Buck shrugged. “He pretty much said as much when he was telling me back in April, right? Like, he said the only problem with putting her in charge was her helping Albert, since he was a probie. And he’s not anymore, so…”

“So.”

“It’s fine,” he said, the words tasting like ash. “I’m tainted goods, and she deserves it more than I ever did.”

“You can be upset about it and still be happy for her,” Eddie said, and Buck squinted up at him.

“Listen to you, all therapized and shit.”

“It’s your fault,” his husband said, laying back down beside him. Buck felt his arms wrap around him before he was suddenly pulled into his chest. “You’re the one who pushed me into talking to Copeland.”

“Sorry, not sorry,” he said, and then, “I really am happy for Hen.”

“I know you are,” Eddie said, and the feel of his breath on his neck had Buck shivering. “Like I said, you’re allowed to be happy for her and still be upset for yourself.”

“I know.”

They fell silent, the only noises the sound of traffic in the distance. Buck’s eyes traced the glow-in-the-dark stars on the ceiling, making new constellations out of the patterns. The ones in here had been the ones left over from the two or three bags they’d used for both Chris’ room and the currently empty one, the one between them and Abuela. It meant the stars on their ceiling was sparse, reminiscent of the actual night sky outside their window.

“I can’t believe I’m about to say this,” Eddie groaned, and Buck turned his head to look at him.

“Say what?”

“What would Taylor do?” he asked, and sounded very grumpy about it.

“What would- What?”

“What would Taylor do?” Eddie repeated, and even in the dark, his scowl was noticeable. “About this. The brass.”

“Something petty,” Buck answered, briefly imagining the scenario of Taylor cackling while the department HQ burned down in front of her. There was screaming and toasted marshmallows involved. “I don’t know. Put on her best dress and sharpest heels and… I don’t know. Make them eat their words, I guess.”

“That’s what we’ll do, then.”

Buck rolled onto his side so he could look at his husband better.

“I don’t have any dresses, babe, and the last time I wore heels I almost broke an ankle,” he told him. Eddie didn’t reply immediately, and Buck rolled his eyes with a laugh. “You’re imagining me in a dress and heels, aren’t you?”

“I’m imagining you in a skirt,” the other man corrected. “But that’s something for later.”

“Oh my god.”

“I meant, we’ll make them eat their words. You’ll make them eat their words.”

“And how do you propose I do that?” he asked, using his arm to prop his head up. “I don’t exactly have a time machine to go back and stop myself from being an idiot.”

“You’ve mentioned the leadership courses the department offers,” Eddie said. “I say that you take them and ace them. Break records like you did when you were recertifying.”

Buck had been thinking about signing up for them, that was true. He’d said as much to Bobby. But he’d been hesitant, and the brass slapping him down had seemed like a sign from the universe, because,

“I flunked out of college, babe,” he reminded him. “If the fire academy didn’t have so much of it geared towards the physical side, I probably would have flunked out of that, too. I don’t… I don’t do well in a classroom.”

“I wonder if that has anything to do with the thing you refuse to go ahead and get diagnosed for, like Copeland’s been pushing,” Eddie sniped, and Buck rolled his eyes. “You’re not dumb-”

“Thanks, babe.”

“Callate, mi sol. We both know that if something catches your interest, you devour it right the hell up. And you’ve been working through those books they suggest, right?”

“I have,” Buck admitted.

“So, we’ll get you through those leadership classes. I’ll even help you study for them, as long as there’s no math.”

“If there’s any math, I’ll ask Chris for help,” he said, making them both laugh. “There’s a term paper at the end, y’know. Twenty pages.”

“And we’ll help you with that, too,” Eddie promised. “You’ll ace everything and rub it in their faces.”

“You really want me to take those leadership courses, don’t you?”

“I don’t like how sad you’ve been,” his husband confessed in a whisper, reaching up to rub his thumb across his birthmark. “I know you hated having to stand back while I ran into that fire without you, and how anxious being in charge made you, but I also know how proud you were that Bobby trusted you with the job.”

“And you think stressing myself out with these classes will make me less sad?” Buck asked.

“I think having other people tell you that you’re good at the job will make you less sad. I think standing up there and being able to tell the brass to go fuck themselves will make you less sad.”

“Pretty sure doing that will get me fired, Eds.”

“I clearly didn’t mean it literally,” Eddie said, and even though it was too dark to see, Buck knew he was rolling his eyes. “It’ll be a subtle ‘fuck you’.”

“I think it’s too late to sign up for this round of courses,” he said, giving in. “But… Yeah. Okay. I guess I’m going back to school.”

“I’ll get you a new clipboard, to terrorize your classmates with.”

Eddie really was the best, Buck thought as they settled back under the covers, twining their limbs around each other. He really was the best, and he deserved the goddamn world. But since Buck couldn’t actually give him that - their budget didn’t allow it - he’d have to settle for something else.

He’ll look up those dance classes, he decided. And then he’ll surprise Eddie with a night on the town for their anniversary.

When they arrived at the station for their next shift, the captain’s door was closed. Bobby wasn’t in the kitchen fixing breakfast when they climbed up the loft stairs, and Hen was nowhere in sight, so Buck figured it was safe to assume they were both in there. Ravi was back, though, and sitting with Albert at one of the tables, heads bent together while talking about something.

“I just want you to at least think about it, Rav,” Albert was saying.

“Think about what?” Buck asked, dropping down to sit next to them.

“I got offered a spot as a peer instructor at the academy for the semester,” Ravi told him. “But I don’t know if I’m going to take them up on it. It would mean leaving the One Eighteen until I was done.”

“I think you should do it,” Eddie said, slapping him on the shoulder.

“Yeah, man,” Buck chimed in. “It’s never too early to get on the brass’ good side.”

He must have let too much bitterness leak into the words, because the statement had the other three all grimacing slightly.

“I’ve been trying to convince him to do it since he told me,” Albert said, apparently deciding to skip over the potential conversational pitfall. “But he’s being difficult.”

“Shut up, Al,” Ravi grumbled, ducking his head.

“If you’re worried about your spot here, you know pops will keep it open for you,” Buck assured him.

“And if you’re worried about not seeing us as much, well. Abuela’s been nagging at us to go out more,” Eddie offered. “And Buck’s mentioned doing something with the two of you.”

“I’m gonna tell Abuela that you said she nagged.”

“I’m her favorite, remember?” his husband said, smirking down at him. “She’ll never believe you.”

“Listen here, you-”

He was interrupted by Bobby and Hen finally joining them, the latter grinning broadly. She looked so pleased with herself as Bobby announced to everyone that she’d be the substitute captain while he was gone. Buck did his best to smash down the ugly feelings that were trying to set down roots within him. Hen was his friend, and she definitely deserved the spot more than him, so he was going to be the biggest, most supportive person in the entire damn station.

Buck glanced at Eddie, standing beside him, and chewed on his lip in thought.

If Ravi was going to be helping at the academy, then their shift will already be down another person. And with Hen acting as captain, they’d be down a paramedic, with Eddie having to aid Albert.

But what if…

“Hey, cap?” Buck said, catching Bobby’s attention. “Can I talk to you about something?”

He was going to be the biggest, most supportive person in the entire damn station.

Chapter 4: This Delicate Balance

Chapter Summary

In which Buck manipulates, teases his sister, and then starts a lie.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

September

Ravi switches over to the peer instructor position at the fire academy at the beginning of September. They have a small party on his last shift, even though it’s already guaranteed that he’s coming back. Albert mopes a bit, but Ravi had spent much of his time at the 118 bouncing between A and B shifts, so he gets over it once it’s pointed out to him that it’ll actually be easier to schedule dates with him working regular people hours.

Besides, it was almost inevitable for the probie to be crashing dinners for some of Bobby’s food.

Outside of Ravi’s minor career shift, there was a bit of tension at the station, with people stepping lightly around Buck and casting not-so-subtle looks his way whenever Bobby takes Hen aside to give her a quick rundown of what she’ll be expected to keep up with while he’s gone. Or maybe it was just Buck’s paranoia-fueled imagination. He tried to ignore it either way, choosing instead to focus on his goal of being The Most Supportive.

Bobby had been a bit of a tough sell with his proposal. For good reason. It had taken a lot of wheedling and blatant emotional manipulation - Bobby was still feeling guilty, even though things were not his fault - backed by the sound logic of how A shift was going to be down two whole people while the captain was away. And then maybe a quick reminder that, while they could get a random floater to fill the spot, the last one had actively tried to murder Buck.

That last bit may have actually been a tad bit too much, with Bobby looking like he was contemplating calling off the cruise altogether. But in the end, Buck walked out of the office victorious, with Bobby promising to see what he could do.

It was meant to be a surprise for Hen, so it had to be hush-hush. But all things considered, Buck figured it was best to not surprise Eddie, as well. It took him some time to actually get the courage to tell him, putting it off until Bobby told him that he’d managed it, and his husband had not been very excited about the news.

The exact opposite of excited, actually.

“You asked Bobby to switch Chimney back to A shift?”

They were in their detached garage, which they’d converted into a makeshift home gym. There wasn’t much to it yet, mostly the workout equipment that had once been at Buck’s loft, but there was a treadmill on Eddie’s Christmas list. The two of them had been spotting each other, and Buck hoped the endorphins would make his husband a bit more amenable to things.

“Yes,” Buck confirmed with a nod, handing Eddie a towel to wipe the sweat off his face.

“The Chimney who punched you?”

“Yes.”

“The guy who I physically threw out of our home,” Eddie continued, strangling the towel between his hands as he sat on the weight bench. “After our son and I walked in on him punching my husband. That Chimney?”

“Yes,” he repeated for the third time. “That Chimney. The same one you’ve gone bowling with and sat next to at the dinner table, without any problems.”

“Why?” the other man demanded.

“Because I promised myself I would be nothing but supportive towards Hen,” Buck explained.

“And how, exactly, does being supportive translate into forcing me to spend twenty-four hours beside the person who punched my husband?”

“Yeah, he punched me , and I’m not holding nearly as big of a grudge about it as you are.”

“You also cleaned his apartment for him right after,” Eddie reminded him, scowling at the memory. “So I really don’t think you’re a good judge on this.”

“This isn’t about me, anyways,” Buck huffed, crossing his arms and digging in his heels. “Or you. This is about Hen. I remember how stressed I was while covering for pops, and how much I depended on you, my husband and partner and bestest friend in the world.”

“Laying it on a bit thick there,” Eddie drawled. “And I think Hen’ll live for the week that Bobby’s gone. It’s only, what? Three shifts?”

“Three shifts where you’d be stuck helping Albert, probably.”

“Hen won’t suddenly stop being a paramedic just because she’s changed helmets.”

“Yeah, well.” Buck slid into Eddie’s lap, straddling him on the bench. “The thing is, I kinda asked pops last month. And the shifts have already been switched around.”

“Ay, Dios mio,” his husband groaned, sounding very much like Abuela when she was exasperated with everyone, and lightly headbutted Buck’s chest. “So my opinion on this doesn’t matter at all, then.”

“Your opinion always matters,” he assured him. “But no, not it doesn’t.”

The blunt statement had Eddie bursting into laughter, burying it in Buck’s shirt.

“I don’t expect you to act like everything’s how it was a year ago,” Buck assured him. “But, just… Hen’s been getting punished just as much, and she had nothing to do with anything. I just thought it would be nice for her to have her best friend working with her again. Like I do.”

“You’ve made your point, mi sol,” Eddie sighed, leaning back to look him in the eye. “You can stop beating the horse.”

“Are you sure?” Buck asked, grinning down at him. “Because I was thinking we should go take a shower, and I could continue making my, uh. Oral argument.”

Buck found himself on the floor, toppled over backwards in his husband’s hurry to get to his feet, before being dragged laughing into the house.

Contrary to popular belief, Buck and Eddie did not do everything together. They merely did most things together. On occasion, before the move, Eddie would go visit his abuela while Maddie came over to have some sibling bonding time with Buck. Now, Buck went to Maddie and Chimney’s apartment, while Eddie stayed with Abuela or took her out somewhere.

That was the case currently. Buck was watching his sister drink a bit too much wine, while Eddie was home baking cookies. It was something he and Abuela had started doing ever since the Unfortunate News earlier in the year. Not that Buck complained, because who the hell would complain about homemade cookies? An idiot, that’s who.

“Everything is so shiny,” Maddie was saying, gesturing with her wine glass. Buck was sitting on the floor in front of the couch, because Jee-Yun had toddled over and pretty much demanded that he join her in smashing her Mega Bloks together into various vague shapes. “And it’s brighter. Like, the call floor at the old center was kinda… Dark? But this one is all white and… Shiny.”

“You said shiny twice.”

“Because it’s very shiny!”

“So we like the new call center,” Buck guessed. He held up one of the blocks. “Hey Jee, what color is this?”

Jee stared at it for a moment, before happily chirping out,

“Red!”

“Yes it is! You’re so smart,” he said, holding out his other hand for a high five. She did it, giving a happy little giggle and a dance as she did so. “It’s also rojo, in Spanish. Can you say rojo?”

“Ro-ho.”

“Close enough.”

“Between you and the Lees, she’s going to be throwing multilingual tantrums,” Maddie complained. “Thanks for that.”

“Y’know, learning extra languages at an early age promotes critical thinking and problem solving skills, plus it-”

“Yeah, yeah, yeah,” his sister interrupted, waving her hand in his face. Buck blew a raspberry at her. “We were talking about me and my things, Buck, not about how my daughter is going to be valedictorian by kindergarten.”

“I’m pretty sure your daughter counts as-”

“Shutty! Howie, more wine!”

“Why don’t you just drink Buck’s?” Chimney asked from the kitchen, where he was trying to make dinner. Buck wasn’t certain how much his skills had improved in the years since he used to pass Bobby’s cooking off as his own, but he assumed he was at least as good as Eddie. Not that it mattered, because Buck was planning on going home for dinner.

“Because he’s drinking it!” Maddie called back, and then slapped Buck’s shoulder until he picked up his glass and took a sip. “See?”

“Okay, okay.” Chimney grabbed the open wine bottle from off the counter and carried it over to them, refilling Maddie’s glass as she held it out for him. “As you wish.”

“Thank you, farm boy.”

Buck glanced between them, before slowly reaching over to cover Jee’s eyes and ears, making her giggle and shake her head.

“Is this like, a roleplaying thing?” he asked. “Should I take Jee-Yun for a sleepover or…?”

Maddie slapped him again, this time on the head, as Chimney tutted on his way back to the kitchen.

“As I was saying,” she said, and then, “What was I saying?”

“You were telling me about work, before we got sidetracked by languages, wine, and weird pet names.”

“You’ve seen The Princess Bride !” Chimney snapped from across the room. “I made you and Eddie both watch it during lockdown!”

“Inconceivable,” Buck retorted, and then ducked as a spatula flew at him. “I’m not picking that up!”

“Anyway, the new dispatch center is very shiny-”

“So you’ve said.”

“Well I’m saying it again,” Maddie sniped. “We got a new team of dispatchers, too. Got to watch Sue nail the traditional welcoming speech.”

“How many of them are gonna stick it out?” Buck asked her.

“Probably about half of them.” His sister sighed. “Oh! One of them knew my name!”

“They knew your name?” Was he going to have to ask Athena to run background checks on the new people before she left for her cruise? Because he would, and she would.

“Yeah! Apparently the group I started with is famous! What with the trial-by-earthquake and all.”

“That and I’m sure you’re practically running the place,” he joked, shoving lightly at her leg. “You’re a Buckley, after all.”

“Clearly being a Buckley has nothing to do with it, Buckaroo,” Chimney chimed in from the kitchen. “‘Else you’d be running the station.”

And Buck knew that Chimney didn’t actually mean anything by it. He knew that sometimes - a lot of times - the man’s humor just landed wrong. But knowing that didn’t change the way it felt like the words punched him in the solar plexus. He hid the way he knew he flinched by messing with some of the Mega Bloks, making sure his voice was steady before answering.

“I’m a Diaz now, y’know,” he replied, not looking up from his niece. “Maybe that’s why.”

“Well, I’m not running anything, either,” Maddie said, and he was thankful that she was on the far side of tipsy, or else she’d have surely noticed something was wrong the moment he spoke. “I leave all of that up to Josh and Sue.”

Buck made a show of pulling out his phone and looking at it, before leaning in to kiss Jee-Yun on the head and standing up.

“It’s about time for me to be heading home,” he said, and it was mostly true. “There’ll be a lot of pouting if I’m not home in time for dinner.”

“Chris?” his sister guessed with a smirk, standing up to hug him goodbye.

“Chris, and Eddie,” he told her. “And Abuela.”

“You good to drive?” Chimney asked.

“I took one sip of wine,” Buck told him. “I’m fine.”

“You were supposed to be getting wine drunk with me,” Maddie complained, plopping back down on the couch. “Chimney won’t, and it’s lame to get wine drunk alone.”

“She’s off tomorrow, right?”

“She’s always off the day after your wine nights together,” Chimney told him.

“Good, good.” Buck slipped on his shoes and grabbed his jacket before heading for the door. “See you at work on Monday, Chim.”

“Yeah, you too,” Chimney called after him, distracted. And then, just before the door closed behind him, he heard, “Wait. Are you working on Monday, too!?”

Buck wasn’t very comfortable about lying to Eddie. Buck wasn’t very good at lying to Eddie, or anyone, really. But, somehow, he managed to come up with one - that May needed him for something - and make it believable enough that his husband let him go without any question. 

The place he actually went to was in the exact opposite direction of USC, towards Santa Monica and LAX. When he parked in front of it, there was only one other car, though the lights were still shining brightly inside. Even though he knew he was supposed to be there, he still felt incredibly nervous as he opened the door and stepped through.

There was a woman in yoga pants and a sleeveless tee leaning over a desk just inside. Her hair was red, more auburn than the strawberry blonde of Taylor and Abby, and she smiled when she looked up.

“Hi, I, uh. I’m Buck,” he said, holding out his hand.

“I’m Myra,” she told him, shaking it. “Though you probably knew that.” Neither of them looked at the sign on the front window reading ‘Myra’s Dance Studio,’ though Buck knew they both wanted to. “You’re the one who wanted the private lessons.”

“Less want- Well, yeah, I want the lessons, but I’m a firefighter,” he told her, feeling the incessant urge to explain himself. “I work odd hours, so I wouldn’t be able to make it to the regular classes.”

“It’s fine,” she said, stepping back from him a bit. The woman wasn’t any taller than Maddie. “The regular classes are mostly made up of couples learning together.”

“My husband already knows how to dance,” he said, and then it was like a switch flipped with Myra. He hadn’t even noticed that her smile was a bit plasticky until it became more real, and it seemed like her entire being relaxed, tension he hadn’t seen before leaking out of her.

“Most people learn before the wedding,” she said, and even her voice was a bit friendlier as her hands moved with the words.

“The wedding was a surprise,” he said, rubbing his neck. “I- I mean, he asked me to marry him. But then I surprised him with the wedding. There was only like a month to set everything up.”

“You surprised him with a wedding,” she repeated.

“You’re judging me, aren’t you?”

“Heavens, no,” she lied. “What did you do for your first dance?”

“Stepped on his toes. A lot.”

The answer had Myra throwing her head back, laughing.

“We’ll work on that, come on,” she said, gesturing for him to follow her into the back, where there was a large and empty room. The back wall was one giant mirror. “I would ask why your husband isn’t teaching you, or isn’t here with you, but I’m guessing-”

“It’s a surprise, yeah. Our first anniversary is on Christmas-”

“You surprised him with a wedding on Christmas?”

“Why does everyone say it like that?” Buck asked, rolling his eyes. “But yeah, it’s… He likes to dance. And I can’t dance. And I know it has to bother him some, so…”

“That’s sweet,” she told him. “We’ll start with the waltz. It’s simple, just four repeating steps. Since you’re surprising your husband, I’ll show you both leading and following.”

She stood in the middle of the room, arms out and holding an imaginary partner.

“You hold your arms like this,” Myra instructed. “Now watch my feet.”

And then she began to dance.

Chapter End Notes

Myra, when when she finds herself alone with this huge, tall, buff guy: "I'm in danger."
Myra, when that same guy starts gushing about his husband: "I've only had him for a day and a half, but if anything happened to him I would kill everyone in this room and then myself."

Chapter 5: Captain Hen

Chapter Summary

Hen's first day as captain, and phone calls from Florida.

“They put him in a medically induced coma, to give his brain time to heal.”

“How bad is it?”

“Bad. They just don’t know how bad yet.”

“How’s ma handling it?”

“She’s… She’s handling it.”

“And what about you, pops? I’ve heard how things were when Mrs. Carter came down.”

“I’m doing just fine, Buck. And shouldn’t you be calling her ‘grandma’?”

“Ha! I dare you to tell her that she’s a great-grandmother to someone her grandson’s age.”

“Athena already has one parent in the hospital, let’s not put them both in there…”

Athena and Bobby didn’t end up making their cruise. The day they were supposed to leave, as Athena was on the phone with her parents, her father had a stroke and drove his car into the house. Suffice it to say, priorities were rearranged and they flew to Belleville, Florida, instead.

At least they’d be close enough that they could, possibly, swing it to see Harry in Miami. Silver linings.

Bobby was, unfortunately, not a miracle worker. He managed to get Chimney switched over to A-shift for a few days, but a couple of shifts - Hen’s first day, for example - Albert was switching with Chimney instead of his brother simply joining them. Which Buck decided was maybe for the best, after giving it some thought. He hadn’t actually considered how things would work out with Albert and Chimney working together under Hen.

One big change at a time.

The first shift didn’t start all that bad, honestly. Hen was still aglow from being trusted with the role of captain, and was ecstatic with the surprise of working with Chimney again. The way she laughed and charged the man for a hug when he arrived had Buck even more certain that it was the right idea. He always felt awful for the way she was collateral damage in what was, for all intents and purposes, a familial spat.

Hen’s very first official call as substitute captain was explosive. Or, rather, it was a call about an explosion. Multiple explosions, actually, from someone’s husband trying to get rid of a rodent in their backyard. Chimney rode in the ambulance instead of the truck, either unwilling to suffer the awkwardness of being in a small, enclosed space with Buck and Eddie, or simply because that’s what he did on B-shift now.

When they arrived, it was pretty easy to figure out which house it was, because it was the one with the audience. All the neighbors were outside, either on their porches or on the sidewalk, gawking and rubbernecking. At least they had the sense to get out of the way of the vehicles.

A woman in her pajamas came rushing out of the backyard when they arrived, a scowling man not far behind. Both of them looked rather banged up: she had blood on one side of her face and he was rather singed, with cuts all over one of his arms.

“Oh, thank God!” she exclaimed. “My husband tried to go shock and awe on a gopher. I don’t know if he’s hurt or not.”

“The gopher?” Chimney asked, looking between them in confusion.

“No, not the gopher.”

“Holly, get them out of here,” the man demanded.

“Is that your husband?” Buck asked, mentally labeling him as The Dick. “He seems pretty banged up.”

“No, not him,” Holly said, sounding like she was getting fed up with them.

So if the ‘him’ she was worried about wasn’t a gopher, and it wasn’t her husband, then…

Buck glanced at Eddie and saw the moment his husband decided that they were not paid enough for this.

“Hey, Cap!” Eddie called.

“Don’t call me that,” Hen said, power walking towards them. “Ma’am, who exactly do you think is hurt here?”

“My neighbor,” Holly said, looking nervous. “Gary.”

As a unit, the four of them looked towards the utterly undamaged house next door.

“His house looks fine,” Hen pointed out to her.

“He wasn’t over there.”

“Then where was he?”

The question had Holly unable to meet any of their eyes, glancing about anxiously, instead. Her husband - aka the Dick - looked at her expectedly, like he knew the answer and was simply waiting for her to confirm it. Finally, she said,

“There’s a tunnel…”

“I’ll get the shovel,” Buck sighed, turning and heading for the truck. He got back as Holly led them all to the backyard, which was cratered with dirt and rock and wood and plant matter sprayed everywhere, managing to catch the end of her explanation about how she and Gary had been having an affair behind her husband’s back.

“He was under there,” she told them, pointing at the crater.

Chimney walked to it and bent over at the waist for a better look.

“A tunnel of… Lust?” he quipped.

“You El Chapoed your affair,” Buck said, leaning on the shovel. He was honestly impressed with the commitment.

“We had no other choice!” the cheating wife exclaimed. “My husband has cameras everywhere.”

They all looked around, spotting them now that they were looking. It did very much look like the Dick had eyes on everything, but still.

“It would have been easier to take your husband whale watching,” he said. The statement had everyone staring at him in confusion, except for Eddie, who barked out a laugh. Which was the entire point, so Buck counted it as a win.

“Alright,” Hen said, shaking her head slightly. “Chimney, see if you can access the tunnel from inside.”

“Copy that, Cap,” the paramedic said, and then apologized when Hen glared at him.

“Buck, start digging up out here.”

“Aye, Cap,” he said, grinning cheerfully when she turned her glare on him.

“Eddie, Benjamin looks like he took a hit. Check him out,” he heard Hen say as he started digging into the dirt. Then, at a quieter volume, “Find out what he knows.”

“The entrance to the tunnel is in my closet,” Holly told them. “Under the rollaway shoe rack, hidden beneath the carpet.”

Buck listened as he worked, to Hen thanking her for the info and relayed it over the radio, and he eavesdropped on Eddie checking the Dick - real name Benjamin - for a concussion. He was doing well enough to make jokes, apparently, since his answers had Eddie laughing.

Buck couldn’t decide if he was on the Dick’s side on this or not. On the one hand, bombing his own yard to either confirm or stop the affair seemed a bit overkill. On the other, Buck had no idea how he would react if he found out Eddie was cheating on him. Maybe he’d turn his own backyard into a minefield as well, or - and this was more likely - spend the rest of eternity as a recluse, refusing to leave his room.

Luckily, he would never have to find out.

“Hey Cap,” Chimney radioed, and Buck paused in the shoveling to listen better. “I found him. He’s conscious, but his pulse is really low. He’s in one piece, but I’m gonna need a hand getting him out of here.”

Hen looked at Buck, who got ready to drop the shovel and run over to help, but then her gaze moved on.

“Jones?”

The other firefighter looked surprised to be called on, but he nodded gamely and hurried into the house, dropping his own shovel as he went.

“Hen, there’s something else!” Chimney broke in before Buck could even begin to figure out what that had been about. “Gary’s got nails in his shoulder.”

“Shrapnel?” Eddie asked. “Against gophers?”

“Yeah.” Benjamin the Dick sounded unrepentant. “The rodent had it coming.”

They paused the digging, a bit concerned that it would cause things to collapse on Chimney and Jones as they worked to get Gary out. Instead, Hen instructed Buck to patch up Holly’s face while Eddie wrapped the Dick’s arm, and had the others fetch the gurney from the ambulance. When they came out with Gopher Gary he was being half-carried by Jones, blood running all down one arm and nails sticking through the bandages covering his shoulder.

Holly fretted over the guy the entire way to the gurney, and Benjamin the Dick glared daggers at them both.

“You knew,” Hen stated, staring at him. “Didn’t you?”

“I suspected,” he confessed. “I didn’t know for sure until she-” He shot a venomous look at his wife. “-called Nine-One-One.”

“Eddie, zip-tie his hands.”

“Wait, what?” The guy was bewildered as Eddie pulled flex cuffs out of his turnout coat and grabbed his hands, wrapping the plastic around them. “Ow, what? No. What are you- Ow! What are you doing?”

“Sir, you’re under arrest,” Hen informed him, grabbing her radio. “Dispatch, this is Captain One-Eighteen. We need a black-and-white at our current location. Our backyard accident seems to be an attempted murder, and we’ve apprehended a suspect.”

“How can you-? How are you allowed to do this?” Benjamin sputtered.

“All LAFD personnel are sworn peace officers,” Eddie explained, sounding smug, and Buck really wanted to make out with him right then and there. “It’s been that way for about, what? A hundred years?”

“That sounds about right,” Buck said, nodding.

“See?” Hen looked very smug, as well. They were all feeling smug today, apparently. Except Benjamin the Dick. He was looking like, well. Like he tried to murder his wife’s lover and was now being arrested for it. “You’ve learned two things today.”

As they waited for a cop to show up, Eddie was put on guard duty and Buck helped put the gear up. By the time officer Williams arrived, Gary had been taken away in the ambulance with Holly riding along.

When they climbed into the engine to head back to the station, Buck pulled out his phone and pulled up his Amazon app.

“What are you looking up?” Eddie asked, craning his neck to see. “Zip-ties?”

“Pops said we’re not allowed to bring department gear home for personal use,” he explained, putting the first ones he saw into the cart. “So I’m ordering some.”

“Why?”

“For personal use, Eds.” He waggled his eyebrows, just so the point got across better.

Hen’s laughter almost covered up his husband’s groan of, “oh my god.” Almost.

“I swear, if I didn’t have Bobby here with me, I’d- I don’t know what I’d do.”

“Things going that bad?”

“Mama’s in denial, and she clearly doesn’t want us here. Accused us of invading her home.”

“Maybe it’d be easier if you stayed at a hotel?”

“Bobby offered, but we’re just going to pull out the trundle bed and set up the guest room ourselves.”

“Is that what pops is doing now?”

“No, he’s getting the car out of the house.”

“It’s still there? I guess that makes sense, it probably wasn’t high on her list of priorities.”

“Mama said that Daddy would take care of it when he got home.”

“She knows he had a stroke, right?”

“She keeps insisting that he’ll be fine.”

“And I’m absolutely certain that he will be.”

“That’s sweet of you, Buckaroo, but… Anyway, how’s Hen holding up?”

“She is absolutely perfect. The best captain ever.”

“Okay, now tell me the truth.”

“I don't know, ma. I’m just gonna sound bitter or jealous if I say anything…”

“But?”

“But nothin’. I ain’t no snitch.”

“Buck.”

“It’s just- Maybe if this was next week, instead, things would be better? But she has that final at the end of the week, right? Whenever we aren’t on a call, she’s burying herself in study guides and flashcards. And now she has all the station paperwork to worry about on top of that.”

“You’re worried it’s too much.”

“I’m worried she’s gonna crash and burn.”

Buck would never, ever disparage Hen’s ability to do anything. If anyone ever spoke badly of her, he’d be one of the first to throw hands. After five years and change of working with her, he knew exactly what she was capable of.

But he also knew that Hen was human.

He hadn’t lied to Athena. If anything, he might’ve understated things. Because quite frankly, Buck wasn’t certain that Hen had remembered to schedule time to breathe. He was no stranger to a structured lifestyle; Eddie enforced one at home, using Chris as an excuse, but he knew it was because they’d both figured out how much it helped Buck. Hen, though, was maybe taking it a bit too far.

Everything the substitute captain did at the moment was scheduled. An alert would ping on her phone, and she’d drop what she was doing to pull out study materials. And then, when the next alert pinged, she’d switch gears to study something else. The only time she ignored them was when they were on a call. Honestly, Buck wouldn’t be surprised if she had alarms for potty breaks and hugging Denny. Buck would be very surprised if there wasn’t one in there somewhere for a five minute crying session, because there’s no way she was going to get through this without some tears being shed.

Unless she did that during the aforementioned pre-scheduled potty breaks. It was probably more efficient that way.

All of that was without taking into account the paperwork that came with being captain, even if only for a day or three. There wasn’t so much that it was overwhelming, but it was enough that it could absolutely ruin Hen’s delicate house of cards. Even worse when one took into account all the extra paperwork that came into play when they arrested someone.

Buck was fairly certain that Hen would have let Benjamin the Dick walk, if she’d known ahead of time.

All of that was why, when dinner was done and everyone else was busy either cleaning up or entertaining themselves, he snuck into the captain’s office. There was a minor worry that he shouldn’t leave Eddie and Chimney alone together unsupervised, but his husband was a big boy, he could manage. The forms he needed were already sitting out on Bobby’s desk, as if Hen had pulled them out and then got distracted before she could start filling them in.

Buck didn’t sit down in Bobby’s chair. He hadn’t done that even when he was the substitute captain. Instead, he pulled open the bottom drawer of the cabinet for the hidden stash of clipboards that Bobby thought Buck didn’t know about and grabbed a pen from the cup by the monitor, before settling into one of the chairs for ‘guests.’

He was halfway through the forms, leaving empty the spots that required a signature, when a voice had him jumping in his seat.

“What are you doing in here?” Hen demanded. She dumped her bag and bundled up flashcards in the empty seat beside him. It was a frighteningly large bundle. “Are those the forms I had sitting out?”

“They might be.” Buck shrugged. “Figured I’d fill them out, since I had the time.”

“You’re not the acting captain.”

“Thanks, Cap, I hadn’t noticed,” he drawled, doing his best to keep from snapping at her. The way she winced told him that he wasn’t as successful as he hoped. Or that might have been from being called ‘Cap.’ “I know how to fill these out just as well as you, though, and I don’t have the next week of my life scheduled out to the minute.”

As if on cue, her phone chimed with another reminder. Hen didn’t even bother pulling it out to see what she was being told to study.

“We haven’t had time to talk recently, have we?” she asked, walking around the desk to sit down in Bobby’s chair. She looked at ease, like she was supposed to be there. “Not an actual talk.”

“You’ve been too busy cramming.”

“You’re right,” she said, and then, “Are you alright?”

“Is this because you saw me trip on the stairs earlier? Because Eddie caught me before I face-planted.”

“I meant, are you alright with this? With me being…” She trailed off, but Buck knew what she was asking.

“Why wouldn’t I be okay with it?” he asked.

“You were supposed to be doing this, and then… You weren’t. And now I’m doing this.”

“It’s fine, Hen,” he said, giving her a reassuring smile. “There’s no hard feelings.”

“If you’re sure.”

“I’m sure.”

“Alright then.”

There was an awkward few minutes of silence, where Buck went back to filling in the forms and Hen sat there, playing with a pen. He thought she was going to get out one of her study guides, but she didn’t. Instead, she started talking again.

“How’s it going?” she asked. “With the adoption process? You’re in your new house, you’ve got room. Have the two of you looked into it more?”

“There’s an orientation we have to do,” he told her, scribbling away as he did so. He was almost through the forms. “And then we gotta submit a written application before- I’m pretty sure it’s the same as what you and Karen had to do to become foster parents.”

“It probably is,” she said, nodding. “Do you know what age you’re hoping for?”

“Eddie and I haven’t actually talked about that, yet.”

“Buck.”

“What am I supposed to say, Hen?” he asked, dropping the clipboard in his lap. “Oh, sorry kid, you’re too old to have a family? Literally the only thing we’ve decided on is that they can’t cause problems with Christopher, which is pretty much just. Duh.”

“So you and Eddie, you’d both be fine with getting a, I don’t know. A senior in high school?” Hen asked, propping her head up on her fist.

“Yeah?” Buck shrugged. “If that’s who needs a family, that’s who needs a family. I mean, sure, we've been envisioning someone Jee-Yun's age, or Nia's, but we aren't going to slam the door in a kid's face.”

“Chamomile delivery!” Chimney’s voice came from the doorway, and Buck twisted around to see him leaning through, a pair of mugs in his hands. “Oh, I thought you’d be hitting the books. Am I interrupting something?”

“We were just talking,” Buck told him, putting the clipboard on the desk in front of Hen and getting to his feet. “You just need to put your signature down wherever it says.”

“Thank you, Buck,” she said, smiling up at him.

“Anytime, Hen.”

He stepped around Chimney, who was glancing between them curiously, and headed for the door. Behind him, he heard Hen say,

“Hand me that tea and sit down, Chim. You’re gonna quiz me with those flashcards there.”

Chapter 6: The Secret to Happiness

Chapter Summary

Unhappiness at the Happiness Convention

Chapter Notes

Happy New Years!

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Hen’s second shift as captain went about as well as the first. It was thankfully only a twelve-hour one, but Buck could definitely still see the cracks starting to form as the stress began to eat at her. At one point, she managed to fall asleep in the captain’s seat of the engine on the way back from a call. At another, she started taunting the Dalmatian statue someone had placed by the couches, after Buck spent one shift too many trying to convince Bobby to get a station dog.
