Buck tried to help with the workload where he could, but there was only so much he could do. Like the 902Ms, for example. Those were part of her duty as a paramedic, and Buck couldn’t do much of anything for her there. And she was falling behind on them, he knew, because the time she usually spent filling them out was now spent quizzing herself on medical terminology and scenarios.

Besides talking to inanimate objects - not that Buck or Eddie really had room to judge her for that - there were other signs of the stress wearing down on her. According to Chimney, she spent an entire ambulance ride interrogating their patient on his dental health, because she had just reviewed the side effects of a medication taken to prevent transplant rejection.

He was being taken to the hospital for the scissors sticking out of his leg.

Although, apparently watching Hen’s quick descent into madness was a good bonding activity. Whatever awkwardness lingered between Eddie and Chimney, it was overshadowed and washed away by their mutual concern for their friend. It wasn’t quite to the point that things were how they’d been a year or two ago, but Buck felt like it was getting close.

Hen’s third shift would be the first shift Albert and Chimney would be working together. It was also scheduled for after her final. He was hoping that getting past that, and having another paramedic on hand, would relieve her of much of the stress. Hoping, but not holding his breath.

At the very least, maybe she’d have time to eat something between shifts that wasn’t from a drive through. The inside of her car, when he passed by in the parking lot, was starting to remind Buck of those times he was essentially living out of his jeep.

“She’s talking to the dog, pops.”

“The Dalmatian Porter brought in forever ago?”

“That’s who got it? But yeah, that one. I think I caught her in like, a staring contest with it at one point.”

“What did you do?”

“Walked away. And then we dumped popcorn on her.”

“Why?”

“Chimney and Eddie both thought it would be funny.”

“...You know what, I’m just going to be glad that everyone’s getting along. Everyone is behaving, right?”

“Why do you make it sound like I’m babysitting a bunch of kids? No, better question, why am I the babysitter? This sounds like something you should be asking Hen.”

“I would, but when I talked to Hen last, she mostly just ranted about… I think it was gingivitis.”

“You must have caught her right after the guy with the scissors in his leg.”

“How do scissors relate to gingivitis?”

“I don’t know, but Chimney said she was really insistent that the guy talk to a dentist.”

At the start of the school year, Buck had joined the PTA for Chris’ school. Or rather the PTO, Organization not Association, because apparently there was a difference. Joined it, dragged Eddie to the first meeting in August, and quickly realized that a lot of the other parents there were utterly insufferable. Especially the mother who tried to flirt with Eddie with really, really bad Spanish right in front of him. And then she cast aspersions upon Buck’s ability to bring something homemade to the first bake sale of the year.

Suffice it to say, if there had been a fire, he would have happily tripped Lyndsay-with-a-Y on the way out the door.

None of that actually matters, though. What matters is that Eddie absolutely hated the whole experience and vowed to Buck that he would never go to another one. “No matter what,” Eddie had told him, which sounded like a challenge at the time. It still did, but it was also very, very convenient as an excuse to go somewhere without him.

On the same day that Hen was taking her test, Buck told Eddie he was going to a PTO meeting. His husband practically ran away from him, probably to hide behind Abuela, and it left him free to drive to Myra’s. It worked extremely well as an excuse, but it was just too bad that Eddie knew the PTO only met once or twice a month.

(Buck had actually missed September’s meeting because of work, and was absolutely certain that Lyndsay-with-a-Y would be unbearably smug about it come October’s.)

When Buck got to the studio, there was a tap dancing class that was finishing up, so he found a seat and waited. It sounded fun, actually, if only as a way to further annoy everyone at the station. Not that he would ever intentionally go out of his way to aggravate his coworkers or anything.

He didn’t have to wait long, thankfully, before there was a parade of people clacking into the front lobby. Some paused to change into normal shoes, others just continued straight on to their cars, metal tapping against pavement the entire way. Myra came out last and plopped down behind the front desk, propping her feet up so she could take off her own tap shoes, chatting with her students as they left.

“Between you and me,” she said, once the door closed behind the last of them, “I absolutely hate tap dancing. Always end up with a headache at the end.”

As if to underscore what she was saying, she reached into one of the desk drawers to pull out a bottle of tylenol, which she dry swallowed like a pro.

“Why do you teach it, then?” Buck asked.

“People pay me to learn it,” Myra answered, placing the shoes down beside her chair. From under the desk she pulled out, against all assumptions-

“Are you putting on boots?” he blurted out, despite knowing damn well that she was.

“Yup. They’re steel-toed,” she said as she tied the laces.

“Why?”

“Because teaching you without the proper footwear is an OSHA violation.” Myra stood up and headed back into the dance studio proper, leaving him to scramble out of his seat and after her. “My toes are still bruised from having a two-hundred pound man stomp on them for an hour.”

“To be fair, I did warn you.”

“You did.” She led him to the middle of the room before turning, grabbing his arms and positioning him the way she wanted. “We’ll go over what you remember of the waltz, to start with. I’ll lead first, and then we’ll switch. If I’m very lucky, you won’t spend the entire time stepping on my feet or tripping over your own.”

“You were nicer last time,” Buck complained.

“Last time I wasn’t waiting for the Tylenol to kick in,” she retorted. “Now: one, two, three, four…”

Hen looked dead on her feet at the start of the shift after her final. Buck had assumed that, after finishing it, she’d go home and crash until her alarm woke her for work. But that clearly had not been the case, because he watched her refill her coffee twice as she worked through a pile of 902Ms. He didn’t want to know how much coffee she’d had before he and Eddie got in.

Instead, he chose to focus on Albert and Chimney. He wasn’t sure what he was expecting, considering the way Chimney had been when his brother first showed up in their lives. The insecurity he had shown that had been so very reminiscent of Buck’s own upon Eddie’s introduction to the team. The memory of Chimney telling Albert that he couldn’t take them, too, rang a bit sharper in the current context.

So yeah, Buck tried to pay attention to the brothers and hoped that old anger didn’t rear its head in the new context. He knew that the older Han had accused the younger of replacing him, way back when his brother had told him about getting paramedic certified and transferring stations, Buck just wasn’t sure if Chimney still felt that way. The last thing they needed was for the two of them to get into it while their acting captain shambled about like an extra from the Walking Dead.

Luckily, though, there didn’t seem to be a reason for Buck to be worried. On calls, Albert happily followed Chimney’s lead just like he did Hen’s. At the station and in the trucks, they sniped at each other a bit and generally acted like they did outside of work, which… Okay, that might actually become a problem, Buck wasn’t sure, but that literally was not his problem.

He’d just let Hen handle it if they crossed the line from Friendly Sibling Teasing to something not-so-friendly.

(Did they still have any popcorn left, or did all of it end up dumped on Hen?)

Part way through the shift, they were called to an accident at the WestPlex Convention Center for an accident of some sort. When they got there, people covered in cement dust were stumbling out the front doors, and inside they found a walkway had collapsed. There were people sticking out from under the debris and sparks falling from the ceiling in curtains.

“Buck, Chim, you’re with him,” Hen ordered, pointing at the first person pinned by rubble. “Eddie, Albert, you get these two. The rest of you, let’s get these people to the treatment area.”

Buck got to work clearing as much of the wreckage as he could as Chimney started checking their guy. Lev, according to his nametag. He kept one ear open as Eddie and Albert treated the other two, Ronny and Angelo. The three were apparently friends, because one of them started shouting for,

“Lev!”

“Kind of in the middle of something,” Lev groaned, and then, “or I guess, under the middle of something.”

“Vitals are holding,” Chimney said, handing Buck his medical bag. “Let’s run two lines on him, wide open.”

“I can’t feel anything,” Lev complained. “Is that bad?”

“Not necessarily, there could be some swelling causing the numbness,” the paramedic reassured. “Let me take a look.”

Chimney reached a gloved hand under the debris and felt around. Whatever it was he found, his face said it wasn’t good.

“Hey, uh.” Buck cast about for something to say to distract the guy as he pulled on his own gloves. “You guys were attending the convention together?”

“Post-retirement boys’ trip.”

“And here I thought everyone just took up golf when they retired.” He grinned at his husband, who rolled his eyes at him. The other two friends grumbled and complained, though Buck couldn’t really make out their words. 

He split his focus between hooking up the IV and what was going on with the other two. That meant he was only slightly startled when, as Eddie and Albert managed to get their pair unpinned, the two men let out sudden cries of pain. Whatever the cause, Eddie looked worried. Buck managed to catch something about a fibula puncturing someone’s thigh.

“Are we gonna be okay?” Lev asked.

“They’re in great hands,” Buck told him, tearing his attention away from his husband. “And you’re next.”

“Those two,” he said, sounding weaker by the moment, “they’ll kill each other without me.”

“Uh, hey, you never told me why.” Buck tried to ignore the worried glance Chimney gave to the Lifepak tracking Lev’s vitals. “Why a happiness convention?”

“Trying to figure out some stuff,” Lev mumbled. “Thought maybe we’d find an answer here.”

“You guys weren’t happy?”

“You live your whole life, doing everything you’re supposed to do,” he told him. “Marriage, kids. Big house, nice cars. Weekends at the shore.”

“That doesn’t sound too bad,” Buck said, a queasy feeling unsettling his stomach. The guy was pretty much describing his own life.

“It wasn’t,” Lev agreed, gaze trained on the ceiling above. “It’s just this forty year blur. Work and family, never enough time. Until one day work stops and everything finally comes into focus. You’re not sure what you’re looking at, what the point was of… Any of it…”

The words had that queasy feeling settling into a block of ice. It was the life Buck was living right now, the life he’d always chased after. And here Lev was, standing - well, lying - on the other side of it. Looking back on it, and finding it wanting.

A forty year blur?

“Captain, One Eighteen.” Their radios coming to life distracted Buck from the existential crisis looming upon his horizon. “We’ve got another victim on scene. His name is Frankie, says he’s completely buried.”

All of them started looking around, trying to spot Frankie in the mess that had used to be a Happiness Convention. But there was no one sign of another person. And in this mess, if he was completely buried, he could be anywhere.

“Dispatch, need help finding a location,” Hen spoke into the radio.

“He says he was moving some chairs from the ballroom.”

After another moment of looking around, Hen clearly spotted where she needed to go, because she rushed over. After a few moments digging, she found him and called others over to help.

“Dispatch, the victim’s showing signs of a punctured lung,” she said, moving back to give them room to work. “We’ve already got two complicated rescues in progress.”

“The One Thirty-Three’s already en route,” the dispatcher responded. “They’ll be there in twelve minutes.”

That was not enough time, Buck knew that. So did Hen, going by the way her face fell, and so did Chimney, who got up and went over to her. Buck couldn’t hear what they were saying, but he caught Hen gesturing at Lev and at Frankie, looking unsure and upset. Whatever Chimney said in response did not make her feel better, but it did have her coming over to where Buck and Lev were waiting.

“Hey,” Hen greeted, kneeling next to the victim.

“You ready to get this thing off of me?” Lev asked, and Buck did not like the look Hen exchanged with Chimney before she answered.

“There’s a kid buried over there, and I think he’s got a punctured lung,” she told him, and Buck felt his heart drop to join that block of ice now residing in his belly. “I- I need to get him out of there.”

“And you don’t have enough guys.” It wasn’t a question. “Get that kid out, I ain’t going nowhere.”

“Go help the kid,” Hen ordered Chimney, who in turn held his hand out for the hydraulic jack. Buck handed it to him and he rushed off to where Frankie was buried.

Buck’s attention bounced around, from where Eddie and Albert were working to where Chimney and the others were freeing Frankie. He wanted to shout at them to hurry up, to go faster, but he knew they were all going as fast as they could. It was just that, ‘as fast as they could’ simply wasn’t fast enough.

Eddie and Albert got their two separated and stabilized; Chimney got his free and safe.

“We got him!” he cried.

“They saved the kid?” Lev asked, unable to see what was going on with how he was pinned.

“And you’re next,” Hen told him, smiling down at him.

“It’s okay,” Lev breathed out. “I got what I came for… I think I get it now…”

The monitor screamed as it flatlined.

Buck and Hen rushed into motion, grabbing halligans and leveraging the slab off him. Chimney came running back with the jack. They got Lev free, got him bagged, did compressions. 

They did all they could.

It just wasn’t enough.

“Buck’s not been texting us constant updates for a while. Everything okay down there?”

“Yeah… No. There was a call, he lost someone.”

“Ah.”

“Yeah, he and Hen are both taking it hard. He’s been qu- Not talking as much ever since. But I think something else is bothering him, too.”

“What do you mean?”

“You know how he is, when something’s on his mind. He nearly burnt everyone’s dinner. But how’re things over there, ‘Thena? Any news from the doctors?”

“No. We’re just… We’re just waiting.”

“What do you think he meant?” Buck asked, breaking the silence of the locker room. Albert and Chimney had invited them to breakfast, but both Buck and Hen had declined. Eddie was putting their bags in the jeep before coming back, probably with a stop to call Abuela and tell her they were on the way home. “When he said, ‘I get it now.’ His eyes got all bright like he’d just found happiness.”

“I was there,” Hen reminded him. She was putting things into her bag, looking and sounding no more rested than he did.

“Yeah, but, it was like he solved some great mystery,” he told her, sitting down on the bench beside her. “And then he died before he could share it.”

“Losing someone is never easy,” she said.

“To a design flaw.” Buck had seen it on the news the night before. “The hotel put the walkway in a couple years ago and tried to sue the contractor after they found some cracks. But the repairs just…” 

“Fell through the same cracks,” Hen finished for him. “It was bound to come crashing down. But not before Nathan Levinson learned the secret, right? Maybe that’s what we should focus on. He found it before he died.”

“So, what? He spent his whole life trying to figure it out and he finally gets the answer right at the end?” he asked. “What do you think it is? The secret to happiness?”

“Aren’t you happy?”

“I’m married, I have a kid. We spend weekends at the shore,” Buck said, not looking at her, keeping his eyes on the lockers in front of him. “He was married, had kids, spent weekends at the shore. And he wasn’t happy until the very end.”

“You’re afraid you’re going to be like him,” Hen stated, almost delicately.

“He had everything that he thought he wanted, and he still wasn’t happy,” he said. “I just… I just don’t want to wake up forty years from now and realize my whole life was a blur. I don’t want to be retired and going to a Happiness Convention and wondering what the point of it all was, you know?”

Hen opened her mouth to say something, but before she could her phone started ringing. Smiling apologetically at him, she pulled it out and answered.

“Hello?... Hi, Dr. Simmons, this is a surprise.”

Whatever the doctor was telling her, Hen didn’t look happy. She glanced at him before rushing out the locker room doors, which were being held open by a frowning Eddie. Buck hadn’t even heard him show up.

“Hey, everything okay?” Buck asked, standing up and walking towards him.

“Yeah.” Eddie nodded slowly, but he was still frowning. “You ready to go?”

“Yeah…”

Chapter End Notes

Now here's your question: How much of that at the end did Eddie hear?

Chapter 7: Schrodinger's Cat

Chapter Summary

Eddie muses

Eddie tried not to let what he overheard between Buck and Hen get to him. He’d only heard a snippet of conversation, after all. He didn’t have the full context, so he couldn’t just make a snap judgment. He shouldn’t, at least.

Maybe - probably - Buck was just being Buck and overemphasizing with yet another dying old man. It was both a strength and a weakness of his, the way he went into everything with his whole chest and his heart in his hands. Buck could’ve been, had probably been, just putting himself in a stranger’s shoes. It just hadn’t sounded quite like that.

It sounded like Buck wasn’t happy with him, or was afraid he wouldn’t be happy in the future. Which didn’t make sense, because how many times had Buck said that he was living the life he always wanted? His husband had literally once told him, to his face, that he was living the life he always daydreamed about. There were witnesses, even!

(“He had everything that he thought he wanted, and he still wasn’t happy.”)

Okay, alright, what Eddie did hear had been rather damning. But that didn’t have to mean anything! If Buck wasn’t happy with him, then why would he go out of his way to show off the silicone wedding bands he finally got around to ordering? He pulled up his Amazon app and checked their progress on the way home after that shift, cheerfully informing Eddie that they were going to arrive the same day as the Flex Cuffs he impulse bought.

Both rings were bright red, not quite the color of the station trucks, and Buck had been so proud of that fact. He said he wanted them to be as bright as possible, so everyone would see them. He wanted to show off that he was married to Eddie, which meant that he was happy that he was married to Eddie. Happy with Eddie and Christopher. Happy with their home and the family they had built, were building.

Right?

Right.

There was nothing to it, this was just the old insecurities from Shannon popping up. Copeland agreed, when he snagged a chance to call her without Buck knowing about it. She also happily reminded him that it didn’t make his feelings any less valid, and then urged him to talk to Buck. Which…

He was definitely going to do that. Definitely.

At some point.

Back in March, he’d overheard a different conversation, one between Buck and Bobby. In that conversation, Bobby had asked Buck hadn’t asked Eddie about having any more kids. And Buck had told him that, as long as he didn’t ask, then the answer could be anything. How a flipped coin could be either heads or tails, until it landed. And then it would always be one or the other.

Maybe Eddie should stop eavesdropping on his husband’s conversations. Maybe his husband should stop having these kinds of conversations at the station where they could be overheard.

The longer he put off talking to Buck about it, the longer he could continue living in a perfect world where his husband was absolutely happy with their marriage. Conversely, the longer he put it off, the longer he also lived in a world where his husband wasn’t happy with their marriage. A world where both were potentially equally true, which was far more preferable to the possible world where only that latter option was the definitive one.

Schrodinger’s Cat, both alive and dead until he opened that box.

“You found what under the house!?”

“So let me recap,” Ravi said, setting down his drink. They were at Albert’s apartment, having gone over for drinks and take out and whatever video games the younger Han had. Currently, Eddie and Albert were taking their turn at said games. “Hen couldn’t manage to juggle both being captain and medical school, and now she’s probably going to have to retake the year. Meanwhile, Athena’s dad had a stroke and drove his car into his house, and while helping dig up the foundation for repairs, Bobby found a dead body. And the cops think Athena’s dad was the murderer.”

He paused for a moment, before picking his bottle back up and taking a swallow.

“Am I missing anything?” he asked.

“No, you pretty much covered everything,” Eddie told him, tongue sticking out as he tried to murder Albert’s pixelated avatar on the screen. 

“He missed the part where Ma got arrested for interfering with an active investigation,” Buck said. The words were apparently a shock enough to have Albert twist around to stare at him, allowing Eddie to viciously kick his character across the stage.

“Athena got arrested!?”

“She really is your mother,” Eddie said, exuding smugness as he leaned against Buck’s legs. He was sitting on the floor, while Albert was perched on the edge of what had been Eddie’s coffee table. More than a few pieces of furniture in the apartment had once been at Eddie’s house, before they moved and made room for Abuela’s things. “How many times has she gotten after you for doing exactly that?”

“Not that often,” Buck protested, reaching down to play with his husband’s hair. “Twice, maybe. Or- Or three times.”

“Coulda sworn it was more than that.”

“Didn’t she have to stop you from dumpster diving for evidence?” Albert asked, moving to sit next to Ravi and handing him his controller.

“Literally a week after Bobby and David did the same thing,” Eddie helpfully added. “Hurry up and choose a character, Rav.”

“Well I was going to choose the dog,” Ravi grumbled.

“I like the dog, get over it.”

“What’s the gossip over at academy-land?” Buck rested his drink on top of Eddie’s head, grinning at his husband when he ducked and glared at him. “Al hasn’t been sharing.”

“No one’s under investigation for murder,” Ravi told them, glaring at the screen. “Damn it, stop cheating.”

“Being better than you isn’t cheating,” Eddie retorted.

“I knew I shouldn’t have let you two choose the game,” Albert grumbled, sinking down into the couch cushions and leaning against Ravi. “Next time I’ll know better.”

“Yeah, yeah, you’re both grumpy because my husband is awesome,” Buck interrupted, flapping his hand in their direction. “C’mon Rav, give us the hot gos. Spill the tea.”

“You are thirty years old,” Eddie reminded him as his avatar tossed Ravi’s around. “Stop talking like one of May’s roommates.”

“I’m youthful at heart.”

“There really isn’t a lot to share,” Ravi said. “There’s this one really macho guy. I don’t want to call him a douchebag, but…”

“But he’s a douchebag,” Albert finished for him. “He asked Rav if he was a Slurpee or a casino.”

“If Rav was a…?” Buck frowned. “I don’t get it.”

“He was asking what kind of Indian I was,” Ravi said, not even sounding all that perturbed about it. “One of the instructors is a woman, and he does not take her seriously. At all. He’s like, middle of the class? But he’s tried correcting her more than once.”

“So he’s not going to last long,” Eddie guessed.

“If the world was fair, he wouldn't last the month,” Albert said.

“I dunno.” Buck leaned forward, resting his arms on top of Eddie’s head, dangling his bottle in front of his face. His husband reached up and smacked at it, like a cat. “I kinda want him to graduate and end up with us, just so I can see Hen tear him a new one. Or Ma.”

They all paused what they were doing, falling silent as they imagined Hen or Athena tearing into the poor bastard. It was a hypothetical scenario that had Buck craving popcorn.

“As fun as it would be to watch the sergeant make him cry, I don’t think- God damn it!” Ravi tossed his controller on the coffee table in disgust while Eddie’s character howled in victory on the screen. “I don’t think I really want to see him at the station every day, when I come back.”

“That’s fair,” Buck said, reaching over to snag the controller. “Now, I’m gonna show the both of you how to do it.”

“Don’t listen to him,” Eddie said. “Chris beats him all the time.”

“Because our son cheats.”

“How does Chris cheat?” Albert asked as the round started.

“Like this,” Buck answered, and then shoved Eddie’s head forward just in time to screw up whichever combo he was about to start.

“Buck!”

“Sorry, not sorry, babe.”

“I distinctly remember you being very upset with me the last time I played detective.”

“The difference, Buckaroo, is that I’m actually a cop.”

“In California. In Florida, you’re a tourist. How’s your dad?”

“Tired.”

“I’m pretty sure hospital beds actually suck out your energy. But, I meant… H- How is he?”

“Awake, aware. They’ve got tests scheduled to… Check everything.”

“I bet your mom’ll be happy to have him come home.”

“Right now she’s just happy he’s awake. But that reminds me, Buck. I’m not going to be able to stay here forever, but I also don’t know yet how Daddy is going to be. I know he’s going to need help, and I know Mama’s not going to be able to do it all on her own.”

“So you need a Carla.”

“We need a Carla. Do you think she’ll mind helping us find someone?”

“‘Course not. I’ll talk to her, ask her to give you a call.”

“There’s not much of a rush. They won’t be discharging him for a few days, yet.”

“Still. The earlier the better, insurance paperwork is a b- Er, pain. Have you talked to Grandma Carter about it, yet? I’ve heard all about how not welcoming she’s been about you and Pops helping.”

“We are putting that argument off until we’re not in Daddy’s hospital room, yelling at each other.”

“That’s probably a good idea. Now c’mon, tell me all about how the two of you did what you hate me doing.”

“I am a cop, Buck.”

“Yeah, but Pops isn’t.”

“And he nearly got shot again. I’ve already yelled at him for acting the fool, don’t go thinking this gives you permission to do it.”

“Uh-huh. I’ll keep that in mind.”

“Do. Not.”

“Okay, okay.”

“...I hit him in the head with a shovel.”

“Pops!?”

“No, Junior.”

The conversation he’d overheard between Buck and Hen continued to haunt Eddie, which was entirely his fault. There was nothing except his own cowardness keeping him from bringing it up with his husband. Nothing except his own fear that he’d open that box and find the cat dead. The cat, in this metaphor, being his marriage. Which wasn’t dead.

It wasn’t.

The silicone rings had arrived Sunday morning, and Buck had flounced into their bedroom to get on one knee and present Eddie’s to him. Like the utter dork that he was. Buck loved him and he loved Chris, and Eddie knew this. He knew it with all his heart.

Buck was currently off visiting his sister, one of their irregular get-togethers. Abuela was bustling about the kitchen while Chris sat at the breakfast bar, finishing his homework, and Eddie had grabbed one of the boxes they’d yet to unpack. There were still more than a few of them sitting around, even though they’d work through them whenever they had the time and the energy. This one had been marked “Living Room - Bookshelves” and was filled with books.

Eddie didn’t really place them on the shelves in any particular order. Most of them were already grouped together, anyways, thanks to Buck and his clipboard while they were packing. So, Eddie simply placed them as he grabbed them, listening to Abuela chatter away to Chris and losing himself to the repetitive motions. It was only interrupted when he got to the bottom of the box, his fingers scraping against the cover of one that had been laying flat.

When he pulled it out, he saw it was the scrapbook from Mitchell and Thomas. Eddie thought it weird that Buck had kept it, though he’d never tell him that. Over the summer, his husband had sat down and did his best to glue the loose pictures back to their proper places. A task aided by the way one of the old men had labeled each of them on the back.

Eddie wondered just how long they were together. Their first date was apparently disco, so since the Seventies. Fifty years of pictures, of memories, kept and displayed. Which…

Hm.

(“I just don’t want to wake up forty years from now and realize my whole life was a blur.”)

Maybe, Eddie thought as he ran his finger along the edge of one of the pages, he’d been focusing on the wrong parts of the conversation. And the first anniversary was supposed to be the paper anniversary. He’d just have to swing by the store one day and grab what supplies he needed.

Chapter 8: Speedbumps

Chapter Summary

In which Chris and Buck both get in trouble.

October

“This is the wrong kind of mayo,” Christopher announced, holding the jar up. He and Eddie had just gotten back from a grocery run; Buck would have gone with them, but there were things that needed picking up around the house. Cleaning that should have been done earlier, and probably would have been, except that maybe certain domestic chores had fallen to the wayside in between work and the chain of lies he’d shackled himself to in the effort to surprise his husband with new dancing skills.

Buck knew that Abuela didn’t mind cleaning, but he didn’t want to give even the slightest impression that they expected her to clean. Because they didn’t. If she wanted to sit in her chair all day, knitting and watching her shows while they vacuumed around her, then Buck would be perfectly happy with that. She moved in with them to make her life easier, not to become their live-in maid, after all. They already got her cooking almost every night.

Anyways, there were groceries sitting on the kitchen island that they were unpacking and putting away, and apparently Buck should have gone with them. Or Abuela.

“It was on sale,” Eddie protested, taking the mayo and tucking it away.

“You know, Dad, sometimes you get what you pay for,” Chris said.

“We’re raising a condiment snob,” Eddie complained, glaring at Buck as if it was fault as he reached into one of the bags. “What is this?”

He held up the box of diabetes inducing cereal he’d just pulled out.

“That looks like cereal, babe,” Buck pointed out, grinning at his scowl.

“Not sure a nutritionist would agree.”

“I got us the healthy one, too,” Chris said, holding up a second box. “Balanced.”

“You’re lucky you’re cute,” Eddie said, shaking his head as he put both boxes up. He paused, frowning, before pulling out his vibrating phone. “It’s your school.”

“Why is his school calling?” Buck asked, moving around so he could look at the screen as well, like it would magically answer the question.

“Wait, Dad.” Chris looked… Nervous. Why would their son be looking nervous about school calling? Buck knew why he would have been nervous about school calling, but that was because Buck did lots of things to have the school call home. Chris was not Buck. “We didn’t finish the groceries.”

“Hello?” Eddie answered, and Buck could just barely make out the voice on the other end. “Yes, this is Mr. Diaz… Really? I had no idea.” He fixed Christopher with a Look, and the boy glanced away. Just what was Buck missing here? “And how long has he been skipping Science Club?”

“He’s been doing what?” Buck blurted out, and then to Chris, “You’ve been doing what?”

Apparently Chris was more like him than he first assumed.

“Por Dios,” Abuela tutted from where she’d been helping put away groceries as well.

“Yeah, we’ll talk to him,” Eddie told the person on the other end of the line, shaking his head. “Thank you for letting us know.”

“Chris has been skipping Science Club?” Buck asked the moment he hung up. “How long have you been skipping Science Club?”

“Two weeks, apparently,” his husband answered, hands on his hips.

“You’ve been lying to us for two weeks?” Buck asked Chris, trying to ignore his shame over the hypocrisy. What right did he have to be upset over their son lying to them, when he’d been lying to them all for so much longer? The difference being, he guessed, that he was lying in preparation of a surprise. Chris was lying for… “Why? Where have you been going instead of Science Club?”

“The park,” the boy said, not looking at them. “With my friends.”

He’d been lying to them to go hang out with his friends, instead. Oh dear. Oh no.

“You really are my kid,” Buck muttered in despair. Maddie must never learn of this.

“Well you won’t have to be worrying about them for a few days,” Eddie said, now crossing his arms across his chest. “Because you’re grounded for a week.”

“Dad-”

“Nope. No friends, no video games. For a week. And you’re going to your next Science Club meeting.”

Chris turned a hopeful gaze on Buck, who immediately raised his hands to ward them off.

“You heard your father.”

Their son huffed and stomped out of the kitchen, abandoning the bag of groceries he’d been helping unpack. A few moments later, they heard his bedroom door slam shut.

“Thank you, mi sol,” Eddie said, letting out a sigh and leaning against the kitchen island.

“You supported me when I grounded him for hitting Chimney,” Buck pointed out, rubbing his back. “One of us will have to brave the dragon’s den and snag his laptop, though.”

“Did he not choose to join Science Club to begin with?” Abuela asked.

“He did. He was excited about it, too,” Eddie said. “I’ll grab it when we’re done with this.”

“We should probably both go,” Buck told him, pulling one of the paper bags closer to unpack it. “So someone has an eye on our juvenile delinquent at all times.”

“He’s our son,” his husband said, rolling his eyes. “Not a crocodile at the local zoo.”

“You two have that dinner tonight with Buckito’s friend, si?” Abuela asked.

“Yup,” Buck said, popping the p. “Unless you need us to stay. Do you need us to stay? Because I’ll totally cancel on him right now.”

“No, no,” she said, waving her hand like she was clearing his words out of the air. “You go. I can handle spending an evening alone with an upset Christopher. I doubt he’ll be any different from Ramon, when he was upset at that age. Or Eddito.”

“I was an angel,” Eddie protested, pouting.

“Si, si,” Abuela agreed with a nod. “For me.”

“He’s still an angel,” Buck said, reaching out to pinch his husband’s cheek and laughing when he got his hand slapped away. He reached into the grocery bag and frowned at what he pulled out. “Okay, seriously babe, just because it was on sale does not make it better.”

“It tastes the exact same, Buck.”

“No it doesn’t!”

“Yes it does! Abuela, tell him.”

“Hay tres niños en esta casa…”

“That’s hurtful, ‘Buela. It really is.”

Eddie did not know much about Buck’s friend, Connor. He knew that Connor used to be Buck’s roommate at what, in his husband’s own words, was essentially a frat house without the Greek lettering. He knew that Buck and Connor had met in Peru, and that he’d been one of the strangers he drifted after because they were “pretty and talked about being a family.” He knew that Buck hadn’t seen or heard from him for three years before now.

And Eddie might not have been any better at math than his husband, but he knew three years ago would put it right around the time of the tsunami and the lawsuit. Or maybe that summer, when he was recovering from the ladder truck crushing his leg. They’d talked about it before, he and Buck and Dr. Copeland, so Eddie knew that Buck had felt isolated in the weeks leading up that clusterfuck. It didn’t mean that it wasn’t a total slap in the face to be reminded of just how isolated he’d been. To be reminded that at that point in his life, with Ali Martin dumping him and apparently all of his non-LAFD friends dropping him, Buck literally didn’t have anyone outside of their circle.

No wonder he had made up an excuse of dropping off the fire drill results in person, just to come see them at the station.

Eddie’s first impression of Connor, when meeting him and his wife - Kameron with a K - at the restaurant was that the man was indeed pretty. He was nothing compared to Buck, but then again, Eddie was extremely biased. And Eddie could just about hear TK calling the man a twink in his ear, which probably meant he talked to him and fiancé far too much.

Connor and Kameron were nice, he decided. Friendly. The introductions were as awkward as he expected them to be, with Connor being more surprised about Buck being married than about Buck being married to a man. They ordered food that was good, but not good enough for what it cost. That was fine, though, because Connor was picking up the bill.

The time waiting for the food to arrive was spent with Connor and Buck catching up. It was nice to hear Connor’s version of how they drew Buck from Peru, if only because it lacked the self-deprecation that coated Buck’s. And despite his husband’s insistence that no-one liked Buck 1.0, Connor recounted their experiences with fondness.

“Alright, important question,” Eddie broke in. “Do you have pictures of him from Peru? Because I’ve heard about the highlights and the tan.”

“There better not be any,” Buck said, threatening Connor with a fork. “I told you to delete all of them.”

“And I absolutely did,” his friend said, and then stage-whispered to Eddie, “I’ll show you later.”

“You-”

“That’s when you moved into the frat house, right?” Kameron interrupted before Buck could say something unkind to his old friend.

“It wasn’t a total frat house,” Connor protested. “We had real jobs.”

“Five guys under one roof?” She sounded skeptical. “That’s a frat house.”

“We weren’t that bad. Or, were we?” Buck smirked at Connor. “There was this one time when-”

“Okay, okay, maybe,” Connor jumped in, cutting off the embarrassing story. “We were smart enough not to bring women-” He glanced at Eddie. “Or guys back to that place. It wasn’t really conducive to… Romantic evenings.”

Eddie almost made a comment about his husband preferring romantic evenings in the backseat of the jeep or a fire engine, but valiantly kept his mouth shut. The smile he felt growing on his face probably said everything, anyway. Enough to have Buck rolling his eyes and kicking his ankle.

“Well, good,” Kameron said. “‘Cause, before me, I’d like to think there were no romantic evenings.”

“Really?” Connor asked, laughing when she kissed him on the cheek.

“I don’t even know,” Buck started. “How did you guys meet?”

“The old fashioned way,” Connor answered, putting his arm around Kameron’s shoulders. “At a bar.”

“He was picking up takeout and I was sitting at the bar eating,” his wife said, picking up the tale. “Alone.”

“Not just eating alone, but reading a book,” he added. “I was intrigued, so I sat next to her and ate my takeout right there.”

“And I thought he was some sort of weird stalker. ‘Cause, I mean, who talks to strangers anymore?”

“And who reads actual books?”

“Buck does,” Eddie answered. “We had to buy a new bookcase a few months back just for all the ones he and Chris keep getting.”

“And Chris is…?” Kameron glanced curiously between them.

“Oh! Our son,” Buck answered, dragging out his phone and showing off his lock screen, a picture of Chris and Jee from the latter’s first birthday party. “Him and our niece, Jee-Yun.”

“Aw, they’re so cute,” she gushed, though she also looked a little sad. “H- How old…?”

“He'll be twelve in a few months,” Eddie told her. “Jee turns two in February.”

“So cute,” she said again, smile slipping a bit.

“A- Anyway, you, uh.” Buck shared a frown with Eddie. “You didn’t finish your story. How did you go from weird stalker stranger to married?”

“Oh, uh, I charmed her with my corny jokes,” Connor answered.

“He did,” Kameron confirmed, nodding.

“And… That was it.”

Personally, Eddie found it a lackluster tale. But then again, not everyone’s love story could start with a live grenade stuck in someone’s leg. Some couples had normal, undramatic beginnings to their relationships.

Those couples were to be pitied.

“And now your own frat house days are behind you,” Buck said.

“Yup,” Connor said while his wife poked at her food. “We actually bought a house last year.”

“Did house hunting hurt you like it did us?” Eddie asked.

“Oh yeah.”

“Marriage, house,” Buck listed. “Kids?”

“Kind of,” Connor said, sharing a look with his wife. “We’ve sort of hit a snag on that last part.”

“It’s crazy,” Kameron said. “When I was single, all I did was pray not to be pregnant. And now…”

“Nothing seems to get the job done.”

That explained why the picture of Chris and Jee-Yun had upset her, Eddie realized.

“I’m sorry to hear that,” Buck said, and sounded like he meant it. “I- I think more people struggle with fertility issues than we realize. But there’s lots of options now, right? There’s IVF, surrogacy…”

“Kameron’s not the problem.” Connor had trouble looking them in the eye. “I am. Not enough swimmers.”

“Oh,” Eddie and Buck chorused, drawing the syllable out. 

“I’m sorry to hear that,” Buck said again.

“That’s tough, man,” Eddie added.

“You have no idea,” Connor muttered. “But uh, we’re looking at other options. Specifically, sperm donor options.”

Buck was nodding along with the words, but Eddie’s paranoia was starting to perk its little head up. Because he was starting to get the suspicion that tonight was suddenly not about catching up. Or, not just about catching up, at least.

“Which is why we invited you to dinner,” Connor continued. “We wanted to know if you’d- if Buck would consider being our sperm donor.”

Buck had frozen in his seat, a fry hanging loosely from his fingers. The way his gaze was fixed ahead of him, not quite focused, had Eddie thinking of 404 errors and blue screens.

“That’s, uh.” What the hell was he supposed to say here? “That’s… That’s.”

“That’s,” Buck echoed, nodding a little. “I don’t…”

“You know what,” Connor said, saving them both from trying to find something to say. “This is a lot to ask. Why don’t… Why don’t you both go home? Think about it?”

“Yeah, alright,” Eddie said, pushing his chair back and standing. He knew his answer to their request. Except they weren’t asking him . “Buck?”

“Oh, uh, right.” His husband shook his head, getting to his feet. “It was nice seeing you again Connor. And meeting you, Kameron.”

The pair said something in response, but neither of them were paying them the least bit of attention at that point.

“They want to have your baby,” Eddie said, after they’d gone to bed that night.

“I know,” Buck said, still sounding stunned, staring at the glow-in-the-dark stars on the ceiling above them.

“You haven’t heard from him in years, and now… They want to have your baby.”

“I know.”

“What are you going to say?”

“I… I don’t know.”

Buck was apparently still in a daze the next day. He covered it up well enough that neither Abuela nor Carla, when she showed up to take Chris to school, noticed that anything was up. But Eddie could tell. Eddie could always tell.

His husband did a little better at work, with calls and Bobby’s return keeping him distracted. Hen was on leave, practically shoved onto her by the captain, and Chimney was still working A-shift as Albert’s partner. It was Chimney’s and Bobby’s first shift together since the blackout and the mess that followed, actually, and so Buck was suitably distracted worrying about that. Not that he really needed to be, since it wasn’t like it was the first time they’d shared space since then.

It was when there was downtime that it was most obvious that something was up. Without anything to really fill his attention, Buck got lost in his thoughts. Like he was doing now. Eddie was venting to the others about Christopher while his husband sat at the dining table, staring blankly at the wood.

It was honestly a bit concerning that their friends hadn’t noticed something was up. Or maybe they had and were just waiting for someone else to mention it.

“He’s been lying,” he complained, scowling in disgust when the coffee pot turned out to barely have enough to half-fill his mug. “To my face.”

“Has he never lied to you before?” Hen was supposed to be on leave, but she’d swung by for… Eddie didn’t know what the reason had been. She’d just shown up, and now she was leaning over Chimney’s shoulder while he played solitaire at the dining table.

“Little things,” Eddie confirmed, joining them at the table. He sat next to Buck and hooked his foot around his ankle, making the other man blink into alertness and smile at him. “Did he brush his teeth before he went to bed, did he put the empty milk carton back in the fridge. But not like this.”

“In his defense,” Chimney said, dealing a new round. “Science Club does sound kinda boring.”

The statement got a flat look from both Eddie and Buck.

“Joining was his idea,” Eddie said.

“Probably knew it would make a great cover,” the paramedic backpedaled. “Smart kid.”

“So if he wasn’t going to Science Club, then where was he?” Hen asked.

“At the park with his friends,” Eddie answered. “Which I don’t understand, because he knows we’d take him there anytime he wants.”

The paramedics shared a heavy look.

“Maybe that’s the problem,” Chimney offered. “He might be at that age where he doesn’t want his parents around all the time.”

“Kids do grow to crave privacy,” Hen said, reaching down to draw and place a card. Apparently, they were turning solitaire into a two-player experience. “Half the thrill sometimes is doing something, and thinking you’re getting away with it.”

“Well, he didn’t get away with it,” Eddie declared. “He’s grounded for a week. No video games, no exceptions.”

“Isn’t that the same punishment you gave him for hitting me?” Chimney asked.

“Yes.” Except this time he would actually be enforcing it.

“The punishment Buck gave him, you mean,” Hen said, and Eddie shrugged.

“Speaking of,” Chimney said, “you’ve been silent over there, Buckaroo. Nothing to add about your son’s descent into delinquency?”

“Huh? Oh.” Buck blinked and shook his head. “No, uh… No. Nothing to add.”

The paramedics stared at him, and then at Eddie, who shrugged and sipped at his coffee. They hadn’t discussed telling the others about Connor and Kameron’s ‘offer,’ but Buck was clearly in no hurry to share, so Eddie wasn’t going to say anything about it.

“Hen.” Bobby’s arrival, joining them from downstairs, cut off any attempt at interrogation. “You’re supposed to be on leave. What are you doing here?”

“I, uh… I swung by to, uh…” She floundered for a moment. “To pick up some kombucha I left in the fridge.”

“Really? Where is it?”

“I couldn’t find it.”

“Mhmm.”

“I guess somebody drank it.”

“Mhmm.”

“My house is too damn empty, Cap!” Hen said, breaking. Eddie shook his head in disappointment, because she didn’t even last as long as Buck in the face of Bobby’s expectant stare. “Karen’s off at work, Denny’s with his friends. I have no tests to study for and the silence is deafening. I- I just needed to come somewhere with a little more noise.”

And then the alarm rang.

“Thanks, Hen,” Eddie grumbled as he and the others got to their feet and rushed for their gear.

The scene they arrived at was an utter mess. Five cars that appeared to have smashed into and bounced off one another, with a bike in the middle of it all. Albert was already kneeling by the cyclist while his brother had continued on to the cars, checking the drivers.

“It feels like he has a broken fibula,” the younger Han told him.

“My left leg took the brunt of the hit,” the cyclist said. “But I was able to ride out of it.”

“Hell of a bike,” Eddie told him. “What happened?”

“I was cruising on my steel frame, and that idiot over there,” he pointed at one of the cars, a silver Prius. Chimney caught the motion and went to check its driver. “Swerved into the bike lane, hit me and then veered into that oncoming car. He was driving like a crazy person!”

“Or a drunk one,” Chimney called over as he leaned through the Prius’ window. “Smells like a distillery in here.”

“Chim, you need a coroner?” Bobby shouted from where he was overseeing everything.

“No, he’s still alive,” the paramedic shouted back. “Pulse is strong with this one.”

Eddie stopped paying attention to what Chimney was doing. His attention was focused on the cyclist and helping Albert set his leg for transport. The sound of a car starting was barely worth the effort of looking, not until he heard it hit and scrape against the curb. He snapped his head up to see the silver Prius driving away.

“Where the hell is he going?” Eddie asked no one in particular.

“Is Chim still in the car?” Buck had run over from where he’d been helping with the heavy-lifting, drawn by the commotion.

“Dispatch, be advised,” Bobby spoke into his radio, “a possible drunk driver just drove off with one of our paramedics in a silver Prius.”

Dispatch radioed back a confirmation, but the exact words were lost to Eddie. Because at the same time, his stupid, stupid husband had grabbed the bike off the ground and started riding it, chasing after the Prius and Chimney.

“Buck!” Eddie shouted after him, getting to his feet. “Where the hell are you going!?”

Buck, of course, did not answer. Because he was already too far away to answer.

“Did… Did your friend just steal my bike?” the cyclist asked, stunned.

“His husband,” Albert corrected, because Eddie was too busy taking calming breaths to do it himself. “And… Yes?”

“I,” Eddie said, forcing the words to come out calm, “am going to kill him.”

“Get in line,” Bobby told him.

The anger did not abate by the time they’d gotten the cyclist on a gurney and away in an ambulance. It simmered the entire time he worked, and only to grow hotter when they picked up Chimney and Buck. Buck, who looked so proud of himself, until he caught sight of Eddie’s face and that self-satisfied grin slid right off. Buck, who did his best to shrink into himself as Chimney told them how the utter idiot had rode the bike into the middle of the road and into the path of the car.

How Buck tried to turn himself into a living speedbump.

The entire ride back to the station was silent, with Eddie fuming and Buck trying to hide in his turnout coat. Chimney’s gaze bounced between them, while upfront, Bobby was utterly stone faced. When they finally parked in the bay, after they piled out of the engine, the captain looked between them and sighed and said,

“Use my office.”

Eddie nodded and grabbed his husband’s arm by the sleeve, dragging him through the bay and into the captain’s office. He wrenched the door open and shoved Buck inside, closing it behind them.

“Okay, I know you’re mad-”

“I’m pissed!” Eddie yelled over him. “What the fuck were you thinking!?”

“I- I was thinking I needed to get to Chimney-”

“That wasn’t your job!”

“But I could do something!”

“Do what? Throw yourself in front of the drunk driver?” His question was met with shame-filled silence. “Congrats, your stupid ass plan worked! But what if it hadn’t, huh? What if the asshole hadn’t stopped?”

“I don’t…” Buck’s throat bobbed as he swallowed. “I don’t know.”

“I would have had to go and tell our son that you weren’t coming home,” Eddie answered for him. “Because his pops went and got himself hit by a car. Did you think of that? You could have died! Did you, for one moment, even think about that?”

“Eds-”

“I could have been going home alone tonight, to tell our son that you’re dead! And for what? Chimney? You went and nearly got yourself killed for Chimney!?” On the other side of the closed door, there was a muffled “Hey!” Eddie thumped it with his fist, eliciting a couple yelps. “I am… I am so angry with you right now, Buck. Angry, and… And disappointed.”

“I know, I-” Buck was close to crying, Eddie noticed. And he felt so guilty about that, but he was still just so furious. “I’m sorry.”

“You left me behind,” he said, voice far quieter than his earlier shouting. “You left me behind to throw yourself in front of a car.”

“I’m sorry,” the other man said again, hanging his head. 

“I love you,” Eddie told him, because he couldn’t forgive him. Not yet, not when he was still so angry. Instead, he reached across the distance and tugged Buck closer, so he could wrap his arms around him. “I love you, and I am still so pissed off at you.”

The door slowly opened and Bobby poked his head through the gap. Behind him, Eddie could see Chimney doing a very poor job of acting like he hadn’t been eavesdropping.

“Are you done?” the captain asked, glancing between them. “Is it my turn yet?”

“Yeah,” Eddie sighed, nodding and stepping away from Buck. He gave his husband’s arm a reassuring squeeze before moving past Bobby and out of the office.

“Sit down,” he heard the captain say, before the door closed again.

Chapter 9: Doggone It

Chapter Summary

In which Buck gets yelled at some more, and then gets drunk with Hen.

Chapter Notes

This would've been up sooner, except AO3 decided to be a prima donna.

Buck slunk out of Bobby’s office like a kicked puppy, afterwards. He was man behind for the rest of the shift, and was suspended for his next one. There hadn’t been any yelling from the captain, probably because Eddie had done all of that for him, but there had been disappointment. Cold and bitter disappointment.

“That is not how a captain acts,” Bobby had said, and while the words may not have been as loud as his husband’s, they still slammed into him with all the weight of an anvil.

Things between him and Eddie had been awkward for the rest of the night. His husband was still very angry at him, though there wasn’t any more shouting. Instead, there were periods of silence interspersed with curt words. And that would have been worse than the shouting, except the man was still stuck by his side, as he always was. It was just… It was like he didn’t trust himself to not start yelling again, and so he bit his tongue instead. 

“I’m sorry,” Buck said again the morning after, as he drove them home in the jeep.

“I know,” Eddie said with a nod, gaze fixed straight ahead.

“I won’t do it again,” he promised, and that got the other man to look at him, skepticism clear as day on his face.

“You better not, Evan,” Eddie said and after a moment’s hesitation, laid his hand on the console between them, palm up. Buck smiled gratefully and took it with his own, entwining their fingers. The smile fell with the man’s next words. “You know he told Abuela.”

“Aw, fuck.”

When they pulled into the driveway, any and all thoughts of being able to sneak into the house like a thief in the night was viciously murdered. The very instant Buck got out of the jeep, the front door was thrown open and a very, very angry Abuela stepped out to greet them. She was a tiny woman, but in the face of her wrath, he wanted to hide behind his husband.

Not that his husband was going to protect him from this.

“Evan Buckley Diaz!” was followed by a tirade of rapid Spanish that he had absolutely no hope of keeping up with to understand. She hounded him the entire way into the house and through the living room, finger wagging, while Eddie brought up the rear with their work bags. He was laughing, the jerk.

“Abuela-” he tried, when she paused for a breath, but that just seemed to piss her off more.

“No! Escúchame!”

Buck threw himself into one of their dining chairs and looked to Eddie for help, only for the man to walk past him and into the kitchen. Over the breakfast bar, Buck watched him rummage through the cabinets and pull out a package of popcorn that he then threw into their microwave, a housewarming gift from TK and Carlos.

It was impressive how long Abuela could go when she got started. Buck had gotten scolded by her before, usually at the same time as Eddie, but never with such spirited rage behind it. She yelled at him for what had to have been a full fifteen minutes, long enough for Eddie to finish off his bag of popcorn all on his own. Her words were flowing too fast for Buck’s Spanish skills, so he settled for trying to keep count of how many times “estupido” got tossed out.

He lost count at twenty-five and was starting to tune her out completely when Eddie finally remembered his vows.

“Abuela,” Eddie started, only to recoil a little when she turned on him, finger raised. “Bobby and I both yelled at him last night.”

“So?” she asked, and Eddie let out a sigh.

“How was Chris yesterday?” he asked, distracting her with a change in topic.

“He was fine,” she answered with a flippant shrug. “Tried to get me to let him play one of his games and sulked a little when I said no. Perked up when I let him help me make dinner.”

“He has Science Club today, right?” Buck asked, and then immediately regretted bringing Abuela’s narrow-eyed attention back upon himself.

“He does,” Eddie confirmed, cutting his abuela off just as she drew in a breath to start yelling again. “One of his teachers is going to make sure he actually goes, this time, instead of ditching for the park.”

“He liked Science Club. What changed?” Buck wondered, leaning on the dining table and propping his head up with his hand. “Is it his friends?”

“I have no idea. We’ll talk to him after we pick him up.”

“Assuming he talks,” Abuela said, shuffling into the living room. “I remember how Ramon was at that age. And Helena has told me how you were, too.”

“I was an angel,” Eddie protested.

“Si, si. And a very silent one, when you were sulking.”

“He’s still a silent one when he sulks,” Buck teased.

“No, nope. You,” Eddie fixed him with a glare and a finger, “are still in trouble. No joking from you.”

“Lemme guess, I’m banished to the couch?”

“Don’t tempt me, mi sol.”

Eddie was, to put it simply, grumpy. He was not sulking, no matter what Buck and Abuela teased. Or maybe he was, but he wasn’t going to admit to it. What he would admit to was being grumpy.

It wasn’t even because of his husband’s potentially fatal stupidity from the evening before. Oh, to be sure, he was definitely still angry about that. But he’d done his yelling, allowed Bobby to dole out punishments, and now he was trying to move forward. No, what he was grumpy about was the rock wall he was hitting with Chris.

They’d managed to get him to go to his Science Club, only to have the teacher call them with the request to come pick him up. Because he was refusing to participate, and since it was an extracurricular club, there was no point in him being there if he didn’t want to be. And Eddie had tried to talk to him about it in the truck on the way home, but he’d gotten the ‘Diaz Family Cold Shoulder,’ as his father so helpfully called it.

Having to interact with Lyndsay-with-a-Y in the time it took to pick his son up also did not help the grumpiness. Nor did the confusion brought on by her asking if Buck was “actually” - her word - going to show up to this month’s PTO meeting. Which, yeah? Buck hadn’t missed September’s, why would he miss October’s?

When they got home, Chris elected to hide away in his room. He barely even said hello to Abuela and Buck along the way. Eddie had called his father to vent, because even if Ramon had been very hands-off with raising his children, he could presumably relate. But the conversation hadn’t helped any.

The mere idea that he didn’t give Chris any independence was laughable. Sure, yes, okay, his initial instinct was to always protect his son. Exhibit A: the way he nearly made Ana Flores cry after the incident with the skateboard, years before. And maybe, if it was just him, he would let that overshadow everything.

But they had Buck, the guy who went out and found a way to let Chris skateboard safely. The guy whose preventative solution to Chris’ growth spurts causing problems with his CP was to put him on a horse. Buck made sure their son was very independent. It might’ve been a structured independence - Eddie was not about to let Chris wander willy-nilly around Los Angeles - but he wasn’t trapped in some tower like a bespectacled Rapunzel.

Although the temptation had definitely been there, after the mess with Harry and Hudson the Dickless Wonder.

Eddie knew that his little boy was growing up. As much as he hated it, as much as he’d like to prevent it, he knew that it was happening. He and Buck had even given him The Talk over the summer, an event that had been harrowing for everyone involved. Especially when one factored in Buck’s habit of over researching and then overexplaining.

So Eddie was grumpy and maybe sulking on the couch, with Buck curled up beside him, when knocking pulled his attention to the door. Buck lifted his head and frowned at him in confusion, before reluctantly standing and seeing who it was. That it was Connor on the other side, looking anxious, did not help Eddie’s mood.

“Uh, hey,” Buck greeted him, glancing back at Eddie.

“Hey man,” Connor responded. “Do you have a minute?”

Buck glanced at Eddie again. Eddie shrugged in answer.

“Sure,” Buck said, stepping back and opening the door wider, letting the other man in.

“This is a nice place,” Connor said, not looking any less nervous. “Homey.”

“Gracias,” Eddie said, because if he didn’t, then Abuela would come in from gardening and chide him for being impolite.

“Look,” he said, gripping the back of one of the comfy chairs. He didn’t move to sit, and Eddie didn’t move to stand. “I just wanted to come by and apologize for the other night. We pretty much blindsided you, the both of you, and I feel terrible about that.”

“You did catch us a bit off guard,” Buck admitted. He didn’t move to sit again either, instead standing awkwardly by the coffee table.

“I know, I’m sorry,” Connor apologized. “I haven’t seen you in three years and the first time we reconnect, I asked for bodily fluids. That… That wasn’t the right way to ask you.”

“Is there a right way to ask that question?”

“Probably not.”

They both laughed awkwardly, and then Buck glanced at Eddie. Eddie let out a sigh, repressed the urge to roll his eyes, and got to his feet.

“Do you want something to drink, Connor?” he asked, heading for the kitchen. He heard the other two falling behind.

“I wouldn’t mind coffee, if you had a pot ready.”

They didn’t, so they offered him one of Buck’s colas instead. Connor took a seat at the breakfast bar, while Buck entered the kitchen with Eddie, the counter serving as a physical barrier between them and the topic of conversation.

“Are you doing okay?” Buck asked him, setting a can of off-brand coke in front of his friend.

“Do you know what it feels like,” Connor started, pausing to pull the can’s tab, “to feel like a total failure as a man, and like you’re letting down the woman you love?”

“Yes,” Eddie answered.

“Probably not, uh, in the same way you feel right now,” Buck added.

“Obviously not.”

“I assumed it was her,” Connor told them. “We were trying for months. Changing her diet, ovulation thermometers, the whole bit. Never occurred to me that I couldn’t do this for her, for us.”

“Yeah.” Buck shared an awkward glance with Eddie. “I gotta ask, why me?”

“Why not you?” Eddie blurted out.

“I mean, he- You. You could go to a sperm bank, pick someone with perfect genes,” he said, and Eddie didn’t contain the eye roll this time.

“Yeah, we could,” Connor said, nodding. “We could flip through a book and know stuff like their eye color, whether they’re a scientist or an artist, what their body type is. But…” He put down his drink. “But we wouldn’t know if they’d be a good person. How they treat the people they care about. I know those things about you, and that’s way more important than the superficial stuff.”

Buck was looking a bit taken aback and choked up, which was just insulting. Eddie said this kind of shit about and to him all the damn time, even said it with more verbosity, and all he got was an eye roll and a denial. Although yeah, having someone new join in with the chorus was nice.

He’ll vent to Copeland about it later. Or maybe Stanley.

“I swear, this isn’t me trying to convince you,” Connor continued, and Eddie bit his tongue to keep from scoffing.

“No, it’s uh. It’s nice to hear,” Buck said, and this time Eddie did scoff. “From someone who doesn’t have to say it.”

“I don’t have to say it either,” he said, elbowing his husband.

“I know this whole thing is probably asking too much,” Connor said. “So, I- I’m letting you off the hook. You don’t need to do this.”

Eddie narrowed his eyes at the man, though he wasn’t looking at him. Connor was focused on Buck, instead. The whole thing reeked of manipulation, in Eddie’s opinion. Coming in here all, “oh woe is I”, telling Buck that they chose him because he was just such a good person, and then saying that Buck didn’t actually have to say yes? Classic reverse psychology.

Maybe Eddie was just being paranoid and cynical.

Or maybe Connor was being a manipulative little shit.

The worst part was that, after Connor had said his goodbyes and taken his leave, Eddie could see that it was working. Buck was absolutely thinking about doing it now, whereas before he’d been on the fence. And Eddie couldn’t do anything about it. Sure, he could give Buck his opinion about it, but he couldn’t tell his husband not to do it.

It was Buck’s body, Buck’s bodily fluids, and therefore Buck’s choice.

“Did you tell him our address?” Eddie asked, instead of voicing his as of yet unasked for opinion about things.

“We have a landline, babe,” Buck told him with a fond smile. “We’re in the phonebook.”

They were definitely getting a punching bag for their home gym, Eddie decided.

The next day, Buck pouted as Eddie left for what should have been their shift together. Except that Bobby had suspended him after the incident with the bike, so he was stuck at home. He was also the one who took Chris to school, the boy still giving him and Eddie both the silent treatment. Which was just disheartening. And painful.

The rest of the morning was spent with Abuela, first helping her in her garden and then sitting down to watch her shows in un-subtitled Spanish. It was all enough to keep him busy and his mind off of Connor and Kameron. Until Pepa picked her up to go to lunch with some of her church friends.

Buck was invited to go, of course, but the last time he was around them, one of them pinched his ass. He was fairly certain it had been tiny, ninety year old Mrs. Salcedo. It had been a sharp pinch, and she kept her colorful nails rather pointy.

Except, not going with them meant he was left alone. Without distractions. Which was never a good thing, because it meant his mind was allowed to seed down any and every track of thought it wanted to. He tried, he really did try, to keep himself too busy to think. But monotonous like cleaning the bathrooms - the toilets sparkled when he was done - just let his mind wander even more.

Buck needed to talk to someone, except everyone was working. Maddie was at dispatch, Ravi was at the academy, and everyone else was at the station.

Everyone except Hen, that was.

Decision made, he soon found himself at the Wilsons’ front door. Within moments of him knocking, Hen was throwing the door open.

“Sorry, I know you’re supposed to be relaxing. I-”

“No, no, no,” she cut him off. “I have never been happier to see someone show up unannounced at my house, ever. Please.”

“Uh, guessing the relaxing isn’t going so well,” he said, stepping inside. Hen scoffed at him.

“Doing nothing is giving me more anxiety than trying to do everything,” she told him, and he could easily relate. “I’m not wired for this. What’s happening?”

“What do you know about sperm donors?”

“What?”

“One of my old roommates, from before Abby?” He waited for her to nod. “He and his wife asked me to be their sperm donor.”

Hen slowly closed the door, eyes never leaving him.

“This,” she finally said, “is a vodka conversation.”

Buck shrugged and followed her farther inside, taking a seat at her dining table while she went ransacking her own liquor cabinet. She soon came back with an unopened bottle and a pair of shot glasses.

“Tell me everything,” she instructed, twisting the bottle open and pouring them both a shot.

He did, with both he and Hen refilling their glasses at their own discretion. By the time he was done, having told her of Connor’s visit to the house the day before, he was maybe a bit tipsy. Or more than tipsy.

“This doesn’t seem like a Karen drink,” Buck said, squinting at the label on the bottle. “Or a Ma drink. Who do you usually drink this with?”

“Chimney,” Hen answered, and he let out a silent ‘ah.’ “Back to the sperm donor thingy. You didn’t say no?”

“I didn’t feel like I could.”

“Why?”

“I dunno.” He shrugged, letting her take the bottle. “Felt bad for ‘em.”

“That’s very kind of you,” she said, tipping the bottle at him in a salute, before refilling first her own and then his shot glass. “I mean, me and Karen, we’ve been there. You try and ya try, and it just doesn’t happen. It’s devastating.”

“I remember,” he said, before throwing back his shot. She quickly refilled his glass.

“If you said yes,” Hen continued, “you’d be changing their lives.”

“Right, which- which is a pro,” Buck said. “Y’know, a- a big pro. If I can help, then… Why wouldn’t I help?”

“‘Cause you’d also be changing your life,” she told him. “And Eddie’s.”

“For a few minutes.”

“Ew.” The look on her face was so over-the-top disgusted that he couldn’t help the chuckles he let out. “Seriously, for the rest of your life. This isn’t an anonymous donation, Buck. There’ll be no, y’know. No doubt. You’ll know. There might even be Christmas cards.”

Hen paused to take a drink.

“You have to ask yourself,” she said, “if you’re capable of doing this.”

“I- I think we both know that I’m… That I’m capable,” he retorted. “That’s why they asked.”

“Are you capable,” Hen drew the word out, rolling her eyes, “of being a father and walking away? That’s what they’re really asking.”

“Donor, not dad.”

“Exactly. You’re changing their lives, but they’re not exactly inviting you in. I mean, you said this is the first time Connor’s reached out to you in… How long?”

“Three years,” Buck answered, twisting his shot glass between his palms. “I- I mean, I think we both know that I- That I’m familiar with the concept, y’know? Donor, not dad.”

“You do,” she agreed, nodding. She held up the bottle and he let her give him a refill.

“Ma and Pops, they aren’t my parents, except they are,” he said. “And Chris isn’t my kid, except that he is.”

“But can you be on the other side of it?” she asked him. “Have a kid that’s your kid, except they aren’t?”

“I didn’t think about that,” Buck admitted, frowning at his drink.

“And have you talked to Eddie about this?” Hen asked, pointing at him with her shot glass. The action sloshed some of her own drink onto the table top, not that she noticed. “Because you need to. This affects him, too.”

“He’ll go along with it if I really wanna do it,” he said, and he knew that to be true.

“But you have to figure out if he’s okay with it, before you say yes to anything.”

“I know, I know.” He threw back his drink and refilled it again. “I just, I need to figure out if it’s something I even want to do, y’know? If I don’t want to do it, then it doesn’t matter if he’s okay with it or not, right?”

“But if he’s not okay with it…” Hen trailed off, but Buck got the somewhat slurred message.

“I know, I-” He was cut off by Hen’s phone ringing. It took a moment for her to dig it out, and then she frowned at whoever was calling her.

“Hey Cap,” she greeted, and Buck took the moment to cross his arms on the table and rest his head on them. “What? You’re… You’re coding a what? N- No, hang on, hang on. I- I need someone to confirm this is actually happening.” She put her phone on the table between them, hitting the speaker button. “Tell Buck what you’re coding.”

“Uh, it’s a dog. OD’d on opioids,” Bobby said, evidently repeating himself, and then demanded, “Are you two drunk?”

“Absolutely,” Buck answered.

“It’s two-o'clock in the afternoon,” he heard Chimney say.

“It’s five-o’clock somewhere,” he replied, which got Hen to laugh.

“How much does it weigh?” she asked.

“Fifteen pounds, give or take, ” Eddie answered, and his voice had Buck lifting his head.

“Oh, it’s Eddie. Hi Eddie!”

“Hi, Buck,” was accompanied by a very put-upon sigh.

“Point-oh-four kilograms per milligram,” Hen was saying, eyes shut in thought. “That’s six-point-eight… Two-point-seven milligrams Narcan.”

“Thanks Hen,” Bobby said, though he didn’t hang up immediately. On the other end of the line, there was anticipatory silence for several long, long moments. And then a yelp and a bark, followed by the cheers of what sounded like a crowd of girls.

And then Bobby hung up, which was kind of him.

“You did math in your head,” Buck said, when the line went dead.

“I know, right?” Hen sounded pleased with herself, as she should. “Take that, doctor professor lady who flunked me.”

“Fuck her,” he said, and his friend laughed.

“Buck!”

“She’s an idiot!” he told her. “So- So what you failed some stupid test. You just did math in your head, for how much Narcan to give to a dog. You just saved a dog. By doing math in your head.”

“You’re very impressed by that.”

“Eddie and I can barely do math on our phones.”

“Who helps Chris with his math homework?” she asked, sounding genuinely curious.

“Google. Or Carla,” he admitted. He raised his shot glass. “You would make a helluva doctor.”

Hen rolled her eyes but clinked her glass with his.

“Why are you drunk in the middle of the day?” Eddie asked, the moment Buck answered his phone.

It was a little bit after the call with Bobby, where Hen did math in her head and helped save a dog. Buck was stretched out on the Wilsons’ couch, feet hanging over the arm. Hen was curled up in an armchair, the vodka bottle and shot glasses left forgotten on the dining table.

“Hen,” he answered, ignoring her betrayed look. “I came over to talk and she said it was a vodka conversation. So. Vodka.”

The line crackled with his husband’s sigh.

“I’ll call Carla, see if she’s still okay to pick up Chris. Or Pepa, if she's not."

“You’re angry, aren’t you?” Buck asked, pouting.

“I am annoyed,” his husband admitted. “What were you talking about that required day drinking?”

“Connor and Kameron wanting my baby,” he answered, which had Hen laughing. “Hen told me I need to talk to you about it.”

“And she’s right. When I’m home, and you’re sober,” Eddie said, and then, “Bobby took the dog into protective custody.”

Buck sat up.

“Pops stole a dog!?”

Chapter 10: Donor, Not Dad

Chapter Summary

In which Buck gives an answer.

They did not talk about it that day.

Apparently, after their phone call about Bobby stealing a dog from a sorority house, the station got absolutely hammered. Eddie evidently had enough time to call Carla and get Chris a ride home from school, and then no time for anything else. Which meant that by the time he arrived at the Wilsons to pick Buck up - he was not trusting him to drive home - he was looking exhausted. It all served to make Buck feel even worse, for getting suspended for a day and for getting toasted with Hen.

When they got home, Eddie hurried through a shower and then collapsed into bed. Buck joined him, already feeling the start of the hangover and regretting his decisions. There used to be a time when he could drink people under the table and then spend the next day on a bright and crowded beach, no problem. He usually didn’t miss those days, but he did now. If only because that Buck - that Evan - didn’t suffer as much when sobriety returned.

It was with a pounding headache that Buck later peeled himself off of a sleeping Eddie and got out of bed. After a stop in their en suite, he was intending to head to the kitchen for some water, except the moment he stepped into the hallway he heard Christopher talking to someone. Normally, that wouldn’t be noteworthy. Except that he couldn’t hear whoever he was talking to, and his son was still very much grounded.

Padding down the hall and through the dining room, he found Chris sitting on the couch in the living room. He had on his headset, and he had a chatroom pulled up on the TV.

“It’s gonna be so cool,” he was saying, not noticing that he had company.

“What’s goin’ on?” Buck asked, announcing himself, and pushing away his amusement over how Chris startled at his voice. “Dad counts this as a video game. Shut it off, kiddo.”

“I’ve gotta go,” his kid told his friends, sounding so put upon as he took off the headset.

“We said no video games,” Buck reminded him, going over to pick up the remote and turning off the television. “Pretty sure there was something about no friends outside of school, too.”

“It’s not fair!” Chris yelled at him, and he reeled back in shock. “You two never let me do anything on my own! I’m not a baby, but you always treat me like one!”

“We don’t think you’re a baby,” he told him. “I also think you’re not acting very adult-like right now.”

“I think you’re acting like a jerk.”

“Hazards of being a parent.” Buck sighed, resisting the urge to rub at his temples. Being yelled at did not help his hangover any. “C’mon, bud. Pretty sure breaking the rules of your confinement gets you an extra week.”

“You’re not my dad!” Chris snapped, and Buck wasn’t sure what was worse. The way the words stabbed into his heart and twisted, or the way his kid looked so frightened the moment the words left his mouth.

Frightened and immediately remorseful.

Pulling on his own big boy pants, and deciding he could cry over sticks and stones and words that could hurt at another time, he knelt down in front of the couch. This had him looking up slightly at Chris, who was growing so damn tall. Too tall. Maybe if they fed him less…

“You’re right, I’m not your dad. I’m your pops,” Buck told him. “Do you know why we’re so upset that you lied about where you were?”

Chris didn’t say anything, but he shook his head, not quite meeting Buck’s eyes.

“Because,” he said, poking at Chris’ knee as he spoke, “what if there was a fire? What if your dad and I were called there for an emergency and no one could find you? Do you know how scared we’d be, if that happened?”

“Oh.”

“Yeah, oh. Your dad and I, we just want to make sure you’re safe. To protect you.”

“I don’t need you to protect me,” the pre-teen complained.

“Too bad,” was Buck’s blunt response. “You’re our kid. We’re gonna keep protecting you until we can’t anymore. And because you’re my kid, you’re probably gonna do stupid and reckless things and give your dad and I gray hairs. Just like I do to your grandpops.”

This finally got a smile to appear, however slight, on his unhappy looking son.

“When you’re released for good behavior - in another week - we’ll talk about you going to the park with your friends without us.
