As long as you keep your phone on you, and ask us and tell us where you’re going first, okay?” Buck said, and Chris nodded. “I’ll talk to your dad about it. You know he can’t say no to me.”

“He says no to you all the time.”

“Look at you, being all lippy now.” He stood and urged the boy to get up, too. “C’mon, time for bed. You’ve got school in the morning.”

“Okay,” Chris said, once again sounding put upon. He paused for a moment when he got off the couch, before throwing his arms around Buck. “I’m sorry, Pops.”

“You’re already forgiven,” Buck told him, squeezing him back, before urging him off. He watched him go, and was surprised to find a sleep-rumpled Eddie leaning against the doorway into the dining room.

“Night, kiddo,” his husband said, brushing Chris’ hair as he went by.

“Night, dad.”

“You okay?” Eddie asked, after they heard Chris’ door open and close. Buck let out a sigh and let himself sag against him, pressing his face into the other man’s neck.

“I’m fine. I’ll be fine. At least he’s talking to us again,” he said. “What are you doing awake?”

“I heard him shouting,” Eddie explained. “Came to check on things.”

“So you heard, then.”

“I heard.”

“I’ll cry about it when I’m back in bed,” Buck said. “He didn’t mean it. He was just upset and saying things.”

“C’mon,” Eddie said, tugging him down the hall and to their bedroom. “There’s a glass of water and some Tylenol already waiting for you on your table.”

“Best husband,” he gushed.

“I know.”

“And his temper is all yours.”

“I know,” his husband repeated, this time with a sigh.

“So you’ve weathered your first teenage tantrum.” Maddie sounded far too pleased about it as they watched Jee-Yun run around the backyard while they sat in the patio chairs. Every once in a while, she’d stop to try and “help” Abuela with her garden.

Buck’s sister had showed up sans Chimney - who was spending time with Albert - sometime after lunch, just as he and Eddie were about to sit down and have that talk. But that was fine, he was perfectly okay with putting off uncomfortable conversations for as long as possible.

“Mi sol was so brave,” Eddie said, reaching over to pinch his cheek. Buck groaned and slapped his hand away.

“It wasn’t my first time being yelled at this week,” he said, leaning his head back and closing his eyes.

“Ah yeah, I heard about that, too,” Maddie said, sounding like she was laughing at him. “What were you thinking?”

“He wasn’t,” his husband answered for him, and he blindly reached over to swat at his arm.

“Could we not? Eddie’s yelled at me, Bobby’s yelled at me-”

“He didn’t raise his voice for a single instant.”

“-Abuela yelled at me. I’ve been made to feel bad enough, okay?”

“No,” Abuela answered, not even pausing in yanking out weeds as she raised her voice to be heard from across the yard. “No you have not.”

Buck lifted his head to blow a raspberry at her.

“Okay, okay,” Eddie said, laughing. “How are things over in Buckley-Han land?”

“Well, I had a domestic abuse call the other night.”

Immediately, Buck was sitting up.

“Oh god, are you okay?” he asked.

“I’m fine,” she told him. Her smile was a bit wobbly, but he believed her. “The guy is being put away, and the daughter is safe.”

“There was a kid?” Eddie let out a low whistle. “Ay, Dios.”

“Like I said, she’s safe, and the mom is- will be fine,” Maddie told them. “I even got her help to get a green card, so. Happy ending all around.”

“But you’re okay?” Buck asked, to make sure.

“I’m okay. I’ll talk about it at my next appointment, but I’m okay,” she assured them both. “Now if only I can find a nanny for Jee-Yun.”

“What do you need a nanny for?” he demanded, feeling slightly insulted. “What, are we not good enough for you anymore?”

“Well, with Howie back on A-shift with you two and Albert, it just makes sense,” his sister said, shrugging. “But finding one is so difficult. They’re all either twelve or a potential serial killer.”

“Or both,” Eddie said, and Maddie rolled her eyes. “You get to tell Abuela that you’re hiring a stranger to watch her bisnieta.”

“When did I get adopted?”

“I think when Pepa started inviting you to Sunday dinners,” Buck told her. “Either then or after that dinner with our parents.”

A squealing Jee-Yun came running up to them, her chubby hands clutching some of the weeds Abula had been pulling.

“Mama!” she called out, holding out her bounty.

“Dandelions? For me?” Maddie reached out for them, but Jee yanked her hand back, shaking her head. “Oh, for you? Okay. They’re very pretty.”

“Very pretty,” Eddie agreed, and Jee giggled before sprinting off again.

“Oh, getting her into a bath later is going to be so fun,” Maddie complained. “She’s gonna be tired, and she’s a very grumpy little lady when she’s tired.”

“At least you’ll be able to take a nap while she takes one,” Buck pointed out.

“That is very true.”

True to Maddie’s word, when Jee-Yun began wearing down a short time later, she started whining and getting generally irritable. At least until she trundled back up to them and demanded to be allowed into Eddie’s lap.  She calmed down once she was, happily curling up against his stomach and chest as he wrapped his arms around her.

Buck could relate. He very rarely stayed in a bad mood when Eddie was holding him.

“We need to send that application in,” he said, reaching over to run his fingers along his niece’s cheek. She attempted to glare at him, but her eyes were drooping fast.

They’d sat through the adoption orientation, which had thankfully been available online. That made it really convenient, since they managed to squeeze it in during a particularly slow shift at the station. After they submitted the written application, they’d just have to do the family assessment and home inspection. And after that …

“We do,” Eddie agreed. He looked past Buck and snorted.

“What?” Buck asked, and looked over at Maddie, to find that she’d conked out in her chair. “Oh.”

“Nap time for both of them, I guess.”

“I guess.” He reached for his phone, calling Chimney to let him know that Maddie would not be getting home any time soon. “Hey man, listen, both your ladies are currently conked out at my place…”

“So,” Eddie started, sitting on the bed. “Connor and Kameron.”

“Connor and Kameron,” Buck agreed, sitting beside him. It was later, much later. Little things had kept piling that proved to be convenient excuses to put off talking about it. But finally, as it neared time for bed, the excuses ran out. They could push it off until tomorrow, until after Chris’ equine therapy and Eddie’s veterans group, but Eddie was clearly not letting that happen.

“You want to do it.”

It wasn’t a question.

“I- I mean, maybe?” Buck avoided his husband’s gaze, picking instead at the bed sheets. “I can do this for them, I can help them. So- So why shouldn’t I?”

“Are you actually asking me?” Eddie’s voice was steady, contained. He clearly had an opinion, but he wasn’t letting it show. “What did you and Hen talk about in between vodka shots?”

“What it would mean for me, mostly,” he answered. “The whole ‘donor, not dad’ thing. And I- I mean, like I told her, I’m very familiar with it, y’know? With Ma and Pops, and with Chris. I know that genetics don’t have to mean anything when it comes to family.”

“Okay.”

“And- And she asked if I could, if I was capable of, y’know. Walking away afterwards.”

“And you think you’re capable of it.” There was doubt coloring Eddie’s words.

“Yeah? Yeah, I think I am.” Buck looked up to see his husband shaking his head. “You don’t.”

“If you really want to do this for them, I’ll support you,” Eddie said, and Buck never had any doubts about that. “Just don’t ask me to be okay with it.”

“Why not?” he asked, because if Eddie wasn’t okay with it, then it wasn’t happening. But he needed to know why. “Why can’t you be okay with it?”

Eddie looked at him for a moment, before silently stretching across to pluck Buck’s phone off the bedside table. He unlocked it with ease, and Buck watched over his shoulder as he skimmed through his photos until he reached one of little baby Gavin.

“The moment Amy placed him in your arms, you got attached,” he said. “You knew him for how long before you named him? Less than half an hour? You were heartbroken, when the CDS lady arrived for him. And when you found out he was spending those first two weeks with Hen and Karen, you went over there anytime you could. And then you were even more heartbroken when they took him away again. For a baby that wasn’t yours, in any way. Because you held him for five minutes.”

Buck ducked his head, or tried to. Eddie put his fingers beneath his chin and forced him to meet his eyes.

“The moment you knew for sure that Kameron was pregnant, you’d become attached. You’d think of it as yours, as your kid, as Chris’ sibling. You’d probably start shopping for, I don’t know. LAFD onesies.”

“An LAFD onesie would be cute,” Buck muttered.

“I’ll never be okay with it, Evan, mi sol, because being okay with it would mean being okay with watching you hurt yourself,” Eddie explained, letting the phone drop to his lap and moving his hands so he was cupping Buck’s face. “And I will never, ever be okay with watching you hurt yourself.”

“So,” Buck started, pausing to swallow back the torrent of emotions he was feeling. “So I guess I… I’m saying no to Connor, then.”

“Do you want me to be there, when you tell him?”

“Please?” He reached up to wrap loose fingers around his husband’s wrists. 

“I have your back,” Eddie told him. “Always.”

Connor had said that he was letting Buck off the hook, that he didn’t need to do it. But Buck never once believed that he actually meant that. He and Kameron had pinned their hopes on him, for whatever reasons, and now he was about to dash those hopes upon the rocks.

The day after Buck and Eddie talked, after Chris’ equine therapy and Eddie’s veterans’ group, they retreated to the privacy of their bedroom. Abuela and Chris were in the living room, watching TV together, and they didn’t want either of them to overhear. After all, neither of them even knew about Connor’s request.

No one knew, except Eddie and Hen.

“Hey, man,” Connor greeted him. The phone hadn’t rung for long, and his old friend sounded excited to hear from him. “What’s up?”

“Hey, Connor, uh.” Buck glanced at Eddie, who reached out and squeezed his hand. If his husband wasn’t there, he’d probably cave and say yes, simply out of guilt. “I was just… I was just letting you know that I’ve decided to, uh. To not go through with it. The, um. The donation.”

There was silence on the other end, long enough that he pulled the phone back to make sure he hadn’t hung up. Finally, there was a quiet,

“Oh.”

“Yeah, I… I’m sorry, man.”

“No, no, it’s…” Connor let out a breath. “Yeah, I’ll- I’ll tell Kameron. She’s going to be so disappointed.”

“I’m sorry,” he said again, feeling awful.

“No, it’s- Yeah, okay.”

And then the call ended.

“I hate disappointing people,” Buck groaned, falling forward against Eddie’s shoulder. He let the phone drop onto the bed.

“I know,” Eddie said, reaching an arm up to rub at his back. “I’m proud of you.”

“For what? For saying no? I almost didn’t.”

“But you did.”

“He probably hates me, now.”

“You went three years without him in your life,” Eddie reminded him. “He doesn’t matter enough to worry about things like that.”

“I wish that was enough for me to not feel like shit,” Buck said. “I really, really do.”

But it wasn’t, and they both knew it.

Chapter 11: Filling Out

Chapter Summary

In which Eddie progresses with two different projects.

“There is a dog in my house, Buck. Why did you let Bobby bring a dog into my house?”

“Hey, I wasn’t there to stop him!”

“Oh yes, I’ve heard all about your stupidity. You’re very lucky Bobby waited to tell me about it until after I got home.”

“Pops only told you about it so you’d be upset with me and not him.”

“Oh, he knows I can be angry at more than one person at a time. What were you thinking?”

“Oh, c’mon. Eddie and Pops and Abuela have all already yelled at me. Can we just… Not? Please?”

“I guess I can- Achoo!”

“Salud.”

“Achoo!”

“Dinero.”

“What?”

“It’s what Abuela says whenever someone sneezes. Health, money- Whatever, are you okay?”

“I’m fine, Buck. It’s just a cold.”

“You’re sure it’s just a cold? You’ve got all your shots, right?”

“Yes, I’m very sure. I’m not carrying the next plague, or the old one.”

“If you’re sure. So uh, I guess the help for your dad is working out?”

“Oh yes. Carla helped me find someone who Mama won’t drive to tears…”

Buck had yet to actually lay eyes on Hoover, the dog that Bobby had taken into protective custody. They worked on Sunday, which meant there was no Sunday dinner get-together. It was probably a good thing, honestly, because Christopher still hadn’t remembered - or at least brought up - the desire for a pet. They were totally going to get one, eventually. They were just putting it off for now.

Personally, Buck was planning to push for a snake. If only because he knew Chimney was terrified of them.

On Sunday evening, instead of getting attached to an adorable fur baby, he hijacked the printer in the captain’s office to print out a copy of the adoption application. He stuck it to a clipboard, stole a pen from Bobby’s desk, and herded Eddie into sitting at one of the tables up in the station loft.

“You fill it out,” he said, handing his husband the clipboard and pen. “Your handwriting’s neater.”

“I hope this kid doesn’t have your manners,” Eddie chided, though he took both items easily.

“My manners are awesome.”

“You’ve had burping contests with Chris at the dinner table.”

“And I’ve won every time.”

“What are you filling out?” Albert asked, his curiosity drawing him to the table as well. He sat down on the other side of Eddie, fingers toying with the label of his plastic water bottle.

“Adoption thing,” Eddie explained. “Do I put down Buckley as your middle name or maiden?”

“Middle name,” Buck answered.

“Did you not have a middle name before?” Albert asked, peering at the form. “I thought everyone here had middle names.”

“Nope. And neither does Maddie.”

“We’re probably lucky they named their kids at all,” Eddie grumbled, dutifully filling in the boxes. He reached into his pants pocket to tug out his wallet, and then slapped Buck on the arm with it. “Get out your license, I don’t remember your driver license number.”

“And yet,” Buck said, pulling out his own wallet, “you remembered my social, no problem.”

“I have practice writing that one down.”

“What are we writing down?” Bobby asked, joining them from downstairs. Chimney was trailing after him, and Buck was still distantly surprised that they were once again getting along so well. He’d thought things would have been awkward between them, but the paramedic had worked hard at rebuilding bridges over the summer.

“Adoption thing,” Buck answered.

“Marital history. You didn’t have any secret wives or husbands before me, did you?” Eddie joked, writing down Shannon’s information. There was a brief pause before he filled in her date of death.

“If I told you about them, they wouldn’t be a secret,” Buck pointed out, squeezing his shoulder.

“Criminal history. Have we ever been arrested for an offense other than a minor traffic violation?”

Immediately, four sets of eyes fell upon Buck. Which was just highly insulting.

“I will have you know,” Buck said, crossing his arms and glaring at his supposed family, “that I have not been arrested for anything in my entire life.”

The silence that followed was filled with doubt and judgment.

“I’m not the one with a criminal record,” he insisted to their disbelieving faces. “My husband, on the other hand…”

“I’d hoped you’d forgotten about that,” Eddie mumbled, awkwardly marking down Yes for himself.

“What am I missing?” Albert asked, looking between them. “What did Eddie get arrested for?”

“Would you believe that I’m his impulse control?” Buck asked him. “Less than a week of not being allowed to talk to me, he starts punching people over parking spots and joining fight clubs.”

“It wasn’t just because of you,” Eddie grumbled, clearly ignoring Albert gawking at him. “And I punched him because he was a dick.”

“Wait, does that time we were under investigation for bank robbery count?” Chimney asked, heading for the fridge.

“Uh.” Buck looked at Eddie, and then at Bobby. “We were never arrested, so… No?”

“Oh wait!” Eddie suddenly cried, startling him. “You got put into hospital jail!”

“Hospital jail?” Poor Albert was just looking more and more confused.

“There weren’t any handcuffs,” Buck pointed out. “Just disappointment.”

There was a brief pause where everyone around him saw the obvious joke, debated saying it, and then let it go into the aether. No doubt part of the reason why they let it go was the stare Bobby was fixing on all of them. Because the captain no doubt knew that if the joke was made, then Buck would feel the need to refute it.

And Bobby did not want to hear about his underlings’ sex lives, much less his pseudo-son’s.

“If a child has not been identified,” Eddie read out, “please indicate preferences.”

He looked up at Buck, who chewed on his lip and shrugged helplessly.

“Could we just… Not? It just feels kinda cruel, deciding what disqualifies a kid from getting a family.”

“This is why I’m leaving him at home, if we ever get Chris a pet,” Eddie told the others, going through and ticking almost all the boxes, barring the ones that indicated groups of siblings. They simply didn’t have room for triplets. “I do not need the two of them combining forces and convincing me to, I dunno. Get an entire litter of puppies so none of them were left alone.”

“I like that you just accept that you’re whipped,” Chimney quipped, sitting across from them with his own bottle of water.

“I’m not whipped, I just know my limits.”

“Do not doubt the power of the Eyes, Chim,” Buck said, grinning at him. “They’re why you don’t have an arrest record.”

The other man shifted awkwardly in his seat, no doubt uncomfortable with being reminded of his mistakes. He was saved from dwelling on it by his brother, frowning at the application and saying,

“‘Medically fragile’ is a vague term.”

“Chris would be considered ‘medically fragile’ with his CP. It’s things like that,” Eddie explained. “Hey, who all are we putting down as references?”

“Carla,” was Buck’s immediate answer, and the look of insult on Bobby’s face was almost funny. “How much room is there?”

“There’s five rows.”

“Carla, Pops,” he listed. Bobby nodded, mollified. “Ma, Hen.”

“And?” Eddie prompted, writing down their names and info. “We have room for a fifth. Who should it be? And do I put Hen or Henrietta down?”

“Just put Hen, she’ll kill us if someone calls asking for Henrietta.”

“I think your fifth reference should be obvious.” Chimney looked at them in obvious expectation.

“You’re absolutely right,” Buck agreed, pointing at him. “It is obvious. Karen.”

“Hey!”

“A home health aide, a fire captain, a police sergeant, a paramedic learning to be a doctor, and an actual rocket scientist,” Eddie said, tapping the pen on the table. “Almost makes us sound respectable.

“Are you going to come in with twenty-six specific praises for me to sing when they call?” Bobby asked them. 

“Do I need to, Pops? Just tell them about all of my best qualities.”

“...So you’re going to come in with a list of twenty-six praises for me to lie about, right?” the captain asked again, everyone laughing at the way Buck squawked in indignation.

“So we think we’ve found our nanny. Sorry, household manager.”

“One, that sounds pretentious. And two-”

“Look at you, using words like pretentious.”

“And two, I still don’t see why you need to hire a nanny.”

“I told you, Buck. With Howie back on A-shift, suddenly half our babysitters are working on the days we need them.”

“There’s still Abuela and Pepa. Or the Lees.”

“Pepa and the Lees all have jobs, too. And half the reason you introduced Carla to Eddie was because Abuela couldn’t watch Chris on her own, wasn’t it?”

“Pepa only works because she doesn’t want to sit around the house like Abuela. And… Okay, that last part is true.”

“Mhmm.”

“You did get Ma to run a background check on this nanny, right?”

“Household manager. And yes, of course I did. She kept sneezing while we were on the phone. Is she okay?”

“She said it’s just a cold. So what’s the name of this stranger you’re trusting with my princess?”

“Her name is Magda, and she seems fine.”

“Seems fine.”

“We called eleven of her references. She’s fine, Buck.”

“Only eleven?”

“She’s. Fine.”

The moment the door closed behind Buck, heading off for that month's PTO meeting with two trays of freshly baked muffins and an ominous declaration about making Lyndsay-with-a-Y regret her life choices, Eddie headed for the fourth bedroom. It was empty, save for Jee-Yun’s playpen and the growing pile of toys they kept for when she was over. There wasn’t ever much reason for Buck to be in here, save for the occasional cleaning, and so it made the perfect place to hide the project Eddie had been working on in fits and starts. Specifically, the back of the room’s closet.

He kept it and the supplies for it in a box, which he dragged out and emptied onto the floor around him. It would probably make more sense to do it in the dining room, where he could sit in a proper chair and spread out on the table, but he knew his son. He’d seen how he was the year before, when Buck was planning the wedding behind his back.

Moody pre-teen or not, if Chris found out what he was doing, he’d go all giggly around Buck again and the surprise would be ruined.

The surprise being a scrapbook he’d started putting together. He’d gotten the idea after finding the one from Michael and Thomas again. He was hoping to get it put together in time for Christmas, which was at once a convenient and inconvenient anniversary date. It was proving hard going, because he didn’t have the first clue what he was doing.

Also, it was proving to be an expensive endeavor, with all the supplies he was buying and the pictures he was printing off.

It took him forever to figure out where to even start. His first instinct had been that Christmas party at the station, where he got his head unstuck from his rear and took that brave step forward. But while that had been a beginning, it hadn’t been the beginning. It didn’t feel right to start there.

And then, he nearly settled on the day Abuela broke her hip. The day Chris spent at the station, thanks to Buck and Bobby. But that hadn’t felt quite right, either. Closer, but not there. Though a picture from that day, the one of him and Buck helping Chris down the firepole, had ended up in the center of the third page. Other, smaller ones, like one of his son wearing a helmet far too big for his head, surrounded it.

Eddie had to get creative for the actual beginning. He printed out a couple of newspaper articles and cut the relevant parts out, pasting them to the pages along with some pictures of the wreckage of an ambulance and a severely tilted high-rise.

Putting the book together, he found, was a calming and cathartic process. Sorta like completing a puzzle. It felt nice to look back and piece together this map through their lives. It felt nice to remember everything, even if some of the memories were painful. Like the pictures from his shield ceremony, the ones showing Buck’s leg with a cast that nearly swallowed it whole.

Copeland would probably have a very strong opinion on this project, if she knew what had driven him to starting the book. And sure, yes, her hypothetical opinions would probably be correct. But she couldn’t judge him for things she didn’t know about, could she?

The box was closed, and the cat was alive.

The sound of the front door opening and closing startled him, and he ended up dropping his glue and a picture of them pushing Chris on his skateboard. He hurried to stuff everything back into the closet, as quickly and as quietly as possible. Luckily, it didn’t sound like Buck was coming down the hall.

The surprise hidden away, he crept out of the room and then went in search of his husband. He found him in the kitchen, rinsing the muffin trays.

“Baked goods were a hit?” he asked, and was warmed by the smile Buck sent him.

“It was like a feeding frenzy, Eds. I think someone nearly lost a hand,” his husband said, putting the trays in the drying rack. “I showed that Lyndsay what’s what.”

“Didn’t Abuela bake them?” Eddie asked, boxing him in against the counter.

“Yeah, but it was about whether or not I could bring something homemade to a bake sale,” Buck told him, pecking a kiss on his lips. “Not about whether or not I could bake. And I helped her. What were you up to, when I came in?”

“Oh, just. Picking up some. How’s the PTO?”

“I’ve been drafted to help chaperone a school dance. Party? It’s a something, next month.”

“Drafted?” Eddie repeated, raising his eyebrows.

“They needed someone that knows first aid,” Buck said with a shrug. “Everyone there knew I was a firefighter. I want you to try and say no when there’s two dozen people staring at you.”

“Teachers in California are all required to be CPR trained, mi sol.”

“Oh.”

“Yeah.”

“So I got conned into going because no one else wanted to.”

“Pretty much.”

“Shit.”

“Have you talked to Taylor recently?”

“Not since she called to tell me about Eddie leaving her angry voicemails.”

“Eddie left her angry voicemails? Why? When was this?”

“Back in August, after- It doesn’t matter. Why are you asking, Josh?”

“Because she’s being sued for slander.”

“...Huh.”

Chapter 12: Detective Diaz is On the Case

Chapter Summary

In which Taylor Kelly reveals there are consequences to actions.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

On Tuesday evening, in the middle of a twenty-four, they were summoned to what Buck would feel reasonably comfortable calling a mansion for a medical call. It was late, honestly closer to being Wednesday morning, and was one of the irritating shifts where Eddie was partnered with Albert. Thanks to Hen’s enforced leave, the station was down a paramedic, and so the Hans were occasionally bounced between A and B shifts as needed.

The engine and ambo had to squeeze between police cruisers, and there were officers scouring the property.

“We responded to a home invasion in progress,” Athena explained to them when she met up with him and Bobby, Eddie and Albert joining with the gurney as they passed the ambulance. “Found the homeowner unconscious.”

A home invasion explained the heavy police presence, Buck thought.

“And the invaders?” Bobby asked her.

“House is clear.”

“No signs of forced entry?” Buck asked her as they walked through the front doors, which were wide open. The caller, an older woman, was sprawled on the floor of the entryway.

“Just some spilled food in the kitchen,” Athena answered as Eddie and Albert knelt by the unconscious woman’s side. “Dispatch says she called from upstairs. Spotted the intruder in the kitchen and ran, before taking one hell of a tumble.”

“Pulse is elevated,” Albert reported, fingers at the woman’s neck.

“Something must have really scared her, right?” Buck looked around, taking in the utter lack of anything suspicious in the house. Which was, in its own way, suspicious. “You- You’re sure there’s no one else here?”

“I’m positive, Detective Diaz,” Athena said, exasperation clear in both tone and expression.

On the ground, Eddie was rubbing his knuckles against the caller’s sternum. She began to stir, letting out a groan.

“We have to get out,” she said, waking up and struggling to rise. “We have to-”

She was cut off by the sound of something cracking, falling back to the ground with a pained yell.

“That was her pelvis,” Bobby helpfully supplied.

“Ma’am, you need to stay still,” Eddie told her, using his hands to ensure she didn’t try to get up again. “A pelvis injury can lead to internal bleeding.”

“Starting a line,” Albert said, getting the IV ready. “Four milligrams morphine.”

Eddie held a hand out and Buck yanked the folded splint sheet off the gurney, handing it to him.

“Ma’am,” Athena started, getting the woman’s attention. “You told Nine-One-One that you saw someone in the kitchen. Who did you see?”

“It wasn’t a man,” the woman said, gasping in pain while Eddie and Albert lifted her up some to slide the sheet under her. “It was- It was a monster.”

It was a statement that got a variety of looks from the gathered first responders. 

Curiosity piqued, Buck pulled out his flashlight and went monster hunting. Athena said that the woman spotted the intruder in the kitchen, and so he crept farther into the house until he found it. Casting the beam of light around, he spotted crumbs on the floor. An actual, literal trail of breadcrumbs leading across the room to a door that was half-open.

He walked towards it, trying to be as quiet as possible. There was no way that the intruder would still be in there, right? Athena had said the house was clear, and if somehow the police had missed them, there was no way an intruder would stick around without at least closing the door, right? Right.

Except wrong.

Buck threw the door wide open. His light illuminated the inside of what turned out to be a well-stocked pantry. An adorable raccoon chittered at him in distress, attempting to hide in the corner with its pillaged chunk of bread.

“This is Buck,” he announced into his radio, feeling smug. “I’ve apprehended the suspect.”

He closed the door to the pantry, ensuring the furry bandit couldn’t scurry away to hide elsewhere. Turning to rejoin the others, he ended up jumping backwards in shock, instead.

“¡Ay mi Madre!” he shouted as he flailed, probably because he’d spent too much time with Abuela in recent weeks.

“Careful,” an amused Athena chided with a wagging finger. “You could break a hip.”

“Funny, Ma,” he groused, before gesturing at the closed door. “Your guys missed Rocket Raccoon in there. But don’t worry, Detective Diaz caught him.”

“No,” she said, shaking her head and leading him back to Bobby and the others. “I am not encouraging this.”

“I followed the trail of evidence straight to the culprit,” he said, clicking off his flashlight and pocketing it.

“I just told you, we are not doing this.”

“Is my husband being unbearable again?” Eddie asked. He gestured and Buck knelt beside the woman, working with him and Albert to get her onto the backboard.

“The monster was a raccoon,” Athena announced. “Looks like it got in through the doggy door in the kitchen. Our boy trapped it in the pantry.”

“I’ve told my husband to get rid of that thing,” their victim complained. “We don’t even have a dog anymore.”

“Don’t worry, ma’am,” Bobby assured her, but smirked at Buck. “Detective Diaz is on the case.”

Buck let out a sigh.

“I’ll get the drill,” he said, resigned to the fact that he was apparently going to be boarding up a doggy door.

“I cannot believe,” Eddie said, for perhaps the fiftieth time, “that you’re having lunch with Taylor Kelly.”

“Really?” Buck paused in tying his shoelaces to smirk at his husband. “You can’t believe any part of this?”

The other man scowled and crossed his arms.

“Okay, fine,” Eddie finally said. “I can believe it. I just don’t like it.”

“You’re more than welcome to join us.”

“Hell no, mi sol.” The look of disgust on his face was enough to make Buck laugh. “I don’t feel like making nice with her while trying to eat.”

“You were almost friends half a year ago,” Buck pointed out, getting to his feet. “Bonding over a mutual hate boner for Chimney.”

“That was before all of…” Eddie trailed off, making a vague waving gesture with his hand as he did. Buck understood what he meant.

“Does it help that this is mostly a gossip gathering mission?” Buck asked him. “She’s being sued. I want to ply her with wine and find out why.”

“Because Taylor Kelly is a bitch, that’s why.”

“I don’t think you can sue someone for being a bitch, babe.”

“You would know.”

Buck stood there for several moments, simply gawking at his husband, while the other man grinned proudly at him. Eventually, he threw up his hands and walked for the door, shaking his head.

“Love you!” Eddie called after him.

“Yeah, yeah. Love you, too.”

Buck was meeting with Taylor at the exact same cafe that he met her last time. He wasn’t making any sort of statement, it was simply that it was close to home and had a nice enough wine menu. There was a possibility that she’d cut down on the wine intake since May, but he doubted it. Lawsuits could drive one to drinking.

He would know.

Unlike last time, she’d beaten him there. An impressive feat, since it was literally a two minute drive from the house. She was waiting for him at a table inside the cafe proper, glass of merlot in hand, perusing a menu.

“So.” Buck plopped down in the chair across from her. “You’re being sued?”

“I’m not supposed to talk about it,” Taylor answered, almost automatically. A second passed, and then she looked up from the menu. “Wait. How do you know about it?”

“First responders are horrible gossips,” he told her, picking up a menu for himself to look through. “That includes dispatchers.”

“Josh,” she guessed, a pinched expression on her face.

“Josh,” he confirmed, nodding.

“Is this really the only reason you asked me to lunch?” she asked. “To sate your curiosity and rub my nose in it?”

“Why would I rub your nose in anything?” he asked in turn.

Taylor let out a sigh.

“It’s not just me being sued,” she told him. “It’s the news station, too. And this gossip stops with you, got it?”

Buck stared at her.

“Okay, fine, you and Eddie.”

“Got it,” he said, nodding. He hid his crossed fingers under the table.

“You’re lucky I’ve been wanting someone to vent to,” she grumbled. “Josh is fine, but it’s just not the same.”

They were interrupted by the waiter arriving to take their orders. Buck got a coke, because he didn’t feel like day drinking again so soon after the vodka shots with Hen. He also got a burger and fries, while Taylor got a salad.

“So. What are you being sued for?” Buck asked her.

“Slander,” she answered, and then glared at him. “Yeah, it's all horribly ironic. We have an argument about me putting the truth out there-”

“Rushing to get the credit,” he idly corrected.

“-And now I’m being sued for lying!”

“Well.” Buck paused, debated it, and then decided to just dive in. “ Did you lie?”

“No!” she denied, and then, “I don’t think so. I called him a serial killer.”

“You called him-? Wait.” He sat up in his seat. “You didn’t say, who’s suing you?”

“Jonah fucking Greenway.”

Buck wondered, as the waiter showed up with his drink, if perhaps he should go ahead and order a wine as well. Or maybe ask if they had something stronger hidden away in the back.

“But, he’s in jail,” he pointed out, probably needlessly. “You can sue people from jail?”

“Apparently!”

Huh. He learned something new every day.

“Okay, but what’s the argument here?” he asked her, absentmindedly stirring his coke with his straw. “He got arrested for killing Claudette and… I think it was attempted murder with me, but I’m not sure. Abduction and false imprisonment for both me and Hen, definitely.”

“The argument is that no one can prove he killed anyone else,” Taylor told him. “Not that they haven’t been looking. I know the department here’s been dealing with families demanding they look at any deaths on calls he was involved in. Didn’t you know about this?”

“No, I… I gave my statement, let them take photos of the house, and then stayed the hell away from it all.”

Though, now that he was hearing about it, it did kinda sorta explain why the department brass were still so annoyed over everything. He’d thought it was a public relations nightmare when it first happened, but this was so much worse. And the headache that had to be involved on an administrative level…

It didn’t make him any more forgiving, though.

“And it’s been pointed out to me,” Taylor said, taking a sip of her wine, “that I’ve given him grounds for a mistrial, or at least an appeal. Since, y’know. I told all the potential jurors around here that he’s a serial killer. Sixth amendment, and all.”

“So basically, what you’re saying,” because Buck was a little shit and couldn’t help himself, “is that you should have waited, or worked with the department, or let someone else break the story.”

Taylor glared at him and drained her wine, before slamming the glass down on the table.

“Yes,” she hissed. “That is exactly what I’m saying. You were right. Are you happy?”

He shrugged at her and took a long, obnoxious slurp of his coke.

“I need a refill,” she declared, and started waving down their waiter.

“She’s cleaning the apartment.”

“Who is?”

“Magda.”

“She does know that she’s the nanny, not the maid, right?”

“I’ve invited a monster into my home, Buck.”

“A monster that’s cleaning up after you. I’m not seeing the downside.”

“The downside is that she’s taken control of everything. She’s not a household manager, she’s a household tyrant”

“...I’m just not going to say anything, even though I really want to, right now.”

“Probably for the best.”

“Where are you right now, while the tyrant cleans your toilet?”

“Actually, she’s cleaning the kitchen. I’m drinking wine in my bathtub.”

“She watches the kid and cleans the house while you take relaxing baths. Maybe I should steal her away.”

“I’m not taking a bath. I’m… I’m hiding, okay?”

“Wow.”

“Shut up, I couldn’t deal with her anymore. Work sucked today.”

“Bad calls?”

“No. Well, one, but… Someone’s using my system I.D. to pick out targets for burglaries.”

“Oh. Wow.”

“Yeah.”

“...Taylor’s being sued by Jonah Greenway.”

“What!?”

“So you just showed up to Hen's house and left the dog?” Eddie asked, sounding absolutely appalled. And maybe a little amused. They were in the engine, heading for a call. “You didn’t even call ahead?”

“If I called, she might have said no,” Bobby pointed out.

“So why aren’t you keeping Hoover?” Albert asked, twisting around in his seat to look towards the captain.

“Because apparently, both Athena and May are allergic to dogs.”

“Ma’s allergic? Is that why she’s been sneezing?” Buck asked, offended. “She told me it was a cold. She lied to me!”

“She lied to us both, Buck,” Bobby told him. “Okay, according to the caller, her brother was in the attic and had some sort of accident. Now he’s not answering and she can’t reach him.”

They pulled up to the scene, parking right behind a trailer truck with insulation equipment, a long hose leading from the back of it and into the house. They clambered out, joined by Chimney from the ambulance, and headed inside. Buck hopped into the back of the trailer to make sure everything was turned off before joining the others, grabbing a thermal camera from one of the engine’s compartments on the way.

Inside, the house was clearly in the middle of renovations of some sort. Equipment and paint cans and sheets of plastic were everywhere, and the walls had patches of plaster. The hose led up into the attic, through an entrance in the ceiling that was blocked off by a waterfall of purple insulation goo.

“We’re not getting to the attic that way,” Bobby declared, and Buck only just resisted saying something snarky as he peered around with the thermal cam. “Do you know if he has his phone on? Have you tried calling him?”

“I’ve been calling him since before I called you,” the victim’s sister said, phone in hand and calling again anyway. “He’s not answering.”

“I’m not hearing any ringing,” Eddie told them, pacing the room as he listened.

"He said he would soundproof the house."

“And it looks like he insulated it pretty good, too,” Buck said, following behind him, the camera focused on the ceiling above. “I’m not getting any kind of- Wait! Found him, Cap!”

Everyone crowded around him behind to peer at the screen, which showed a human shaped blob of red and yellow. It was stark against the cold purples and blues surrounding it. It also wasn’t moving.

“It looks like he’s unconscious,” Chimney commented, craning his neck to see.

“The longer we wait, the hotter he’s going to get,” Buck said, lowering the camera a little so the shortest of them could see it a little easier. He also lowered his voice, so the sister couldn’t hear. “All that insulation, he’s going to start cooking.”

“Alright.” Bobby clapped his hands together. “We gear up, mark it out and then cut it out.”

“Hang on.” The sister sounded worried. “Cut what out?”

“The ceiling,” Buck cheerfully told her, pointing above him.

“A piece of the ceiling,” Albert clarified, though the clarification did not make her any less anxious looking.

Bobby gently steered her out of her own house, before he and the others went to the truck. They put on respirators and oxygen tanks, before grabbing the chainsaws and one of the pike poles. Chimney and Albert grabbed the gurney from the ambulance and dragged it inside with them.

Using the pike, and referencing the thermal cam every other second, Eddie etched a vague rectangle into the ceiling. It was bigger than it needed to be, probably, just so there was more room for error. Supports were wedged underneath it, near the center, to reduce the risk of their vic crashing to the floor. There were already a pair of ladders present, so those were pulled over, so Buck and Eddie could climb them.

It was slow going, carving out the rectangle. Dust, both insulation and wood, filled the room. At one point, they had to climb down and reposition the ladders, so that they weren’t at risk of overextending and toppling over. Eventually, though, they came full circle. Or, full polygon.

The saws were exchanged for halligans, and they broke off pieces of living room ceiling and attic flooring, letting them fall to the ground below. When they were able to reach the trapped man, Buck and Eddie held him in place while the others removed the supports, allowing the last pieces of wood and plaster to fall away.

“He got Han Solo’d,” Buck said, staring at the way the insulation had formed and solidified around him.

“Just peel him out. Chimney, Albert, get ready to help lower him down,” Bobby ordered. And then, “Chimney?”

“Are you fake crying?” Albert asked, and Buck looked down to see the older Han pretend to wipe a tear from his mask.

“I’m just… I’m just so proud,” the proud man, giving an exaggerated sniffle. “Our Buckaroo just made a Star Wars reference.”

Buck rolled his eyes.

“Just get over here.”

Through teamwork, they managed to get the guy down safely. There was one heart stopping moment where Buck let go of him too early and he almost hit the ground, but they caught him in time, so it didn’t count. They got him on the gurney, which was then loaded into the back of the ambulance with Chimney and Albert, joined by the guy’s sister.

“I don’t even want to know how much cleaning that up is going to cost,” Buck said to no one in particular as he helped put away the saws and supports. “I kinda feel bad for her.”

“Of course you do,” Eddie said, shaking his head.

“We just cut an entire section out of her ceiling, and her attic is going to need to be excavated,” he said, gesturing widely. “There’s no way she has home insurance that can cover that.”

“You’re probably right. I don’t think there’s an insurance plan that covers Younger Sibling Stupidity.”

“What? No.” Buck placed his oxygen tank inside the compartment and slammed it shut. “He’s clearly the older sibling. The younger siblings get all the brains.”

Eddie paused in putting away the second chainsaw to turn and fix him with a look. It was at that moment that Buck remembered, far too late, that his husband was the oldest of three.

“¿Perdóneme?”

“Uh. Um. I-” He glanced around, spotted Bobby. “I think Pops just called for me.”

“Oh no,” Eddie said, following him as he ran for the captain. “You come back here and explain your logic.”

Chapter End Notes

The fun thing about writing an AU is that I can just straight up rewrite a call that bugs the heck out of me.

Chapter 13: The Eve

Chapter Summary

In which the author realizes that they don't have to be concerned with animal actors.

On Friday morning, just after they’d gotten home from their shift, Eddie’s phone rang. Buck had already crashed into bed, exhausted after doing much of the heavy lifting at yet another crash on the freeway in the predawn hours. Eddie was better off only because he’d had to help Chimney and Albert, so he was lounging in the living room when the call came.

When he pulled out his phone, the name on his screen had him frowning.

“Aren’t you supposed to be busy with your second chance?” he asked when he answered.

“I am about to be,” Hen told him. “But I need you to do me a favor first.”

“What kind of favor?” He made no effort to hide the dubiousness in his voice.

“So it turns out that Hoover is not so great a fit for our family.”

“Why? What happened?”

“He tore up my entire damn living room!” Hen told him. “We thought someone broke in at first. He even chewed up the baseboards!”

“Wow, okay. So you’re wanting me to help find him a new home?”

“Actually, I was hoping that you’d…?” She trailed off, but he got the gist of it well enough.

“You know what?” Eddie got up and went to fetch his shoes. “Sure.”

He should probably spend more time thinking about it. The fact that Hoover supposedly tore up the Wilsons’ living room should definitely be enough to make him pause. But he’d been just fine at Bobby and Athena’s. The only problem there had been Athena’s allergies.

Hen was just exaggerating, he was sure.

“Really?” She sounded both surprised and relieved. “Thank you so much!”

“Chris was wanting a pet,” he told her. “And I know Buck isn’t going to say no to a dog.”

“Buck would have taken the dog with him if  he’d been there, no doubt,” she said. “Okay, I’ll let Karen know. Thank you so much!”

“You’re very welcome,” he said. “ Doctor Wilson.”

“Shut up, don’t jinx it.”

The line went dead as Hen rushed to get off. He slid the phone back into his pocket and, shoes in hand, stepped out onto the back patio. Abuela was lounging in a chair, reading a book that had a woman on the cover who Eddie was certain he’d seen on Nickelodeon, back in the day. Also, he thought it might have been Buck’s book.

“Hey, I’m running to Hen and Karen’s real quick,” he told her when she glanced up. “Tell Buck, if he wakes up?”

“Por supuesto,” she replied, nodding.

“Gracias.”

They lived closer to the Wilsons now than they did before the move, but it was still about a twenty-minute drive. Plenty of time for Hen to have called or texted Karen, because the woman was already opening the door when Eddie was climbing the porch steps.

She already had Hoover on a leash, the pooch sitting patiently by her feet.

“Are you sure about this?” Karen asked him when he reached her. “I know you told Hen it was okay, but it just feels like a little short notice.”

“Oh, Hoover’s great,” he said, crouching down. Immediately, the dog was on him for scratches, which he happily gave. “Chris and Buck are both going to love having a dog.”

He stood back up, and she handed him the leash.

“Here you go,” Karen said, and then held out a hedgehog shaped dog toy. “And this, too.”

He squeezed it, making it squeak, and immediately had the dog’s attention.

“He loves the thing,” she told him.

“Well alright then. C’mon, Hoover.”

They started off for the truck, and immediately Hoover took the lead. He practically yanked Eddie along the yard, and it took a bit of tugging at his leash to get him to stop at the truck instead of going farther down the sidewalk. Though, once Eddie opened the back door, he seemed to understand what was happening. He leaped inside, and happily curled up with the toy once it was thrown in, as well.

Several blocks away, Eddie realized that Karen had forgotten to pass Hoover’s food along, nor had she given him his bowls. There was the option to turn around and get them, but then Karen might think he was already reneging on ownership. In fact, with how relieved both she and her wife had sounded to be rid of the poor pooper, she might very well barricade the door and refuse to answer if he went back so soon. Instead, he took a detour and headed for the nearest pet store.

By the time he made it home, he had a large bag of dry food as well as several cans of wet. Along with bowls and a couple toys that Hoover had taken a liking to.

(Perhaps he’d been wanting a dog, too.)

“And who is this hombre guapo?” Abuela asked, when Eddie led Hoover through the house and into the backyard. She’d still been reading, though she set the book down on the patio table to give the dog the pets he was begging for.

“Hoover,” Eddie explained, unhooking the leash from his collar. “He needed a new home.”

“As long as you do not dig in my garden, you are more than welcome,” she told the dog, looking it straight in the eyes. “¿Entender?”

Eddie doubted the dog actually understood, but Abuela nodded as if they had come to an accord.

Throughout the rest of the morning, it became extremely evident that Hoover was not going to be Eddie’s dog, nor would he be Buck’s or Chris’. It was obvious, within moments of meeting her, that he absolutely adored Abuela. He trailed after her like… Well, like a puppy.  Or Buck. Either simile worked.

While she sat on the back patio, Hoover ran around the yard, coming back to her regularly for pets and scratches. When she moved into the living room to watch one of her shows, Hoover curled up at her feet as she sat in her chair. And when Abuela went into the kitchen to start on lunch, the dog was right on her heels.

“When did we get a dog?” Buck asked, finally pulling himself out of bed. He was standing in the doorway to the dining room, rubbing his eyes, sleep ruffled with his curls free from product.

“That’s Hoover,” Eddie told him, from where he was sitting at the breakfast bar. “Sleep well?”

“Like the dead,” the other man yawned. “Hoover’s the dog Pops stole, right? Didn’t he shove him onto Hen?”

“Right. But Hen and Karen couldn’t keep him, so they called me.”

“Alright.” Buck knelt down and the dog bounded to him, because there was a new person willing to give him scratches and pets. “Oh yeah, you’re a good boy, aren’t you?”

“Hen said he destroyed their living room, but I don’t know.” Eddie moved over to them and knelt down as well. “He did fine at Bobby’s, and he’s been fine here.”

“Yeah, uh, babe? You know there’s a small problem with us keeping him, right?”

“What small problem?”

“Ma and May are still allergic. What are we going to do when they come over for dinner on Sunday?”

“There’s medication for it now,” Abuela chimed in from the kitchen. “They can take some of that before they come. Or, if it bothers them so badly, they can both eat outside on the porch.”

“He’s Abuela’s dog,” Eddie whispered, and Buck nodded in understanding.

“It was Noah.”

“Who was Noah? Who’s Noah?”

“One of the new dispatchers, the one I was training. He stole my password.”

“What a dick.”

“He said his stepdad owed people money. They hurt the stepdad and then threatened his mom. Honestly it sounds like a movie Howie would make me watch.”

“I knew I should have asked Ma to run background checks on the new people. I knew it the moment you said one of them knew your name.”

“Oh, that was Noah, too.”

“Clearly I should follow my instincts. Next group, background checks.”

“They run background checks already.”

“Clearly they need to be better about it. So what happened with Noah?”

“Oh, he got arrested. And Athena ambushed the guys at the next house they broke into.”

“Because Ma is awesome.”

“She- Is that barking?”

“That’s just Hoover. I got it, Eddie. C’mon boy, you need to go outside?”

“Since when did you guys have a dog?”

“Since this morning, when Eddie picked him up from Hen and Karen.”

“Wait, isn’t Hoover the dog that Bobby took into protective custody?”

“The one he stole, yeah. I need to remember to get a pooper scooper.”

“Ew.”

“We have both been puked on. Talking about picking up dog poop is barely worth the ‘ew,’ Mads.”

“Yeah, yeah.”

On Saturday, everyone gathered at the Grant-Nash residence for a party.

A celebration for Hen passing her practical exam.

It was an impromptu party put together on short notice, so there wasn’t time for decorations or embarrassing cakes. There was time, however, to get enough hotdogs and hamburgers to keep the station fed, along with various veggies and whatever else Bobby decided to grill. Bobby and Buck, because he got dragged into helping almost as soon as he stepped foot in the house.

Not that he minded, obviously.

Hen and Karen were actually ones of the last to arrive, after most of their shift showed up. Ravi arrived with Albert and was greeted with hugs and cheers, since it had been a month and a half since most of the people there had seen him. Chimney arrived sans Maddie and Jee, which had Buck pouting a little. But then again, it’s not like anyone else had brought their kids. Even May was absent, which was unsurprising for a college student on a Saturday evening.

“So how’s Hoover?” Bobby asked, flipping one of the burgers as he did so.

“Hoover is the dog?” Ravi asked, hovering around the grill like Eddie was doing. He’d been catching up with Bobby, since they hadn’t actually laid eyes on each other for a bit.

“Hoover is the dog.”

“He’s getting spoiled,” Eddie answered Bobby. He snagged one of the pieces of pepper off the top level of the grill, hissing when he singed the tips of his fingers in the process.

“Seriously?” Buck rolled his eyes. “You are a firefighter.”

“I’m hungry. I’m stuck here, smelling the food-”

“You are not stuck here, oh my god.” Using a BBQ fork, he speared a couple more of the peppers up, holding them out for his husband to take. “Here. Do you want any, Ravi?”

“I can wait,” the younger man told him, waving a hand.

“Gracias, mi sol,” Eddie murmured, snagging them. He juggled them between his palms for a couple seconds, before devouring them in a couple bites. 

“These burgers and hotdogs are done,” Bobby said, long suffering. “Grab a plate and buns, Eddie. You too, Ravi.”

“Thank you, Cap.” The man practically lunged for one of the paper plates and just about tore the buns in two in his hurry. Ravi was far more sedate, in comparison.

“What, not even going to get me a plate?” Buck complained, poking at him with the fork.

“You’ve got two working hands.”

“Wow.”

“Buckaroo!” Athena called from inside the house. “Get in here!”

“Comin’, Ma!” Buck yelled back, handing Eddie the fork. “Here, load yourself up.”

“Tell everyone the food’s ready,” Bobby said.

He passed Hen and Karen on the way, the pair of them coming out to the backyard, and he paused to wrap Hen in a tight hug. She grunted and laughed and pushed him away. Continuing into the house, he found Athena with Albert and Chimney at the kitchen island. She had a bottle of champagne and was carefully pouring it into several tall glasses. Next to them sat a glass of mineral water.

“We’re going bubbly,” Buck remarked. “How can I help?”

“Carrying these,” Athena explained. “We needed another pair of hands.”

“You got it,” he said, taking two of the flutes once they were filled, as well as Bobby’s glass of water. He and the Han brothers followed along behind the sergeant as she led them out through the patio doors.

“Paging Dr. Wilson,” she said, announcing herself. When Hen turned around, she handed her one of the drinks in her hand.

“I’ll toast to that,” Chimney declared, as the other drinks got handed out. “To Hen, and her beginning this new journey on a high note. Congratulations, but we’re still gonna miss you.”

“Hey,” Buck said, once they’d raised their glasses and drank to the toast. “Do you guys think that this might be, y’know. The last time we’re all together like this?”

It was an old, recurring anxiety. Since Red. Since before Red. He’d made peace with the fact that this worry, the fear of everyone drifting out of touch, would always haunt him. It didn’t mean that he didn’t still seek out reassurances that it wasn’t going to happen.

Eddie, standing beside him, pressed against his side. He understood, Buck knew.

“No,” Hen said easily, waving the prospect away like an annoying fly. “We live like, twenty minutes away from each of you. This will not be the last time that we’re together.”

“I certainly hope not,” Bobby said. “Although you’re gonna be pretty busy.”

“Never too busy for all of you, Cap.”

Buck frowned and chewed on his lip for a moment, before raising his hand between him and Hen, his pinky sticking out.

“What is-?” She stared at him. “What are we, nine year old girls?”

Buck simply wagged his hand at her in silent demand.

“Just go along with it, Hen,” Chimney said, slapping her on the shoulder. “It’s a Buckley thing.”

Rolling her eyes, Hen did just that, twisting her own pinky around Buck’s.

“There, happy now?” she asked.

“Yes,” was his simple answer, letting their hands drop away.

“I can’t believe I’m going to miss you guys,” she huffed.

“No, nope.” Eddie shook his head. “You just promised you’ll keep seeing us. You won’t have time to miss anyone.”

“Buck’ll probably put Hen Days into his calendar or something,” Ravi told her, sipping at his champagne. “Or just. Surprise you when you’re trying to catch a nap.”

“Is that what he did with you?” Karen asked.

“A couple weeks ago he texted me, saying to cancel any plans I had, and then showed up at my door ten minutes later to drag me out. My roommates thought I was being kidnapped.”

“To be fair,” Eddie said. “You kinda were.”

“They’re exaggerating,” Buck told Bobby and Athena, who were staring at him judgmentally. “I didn’t- I didn’t really kidnap him.”

“The way he told it, you threw him into the jeep and didn’t tell him where you were taking him,” Albert said, grinning.

“I- Okay, maybe…”

“Was that better or worse than having him chase you around the station with a chainsaw?” Hen asked Ravi, ignoring Buck’s sputtering.

“I’ll say better,” the young man said, after a moment’s thought. “Because I got free food out of it.”

“Hold on,” Chimney said, cutting in. “When did Buck chase you with a chainsaw?”

“Uh.” Everyone glanced at each other, but it was Hen who answered.

“It was right after the blackout…”

It might have been one of the last evenings he got to spend with all of them, it might have been heavy on the bittersweet. But it was a good evening, a good memory in the making. One that Buck planned to cherish.

As soon as his friends stopped bullying him.

And then everything went to hell, just a few days later.

Chapter 14: Karen

Chapter Summary

In which there is an explosion at a lab.

Fairly early into a shift, just a few days after the party for Hen, they were called to an explosion at a science lab. Buck knew, even as he climbed into the engine, that it wasn’t going to be a “fun” call. There were so many things that could go wrong with a fire at such a location, so many chemicals and fumes that could ignite when they weren’t looking.

Every science lab was a bomb, just waiting for a lit fuse. It was one of the reasons why there were usually so many safety protocols in place. It was why, when those protocols failed or were ignored, things got so very messy, so very quickly.

The lab they were called to was at PAAL, an acronym with a meaning that Buck didn’t quite catch. Though, the acronym sounded vaguely familiar. They were not the first station to get there, with the campus first responders already swarming the building in their bright yellow turnouts, working to evacuate everyone. The moment the engine came to a stop, Bobby jumped out and rushed to converse with the incident commander.

Buck and Eddie went for the compartments on the side of the truck, giving free access to the oxygen tanks and pulling out the hoses. Chimney and Albert, meanwhile, snagged their medical bags and waited to be told where to go.

“Alright, guys,” Bobby called out, done getting the update from the IC. He jogged over to them. “Suit up, we’re heading inside.”

“Isn’t this where Karen works?” Eddie asked, staring at the building’s name.

“Yep,” Chimney confirmed. It explained why PAAL sounded so familiar to Buck.

“Do you think Hen knows?” he asked.

Behind him, there was the sound of a car screeching to a halt. He saw it was Hen’s car, when he turned to look.

“Yep,” Chimney said again.

They watched as she sprinted across the parking lot, ignoring them in favor of intercepting-

“Denny’s here!?” Buck yelped, slipping an oxygen tank onto his back and hefting a roll of hoses over his shoulder. “Shit.”

“Uh, I don’t see Karen anywhere,” Albert said, craning his neck around. The others did the same, to no avail. And Buck knew there was no good reason for Denny to be coming out without her.

Double shit.

“She must still be inside,” Bobby told them. “Come on, we’re going for the source, a lab in the southeast corner.”

Each of them gave a grim nod and started for the entrance, only for Hen to cut them off.

“What do we know?” she demanded.

“Not much,” Bobby told her, the others pausing on the stairs leading in. “We’re headed inside to put out some fires and look for survivors.”

“Okay.” The former paramedic nodded. “I’ll suit up.”

“Hen, no.” The captain shook his head, the motion slightly exaggerated by his helmet. “You’re not going inside.”

“My wife is in there,” she said. “Of course I am.”

“You’re not a firefighter anymore, and you’re too close to the situation,” Bobby said. Which was rich coming from any one of them, much less him. “I love you. Now let us do our jobs.”

Hen looked past him to where they were standing, looking slightly down at the pair. Buck felt for her, remembering each and every time Eddie was in danger and he could not be there to help. Beside him, Chimney gave her a nod.

“Bring her back to me, Cap,” she finally said, and seemed to shrink a little with the resignation.

“Guaranteed.”

It was dark inside, with nothing illuminating the way except for the flashlights hooked to their coats and helmets. There was the occasional flashing emergency light, which honestly served only to make things more disorienting, along with the droning alarm. Occasionally, Buck caught glimpses of work that had been dropped and projects left abandoned. Papers scattered across the floor in a glass-walled room. In another, a wheeled robot repeatedly rammed itself into the doors. One of the rooms they passed had unfinished equations scrawled across the glass.

The whole thing gave him too much of a horror movie vibe.

“Keep your eyes peeled for any victims who didn’t make it all the way out,” Bobby instructed. 

They trooped along the halls, seeing no one left behind yet. It was easy going, at least until they got to the entrance to the lab they needed to get to. It looked as if office furniture and various debris had been thrown into it by the explosion, and now there was a barricade pressed against the twisted doors. It was backlit by an orange glow.

And there was a woman shouting for help on the other side.

Bobby’s light fell on a hazard diamond plastered upon the wall. Buck spotted a 2 in the red Fire quarter and a worrisome 4 in the yellow Reactivity.

“Everybody be aware, we’re entering an area with toxic gasses, pressurized oxygen, and organic peroxides,” Bobby told them. Chimney stepped past him to try and peer through the gaps in the barricade, and Buck stepped up on a toppled table to look through an observation window. “Once we’re in, keep a close eye on your air. Eddie, get the saw. We’ll have to cut through, piece by piece.”

“On it,” he heard Eddie say, though his own attention was focused on the utter lack of activity from the fire suppression on the other side.

“Looks like the sprinkler system was destroyed in the explosion,” Buck reported. “There’s spot fires everywhere.”

“Alight, I’m gonna put you in charge of putting those fires out,” the captain said.

“I’ll grab CO2s.”

It ended up taking all of them to get through the impromptu barricade. Even with the use of the saw to cut through the metal, and the jaws to tear it apart. Burnt office chairs were flung aside, a dented cart was pushed behind them, a twisted metal table was shoved out of the way. Even with everyone working on it - and there was more than just the core group of them, there was Jones, and Ortiz and the others who came in on the ladder truck - it took far longer than anyone was comfortable with. The woman they’d heard had gone silent, either because she knew they were coming, or…

“We’re in, Cap!” Eddie shouted, shoving the last of it out of the way.

“Alright, let’s move. Go, go.”

The other side was a hellscape. The air was hazy with smoke and fumes, there was fire and debris everywhere. The emergency lights were still flashing and the alarm was still droning. And there were people, too, sprawled on the ground and collapsed around the room. A tank by the wall was hissing, spewing forth the gas it held until Bobby reached it and turned the emergency valve off.

“Chim!” Bobby shouted, indicating one of the people laying on the floor. The paramedic knelt beside them, and shook his head.

“No pulse,” he reported. “Brain matter’s on the ground. He’s gone.”

“Alright. Chim, Al, keep looking for survivors,” the captain instructed. “Buck, start spraying.”

“On it.” Buck rounded up a couple of the others and started hitting the spot fires with extinguishers. It was too dangerous to use the hose, not without knowing precisely what kind of chemicals were potentially at play. He did not want a repeat of that nursery home, where he wasn’t paying enough attention and needed Ravi to point out that the water was simply making things worse.

He kept an ear on the barely controlled chaos around him. Eddie found a survivor, who got evacuated by Ortiz. Bobby called out for others, and a familiar voice shouted back in response.

“Here!” Karen said, though Buck couldn’t see her. “Over here!”

Bobby went to here, rounding a burning tank of something, and Buck saw him pause momentarily before reporting that,

“We got someone missing an arm.”

Buck immediately turned his head to locate Eddie. The other man was looking in his direction, though the mask kept him from seeing his expression. Buck jerked his head towards Bobby and Karen, and his husband nodded in response before rushing over with Chimney and Albert.

More and more, the hellscape of a lab was resembling a warzone, and Buck figured it was a safe assumption that a former army medic would be needed.

Focusing back on the fighting fires part of being a firefighter, Buck allowed the commotion to wash over him. He could make out Bobby reassuring Karen about Denny, heard a snippet about someone’s artery. Buck hoped it wasn’t Karen they were working on, but couldn’t spare the time or attention to check. Mostly, though, the near entirety of his attention was directed at spotting flames and smothering them.

See the fire, put out the fire, as he phrased it so very long ago.

“Buck!” The captain calling his name had him turning slightly. “What’s your ETA? Karen needs an escort out.”

“On my way,” he responded, putting out the last of the flames in front of him. He handed the extinguisher to one of the others and jogged over to where Bobby was standing.

There was, indeed, an arm on the floor that he had to step over. It thankfully wasn’t Karen’s, he could tell that at a glance. She was kneeling beside the head of the person Eddie and the paramedics were working on, an oxygen mask on her face.

“Alright, Karen?” She glanced up at Bobby’s voice. “It’s time to go.”

“No,” she said, shaking her head. “I’m not leaving.”

“I’m not asking,” Bobby responded. “Buck’s gonna take you down. Denny and Hen are waiting outside, let’s go.”

“I need to stay with her,” Karen argued. “She’s my responsibility.”

“She is in the best hands,” the captain said. “We’ll be right behind you. Let’s go.”

Karen was not happy, and was clearly reluctant, but she still got to her feet. She ignored the arm that Buck held out to steady her with, leading rather than following him out of the destroyed lab. She was covered in blood, he noticed as they exited into the hallway. Her shirt was practically drenched in it, and he thought for a moment that he should stop her and check for injuries.

But she was ambulatory, so even if she was hurt, she wasn’t hurt badly enough to justify lingering inside. Once they were outside, he figured, where it was safe. He’d even let Hen do it herself, just to set the woman’s mind at ease. And also because he trusted Hen more than any of the other paramedics currently outside.

Her hand grabbing his wrist stopped both his thoughts and his feet.

“Did you hear that?” she asked.

“We already cleared this floor,” he assured. “It’s probably nothing.”

“Well, I- I swear I-”

There was a groan and a crash from behind them, and they both swirled around to look.

“Okay, I- I heard that, too,” he admitted. And then Karen snagged the light from off his coat, rushing for the room it sounded like it came from. He chased after her. “Hey! Wait!”

They were all as bad as each other, and none of them were ever going to be allowed to yell at him for something like this again. Except Eddie. And Bobby. And Athena. But only those three! And Abuela, too, obviously. So those four and only those four.

Wait, Maddie.

Those five!

“Let me go first,” Buck said, catching up with her. The room looked more like an office than a lab, with desks and cabinets. “Stay behind me.”

Karen, of course, ignored him entirely. She pushed farther into the room, coming to a stop once she rounded one of the desks and shoved the light back into his hands. There was a man on the ground, whimpering and groaning.

“Are you okay?” she asked him, kneeling.

“I fell,” the man said. He had a bleeding wound near his temple. “Hurt my leg.”

“Can you stand?” Buck asked him, clipping his light back to his coat to free up his hands.

“I hit my head,” the man told him. “Could only make it in here. I tried to get up, but… I’m too dizzy.”

A severe head wound, then. Definitely a concussion of some sort.

“I’m going to carry you out,” Buck explained to him, urging Karen out of the way. “The paramedics outside will take a look at you.”

The man did not weigh nearly as much as Eddie did. It was easy to lift him up and put him over his shoulder, an action which had the man grunting in complaint. Carrying him out into the hall with Karen once again leading the way, Buck frowned at the way that the blood on her shirt seemed darker than it had before.

“Are you-?” he started to ask, but got interrupted by his radio.

“Captain One Eighteen, this is Sergeant Grant.” Why would Athena be on their radio channel? He knew she didn’t work today.

“Go for Captain Nash.”

“We have a young man out front who seems to have been in one of those labs.” Athena’s words had Karen once again yanking Buck to a stop, this time so she could grab at his radio. Did she manhandle Hen like this? “He’s got on a white plastic suit.”

“That’s Alvin,” she said into the radio. “Is he okay?”

“Hen is working on him now,” Athena replied. “He kept trying to warn us.”

“What did he say?”

“Coolant, danger, warn them.”

For some reason, the combination of words had Karen looking utterly horrified.

“The clean room,” she said. “Bobby, you need to get to the clean room lab and make sure nobody opens that door! If the coolant system is disabled, there’s no control on the combustion systems inside the device. An infusion of oxygen will cause a dangerous fluctuation.”

“Dumb it down,” Buck requested, at the same time Bobby radioed with, “What does that mean, Karen?”

“The room is a bomb,” she stated plainly. “And the door is the fuse.”

Well, then. Fuck.

“Copy that.” Bobby didn’t say anything else for several moments, probably giving orders to Eddie and the others. “Alright, Karen, talk to me. Where am I going?”

Karen gave Bobby directions as they started for the exit again. Buck felt remarkably like Hoover, being led around on a leash. Or, in this case, by his radio. They had apparently wasted so much time stopped for one reason or another that the others were able to catch up to them near the exit, wheeling the armless patient along.

In the sun, Karen looked worse. Without the cover of shadows and the disorientation of flashing lights, Buck could see just how red her previous white shirt had become. And there was a cut on her forehead that he’d missed before. But she rushed for Hen and Denny, and Buck knew that Hen would check her over.

Instead of pausing the reunion to tell Hen how to do her job - old job, at least - he helped get the man he’d been carrying to the other paramedics. The woman Eddie and the others had been working on was being lifted into one of the waiting ambulances, Eddie slamming the doors shut behind her.

“She gonna be okay?” Buck asked, when they reached each other.

“She’ll be- Well, maybe not fine, but she’ll live,” his husband answered. “What took you so long getting out?”

“Karen is bossy,” he complained. “She kept yanking me around everywhere.”

“That sounds like- Karen!”

Eddie’s eyes were fixed behind him, going wide.

Buck turned, just in time to see Karen finish collapsing to the ground.

It turned out that a piece of shrapnel from the explosion had embedded itself into Karen’s side. Adrenaline and shock, and maybe a minor concussion, had kept her going through the blood loss and kept her from even realizing something was wrong. When she got outside, when she got to safety, her body decided it was a good time to scare the living shit out of everyone.

But one emergency splenectomy later, and Karen was going to be okay.

Karen, though, was not the person Buck was most worried about. Trauma had a splash zone, after all. Even though Karen had been the one hurt, even though she’d been the one to collapse and code in the ambulance, it was Hen who had to watch it happen to her wife. Hen who had to put on a strong face for their son, even as she tried to cope with the fact that her world had nearly collapsed around her.

Buck remembered far too well what that was like. It was why, the day after, he loaded the jeep up with tupperwares of food and made the twenty minute journey to the Wilsons’.

It took a bit of work once he got there, trying to juggle the containers as he carried them to the front door. And then he had to figure out how to knock on the door, eventually settling on kicking at it when he nearly dropped everything. Hen, when she answered, was not smiling at him.

“Why are you kicking at my door?” she demanded.

“I bring gifts!” he announced in lieu of answering, bullying his way past her. “Denny at school?”

“He stayed home, I let him sleep in,” Hen said, closing the door and trailing after him through the living room and into the kitchen. “We didn’t get much sleep last night.”

“Understandable.”

“Why are you bringing me- Is that Bobby’s cooking?”

“Abuela’s,” he corrected, setting them down on the counter. “I kinda figured, with Karen hurt and you so busy with your third year of medical school, you wouldn’t complain about having meals you can just heat up.”

When he glanced up, he found Hen looking somehow sheepish.

“I’m not,” she said, “going to be busy with school.”

“What do you mean?”

“I mean I’m not going to be going to school, Buck,” Hen told him, opening the fridge to make room for the possibly unreasonable number of tupperware containers. Abuela, upon being informed of what had happened, had spent the entire previous night cooking up a storm. “I’m going back to work. After, you know. A couple weeks from now. When Karen is back on her feet.”

“I don’t understand,” Buck told her, handing her tupperwares to put into the fridge. “The way you talked about it, I thought being a doctor was your dream.”

“And it was okay, great even, when it was just a dream,” she said. “But then the dream started becoming a reality and…”

“And reality isn’t the same.”

“No.” Hen shook her head. “I didn’t- I didn’t realize how much was going to change. How much I was going to be giving up for it. We weren't going to be fostering anymore. I wasn’t going to be working with you all anymore. I just… I guess I just thought I’d be able to have my cake and eat it, too. That I’d be able to go to school and become a doctor, and keep doing all those other things at the same time.”

Buck nodded in understanding.

“When’s Karen coming home?”

“Tomorrow,” she replied, looking exhausted all of a sudden as she closed the fridge door. “I was going to go visit her after Denny woke up and had breakfast. Or brunch, I guess.”

“Are you okay, Hen?” he asked, though it was a very stupid question. Of course she wasn’t okay.

“I’m fine,” she lied. And he knew it was a lie. Bobby had told him about the ambulance ride, how Karen had coded and Hen had been the one to give her CPR. How she’d almost collapsed herself, once her wife was wheeled away into the ER.

His friend was not okay. She wasn’t anywhere close to it.

“I haven’t ever talked to any of you about last year, have I?” Buck asked. “The, uh. When Eddie was…”

Hen shook her head.

“He asked me if I was hurt,” he told her. “In the truck, on the way to the hospital. He was the one bleeding out, and he asked if I was hurt.”

“You were covered in a lot of blood,” Hen said, reaching out as if to grip his shoulder, before letting her hand drop back to her side. “I know I was worried you were hurt.”

“Those hours, between him getting- Getting hurt, and Bobby telling me he was okay? Those were some of the worst hours of my life.” Buck chewed on his lip, trying to keep from losing himself in the memories completely. “That could have been the last thing he said to me, Hen. He asked me if I was hurt, and the moment I told him I was okay, he just… He closed his eyes, and he wouldn’t wake up no matter how much I begged. And I begged, Hen. I don’t think I stopped begging until we were pulling up to the ER doors.”

(“Eds, please. Wake up. Eddie, baby, c’mon. Please. Please, wake up. Wake up. Wake up, wake up, wake up-”)

“I was not okay after that,” Buck told his friend. “No matter how much I tried to pretend to be for Chris, I was not okay.”

“She was gone,” Hen whispered. “She was gone, Buck. She was- She was-”

He reached out and pulled her into a hug as she finally broke. Her entire body shook as she cried, and all he could think to do was rub a hand up and down her back until she settled. It took a while for her to run out of tears, and there was a large wet patch on his shirt by the time she was done.

“I’m sorry,” she said, wiping her eyes and sniffing a little as she pulled back. “I- God, your shirt.”

“It’s fine,” Buck told her, waving it off. “You should have seen me after I got the text from Bobby. About drowned poor Chris.”

“Still…”

“C’mon, go wash your face and then wake your kid,” he said, pushing her towards the kitchen door. “I’ll pull out one of the tupperwares and heat it up. Abuela’s tamales are the best way to start the day.”

“Okay,” Hen said, giving in. She paused in the doorway, looking back at him. “Thank you, Buck.”

“It’s what family is for,” he told her. 

She smiled gratefully at him, and then ducked down the hall, leaving him to find which of the tupperware contained the tamales.

Chapter 15: Dia de los Muertos

Chapter Summary

The last few days of October.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

“I can’t believe,” Buck complained, opening up the tub, “that we put this off for so long.”

It was the week leading up to Halloween, and they were only now pulling out the decorations. To be fair to them, the boxes they were in had ended up far in the back of everything when they moved in.
