Somehow, for some reason, the five to twenty boxes of Christmas lights and ornaments were closer to the door of the storage shed.

It was their day off, and they’d spent it by braving the shed and dragging out the decorations, theirs and Abuela’s. Now they were sitting together at the dining table while they went through them.

“We’ve been busy,” Eddie told him. “With work and Chris and everything else. Hell, with how often you’ve been over to Maddie’s or helping May and Hen or going to the Parent-Teacher things, it feels like you’re hardly home.”

And it was the truth. It was starting to get to the point that Eddie wondered if he should put his foot down. He wouldn’t, he would never, but sometimes he wondered if he should. Or at least, if he should start volunteering to go with him to Maddie’s or - god help him - the PTO meetings. Just so he could actually see his husband more often.

“Yeah, sorry.” Buck was looking like a kicked puppy again, which had not been his intent. “I- Sorry.”

“It’s fine, mi sol,” Eddie said, putting down a plastic pumpkin so he could squeeze the other man’s shoulder. “Abuela misses you, is all.”

“Oh, Abuela misses me, huh?”

“Hoover does, too.”

“Well, if they’re the only ones who do…”

Eddie shoved at him, rolling his eyes, before going back to digging through his tub. There was a sting of skulls, a goofy looking bat, a large tarantula that he was almost tempted to bring to the station. If he hid it in the right place, he could scare the hell out of Bobby and then blame it on someone else. Chimney, maybe.

“Hey, Eddie, look.” Buck’s words had him looking up. The other man had a calavera - a sugar skull - in his hand, sitting in his palm. It was colorful, with pastel swirls and shiny beads, and a sombrero perched on top. He held it up and in a really bad English accent, recited, “To be, or not to be…”

“Pretty sure that’s the wrong scene.”

“What? No it isn't. He’s holding the skull, right?”

“I think that’s the Yorick scene.”

“Who the hell is Yorick?”

“You never read it in school,” Eddie guessed, propping his chin on his fist. 

“I mean, I was supposed to.” Buck shrugged, and then frowned as he pulled out a string of colorful paper decorations. “Aw man, these are torn.”

“Are they? I’m not surprised,” Eddie said, taking the papel picado from his hands. “They’re literally just tissue paper. We’ll get more. Abuela used to make them herself, but her fingers don’t let her anymore.”

Buck let out a thoughtful hum, and Eddie could already see the internet search that inevitably lay ahead. His husband would probably go out and buy craft supplies, invite Chris to help him with his new project, and then pout when the pre-teen opted to do literally anything else. It would be simpler to just hit the market and buy something premade, but since when did Evan Buckley Diaz do things the simple way?

“We’ll hit the crafts store when we pick up Chris,” Eddie told him, rolling his eyes.

“Thanks, babe.” Buck leaned over to smack a kiss on his cheek.

“If you’re making a store run, I have requests,” Abuela herself said, entering from the living room. As always, Hoover trailed behind her, claws lightly tapping against the hardwood floors. They’d probably need trimming soon, and Eddie was not looking forward to that task.

Maybe he could get Buck to do it?

“And what is this request?” he asked, already resigned to hopping from store to store, while trying to keep Buck and Chris from impulse buying at every stop.

“Marigolds,” his grandmother said. “Cigars for your Abuelo, and that tea for Shannon.”

“Anything else?”

Abuela turned her gaze to Buck, who was setting her decorations out in nice little rows on the table.

“Anything for your brother?” she asked, and he looked up in confused surprise.

“Anything for- What?”

“For your brother, for the ofrenda,” she said. “We have that picture of him on the mantle, but we need an offering. What did he like? Food? Drinks? Candy?”

“I…” Buck trailed off, looking a bit lost. “I don’t know?”

“Ask your sister,” she said, patting him on the shoulder as she walked past him to the kitchen. “And then let me know.”

“I- I will.”

“What did Daniel like?”

“What did- What?”

“What… What did he like? Like- Like, favorite foods, I guess? Or- Or snacks? Candy?”

“Why are you asking about that?”

“Abuela. I think she’s going to put stuff out for him on the altar this year. Offerings and stuff. She does it every year for Eddie’s grandfather, and for Shannon… Mads?”

“Sweet Tarts. And Skittles. He- He loved Skittles. Could never have enough of them.”

“...I love Skittles.”

“I know.”

It was easier, and quicker, to make a grocery run without Chris and Buck. Eddie felt bad for thinking it, because he loved going to the store with his boys. They could make even the more dull trips into an adventure. But sometimes he didn’t feel up to an adventure, sometimes he just wanted to get in and out, without having to smack at people’s hands or have them sneak some sugar-filled monstrosity into the cart behind his back.

And he definitely didn’t want to hear Buck’s complaint about “no, Eds, this brand is better.” Even if he actually loved Buck’s little rants. What was better was being able to escape the grocery store with his savings intact, thanks much.

Eddie had been tasked with getting everything they needed for Sunday dinner. It was Friday, but they were working on Saturday, so they couldn’t put it off for much longer. It was also the last opportunity to grab the last of what Abuela needed for the ofrendas. The altar itself was already set up in the living room, near the gas fireplace and bookcases, having been set up just the day before.

The last aisle he went through was the candy aisle, one he’d been avoiding because it was filled with so many people. But he needed Skittles, along with candy to be passed out on Monday. So, finding his bravery and clutching it tight, he pushed his cart into the maelstrom of bodies.

Eddie had just tossed a bag of fun size packaged Skittles into the basket, and was trying to quickly figure out what bag of assorted candies wouldn’t be devoured before Halloween, when a familiar - and unwelcomed - voice startled him from behind.

“Eddie!” Lyndsay-with-a-Y squealed obnoxiously. “What a lovely surprise!”

Her hair, when Eddie turned around, was freshly dyed. A bright yellow that made her brown eyebrows stand out. Behind her, a girl Chris’ age was waiting impatiently.

“Lyndsay,” he greeted her, trying his best to smile. And then he realized that she shouldn’t be there, because, “Shouldn’t you be at the PTO meeting?”

“What?” She looked confused. “We already had this month’s meeting, Eddie. We won’t have another until next week.”

That did not make sense to him. He knew, or thought he knew, that Buck had gone to a PTO meeting that afternoon. It was why he wasn’t there, turning the shopping trip into a longer - if more enjoyable - adventure.

“I-” Eddie frowned. They had two meetings this month, didn’t they? “Are you sure?”

“Of course!” She gave him a sickeningly sweet smile. “Didn’t your husband, Beck, tell you? His muffins were… Okay, I guess. Not as good as mine.”

“It’s Buck,” he corrected automatically. “And he- He did, I guess I just… Misheard. Or remembered wrong, or something.”

“Oh, that’s alright.” She reached out and laid a hand on his arm, her wedding ring glinting in the fluorescent light of the store. Eddie wondered if it would be rude to shove her off. “You must be so busy. It must be… Hard, to keep in shape like this.”

“Right.” Yeah, that was enough of this. “I’m gonna…”

He didn’t finish the sentence. Grabbing a random bag of assorted candies, he mumbled an excuse and fled for the check-out lanes. His thoughts were swirling around his head.

Eddie must have misheard earlier, when Buck left. He must have heard “PTO” when Buck had actually said something else. That made sense, right? Or! Or! Everyone had lied to Lyndsay, because she was just so awful and no one wanted her there. So there, that was it. Either Eddie had misheard, or Lyndsay-with-a-Y was simply uninformed.

Right?

“This is nice,” Maddie commented, looking at the Dia de los Muertos altar in the corner of the living room. 

It started out as a folding table that Abuela had Eddie drag out before covering it with a striped tablecloth. There were pictures of the older Edmundo, along with Shannon and the one picture of Daniel that Buck had. In front of them was a cigar, a cup of tea that was gradually getting cold, and a small bag of skittles. There was a stick of burning incense and a pair of lit candles, too, along with salt poured out in the shape of a cross, decorated sugar skulls and a small bowl of water. Marigolds ringed everything.

Freshly baked bread - pan de muerto - sat on a plate, and the paper decorations Buck had spent a day cutting out were pinned to the wall behind it.

“That’s the picture I gave you, isn’t it?” Maddie asked, pointing at the one of Daniel. “The copy I had made?”

“Yeah, it is.” Buck nodded. “It’s usually on the mantle.”

He nodded at the fireplace mantle, where there were various plastic skulls filling the empty spaces. There were also rubber bats and paper ghosts hanging from the edges. Those weren’t the only Halloween decorations: outside on the porch was a carved pumpkin for each inhabitant, the dog included. And the yard was littered with glowing eyes and hanging skeletons.

It was early on Sunday, early enough that everyone who went to church was still at church. Maddie and Chimney had come over with Jee shortly before lunch, which was probably a good thing. Poor Hoover was looking especially depressed over not being able to go with Abuela, when Pepa had picked her up.

Being able to chase after a toddler in the backyard, under the watchful eyes of Chimney and Eddie, was apparently just what he needed.

“So, what are you going to do with everything afterwards?” Maddie asked, gesturing at the food sitting out. “Do you… Do you eat it? Throw it away?”

“I have no idea,” Buck admitted. “I don’t think you’re supposed to eat the stuff you leave out of the spirits, but I also don’t think you’re supposed to throw it away.”

“Maybe you could bury everything,” she suggested, and he let out a snort.

“We don’t want Hoover to start thinking he can do that.”

Not being left alone, and getting plenty of exercise, had seemingly curbed whatever destructive inclination the dog had shown at the Wilsons’. There had been no chewed up couch cushions nor destroyed baseboards. Not yet, anyways, and Buck was hoping it stayed that way.

“I can’t believe you guys ended up with Bobby’s stolen dog,” Maddie said, giggling at the thought. “I can’t believe Bobby stole a dog.”

“He took him into protective custody,” Buck loyally contested. “But uh, between you and me? I was kinda thinking of getting a snake, if we went pet shopping.”

“A snake?” His sister blinked at him. “Why?”

“Because Chimney is terrified of them.”

Maddie giggled and slapped at his arm.

“You’re awful!”

“I know,” he agreed, nodding solemnly. “I got a fake one hidden away, though. And there’s a fake spider for Bobby.”

“And what are you going to do with those?” she asked him, making an admirable effort to keep from smiling.

“Scare them, obviously.”

“Awful.” Maddie shook her head, and reached out to run her fingers along the picture of Daniel. “This is nice. I should have brought flowers or something.”

“There’s nothing stopping you from having one of these at your place,” Buck told her, nudging at her with his shoulder. “Or something like it. I mean, I- I don’t remember him, obviously. But you do. So…”

“Maybe I will,” she murmured. “I doubt they’ve visited him at all, and they’re right there.”

Buck didn’t have to ask who ‘they’ were. He would never understand the way Margaret and Phillip had done everything they could to erase their child from existence. He did understand that they were hurting, but so was Bobby, and he still spoke about Brooke and Robert Jr. Not often, but often enough. More now than in the beginning, certainly.

“This is nice,” Maddie said again, sniffling a bit. “We should probably go make sure Eddie and Howie haven’t killed each other.”

“I figure Hoover would let us know,” Buck said, following her as she headed out of the room. “Or Jee. Jee would definitely come rat them out.”

“Are you kidding? She’s your niece. She’d probably stand there and laugh while Eddie strangles her father.”

“That’s… Probably true. And I like that you admit that my man can beat up yours.”

“Howie is a lover, not a fighter.”

“My face would argue otherwise…”

Bobby let out a manly bellow when a (fake) spider was dropped on his shoulder in the middle of dinner. He also cursed like a sailor when the first spider was then accompanied by a bunch of tinier (plastic) ones that spilled all over his lap, leading to him toppling his chair over in an attempt to rid himself of them.

There was nothing manly about the shrill scream Chimney let out, when Chris draped a rubber snake around his neck.

Halloween was a bit glum. Not only was it a Monday - blah - but it was a Halloween spent without Chris. The pre-teen had opted to spend the evening at one of his friend’s houses, leaving both Eddie and Buck feeling bereft. Abuela had laughed at them, before leading Hoover to her room and leaving them to their morose moods.

Chris hadn’t even wanted to dress up this year. A fact that made Eddie want to weep.

They spent the evening watching bad slasher flicks and handing out candy to anyone that knocked on their door. Buck had volunteered to do that part, leaping up for a Golden Retriever to greet the strangers. In between, to alleviate them from their Chris-related doldrums, they were sent pictures of Jee-Yun’s first night trick-or-treating. 

She was dressed up as a little pumpkin, and it was adorable.

“I can’t believe Chris is too cool to dress up now,” Buck grumbled, setting down his phone. “I can’t believe he didn’t want to spend Halloween with us!”

“Our baby boy is growing up,” Eddie grumbled as the masked killer on the screen killed yet another promiscuous teenager. “Oh, that is so fake looking.”

“Why are we watching this?”

“Because it’s Halloween and our kid has abandoned us.”

“Right, right. But why are we watching this ? Isn’t there a better movie to watch?”

“I don’t know. It’s what was on when I stopped flipping channels.”

“We pay for four streaming services,” Buck groused. “Which we should probably cut back on. But we have them now, so surely we can find something better to watch.”

“And what would you like to watch?” Eddie asked, picking up the remote.

“I don’t know. It’s Halloween. Find a scary movie.”

“This is supposed to be a scary movie,” he pointed out.

“This isn’t a scary movie, this is…” Buck glanced back at the screen. “I don’t even know what this is. Are they in space?”

“I think so. I think this is the one where he turns into a cyborg.”

“That’s stupid. This is stupid.” Someone new knocked on their door and Buck leaped to his feet, grabbing the bowl of candy. “Find something else.”

“Aye, aye, mi sol,” Eddie said, mockingly saluting. He started searching for a new movie, listening to his husband greet and gush over the children at their door. A buzz and a pair of chimes from Buck’s phone distracted him and he automatically reached for it, thinking it was a new picture from Maddie.

It wasn’t.

It was instead a pair of messages from someone whose name he did not recognize, reading,

Raincheck Tues 🌧️ 🌧️ 🙁

Thurs ok? 💃🕺

He set the phone back down as he heard Buck saying his farewells, for some reason not wanting his husband to catch him looking at his phone. Normally it wouldn’t bother either of them, but this time… Eddie wasn’t certain, but there was an uncomfortable feeling brewing in the bottom of his stomach.

Just who the hell was Myra? And why was Buck meeting up with her behind his back?

Chapter End Notes

So is everyone keeping up with the in-story months? Yes? No?

Chapter 16: November

Chapter Summary

In which we return to the beginning.

November

Eddie tried not to let it bother him, tried not to let the doubts and the questions get to him. But ever since seeing the pair of texts from the unknown Myra on his husband’s phone, the ones he knew Buck had seen and read and then didn’t mention to him, he could not stop thinking about it. Just who were they? He could assume it was a woman, it certainly sounded like a woman’s name to him, but…

But why would Buck be seeing a woman behind his back?

There was an obvious answer. Eddie did not like the obvious answer. Eddie did not want to think about the obvious answer. But just because he didn’t want to think about it, it didn’t mean the obvious answer wasn’t a vulture circling his head.

He felt like he was thrown right back into September, having a crisis over that conversation between Buck and Hen. Except, it was the same crisis, wasn’t it? It was the same question that he hadn’t wanted to ask. He’d treated it like Schrodinger’s Cat, refusing to open the box because he was terrified to see if it was alive or dead.

And now the box was starting to smell.

But no, no. That wasn’t Buck. That couldn’t be Buck. His husband once spent months loyally waiting for a woman that had ghosted him. He had spent months haunting Abby’s apartment because he had promised to be there when she came back.

Buck wouldn’t…

(“He had everything that he thought he wanted, and he still wasn’t happy.”)

Buck had not been spending much time at home recently. At least once a week, squeezed in between their shifts at work, he was heading out somewhere. Alone. Without Eddie. And Eddie wasn’t possessive- Okay, he was, he very much was, but he knew that Buck could exist independently from him. They could exist independently from each other. They just usually didn’t. Where one went, so did the other, with the exception of Buck’s wine nights with his sister that Eddie spent with Abuela.

But now, recently, it seemed Buck was always finding a reason not to be with Eddie. The evenings with Maddie, going to help May with something, and recently checking on Hen and Karen. The PTO meetings at the school.

The PTO meetings at the school…

A memory came to him of a conversation from the beginning of October. Eddie hadn’t dwelled on it, because it was Lyndsay-with-a-Y and he didn’t like dealing with her. But she had asked if Buck was “actually” going to show up for that month’s PTO meeting, as if he hadn’t shown up at the last one. Which had been weird at the time, because Eddie knew that his husband had gone to one in September.

Buck also said that he’d gone to two meetings in October, when Lyndsay-with-a-Y seemed rather insistent that there had only been the one.

No, no. He would not take that woman’s word as gospel. He couldn’t.

Eddie would get to the bottom of this and find out what was really going on.

(“Aren’t you happy?”)

Lyndsay hadn’t been lying.

The PTO for Durand had its schedule up on the school website for everyone to see, if they only bothered to look. And there was nothing for the last Friday of October. Which didn’t have to mean anything, right? Maybe he’d just misheard what Buck told him, like he’d thought originally.

Eddie checked the date for September’s sole meeting and felt his stomach drop.

They’d been working that day, he remembered. They’d be in the middle of a twenty-four when the meeting was scheduled. But Buck had told him, a week later, that he was going to one. Eddie hadn’t even questioned it, he’d stuck his fingers in his ears and hid behind Abuela before his husband could invite him to come along.

Buck hadn’t invited him.

Lyndsay hadn’t been lying.

(“Half the thrill sometimes is doing something, and thinking you’re getting away with it.”)

Proof. Eddie needed proof. This, all of this. It meant nothing. Not really, not truly. Eddie needed proof.

He snuck a look at Buck’s phone the next time his husband was in the bathroom. They knew each other’s passwords, so it was a simple matter to get in and read his texts. And, normally? Normally he’d feel like utter shit for invading Buck’s privacy like this, but he needed to know.

There, fourth from the top, below the group text with the whole 118, below the text chain they had with TK and Carlos, below the one with just Eddie that almost entirely consisted of stupid puns and reminders and random facts and grocery lists. There was the mysterious Myra.

Raincheck Tues 🌧️ 🌧️ 🙁

Thurs ok? 💃🕺

Wrk Thurs 

🚒 💔

Fri?

Fri works 👍

Its a date 

🕺 🤠

🙄

On the afternoon of the first Friday of November, Eddie watched from the couch as his husband pulled on his shoes. The queasy feeling in his stomach, the one that never quite went away after Halloween, got so much worse.

“Going to Maddie’s, right?” Eddie asked, going to great lengths to keep his voice calm.

“Uh, right, yeah,” Buck lied, stumbling a little as he tugged his right shoe on.

“Tell her I said hi.

“I will.” His husband pulled the door open, smiled at him. “Love you.”

“Love you, too.”

His husband had just lied to him. And he was such a bad liar. How hadn’t Eddie caught on to anything before now?

Because he hadn’t been looking, was the answer. Because he trusted his husband.

From the front window, he watched Buck pull out of the drive and drive away, phone clutched in his hand. There was a simple way to test things, an easy way.

He pulled up his contacts, his thumb hovered Maddie’s name.

He thumbed the screen off, and slipped his phone into his pocket.

Eddie needed to know.

Eddie did not want to know.

Ignorance had been blissful, and he wished terribly that he could go back to that point in his life. He wished he could go back to Halloween and tell himself to leave Buck’s phone on the table. But he couldn’t.

Eddie could carry a grown man out of a burning building, could clamp an artery and keep a woman from bleeding out, but he could not turn back time.

What he needed was a second opinion. Yeah, that was it. Someone who could tell him that he was simply overreacting. But his social circle was not the biggest, and he didn’t know who he would feel comfortable talking to about this.

Athena, maybe. He knew through the family gossip that Michael had started dating other people before he came out to her, before they divorced. But he didn’t feel comfortable going to Buck’s mother and telling her that he thought her stepson was having an affair. She’d either laugh in his face or shoot him.

Not Athena, then.

That really only left one option, didn’t it?

Quickly putting on his own shoes and telling Abuela where he was headed, he got in his truck and drove. The trip took twenty-minutes, and it was twenty-minutes he spent second- and third-guessing everything. When he pulled up to the curb, it took him another five minutes to convince himself to get out of the truck and go to the door.

The moment he raised his fist to knock, it opened.

“We’re not taking the dog back,” was the way Hen greeted him.

“My abuela would kill us both,” Eddie assured her. “No, I, uh. I came to check on Karen?”

It came out as a question. It wasn’t supposed to be a question.

“Good timing, then,” Hen said. “You can sit with her while I go to the store for…” She turned her head to the side. “What am I getting?”

“Ice cream!” Karen shouted from just inside the living room.

“Ice cream,” Hen repeated.

“Then uh, I’ll sit with her then.” Perfect. That was perfect. Eddie hadn’t considered Hen when he first thought to talk to Karen. Her being away was perfect.

Inside, Karen was on the couch, propped up by a mountain of pillows. She was looking much better than she had the last time he’d laid eyes on her. She smiled at him as he awkwardly stood by the door and listened to Hen drive away.

“She’s been hovering and it is driving me insane,” Karen told him.

“So you sent her to get ice cream?”

“Two birds with one stone,” she said, looking pleased with herself. “How have you been?”

“How did you know?” Eddie blurted out, wrapping his arms around himself. “About Hen and Eva? How did you find out?”

“She sued us for custody,” Karen answered, frowning at him. “Eddie, what’s going on?”

“Buck’s been lying about where he’s been,” Eddie explained, eyes fixed on the wall above her head. “He’s been lying to me. He’s been telling me he was going to PTO meetings for Chris’ school on days when there weren’t any meetings.”

“Alright.” She nodded encouragingly. “That doesn’t mean anything, though. There’s no need to-”

“He’s on a date right now.”

The silence was heavy.

“How do you know?” Karen finally asked, wincing as she sat up.

“There’s someone named Myra, on his phone.” God, he hadn’t thought to take a picture of Buck’s screen. “She- They were supposed to meet up on Tuesday, apparently. But she had to cancel on him.”

“So he was supposed to meet with this Myra on Tuesday. How do you know he’s seeing her now? Did you see more texts?”

“They rescheduled for today. He called it a date, Karen.”

“Where did he tell you he was going?” she asked, and he felt something in him break at how she didn’t even try to offer alternatives.

“To see Maddie.”

“You know the best way to find out for sure, then,” she told him, and he did. He’d known before he even came here.

Eddie pulled out his phone again, hand trembling as he thumbed open his contacts again. He couldn’t do this. He couldn’t-

“Please,” he begged, holding his phone out to Karen. She didn’t question him, didn’t push. She simply took it and pressed Maddie’s name, putting it on speaker as the call went through.

“Hey Eddie,” his sister-in-law chirped. “What’s up?”

“Uh- N- Nothing, just uh…” He glanced at Karen, who nodded again. “Is Buck there?”

He held his breath.

“No.” The word was another bullet tearing through his chest. “Why? Is something wrong?”

Yes, he thought. But he didn’t say that. Not that he could remember what it was that he did say. He knew that he said something, platitudes and assurances as he hurried to get off the phone with her.

He covered his face with his hands.

“I’m so sorry, Eddie.” 

The pity in Karen’s voice nearly destroyed him.

“So why couldn’t we meet on Tuesday?” Buck asked, glancing down every other step to keep track of his feet. Myra was wearing the boots again and was running him through a review of the dances she’d taught him so far. Both leading and following. 

“Because my boyfriend’s mother decided to surprise us with a visit,” she said, frowning when he stumbled slightly and narrowly avoided treading on her feet. “It was perfect timing, too. The monster-in-law showing up at our door on Halloween, bags packed to stay for multiple days.”

“Monster-in-law?”

“Oh, she is awful. The entire time she was in town, she was all up in our business,” Myra huffed, bringing them both to a stop. “I caught her going through our things on Tuesday. And she insists on calling Gabe by the wrong name. Like, Regina, you gave birth to a son. Accept it and move on, the IRS certainly has.”

“So she’s…”

“A total bitch, yeah,” Myra finished for him. “How about your in-laws?”

“I’m getting better, right?” he asked, changing the subject. “I mean, I didn’t step on your foot once that time.”

“You’re getting better,” she said, letting the change in topics go with a smirk. “And an oval is pointier than a circle.”

“Y’know a circle is just a type of oval? Like how a square is just a special rectangle.”

“I didn’t know that,” she said, stepping back and tapping her lips with a finger. “You are getting better, jokes aside. At this rate, you might be able to surprise your man with a proper dance at his funeral.”

“Wow,” was all Buck could think to say.

“The Danse Macabre was all the rage, back in the day. Not an actual dance, before you ask.”

“I wasn’t going to.” He had totally been about to.

“You just have to be a bit more… Confident, with your steps. More aware of where your feet are, and your partner’s,” she told him. “And stop looking at your feet.”

“But then how am I supposed to know where my feet are?”

“How do you keep from tripping and dying on your way to the bathroom?” Myra asked, in almost the exact same kind of tone Athena would use. It was uncanny.

“Practice?” was the answer he gave, getting an eye roll in return.

“I have an idea,” she said, heading for the front room and the desk. He heard her pull open a drawer and rummage through it, and then she was back. She was holding a sleep mask, bright pink with cartoon eyes on the front. “We’re gonna break your habit of watching your feet.”

“I’m not sure about this, Myra.”

“It’ll be fine,” she told him, pulling on his shirt until he bent down enough for her to slip the mask on him. The strap dug a little at the back of his head, and the only light he saw was on the edges of his vision. “But if you feel uncomfortable, just safe word out.”

“Daddy,” Buck said, and there was a confused silence that followed the word.

“What?”

“You said to safe word out if I felt uncomfortable,” he explained. “And that’s…”

“Your safe word is ‘daddy’?” He wasn’t sure if Myra was appalled or about to laugh.

“It kills the mood.”

Once, very early on in their relationship, Buck had called Eddie “daddy” in bed as a joke. He’d never seen an erection wilt so quickly before, and still felt awful for how much he had laughed over it.

He wasn’t going to tell any of that to Myra, though.

“Right, well. If you feel uncomfortable, just say- Christ, y’know what? You made it weird. Just say you’re uncomfortable and want to stop.”

“I made it weird?” He reached up and pulled the mask off his eyes, so he could see her again. “You brought out a blindfold. Why do you even have this here?”

“It’s a sleep mask, not a blindfold. And… I don’t actually remember.” Myra kicked at him and he hopped a little to avoid getting a steel toe to the shin. “C’mon, put it back on. Then hold out your arms. I’ll lead first.”

“If I trip and break my neck…” Buck grumbled, slipping the mask back over his eyes and doing as he was bid.

“If you trip and break your neck, I promise to bury you somewhere nice,” Myra said, moving his limbs to where she wanted them. “Actually, no, I don’t. I’d never be able to carry you.”

“Thanks.”

“You’re welcome. Now: one, two, thr- Shit, this was a bad idea!”

Eddie was no stranger to the sensation of the world collapsing beneath his feet. He had felt it many, many times before. Every time he thought he stood on stable ground, every time he thought he could rest easy and breathe, that complacent illusion was torn away from him. Maybe not right away, but eventually.

Every time.

When he got home from Hen and Karen’s, he did his best to act normal with Chris and Abuela. He did well enough, he thought, even though he caught Abuela frowning at him. But he couldn’t focus on that. Not when his entire being was stuck on the fact that he was about to suffer through the end of yet another marriage.

He didn’t bother to wait for Buck to get home - from his date - to have dinner. When Chris brought it up, he simply said that Buck was eating with Maddie. That had the boy frowning, too, but what was Eddie supposed to say? The truth?

No.

They ate silently, and it seemed despite his best to act natural, everyone in the house felt something was wrong. Abuela kept shooting him looks, and Chris was not nearly as chatty as he usually was. Even Hoover abandoned his post by Abuela’s feet to rest his head on Eddie’s thigh.

Afterwards, when dinner was done and both Abuela and Chris were in their rooms - one watching her shows, the other playing games - Eddie paced the living room. Paced and paced, and tried to keep from seeing the way his life had melded with Buck’s. Tried to keep from imaging the gaping holes that were going to be left, once everything was ripped and torn away.

He was about to start another circuit around the room when Buck finally got home. The man stumbled through the room as he always did, loud and boisterous, filling up the space with his entire personality.

“Hey, babe,” he said, toeing off his shoes and putting them by the door. “Did you guys already have dinner? I didn’t think I was gone that-”

“I talked to Maddie,” Eddie cut him off. The old rage, once his constant companion, was rushing back to the fore. A fire that filled his entire being.

“Y- You did?” He looked surprised, and then worried. “Oh, uh-”

“Who is it?” Eddie spat the question out, glaring at Buck from his spot by the fireplace and the altar with the ofrendas that none of them had the heart to dismantle just yet. He was falling back on old habits, toxic habits. He was falling back on the old safety of anger to cover up the hurt.

He knew he was doing it, but the alternative was to break, and he could not do that. Not now. Not when he already had to figure out how to clean the mess of yet another soon-to-be-broken marriage. Not when he’d have to help keep Chris together.

“Who’s what?” his husband asked, and had the audacity to try and look confused, which just pissed Eddie off even more.

“The person you’ve been seeing,” he snarled, doing his best to keep from just screaming the words. He should not have been doing this tonight, with Chris in his room and Abuela watching her shows in hers. But Eddie was just so… Hurt.  “The person you’ve been fucking behind my back.”

And Buck just stared at him.

In utter silence.

“Well?” Eddie snapped once the silence had gone on long enough. “Are you going to tell me?”

“Eds, babe, I don’t-”

“Don’t bullshit me. I saw the texts from Myra, whoever the hell she is.” A thought struck him, and he let out a bark of humorless laughter. “At least I don’t have to wonder what she has that I don’t.”

“Eddie, no, that isn’t-” Buck looked shattered. That was the only appropriate way to describe his face. As shattered as Eddie’s heart felt. “That isn’t- It’s not like that.”

“Then what is it like? Huh? You’re lying to me about going to PTO meetings and seeing your sister, you’re planning dates with some stranger-”

“No, that-”

“So if you aren’t fucking around behind my back, what are you doing?”

“Dancing lessons.”

The answer was nearly as quiet as a whisper, but it was a thunderclap to Eddie’s ears. It hung in the air between them like the copal incense Abuela burned for Dia de los Muertos . It was an answer that made no sense, however, because,

“You can’t dance.”

“I know.” Buck wasn’t crying. He looked like he wanted to, but he wasn’t. Like he was making a physical effort to keep everything at bay. Eddie thought that was somehow worse. “That’s why the- You like to dance. And I can’t dance. And I knew it had to bother you, so… So I- I was going to surprise you with…”

The words trailed off with a little shrug.

With dancing. Buck was going to surprise him with dancing. Because Eddie liked to dance, and Buck thought Eddie was bothered that his husband had two left feet. Buck stepped out of his comfort zone, simply to do something nice for Eddie.

And Eddie had just accused him of having an affair.

Oh. Oh, God. Oh no.

“You actually thought that I…?” Buck didn’t finish the question. As Eddie watched, unable to say a word or make a move, the other man glanced at the door behind him. For one heartstopping moment, he was certain that the other man was about to leave. That he had driven the man into leaving him.

But then he turned and headed deeper into the house.

“Buck!” he called after him, hurrying across the room, reaching the dining room just in time to hear the back door open and close.

Abuela was peering out of the hallway, frowning at him.

“Eddito? What is going on?”

“I messed up, Abuela.” Eddie gasped the words out in a sob, clinging to the doorframe. The burning rage was gone, and all that was left was cold, cold shame. “I- I messed up…”

Chapter 17: Something Soft, Sad, and Delicate (Or Loud and Out of Key)

Chapter Summary

In which two idiots realize they've messed up.

Chapter Notes

*slaps a Band-Aid on it*

Buck had fucked up.

Curled up in the patio chair, facing away from the back door and listening to the sounds of the city around him, that was all Buck could think about. He had fucked up. Horribly, terribly. Perhaps even unforgivably. 

He had not expected to be ambushed when he got home. He had thought himself so clever, with his little web of lies. He had felt so proud of himself for getting away with the whole thing, with the reward being Eddie’s surprised and happy face come Christmas.

That wasn’t what Buck had gotten, though. Instead, he’d come home to a hurt and angry Eddie, demanding to know if- No, not if. There’d been no “if.” Eddie had been demanding to know who Buck had been cheating on him with. He had been demanding to know who Myra was.

It stung a bit, to realize that Eddie had felt the need to hopscotch over Buck’s privacy. But, then again? Buck had clearly demolished whatever trust the other man had in him, so he couldn’t really blame him. What right did he have to be upset over the consequences of his own actions?

Buck had fucked up. He had fucked up so thoroughly, so monumentally, that Eddie truly believed he was cheating on him. And looking back on it, Buck couldn’t even blame him. After all, he’d been lying to Eddie about where he was going and what he was doing so that he could see a woman behind his back. 

It hurt, it hurt so fucking much, that Eddie hadn’t trusted him. That Eddie thought him capable of such betrayal. But that was obviously Buck’s fault. He obviously hadn’t given him enough cause for trust in their relationship, in their marriage.

(Everyone else had believed him capable of it, during the catfishing mess.)

Buck remembered the way Eddie used to literally mark his territory, leaving hickeys all over his neck and shoulders, back when he was first rekindling the friendship with Taylor. Enough that he went through three tubes of concealer at work in as many weeks. The reporter had mocked him for it and called him jealous, and Buck had never taken Eddie’s concerns seriously. So this was clearly a years-long fuck up of Buck’s own making.

The sound of the front door slamming closed drew him out of his misery-ridden thoughts, and then he heard Eddie’s truck start up before driving away. And oh, oh, that was so much worse. Buck had just driven him away, too, hadn’t he?

Buck had almost left. There’d been such a temptation to run out that door. He’d wanted to run away from his mistakes and run into Maddie’s arms so she could make it better again. Except with Maddie came Chimney, and the moment the paramedic heard what was going on, it would become workplace gossip in a heartbeat. Eddie, he knew, would not want their marital problems to become gossip.

And then he thought about going to Bobby and Athena’s, except he knew the captain had no hope of remaining objective about this. All he would see was Buck hurt, and then he’d inevitably take his anger out on Eddie. Eddie did not deserve to be punished for Buck’s mistake.

Buck couldn’t go to Maddie’s, and he couldn’t go to Bobby and Athena.

So he hadn’t left.

His phone rang and he pulled it out of his pocket to answer on autopilot, not bothering to glance at the screen or even register whose ringtone it was.

“Man, I know you hated the game when we played,” TK said when he picked up, “but I just had the best round of Catan ever. I demolished everyone.”

In the background, Carlos said something that had his friend laughing.

“Teek…”

“Buck?” TK sounded worried. “What’s wrong?”

“I-... Teek, I-...”

“C’mon sunshine, finish a sentence. Is Eddie okay? Chris? Your sister?”

“What’s going on?” he heard Carlos ask, only to be shushed by TK.

“I- I fucked up,” Buck finally got out. Behind him, he heard the back door open. He turned to face whoever it was and found Abuela peering out at him. Concern was etched across her features.

“Whatever it is, I’m sure it can’t be as bad as-”

“He thinks I’ve been cheating on him.”

Abuela’s eyes went wide and she glanced back inside, towards the front of the house, before turning back to him. She glanced at the phone he was holding to his ear before nodding and letting the door close, leaving him alone again.

“Who the fuck?” His friend sounded angry. For him or at him, Buck wasn’t sure. “Who thinks you’ve been cheating?”

“Eddie. Eddie thinks I’ve been…”

“What? Why?”

“I fucked up. I- I came home, and he- And now he’s…” Oh, there’s the crying. Buck had been doing such a good job of keeping himself from tipping over that edge. “He left, Teek…”

“Okay, okay. Shit, okay.” There was the sound of something slapping against flesh and TK hissed something to Carlos that Buck couldn’t quite make out. “Okay, now just- Let’s not jump to the worst case, here. Tell me what happened.”

“I’ve been taking dance lessons,” he tried his best to explain. “Because-”

“Because you can’t dance,” TK said. “How did that turn into… This?”

“It was supposed to be a surprise,” Buck said. “I was going to surprise him on our anniversary.”

“So he found out about the lessons and thought you were cheating?” His friend sounded vaguely confused.

“He didn’t find out about the lessons. He- He saw a text on my phone. She had to reschedule and I called it a date.” Buck had just been joking. It hadn’t been a funny joke, wasn’t meant to be a funny joke. It definitely wasn’t a funny joke now. “He found out I’d been lying about where I was going, and…”

“And he thought you were stepping out on him.”

“Yeah.”

“Shit, Buck.” The words were sighed through the phone.

“I know, okay? I know that I fucked up-”

“No, no, I didn’t mean-”

“I know that, and- And now I’ve probably ruined everything and-”

“Buck!” TK shouted over him. “Take a breath, okay? Take a breath. You haven’t ruined anything. This is just an awful misunderstanding. You told him about the dance lessons, right?”

“Y- Yeah, but I don’t know if-” He had no clue if Eddie believed him. His husband had just stood there and stared at him, after all. “He didn’t say anything.”

“And then what?”

“And then I walked outside, and he le- He left, Teek.”

“He didn’t just leave, Buck.”

“You don’t-”

The back door opening again cut him off, and he realized with a sense of hysterical relief that he was even more of an idiot than he thought. Because Chris was there, and Eddie wouldn’t just leave without him. Which meant that he was coming back.

And then he’d probably tell Buck to leave.

“Pops?” Chris was frowning at him. Shit, Buck’s face must be an absolute mess from the crying.

“Hey, kid,” he said, setting down his phone and wiping at his face. TK was saying something, but he couldn’t hear what. “What’s up? Everything okay?”

“Why are you crying?” the preteen asked instead, neatly sidestepping his attempt at deflection. 

“Uh, just uh-” Buck scrambled for an excuse. “Sad video on my phone. There was a, um. Baby cow.”

Chris kept frowning at him, clearly not believing his bullshit. He didn’t call him out on it, though. Instead, he carefully made his way over to him. Once upon a time, he would have climbed into Buck’s lap. Now, he just stood beside the chair Buck was sitting in.

“Abuela’s annoyed about something,” Chris said.

Probably me, Buck thought.

“Oh yeah?” he said instead. “Any idea what for?”

“Dunno. She’s on the phone with Tia Pepa, I think.”

Ah, great. Then they were undoubtedly planning the best way to carry through on their threats from years ago. Buck wouldn’t even try to stop them, because he very clearly brought it upon himself.

“Shouldn’t you be getting ready for bed?” he said, poking at the kid and attempting a smile.

“Tomorrow’s Saturday,” Chris groaned, annoyed enough to roll his eyes. “I don’t have school tomorrow.”

“You have horses in the morning, though,” Buck pointed out. “You don’t want to be so tired that you fall off, do you?”

What was no doubt going to be a scathing rebuke was dropped as they both turned at the sound of Eddie’s truck pulling into the driveway. It couldn’t be seen from the backyard, so they could only listen as the engine cut off, as the driver side door opened and slammed shut, and as the front door opened a few moments later.

“Where did dad go?” Chris asked him, and Buck shrugged.

“He had to grab something real quick,” he said. Eddie’s footsteps were hurried as he rushed through the house, pausing to have a muffled conversation with Abuela that Buck couldn’t quite hear, and then more quick steps. “Go on, bud. It’s getting late.”

“Okay,” Chris sighed, rolling his eyes again. He hesitated for a moment before leaning forward and wrapping his arms around Buck’s shoulders. “Love you, pops.”

“Love you, too,” Buck whispered, reluctantly letting his kid draw back and make his way into the house.

And then Eddie stepped out to join him.

“I messed up, Abuela.” Eddie gasped the words out in a sob, clinging to the doorframe. The burning rage was gone, and all that was left was cold, cold shame. “I- I messed up…”

Eddie had fucked up, to be precise.

“What do you mean?” she asked, tilting her head in concern. “How did you mess up, nieto?”

“I thought- I accused-” Eddie shook his head, ran a hand through his hair and down his face. “I have to fix this. Dios, I hope I can fix this.”

“Whatever you did,” Abuela said, “he’ll forgive you. He’s still here. You can fix it, as long as you are both still here.”

The moment Buck had looked back at the door haunted Eddie. Buck had almost left right then and there, Eddie knew, and it would have been entirely his fault. But Buck hadn’t. He was still there, at their home, and Eddie would just have to cling to that life preserver. 

But where did he even start to fix this?

“After your abuelo and I had our arguments,” Abuela said, apparently seeing the dilemma playing across his face, “he used to get me flowers.”

Flowers! Yes, brilliant! That was a start. It wasn’t enough, it was in no way enough, but it was a start.

“Gracias,” Eddie said, pecking her on the chick. “Gracias, gracias.” 

He ran for the door, scooping up his keys from the little bowl on the table and his wallet right next to it. Two steps out, he had to backtrack for his shoes, hopping down the porch steps as he yanked them on. The door slammed shut behind him under its own weight, and then he was jumping into his truck and pulling out into the road, passing the jeep parked in front of the curb.

Flowers, flowers. Where was the closest place to get flowers?

There was a Ralphs on La Brea, not five minutes away, that was open late. He could get there and find the biggest bouquet of flowers that he could, along with… Chocolate? All the chocolate? The entire candy aisle? That was a good start for a “please forgive me for accusing you of cheating on me when you were just trying to be your usual sweet self” apology gift, right?

It was a good plan, it was a wonderful plan. 

Eddie practically flew down the roads, cursing when he managed to catch two different lights. When he got to the Ralphs, he flung the truck into a space as close to the doors as he could manage and parked it haphazardly, before sprinting into the store. Grabbing a cart was an action made as an afterthought as he zeroed in on the large display of flowers, just near the door.

He grabbed the largest bouquet they had available - one bursting with pink roses and what he thought might have been lilies - and was marching for the candy aisle, when his phone rang.

“What?” he barked the moment he dug it out and answered.

“And a howdy to you, too, Eddie,” was Carlos’ dry response.

“Not a good moment, man,” Eddie told him, pausing in front of the shelves. He grabbed a bag of Hershey’s Kisses and flung it into the cart, and then eyed the giant bag of Skittles sitting not far away.

Screw it, that went into the cart as well.

“Oh, I know how bad of a moment this is.”

The comment had Eddie pausing in his pilfering of candies.

“He called TK,” he guessed.

“TK called him,” Carlos corrected. “Someone accused him of cheating on you?”

“I did,” Eddie confessed, closing his eyes and leaning heavily against the cart.

“What the hell, man!?”

“I fucked up,” he told him. “I- I- I just fucked up. Just… How upset is he?”

There was a moment of silence, during which he could just about make out TK’s voice in the background.

“TK’s talking to him,” Carlos finally said. Which, despite not being an actual answer, managed to answer the question quite well.

Eddie blindly grabbed another bag of candy off the shelf and flung it into the basket.

“I have flowers,” he said. “And candy. Lots of candy.”

Maybe he should get more candy? Or more flowers?

“That’s a start,” the Texan told him.

“Yeah, I know it’s a start. Help me out here, hermano.”

“You could get him a puppy?”

“We already have a dog,” Eddie told him. Pictures of Hoover had inevitably made their way to Texas, so it wasn’t news. The suggestion had merit, though.

He opened his eyes and headed for the toy aisle, hoping they’d have what he was looking for. This would be a much easier task if it was February and not November, but maybe they were close enough to Christmas to broaden the choices. Buck liked soft things. Surely there was a stuffed dog somewhere in this store.

The sound of TK shouting Buck’s name had him freezing in his steps.

“What was that about?” Eddie asked, worried.

“Uh.” Carlos sounded unsure. “TK’s handling it… Oh, he’s upset about you leaving.”

“TK?”

“Buck.”

Eddie turned the cart around and sped for the check-out lanes.

“I’m checking out now,” he said, not caring about the price as he rang everything up. He fumbled getting his card out to pay, and almost didn’t ring one of the bags of candy up at all.

On the other end of the line, TK and Carlos were having a hushed conversation that Eddie could not hear, and which worried him all the more for it. He hurried out the store doors and to the truck.

“What happened, Eddie?” Carlos asked him. “I got bits of it from TK’s side of things.”

“I fucked up,” he said again, and the other man let out an impatient noise that was almost lost as he put the phone on speaker and stuck it to the dash.

“Yeah, that I know. But you haven’t said how.”

“He was lying about where he was,” Eddie explained, starting the engine and pulling out of the parking lot. “He was lying to me about where he was, about what he was doing. And there were texts between him and someone named Myra, and…”

“And you thought he was cheating on you.”

“I was an idiot,” he said, glaring at the street light ahead of him. It behaved and stayed green until he was past it. “I fucked up.”

“Anyone would have thought the same thing.”

“He was taking dancing lessons, Carlos.” The second light knew what was good for it, too, and he was soon turning onto his street. “To surprise me.”

“I’m just saying, it sounds-”

“It sounds like I fucked up,” Eddie snapped, cutting him off as he pulled into his drive way. “I’m home.”

“Okay.” The word was accompanied with a sigh. “Good luck.”

“Thanks,” he replied drily, before hanging up and snagging his phone off the dash. He grabbed the candy and the flowers and hurried into the house, practically running through the house and depositing everything on the dining room table. Abuela, in the middle of an agitated conversation on the house phone, frowned at him from the kitchen.

Looking at the candies and flowers, Eddie just couldn’t help but think that it wasn’t enough. There had to be something else that he could-

“Eddito,” Abuela said, putting down the phone and rounding the breakfast bar. Her voice was hushed, like she didn’t want someone - Buck or Chris - to overhear. “You are making it better, si?”

“I am,” he told her, voice equally hushed. “I messed up, and I want to make it right, but this… It doesn’t feel like it’s enough.”

“I’m sure that it is,” the woman tried assuring him. “You know he will forgive you.”

“This doesn’t feel like enough,” he insisted, tugging at his hair. There had to be something else, something more he could-

The idea struck Eddie like lightning and he hurried down the hall, making for the closet in the empty room. He flung it open, diving for the box in the back with the scrapbook. It wasn’t done, it wasn’t anywhere near being done, and loose photographs fluttered back into the box as he pulled the book out, but it was something more.

It still wasn’t enough, it felt like, but it was something.

Eddie heard the back door open and close, and he ran back into the dining room to find Chris poking at the bags of candy curiously. 

“Those are for your pops,” he told him, reaching over his head to pick up the bouquet. His son turned and blinked up at him from behind his glasses, frowning.

“Pops was crying,” Chris stated plainly, and it hurt all the more for it. “Are you fixing it?”

“Trying to,” Eddie told him, and the pre-teen nodded.

“Good,” he said, and headed down the hall for his room.

Taking a breath, and gripping tight to both the unfinished scrapbook and the flowers, Eddie stepped out the back.

“I’m sorry.”

It took a moment after Buck blurted out the words for him to realize that Eddie had said the exact same thing at the exact same time. It also took him that long to realize that his husband was holding a bouquet of pink roses and lilies in one hand, and a book of some sort in the other.

“I don’t-” Buck shook his head, confused. “What are you sorry for?”

“For fucking up,” Eddie said. “What are you sorry for?”

“For fucking up,” he echoed. “For lying to you. For letting you, even for a moment, think that I would ever… How did you fuck up? How is any of this your fault?”

“I didn’t trust you,” his husband said, the words stabbed at him.

“I mean, I guess I didn’t give you enough of a reason to trust me,” Buck said, shrugging and doing his best to not let the hurt show. “Or I gave you enough reasons not to trust me.”

“Buck.” Eddie’s face twisted in anguish as he stepped closer. “I trust you with our son. I trust you with my life in a fire. I should have- I should never have distrusted you, I should never have even thought for a moment that you would ever cheat on me. Not when I know you.”

“Then why?” he asked. “Why didn’t you trust me?”

Eddie didn’t say anything, not right away. He sat down in one of the other chairs and put the flowers and the book on the patio table. And then he pushed the book towards Buck. On the black cover, drawn in blocky white letters, it read, “The Point of it All.”

He recognized the words as they stared up at him.

(“I don’t want to be retired and going to a Happiness Convention and wondering what the point of it all was, you know?”)

Buck wanted to puke.

“I was afraid,” Eddie admitted. “I heard you and Hen talking that day. I heard Hen ask if you were happy, and instead of answering, you… You talked about how you and that man were both married with kids, and how he wasn’t happy until the end. How he had everything he wanted and he still wasn’t happy. Just like how you always say that you have everything you could want.

“I should have talked to you. I should have asked you,” he said. “But I didn’t. Because I was afraid of the answer.”

“Oh god, Eddie. Baby. I’m so sorry.” Oh, Buck had fucked up far worse than he initially thought. What could he say? How could he explain this clearly, and not make things worse? “I guess it just- It messed with me, alright? That someone could have my dream life, and still not be happy. That he could just wake up one day and realize that everything he thought made him happy just didn’t, anymore. That he wasn’t happy until he was literally dying for someone else.”

It messed with Buck, to see a glimpse into yet another could-be future, and once again dread what he saw.

“Are you?” Eddie asked, hesitant and quiet. “Happy with me?”

Buck practically lunged across the space between them, wrapping his arms around his husband. The arm of the chair - of both chairs - dug into him, but his discomfort did not matter.

“Of course I’m happy with you,” he said. “You always make me happy. When I- When I thought I made you leave… You and Chris, our family. You make me happy. I’m sorry I ever, ever made you think differently. I’m sorry for making you afraid that I wasn’t happy with you. Because I am. I am happy with you.”

“I’m sorry,” Eddie said. “For not trusting you. For not talking to you.”

“And I’m sorry for lying,” Buck told him. “For being so, I dunno. Messed up. You married a mess, Eds.”

“We’re both messes, mi sol,” his husband said. “There’s candy inside. And I got you flowers.”

He gestured needlessly at the bouquet on the table.

“I saw the flowers, they’re nice. Vibrant,” Buck said, sitting back. He tapped the book. “What’s this, though?”

“In that conversation with Hen-” If Eddie noticed the way Buck flinched at the mention of it, he didn’t say anything. “You mentioned how you didn’t want to wake up and realize your life was a blur. And the first anniversary is supposed to be the paper anniversary, so…”

Curious, Buck flipped open the cover to find a newspaper clipping on the first page, along with a photo of an exploded ambulance. The next page was another newspaper article, this one accompanied by a picture of the highrise hotel that had been tilted in the earthquake. After that were pictures of Chris and Eddie and him.

Pictures of them.

“You made me a scrapbook,” he said, feeling awed. “You made us a scrapbook.”

“It’s not done yet,” Eddie told him, and he glanced up to see him looking sheepish. “I’d only just gotten to the wedding.”

“You’re gonna make me cry again,” Buck warned, flipping through the pages.

“Been doing that a lot tonight?” Eddie asked, sounding guilty. “I’m sor-”

“No.” He closed the scrapbook and glared at him. “I’m sorry. This is my fault. This whole thing is my fault.”

“It is not. This-” His husband cut himself off, drawing in a breath and letting it out. “What if we just agreed that we both fucked up? And that we’ll both try to do better?”

Buck thought about it for a moment, and then stuck out his hand.

“Deal.”

Eddie rolled his eyes and took it. And then Buck found himself getting yanked to his feet. His husband messed around with his phone for a moment before it started to play music.

“You owe me a dance,” Eddie said, setting the phone on the table and dragging Buck off the patio. “I want to see how the lessons are going.”

“Not well,” Buck warned him. “I’m still stepping on her toes.”

“We’ll see.”

“No, seriously, she’s started wearing boots…”

(Somehow, miraculously, Buck only stepped on his husband’s feet once the entire night.)

Chapter 18: Hi, Felisa

Chapter Summary

In which they rescue a famous person...

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

They ended up staying up later than they meant to, that Friday night. After dancing outside for a while, during which Buck managed to only crush Eddie’s toes a grand total of once, they moved inside to make sure Chris was settled into bed. Buck found a vase for the roses and lilies, placing them in the middle of the dining table, while Eddie made him grilled cheese sandwiches. Eddie’s grilled cheese was the bomb, in Buck’s opinion, so he had no complaints there. And considering that he had missed dinner, he devoured them and then the ones Eddie made after that.

He certainly did complain when their alarms woke them up the next morning, far too soon and far too abruptly. 

“Chris can miss the one horse therapy, right?” Buck groaned, making his phone shut up. The urge to fling it across the room was strong, but he resisted. Beside him, Eddie silenced his own phone. “He won’t mind us sleeping in if he gets to.”

“Stanley knows I don’t work today,” his husband pointed out, and Buck pouted as he watched him sit up. “He’ll worry if I don’t show up.”

Stanley was the man who ran the veterans group that Eddie went to on Saturday mornings, work shifts allowing. Buck had never met him, but his husband liked him.

“That’s sweet,” he commented, getting a hum in agreement. “Y’know, we could stay in bed for a bit longer and make up for it with really quick showers.”

“Or,” Eddie countered, smirking at him as he reached down to run his finger along his birthmark. “We could get out of bed now for a long and thorough shower. Together.”

Buck, being the incredibly smart and intelligent man that he was, went with the second option.

One enjoyable shower later, Buck was in the kitchen making breakfast while Eddie roused their sleeping dragon. Excuse him, preteen son. Once upon a time, the boy had been quite happy to get up in the morning and face the day. Now he almost literally breathed fire, no matter how much he actually enjoyed riding the horses once he was awake.

Abuela was awake as well, frowning as she poked at the HILDY coffee maker. The one that Buck and Chris had pranked Eddie with what felt like forever ago.

“Nunca puedo… Ah!” The machine came to life, startling poor Hoover, who was crowding into her space. “Every morning. My old one was just fine, Buckito.”

“Your old one had two different cords spliced together,” Buck pointed out, sliding bacon and eggs onto their plates. Hoover, spotting an opportunity, abandoned Abuela to beg at his feet.

The dog did not get any bacon. He did, however, get a can of overly priced wet food for breakfast.

“It worked just fine,” Abuela insisted.

“It was a fire hazard waiting to burn down the house.”

She rolled her eyes and then frowned at him.

“You and Eddie, you’re both better now?” she asked him.

“Si,” he said, nodding. “There was just a, um. Misunderstanding.”

Abuela nodded as well, slowly, without taking her eyes off him. She was closer to the knives than he was. He took a step backwards and then another, putting more space between them.

“Breakfast ready?” Eddie asked, interrupting them. He popped up at Buck’s side to give him a kiss, making a happy noise when he spotted the loaded plates. He grabbed two of them to carry into the dining room, and Buck quickly followed with the others, finding it a perfect excuse to run away from Abuela.

After breakfast, they parted ways: Eddie for the library where the veterans group met, and Buck and Chris for the equine therapy. Along the way, Buck picked through one of the bags of apology candy that Eddie got him the night before, the Hershey’s Kisses. Hershey chocolate for the guy from Hershey, Pennsylvania. Buck wasn’t certain if Eddie had gotten it because it was the first bag of chocolate he saw, if it was the only chocolate available, or if it was meant to be some kind of joke.

“Can I have one?” Chris piped up from the backseat of the jeep.

“You want a Kiss?” Buck smirked at him in the rearview mirror. “I thought you were too cool for kisses from your fathers.”

“Pops,” the kid whined, rolling his eyes, and Buck laughed at him.

“Okay, okay.” At the next stop light, he held the bag out to him. “Here you go, bud.”

“Thank you.”

When they arrived at the equestrian center, Buck trailed along behind Chris as he hurried excitedly inside. Sierra, one of the people who worked there, met them and led him to the horses. Immediately, the pre-teen started chatting animatedly about his week and how he spent Halloween with his friends.

Buck lingered behind as he always did, content to give Chris the independence he craved. Instead, he looked down from above at all the riders below. He had a thought and followed through, taking a selfie of himself with the horses in the background that he sent to Eddie with a short, luv u . The other man didn’t take long to text him back with an equally short, but still sweet, Love you too.

And then TK was calling him, just as he was debating if it would be over the top to send Eddie his current GPS coordinates.

“So?” his friend started. “Is everything…?”

“We’re good, Teek,” Buck told him, smiling. And then he grimaced when he remembered that, “I kinda just. Dropped you. Didn’t I?”

“You did,” TK confirmed, not sounding very bothered about it. “It’s a good thing I could hear you talking to Chris, or I’d have been worried.”

“Sorry.”

“No worries. But now I can actually brag about my awesome night of Catan.”

“Why do you even like that game?” Buck asked him.

“Because it’s fun,” TK replied. “Now shut up and act like you care, sunshine…”

On Sunday, they had a twelve-hour shift to work. Eddie, much like Buck, hated having to work on Sundays. It wasn’t even necessarily because the calls on Sundays were any worse than the other days of the week, they weren’t, but because he looked forward to the dinners as much as his husband.

He and Buck didn’t talk about it, but it seemed they both decided to not say a word about Friday night’s Fuck Up to anyone else. Which Eddie was thankful about, because he did not want his mistakes to become station gossip. And he especially did not want Maddie or Bobby or Athena to find out that he accused Buck of cheating on him. He had no clue what his sister-in-law would do, the captain would make his life at the station a living hell, and the sergeant…

Four bullet wounds were enough, thanks. He didn’t need another.

Partway into the shift, just before lunch, they were sent to one of the fancier neighborhoods in the city. It was a call that had Buck asking for clarification about three times on the way there, because it sounded like a Looney Tunes gag. When they arrived at the large two-story home, the caller was waiting for them at the front door.

Her hands were red with blood.

“Thank God you’re here,” she cried, running to meet them as they approached.

“Dispatch reported somebody got hit by a chandelier?” Bobby asked, because it was a Looney Tunes situation that needed clarifying.

“The cables must have snapped or something,” she told them, leading them into the house. “I don’t know what happened. I tried moving it, but it was so heavy and there was… There was so much blood.”

Eddie and Buck shared a look and made sure their work gloves were snug.

“Is she conscious?” Chimney asked her. He and Albert were both carrying their medical bags. Eddie wasn’t entirely certain what was going to happen with one or both of them after this shift: their next one, Hen would be returning. Full time.

Whether that meant Chimney would be returning to B-shift, or someone else was going to be moved, Eddie did not know. And not that he would ever admit it to anyone, not even under pain of death, but he kinda didn’t want either of the brothers to be switched out.

(Not that it meant he was letting go of his grudge against the man. Chimney punched his husband and then acted like a total asshole for months afterwards, Eddie was allowed to hold that against him until the sun went dark. Especially since he knew Buck had already forgiven him for it.)

“Yes,” the caller confirmed for them. “She won’t shut up.”

Just inside the house, beside a staircase that spiraled around the room, was the victim. She was, indeed, pinned to the floor by a large, iron wrought chandelier. Literally pinned, with decorative spikes stabbing into the floor below. Eddie couldn’t tell if any of them were driven through her or just through her clothes.

“Alexis, you left me.” She was capable of complaining, so she clearly wasn’t too bad off.

“To bring help!” the caller, Alexis, shot back.

“Just please get this thing off of me.”

“Try not to move, okay?” Chimney sat his bag by her feet while Albert went to her head, where there was a bit of blood pooling. Buck started trying to lift the chandelier, but it apparently did not want to budge. “We’re gonna get you out of there.”

“Alright, let’s get a backboard and a gurney in here,” Bobby said, taking the situation in.

“Yeah, Cap, this thing’s penetrated the floor pretty deep,” Buck informed him, arm muscles straining with the effort of trying to lift it. Which was perhaps not what Eddie should be focusing on, but his husband’s biceps were bulging in his short-sleeved uniform top and he refused to feel ashamed about appreciating the sight.

“We’re gonna have to cut it apart, piece by piece,” the captain said. “Buck, Eddie, go get the saws and Nomex blankets.”

“Aye, aye, Cap,” Eddie said, the pair of them hurrying to do as ordered.

“Hey, didn’t we watch that with Abuela?” Buck asked him, pointing at one of the many movie and television posters on the walls. Each of them showed the same woman, the one currently pinned to her own floor. “She’s, uh. Fel- Felicia…?”

“Felisa Valdez.” According to the poster they just passed, at least.

“That’s it.”

It didn’t take long to get the saws and blanket, get geared up, and make it back. When they did, they found Chimney having a huddle with Bobby, standing a bit away from Felisa and talking with hushed voices. Albert was still kneeling by Felisa and was wrapping gauze around her head.

“Reflex of the left leg could be a potential spinal injury,” Chimney was saying.

“Is something wrong?” Alexis demanded, a bit hysterical sounding as she butted into their conversation. “She’s not paralyzed, right? That would severely impact her future revenue stream.”

Eddie shared another look with his husband. This woman had questionable priorities.

“Paralyzed?” Felisa yelped.

“I think the chandelier’s the most worrying thing, right now,” Albert said.

“Okay, Chim, contact the ER,” Bobby ordered. “Buck, Eddie, start sawing through it. Carefully.”

Albert gathered up his things and hurried to give them room to work. Buck stepped up to the woman’s head and shook out the Nomex blanket.

“Hi,” he said, smiling down at the actress. “This’ll keep you safe from the sparks, okay?”

“Uh.” Felisa blinked up at him. “Okay.”

Eddie watched his husband put the blanket over the woman’s face, who immediately fell silent. He was reminded, vaguely, of the way a parrot went quiet when its cage was covered.

The saws, when he and Buck started, did not cut through the chandelier like butter. They divided it into sections, clouds of sparks flying off the metal, before starting on the spikes that had been driven into the floor. Every piece that came free was carefully tossed out of the way, making a neat little pile near the bottom of the stairs. The spikes were left sticking out of the wood.

On the bright side, despite the one very close call to her left side, Felisa was pinned down rather than pinned through.

“How are we feeling?” Albert asked, kneeling back down by her head once the last of the metal was tossed aside.

“My left leg is tingling,” she answered, sounding unsure. Her expression brightened. “Wait, that’s a good thing, right? That means I can feel.”

“It’s very good,” Chimney answered. “It means it was probably a pinched nerve or maybe a spinal bruise.”

“Thank God,” Alexis said, overjoyed. “See? The bracelet’s working, just like I said it would.”

Eddie shared a confused look with Buck, before they both looked down at Felisa. She was indeed wearing a - rather tacky looking - bracelet. It was in the shape of a snake coiled around her wrist, with eyes of what he figured were supposed to be rubies or garnets, but which were probably just red glass.

“So you say this thing’s a good luck charm?” Eddie asked, once they got her into the ambulance. He was riding with them because, well. He was as curious as Buck sometimes. And this way he didn’t have to help with the clean up. Chimney was hooking her up with an IV and Albert was driving. “Why do you need it? Seems like you have a good life.”

“She’s had a run of really bad luck,” Alexis told him, using a wet wipe to wash her friend’s(?) blood of her hands. “For almost a decade.”

“It’s called a curse,” Felisa insisted.

“You spent twenty-thousand on a bracelet,” Eddie said, not putting much effort into hiding his skepticism. “Because of a curse?”

“She’s constantly looking for things to turn my luck around,” the actress said, and Eddie’s paranoia was rousing.

“Let me guess, nothing ever works?”

“Don’t mind Eddie,” Chimney interrupted. “He’s unnaturally resistant to anything supernatural.”

“Prove to me something is real,” Eddie insisted. “I’ll believe in it.”

“Don’t you thank your coffee maker to prevent the machine uprising?”

The paramedic’s smug look did not waver in the face of Eddie’s glare.

“There was something up about the two of them,” Eddie complained later, when he and Buck got home. They were sitting down for a late dinner, provided to them by Abuela, while Chris settled down for bed in his room. Abuela herself was in the backyard, letting Hoover do his business one final time.

“The two of who?” Buck asked, already digging in.

“Felisa Valdez and her friend.”

“Okay, so. What was it about them that has you all…” His husband trailed off, gesturing vaguely at him with his fork.

“The lucky bracelet,” Eddie answered. “The whole curse thing.”

“Oh, okay.” Buck started nodding, like things were suddenly making sense.

“Okay?” he repeated, indignant. “What do you mean, okay?”

“I mean that it’s this again. Jinxes and curses.”

“Two things that are definitely not real?”

“Uh-huh.” Buck did not look very impressed with him. “Okay, babe. I dare you to stand in the middle of the station tomorrow and say the Q-word. Loud as you can.”

“Bobby would kill me,” Eddie mumbled in protest, gaze fixed on his food.

“Right, that’s the thing that’s stopping you.”

Eddie, feeling every bit his age, flicked a pea at him. It struck Buck right between his eyes before falling down to land somewhere in his lap.

“Mature,” Buck said, rolling his eyes. “So Felisa believes in curses and Alexis found her a lucky charm. What about it?”

“Alexis convinced her to spend twenty-thousand on a lucky charm,” Eddie pointed out. “And she said she’s constantly looking for things to turn her luck around.”

“Eddie, babe.” The look Buck was giving him was filled with understanding. Too much understanding. “I’m sure whatever is going on, it isn’t what you’re thinking. I doubt what’s going on with Alexis and Felisa is anything like what happened with your abuela.”

What Buck was referring to was the utter puta that had preyed on Abuela when his abuelo passed. The one who had latched onto her when she was lost to her grief and took her for everything she had. Like a leech, or a malignant tumor.

“What about me?” Abuela herself asked, having come back inside just in time to hear the last bit. Hoover ran up and started bumping his head against Buck’s arm, either begging for scratches or scraps. He got the scratches, his tail thumping against the floor.

“We met an actress on a call today from that movie we saw with you,” Eddie told her. “Felisa Valdez.”

“Oh?” She looked excited about that. “And did you get an autograph?”

“Pops frowns on that,” Buck told her, looking apologetic. Abuela let out a scoff, flicking her fingers dismissively. “Eddito can ogle my biceps on a scene, but we can’t ask famous patients to sign our helmets.”

Eddie shrugged, refusing to feel embarrassed about being caught. Buck was his husband. He was allowed to ogle all he wanted.

“At least I can say my boys saved someone famous,” Abuela said. “Luisa from church keeps bragging about how she literally bumped into Kevin Tighe at the store. No matter how often we tell her that there’s no way he’d be shopping at a Bargain Mart in Crestview.”

“Kevin Tighe?” Buck asked, tilting his head like a confused puppy even as he kept up the scratches for Hoover. And for once, Eddie couldn’t even make fun of him for his lack of pop culture knowledge, because he didn’t recognize the name either.

“Thank you for dinner,” Eddie said, changing topics.

“De nada.” Abuela waved the thanks away. “You two work tomorrow, too, si?”

“Yup.” Buck bobbed his head. “And Hen’s supposed to be back.” He frowned at Eddie. “Do we know how that’s gonna work with Chimney and Albert?”

“Bobby would tell you before he told me,” Eddie pointed out. “I’m not the captain, so I don’t have to worry about it.”

Although, there was something about Hen coming back to work that was nagging at him. Like he was missing something…

“I am going to bed,” Abuela announced, distracting him from his mental puzzle. She mockingly wagged a finger at them. “Do not stay up too late.”

“‘Course not, ‘Buela,” Buck promised, grinning up at her. She playfully swiped at him before disappearing down the hall, Hoover trailing after her with his nails clacking against the floor.

“One of us is going to have to trim his nails,” Eddie remarked.

“One of us?”

“You’re right, it’s gonna be a two man job.”

“Uh huh.” His husband’s tone was as dry as the desert. “Okay, but seriously. Who the hell is Kevin Tighe?”

Eddie could only shrug.

Chapter End Notes

For the curious, Kevin Tighe is an actor from the show Emergency!, which walked so shows like 9-1-1 could run.

Chapter 19: Like a Virus

Chapter Summary

In which Hen returns, and so does the Tacky Cursed Bracelet.

Hen came back to work, full time, on Monday.

“Abrams opted to move to B-shift,” Bobby explained to Buck. They were working on the large welcome back breakfast, while everyone else trickled in. “The scheduling fits better with his wife’s hours. He’s been complaining about not seeing her as much.”

“And you’re okay with it?” Buck asked him and glanced over at the couches, where Chimney and Albert were watching the morning news. “Chim staying on A-shift?”

“You’re the one he punched,” the captain pointed out, quite needlessly. He sighed. “Yes, I’m fine with it. We all worked fine together before he lost his mind, we’ve been working fine since he switched back over.”

It was good enough for Buck, who let the matter drop as they continued cooking. They made enough to feed both the incoming A-shift and the outgoing C-shift, and they bullied Eddie into helping spread it out on the island. Everyone had just started sitting down to eat, Buck himself being one of the last ones to get a plate, when Athena showed up as well.

“Well, this is a spread,” she said, taking it all in. She was in uniform, probably about to start her patrol. “The two of you have gone all out.”

“Has Pops ever not gone all out with celebration food?” he asked, handing her a plate and silverware.

“True. Thank you, Buckaroo.”

Buck waited politely for her to load herself up, before piling his own plate with a lot of everything. He carried it over to the table and took the seat next to Eddie, who slid a mug of coffee over to him.

“Thanks, babe,” he said, pecking a kiss on his cheek.

“Hey!” Chimney mock glared, pointing at him with a fork. “I thought there was a rule about PDA at work, Cap?”

“Oh, bite me, Howard.”

“Your brother’s the one making out with people in the storage closet,” Eddie pointed out, grinning at the groan the younger Han let out.

“One time,” Albert said, grumpily stabbing a fork into his hash browns. “Once.”

“One time too many. I’m just glad I’m not the one who walked in on it.” Bobby smirked, and the amusement he showed when he looked over at Buck could not mean anything good. “It was bad enough that I had to catch that one-”

“Hey, hey, hey, hey,” Buck interrupted, scrambling for something to change the topic with. “Uh. Oh! Abuela said that the next time we save a celebrity, you should let us get a picture with them.”

“Why?” 

“She’s in some kind of competition with someone from church,” Eddie explained.

“Which celebrity did you save?” Athena asked.

“Felisa Valdez.”

“Oh, really?” The sergeant grimaced. “Mm.”

“You don’t sound like a fan,” Bobby mildly noted. “What’s the last thing you saw her in?”

“The back of my patrol car,” she answered, and chuckled at the way everyone perked up in interest. “Yeah, she tried to hit a paparazzi with her car and went to rehab. I can’t say I missed her.”

“Who says they didn’t miss me?”

Hen’s voice had everyone turning, greeting her loudly and cheerfully as she crested the top of the stairs.

“Not anyone here,” Chimney assured his friend. “How’s Karen?”
