“Great. I think she was more excited for me to get back to work than I was,” she said, chuckling as she walked around the table to stand behind the two paramedics, leaning over the both of them. “She complained that I hover. Athena, what are you doing here?”

“I wouldn’t miss your first day back,” the sergeant said. “And Bobby and Buck both went all out with your Welcome Back breakfast.”

“Well. Thank you.” Hen smiled at Bobby but, for some reason, that smile slipped from her face when she turned to look at Buck, pressed as he always was into his husband’s personal space. The frown was only there for a moment, gone fast enough that Buck wasn’t entirely certain that he wasn’t imagining things,

Weird.

“Who’s done with their first round?” Chimney asked. “I’m ready for my second, and it’s about to get ugly.”

“I could go for more,” Eddie said. He’d been one of the first to get their food, and so had the longest time to eat. He was about to get up until Buck pushed him back down.

“I’ll get it, babe,” he said, grabbing Eddie’s plate. His husband looked pointedly at his still half-full plate and raised his eyebrows at him. “Shut up and let me be nice.”

“Fine.” Eddie raised his hands in mock surrender. “Don’t let me stop you.”

This time, when Buck rose with Eddie’s plate and spotted the frown on Hen’s face, it lingered a bit longer. She went to get her own plate and Buck followed her, his curiosity pairing unpleasantly with his usual anxiety.

“So, uh, Karen’s doing well?” he asked her as he got his husband a bit more of everything. “Healing up nice?”

“Yup,” was her short answer as she picked through the breakfast pastries.

“And Denny? He’s handling everything okay?”

“He’s fine.”

“O- Okay. Just wanted to make sure that everything was fine,” he said, feeling very unsure about what was going on. “Everything is fine? Right?”

“I don’t know, Buck,” she snapped, looking like she was resisting the urge to slam her plate down onto the countertop. “ Is everything fine?”

“I- I’m confused,” he admitted, trying to think of what he’d done to pissed his friend off. He was already busy trying to make up for one major misstep, he did not like the prospect of handling another. “Did, uh. Did I do something?”

“Did you…?” Hen looked… Irritated? Angry? He wasn’t sure, but he definitely saw disappointment there. And that disappointment had his stomach churning with ice. “I cannot believe you.”

“Hen, what…?” He did have a single clue what she was going on about. The last time he’d been ambushed like this, Eddie was accusing him of cheating. And considering neither of them had spoken about that to anyone else aside from TK and Carlos, he knew this couldn’t be related to that. “What did I do?”

“What did you do,” she repeated, almost mockingly. “Yeah, you’re proof that real change is possible, alright.”

The pointed words sounded familiar, and he could swear that she’d said them to him before. But before he could continue to try and get her to just tell him what it was all about, the station’s alarm interrupted them. Hen shook her head at him, putting down her still empty plate to head for the bay, leaving him feeling so very lost.

Just what the hell had Buck done to piss his friend off? Worse, what had he done that made her disappointed in him?

The mystery of Hen’s attitude did not solve itself over the course of the shift. Buck barely had the opportunity to try and pry it out of her because, outside of calls, she gave him the cold shoulder. And any words she exchanged with him during calls were short and clipped, with a razor sharp undertone of anger.

Like she wanted to yell at him for something, but was doing her best to bite her tongue.

Buck didn’t feel like rocking the boat by confronting her about it. Hen hadn’t done anything to make working with her impossible or even difficult. She was just upset with him for reasons beyond his current understanding. And maybe if he pushed, she’d tell him what those reasons were, but he didn’t really feel like it.

Whatever was going on, it was her problem. Too much of Buck’s emotional processing power was tied up over Eddie to be spared for her anger and disappointment. Even if all their interactions had him feeling like he was in the middle of a Pennsylvanian winter. Instead, he continued to focus on doing nice things for his husband, in the hopes that doing enough of them would ease some of the guilt.

“You seriously need to stop,” Eddie complained, after Buck leapt to his feet to fetch him a bottle of water after he made a passing comment about being thirsty. They were in the bay, making sure the ladder truck was stocked, so he’d had to dash up to the loft and back. “It’s been three days of this.”

“You don’t like me doing nice things for you?”

“If that was all this was…”

“Did you ever think that maybe I’m doing it to make myself feel better?” Buck asked him, as the ambulance backed in through the bay doors and parked. Hen, Chimney, and Albert had been sent on a medical call to some spa place. His husband scowled at him, and then Buck was leaping up onto his toes with his butt cheek stinging. “Again, babe? Seriously? We literally talked this morning about this kind of thing at work…”

“We agreed that we both messed up,” Eddie reminded him, wagging a finger in his face. “So maybe let me do something nice for you next time.”

“I mean,” Buck said, grinning at him. “If you really wanted to-”

“Yeah, no,” his husband said, mercilessly cutting him off. “We literally just talked this morning about this, remember?”

“You are such a tease,” he complained without any heat.

Eddie rolled his eyes at him and made his way to the ambulance and the paramedics, Buck following him as always. The paramedics had been pulling the gurney out to disinfect it, but something clearly had Chimney spooked.

“I don’t think it has ears,” Hen was saying when they got there.

All three of them were fixated on something in the back of the ambulance. When Buck and Eddie peeked their heads around to see what it was, they spotted a very familiar looking tacky snake bracelet laying in the back.

“How did that get there?” Buck asked.

“Our spa call was another Felisa Valdez curse call,” Albert told them. “Her face was covered in wax. Like that guy from Game of Thrones, but without the gold.”

“Oh, wow,” Buck said, and then asked, “Like who?”

Albert looked very despairing.

“It must have fallen off on the way to the ER,” Chimney said. “What should we do?”

The look that Eddie fixed them with made it perfectly clear what his thoughts were regarding their behavior. 

“Give it back to her,” he suggested and started reaching for it. Hen and Chimney lunged to stop him, crying for him to wait. Albert was simply standing back, typing away at his phone.

Probably telling Ravi about what was going on.

“Here,” Hen said, pulling out a disposable glove and holding it out to him. “Just to be safe.”

“Right,” Chimney agreed. “First rule of a curse, they spread like a virus.”

“I don’t think that’s actually true, though?” Buck countered, scratching at his neck as he tried to remember. “Like, I looked up a lot about curses after Ravi jinxed us, and I don’t remember seeing anything about that.”

“You’re all ridiculous,” Eddie complained, tossing the glove onto the gurney. Barehanded, he reached in and plucked the bracelet up.

Almost immediately, there was the sound of someone hitting the floor behind them. They turned as a unit to see one of the other firefighters - a man named Darrell - flat on his back, groaning, having slipped in a puddle of something. Bobby immediately ran over from the other side of the station, kneeling down to check on him.

“I told you,” Chimney hissed at Eddie. “It’s spreading.”

“It is not.”

Buck left them to argue amongst themselves as he went over to check on Darrell. The smell hit him just as Bobby noticed the puddle, the captain swiping his finger through it and taking a careful sniff.

“Gasoline,” he declared, looking up at them. “Evacuate the firehouse. Now.”

“Everybody out!” Buck bellowed, ensuring the ones that weren’t within hearing range of Bobby would get the message.

“Definitely spreading!” Chimney and Hen said together, before they and Albert turned tail.

“There is no curse,” Eddie insisted, coming to help Darrell to his feet while everyone else dashed for the exits.

“That thing is not coming home with us,” Buck told him, pointing at the bracelet he was still holding.

His husband rolled his eyes.

“Argue about this outside, guys,” Bobby urged, shooing them towards the bay doors. “I need to call people to come in and clean up the spill. And find where it came from…”

The bracelet went home with them.

Eddie hadn’t meant to take it home. It simply ended up getting tossed into his work bag after the gasoline was cleaned up and then forgotten about. He found it again the next day, when he was cleaning the bag out. Buck had almost thrown a fit and probably would have called for an exorcist if Eddie allowed it.

On Tuesday evening, there was a PTO meeting. An actual PTO meeting. He knew this because his husband made a point of pulling up the schedule that was on the school’s website, the one Eddie had found, and showed it to him.

And then he had the audacity to ask if Eddie wanted him to stay home and skip it, instead.

Eddie didn’t let Buck stay home. Maybe it was overcompensating in an effort to show his trust, but he practically shoved the man out the door. Buck genuinely enjoyed feeling like he was involved with Chris’ school, after all, and Eddie did not like the idea that him being an insecure asshole could yank that away. 

It also gave Eddie ample opportunity to go vent to someone who might share his concerns regarding Felisa and Alexis. He knew Buck understood them, but he also knew Buck didn’t put much stock in them beyond his general faith in Eddie’s instincts. Because his husband was a good person who tried to see the good in (almost) everyone. And neither could he talk to Abuela about it, for reasons that were quite self-explanatory.

She’d probably break out the sage and then call a curandero over.

“Eddie.” Bobby looked surprised to have him show up unannounced at his door. He peered behind him, undoubtedly expecting Buck to be there. “Well, come on in. Did you come over for a visit or to talk about something?”

“Would you be insulted if I said I wasn’t here to talk to you?” he hedged, following his captain down the stairs.

“Nope. Athena?”

“In the kitchen!” the sergeant called, over the sound of mugs being set out. When Bobby and Eddie entered the kitchen, she was taking a kettle off the stove. “Do you want any tea?

“Sure.”

She filled the mugs with steaming water and dunked a tea bag into each of them. He took one when she handed it to him, and they both went to sit at the table.

“So where’s our boy at?” Athena asked him.

“At a PTO meeting for Chris’ school,” Eddie answered, feeling his phone vibrate in his pocket. He pulled it out to see that Buck had sent him a picture: a selfie of himself in front of the school, holding up that day’s newspaper so the date was visible.

He was in love with an idiot.

(He refused to dwell on the way that it did, in fact, make him feel better.)

“Is a PTO the same as a PTA?” Bobby was busying himself making his own tea over at the kitchen counter. “What does the O stand for?”

“Parent-Teacher Organization. Don’t ask me what the difference is between the two, that’s more Buck’s thing. I went to a grand total of one meeting and had to hide from a married woman trying to flirt with me. And he got pressured into being one of the chaperones at the school dance next week.”

“I doubt it took much pressure,” Athena commented, smirking. “So, what did you need?”

“I have concerns,” Eddie said, pulling the bracelet out of his other pocket and setting it on the table. “About Felisa Valdez and her friend-slash-assistant. Mostly about the assistant, Alexis, and the way she spends Felisa’s money.”

“You think she’s taking advantage of her?” Athena asked him, picking up the bracelet and examining it.

“Felisa said Alexis convinced her to buy it,” he told her, dumping a scoop of sugar into his tea from the container set out. “Supposed to be some artifact from Pompeii.”

“This?” The sergeant laughed. “My mother has costume jewelry, for actual costumes, that looks better.”

“Probably cost her less, too. That thing set her back twenty grand.”

“Twenty thousand dollars?” Bobby sounded appalled as he sat down next to his wife, who set the bracelet down on the table to pick up her tea. “For that?”

“Didn’t seem like it was the first thing Alexis convinced her to buy.”

“You sound pretty sure of that,” his captain noted while Athena shook her head.

“Abuela had a tough time of it when my abuelo died,” Eddie explained. “He passed suddenly, so she never had the chance to say goodbye. There was this woman, she claimed to be a curandera - a shaman,” he translated for their benefit, “who promised that she could talk to him again. For a price.”

“I assume it didn’t work,” Athena said, sounding quietly angry on his abuela’s behalf.

“Not that it stopped Abuela from hoping that this time, this time , it would work. No matter how much money it cost her. She nearly lost the house, but for my father and Tia Pepa stepping in to help.”

“And that’s why you don’t believe in curses,” Bobby guessed. Eddie was quick to shake his head.

“I don’t believe in curses because they’re not real,” he corrected. “Neither are jinxes or ghosts or anything of the sort. No, that-” Puta. “-woman is why I don’t trust people like Alexis.”

“The best grifters know how to play on people’s desperation,” Athena said. “The more desperate a person is, the easier it becomes to fool them.”

“And Felisa is apparently pretty desperate. She’s convinced that she’d been cursed with bad luck for the past ten years.”

“Oh, is that what we’re calling the consequences of our own actions, now?” The sergeant rolled her eyes with a laugh. “I’ll see about looking into things, Eddie.”

“It’s all I ask.”

“You know Buck believes in all those things, right?” Bobby asked him, smiling. “Ghosts and jinxes and all that. At least a little bit.”

“He nearly threw both me and this,” Eddie tapped the bracelet, “out the door today. So yeah, I know he does. But at least Abuela has someone to talk to. He wants her friend to do a Tarot reading for him.”

“Of course he does.”

Chapter 20: The Tragic Backstory

Chapter Summary

In which Eddie presses F to doubt, and Hen vents her frustrations.

“I don’t need her attorney,” Eddie told the man on the other end of the phone, pacing in front of the station’s kitchen island. “I just want to talk to Felisa privately.”

They’d had yet another Felisa Valdez call. This one was to a New Age shop, where Alexis had been impaled or stabbed in the stomach with a carved stone which, coincidentally, reacted to stomach acid. As in, releasing poisonous gas that knocked everyone out, reacted. It was over the top and it was ridiculous and Eddie had to sit next to an all-too-smug Buck the entire way back to the station.

Smug might have been a good look on his husband, but not at his expense, damn it.

To make matters worse? Eddie had been about to tell Felisa about his suspicions regarding Alexis, only to be interrupted by how genuinely concerned the actress was for her assistant. One mention of “she’s all I have” and Eddie found himself unable to say a damn thing. When did he get so soft?

He hadn’t even been able to give her the stupid bracelet back, because it wasn’t like he’d expected to run into her at a scene. It was currently sitting on the kitchen island while Buck cleaned around it. Actually, no, Eddie didn’t think Buck was cleaning anything. The man had been scrubbing at the same spot for nearly the entire time Eddie had been on the phone with the manager.

The bracelet was sitting on the island while Buck pretended to clean around it, but instead was, in truth, eavesdropping.

“Are you a fan?” the man asked, annoyed and impatient.

“No, I’m not a- I’m with the Los Angeles Fire Dep-” A tone interrupted him, and Eddie pulled the phone away to stare disbelievingly at the screen.

Bobby, who had actually been cleaning, stopped to look at him questioningly.

“Her manager’s a dead end,” Eddie told him.

“I thought you were calling her agent,” the captain said.

“I tried him first,” he explained, tucking his phone into his pocket. “He said he’d call back.”

“I dated an agent once,” Buck said. “They never call back.”

“Hey guys, what- Gah!” Chimney, who’d been coming to join them, froze the moment his eyes landed on the bracelet. He demanded, “What is that thing still doing here?”

“Okay, take it easy,” Bobby told him, sounding like he was trying not to laugh. “Eddie is trying to reach out to Felisa to give it back to her.”

“Oh yeah?” Chimney picked up a towel and dropped it over the bracelet, covering it up. “Try harder. I can feel those beady little eyes looking right through me.”

“Listen, I’m starting to think that maybe , Eddie has a point.” Buck reached over and tugged the towel off the bracelet, before picking it up. “This thing wasn’t anywhere near the crystal shop.”

“And nothing happened when we had it at home,” Eddie pointed out. The statement had Chimney going bug-eyed.

“You took that thing home with you!?” he squawked. “Hen!”

“What?” The other paramedic had been coming up the stairs when Chimney shouted for her, so she came over to where they were all standing. And then much like the other paramedic, she froze the moment she laid eyes on the bracelet. “What is that thing still doing here?”

“Eddie’s trying to get it back to Felisa,” Bobby said, exasperation coating his words.

“He took it home with them!” Chimney told her, and Hen looked at Eddie as if he’d grown another head.

“You did what!?”

“I didn’t mean to,” Eddie defended himself. “And nothing happened.”

“I mean, I almost threw him out the house.” Buck’s joke had Hen scowling at him for some reason. All the amusement slid off his face. “But I- I didn’t. Obviously.”

He awkwardly set the bracelet back down, wiping his palms on his pants afterwards.

“Right,” Hen scoffed, and Eddie was getting the feeling that he was missing something. Before he could ask, she was turning back around and heading for the stairs again, passing a startled Albert along the way.

“What was that about?” the younger Han asked as he joined them, voicing all of their thoughts.

“I don’t know…” Buck answered, sounding small. Which immediately had Eddie wanting to chase Hen down and demand answers.

“Well, whatever that was, can we please do something about this, ” Chimney gestured at the bracelet, “before anything else happens? Like, maybe the oven blows? Or the bathroom backs up for the third time this week. Thanks again, Diaz.”

Perhaps Buck had been rubbing off on him, for Eddie to get the idea he did. Or perhaps Eddie had always been just as much of a little shit as his husband. Either way, he picked up the bracelet, doing his best to keep from smiling.

“Hmm?” He held it up to his face. Everyone looked at him. “Catch!”

The bracelet flew through the air at Chimney, who screamed and flailed and backpedaled, as if it was a snake or a spider. The tacky piece of jewelry clattered on the ground; the paramedic glared at him and stomped off in the direction Hen left.

At least it got Buck to laugh.

Perhaps Hen was a hypocrite. She was probably, almost definitely, being hypocritical. It was just that she was so disappointed in her friend. She had honestly thought Buck was better than that.

Sure, once upon a time, she’d barely been surprised when that woman came into the station and accused Buck of ghosting her. It was hard to be disappointed at the thought that the younger man was cheating on Abby when she hadn’t expected much better from him. Not after the months spent watching him chase after every pretty smile, to the point that it nearly cost him his job if not for Athena’s good word.

But now? Years later, finding out that he hadn’t changed? That he was still chasing after pretty smiles? That was disappointing.

Hen had come home on Friday to find a distraught Eddie leaving and Karen seething on the couch. She and her wife tried not to keep secrets from each other anymore, especially secrets that were destined to affect everyone in their group. And Buck cheating on Eddie was exactly that kind of secret. Hell, it was the equivalent of a grenade being launched into the station.

There was no way that things weren’t going to get messy when it exploded.

But, honestly? She kinda wished Karen hadn’t told her. Because the first time she saw Buck on Monday, pressed against Eddie as he always was - as if everything was perfectly fine and normal - she was suddenly so very angry.

He had been there after the mess with Eva. He had seen what it nearly did to Hen’s own marriage. So every moment spent in his presence was another moment she had to resist grabbing him by the shoulders and shaking some sense into him. It was another moment she wanted to scream and yell at him, “why!?”

Worse was the way Buck insisted on acting clueless and innocent. Worse was the way that Eddie was letting him. Letting Buck crack jokes about throwing Eddie out the house when it should have been, by all rights, Buck getting thrown out. Just like it should have been Hen, instead of Karen leaving with Denny.

She wanted to yell at them, but she couldn’t. Because for some reason, Eddie was happily acting as if everything was fine. Which honestly wasn’t what she expected him to do. She expected him to do something like he had during the lawsuit, to snap and to yell and to rage. She expected to come back to a station that was (metaphorically) on fire. But that wasn’t what the situation was. And if that was the way that Eddie wanted to play it, then fine. It wasn’t her relationship, it wasn’t her marriage.

That didn’t mean Hen was fully capable of biting her tongue around Buck. She tried, but she just couldn’t. Especially when he was acting ignorant or making those stupid jokes.

She retreated back downstairs after the joke about throwing Eddie out. If she lingered for a moment longer her self restraint was going to finally run out. So Hen decided to find something, anything, to do that would keep her away from Buck.

Chimney interrupted her just as she started inventorying the (already fully stocked) ambulance, climbing into the back with her.

“So,” her friend said, closing the ambulance’s doors behind him. “What was that about? What’s our Buckaroo done to annoy you?”

“Oh, he knows what he did,” she said, not looking at him. “No matter how much he pretends like he doesn’t.”

“Okay, well, I don’t,” Chimney said. “Did he buy Denny a puppy without talking to you? Mansplain to you about medical protocols? Come on, give me something.”

“He-” Hen started, but shook her head. “It’s not my place.”

“Not your place? So he didn’t do anything to you ,” he said, and then, “What, did he cheat on Eddie?”

It was supposed to be a joke, Hen knew. An example that was so impossible as to be laughable. Chimney even laughed as he said it. But something about her silence, something that must have shown on her face, wiped the humor out of the man.

“Hen. Did he cheat on Eddie?”

She didn’t say anything, but her continued silence was enough of an answer.

“Oh my God,” he said. “I can’t believe this. I mean, I literally cannot believe this. There’s no way that-”

“Well, believe it!” she finally snapped, surprising him. “Eddie showed up at our house on Friday to talk to Karen. Apparently, Buck lied to him about visiting Maddie when he was actually out with some woman named Myra.”

“Oh my God,” Chimney said again. “What do we do?”

“What I want to do is grab Buck and shake him until he tells me what the hell he was thinking.”

“I- I mean, if it was, I dunno. If it was Abby, I would almost understand it,” he told her, shaking his head. “It’d still be shitty, but it would be like you and- Who the hell is Myra?”

“I have no clue,” Hen said, choosing to ignore the near mention of her own personal experience with this particular mistake. “And I’m stuck just watching the two of them go on as if…”

“God. No wonder you nearly snapped at him.” Chimney looked at her. “And Eddie is just, what? Pretending like it didn’t happen?”

“I guess!” she said, throwing out her hands and feeling so very frustrated.

“Poor guy, I can’t believe it. He has to be crushed,” he said, shaking his head again. “This is a mess. This is going to be such a mess…”

“Tell me about it.”

Perhaps, Eddie thought, he should go ahead and get paramedic certified. With how often he got saddled with paramedic duties, he might as well be getting the pay for it. Except he had neither the desire nor the time to go back to classes. Which reminded him: he needed to get on Buck about registering for those leadership courses that the department offered.

Anyways, this time, Eddie found himself transporting a patient to the hospital with Albert. Which he didn’t entirely mind, to be honest. It was better than riding with Hen and Chimney, both of whom he’d decided to avoid for one reason or another. Hen because she was clearly in a mood, and Chimney because, well. At some point during the shift, he started sending Eddie pitying glances.

Eddie didn’t even know why the fuck the man would be pitying him. Was it the curse thing? Did the man think he’d gotten like, curse cancer or something? He’d taken the bracelet home with him and now he was going to die within the week?

Luckily, the hospital they did the transport to was the same one Felisa’s assistant, Alexis, was at. Athena called him to say she’d found something, so he asked her to meet him at the hospital. That way, whatever she found, they could bring it to Felisa and Alexis together.

(And also Eddie could give back the stupid ugly bracelet, which he’d taken to carrying around on the probable improbable chance he ran into the actress again.)

He was just finishing up the intake paperwork when the sergeant arrived.

“Hey, thanks for meeting me here,” he said, setting it down to meet her halfway. “You said you found something?”

“I went to check out the crystal shop,” she said, pulling something up on her phone. “It was weird as hell.”

“Yeah,” he agreed. “Five people in the store, one gets stabbed, four others pass out, but no one sees anything?”

“There were six people at the shop,” Athena corrected him, handing him her phone. It was showing a clip of a man wearing a baseball cap. He was struggling with Alexis. “I checked the security camera.”

“Any idea who this guy is?”

“The hat obscures his face. But it turns out that this,” she reached over and swiped her finger across the screen, bringing up a separate video of the same man, “is not the first time he’s shown up at the scene of one of Felisa’s accidents.”

There was a hint of sarcasm on that last word. Apparently the sergeant was starting to get the suspicion that they were not, in fact, “accidents.”

“Where is that?” Eddie asked, not immediately recognizing the location of the second clip. “Is that the spa?”

“Mhmm. And that’s the same guy running out the shop right before the call.”

“You think he’s working with Alexis.” He didn’t ask, but she nodded anyway. “He’s causing the accidents so Alexis can, what? Cash in how?”

How did nearly killing her cash cow help Alexis in any way?

“That bracelet Felisa said cost her twenty-thousand?” Athena asked, taking her phone back when he handed it to her. “Alexis bought it online for eleven.”

“That is one hell of a markup,” Eddie said. That was $9000 straight into Alexis’ pocket.

“I need to talk to Felisa.”

“This is the same hospital Alexis is at,” he pointed out. “That’s partly why I asked you to meet me here. She’s probably here visiting.”

It was an easy matter for Athena to get Alexis’ room number. The sergeant simply walked up to the nearest nurse’s station and flashed her badge. The nurse sent them to the ICU, which wasn’t that far away.

When they got there, Athena hung back a bit and let Eddie be the one to peek his head through the open door. Felisa was indeed there, sitting by the bed and talking to an unconscious Alexis, who they had hooked up to a respirator. Her back was to him, until he rapped his knuckles on the door frame and drew her attention.

“Firefighter Diaz.” She sounded surprised, which made sense. “What are you doing here?”

“Just Eddie,” he told her. “We had a drop off and,” he pulled out the bracelet, “I’ve been trying to give this back to you.”

Whatever reaction he expected from Felisa at the sight of the bracelet, it was not for her to scoff.

“I can’t believe I thought that was gonna change my luck,” she said, rolling her eyes. She turned back slightly to look at the unconscious Alexis. “She almost died because of me.”

“I don’t think that’s true,” he said.

She turned back to him, curiosity roused, and he motioned for Athena to come to the door. The arrival of a uniformed police sergeant was enough to draw the actress into the hallway. Or maybe it was Athena in particular, because the moment she was out in the hall with them, Felisa was saying,

“I recognize you. Didn’t you arrest me once?”

“I did, after you tried to run over a paparazzi.” Athena pulled out her phone, pulling up the video clips again. “We have reason to believe that your friend in there might not have your best interests at heart. And that she may be working with someone else to steal from you.”

“What do you mean?” Felisa asked, wary.

“The same man was both at the spa and at the crystal shop,” Eddie told her. “We think they’re working together.”

“Or were, anyway,” Athena added, handing the actress her phone.

She watched the first clip, and then the second, immediately shaking her head in denial.

“This doesn’t mean anything,” Felisa said, handing Athena her phone back. “Alexis would never do anything to hurt me. I mean, you’ve met her! You know she wouldn’t do that.”

“I know you feel like she’s the only one you can count on,” Eddie said, in lieu of pointing out that, no, he didn’t know that. 

“Because she is! Everyone else is gone,” Felisa said, turning away from them to look at Alexis through the observation window. “Dead, disappeared, or… Simply doesn’t want to talk to me ever again. She’s all I have.”

Well that was sad and depressing. Even sadder considering Alexis was - probably - only still hanging around for the money.

“What about your family?” Athena asked her.

“My parents got divorced when I was sixteen,” the actress answered. “Got pretty nasty, so I filed for emancipation. The last time I spoke to them was the day the judge legally declared me an adult.”

Eddie wondered, idly, just how nasty the divorce had gotten for the judge to approve that. 

“I don’t know why you think Alexis would do this to me,” Felisa said, walking around them to head back into the room.

“She paid eleven-thousand dollars for that bracelet,” Eddie said. The words stopped Felisa in her tracks. “What’d she charge you again?”

“All you guys have are two grainy videos of two guys in hats,” she said, turning back around to face them. “It’s just a coincidence.”

“That might be right,” Athena said, nodding. “But if it isn’t, that man is still out there and you could be in danger.”

“I can take care of myself,” Felisa declared, before storming into Alexis’ hospital room. The door slammed shut behind her.

“Think we’re wrong here?” Eddie asked Athena.

“You know what I believe in even less than a curse?” The sergeant’s question was rhetorical, and she answered it herself. “Coincidence.”

“On the bright side,” Eddie said, “everyone will be happy that the bracelet is gone.”

Chapter 21: Terms and Conditions May Apply

Chapter Summary

In which someone gets yelled at.

“Dispatch, this is Seven Two Seven L Thirty. The victim’s car has been covered… in asphalt. I need fire and rescue at Riverside… near the equestrian center.”

Late into Wednesday evening, about halfway through their twenty-four hour shift, the 118 was sent to rescue a car that found itself buried under fresh asphalt. Buck felt kind of guilty for it, but he was kinda relieved about the distraction. Hen’s attitude had apparently spread to Chimney, who started giving him looks at some point during the afternoon. Thankfully, it appeared that neither Eddie nor Bobby had noticed, because the last thing Buck wanted or needed was that kind of blow up.

The accident was near the equestrian center, where Christopher had his horse therapy, and Buck knew that there’d been road work in the area. So he could almost guess what had happened. Probably, some idiot was speeding and rammed into the mixer.

When they got there, they found the car almost completely buried in steaming asphalt. There was a second car not far away, surrounded by police cruisers, the driver sitting on an out of the way curb with his hands cuffed. Athena intercepted the six of them as they climbed out of the fire engine, meeting them halfway to the car.

“I couldn’t get to her, and this asphalt is hardening,” she said. “It’s Felisa Valdez.”

Of course it was. That curse was working double time today, apparently.

“We have any kind of status on her?” Bobby asked.

“She was talking to Dispatch,” Athena answered. “But these fumes are pretty brutal.”

“Alright, let’s get all the air bottles we have on standby. Eddie, Buck,” he said, looking at them, “I want you guys to get some rubbish hooks into this asphalt.”

The two of them nodded and jogged back to the engine, leaving the captain and the paramedics to discuss the other problem. That being, Felisa being trapped in an enclosed space that was both heating up and being filled with poisonous fumes. He and Eddie grabbed the hooks and arrived at the car in time to see Bobby smashing out the exposed tail light with a halligan.

“Okay, we can work with this,” Chimney declared between coughs, before running to get the oxygen tanks.

But while the paramedics were having some success, stringing a tube from the tank into the trunk of the car and instructing Felisa to grab it, Buck and Eddie were not having as easy of a time.

Asphalt was not a liquid, but it was viscous when heated enough. Every time they struck at it with the hooks, they tore bits and pieces away from the driver’s door. But that space was quickly filled in, and they couldn’t get enough out at once to make a difference. Things were a bit better, once Albert joined them with a shovel, but even then it wasn’t quite enough.

At least he and Eddie had the forethought to strap on O2 tanks, so they didn’t have to worry so much about the fumes. Buck didn’t want to imagine trying to do this while stopping every other minute to cough his lungs out.

“Any progress?” Bobby asked, coming to check on them.

“No,” Buck answered, shaking his head and leaning on his rubbish hook. “Hardly making a dent.”

“If it was hardened, we could pick at it,” Eddie said. “I was thinking of taking the blowtorch to spots to try and dry it, but-”

“What about the hoses?” Albert shuffled his feet a bit once his suggestion had all three of them focusing on him. “I mean, a blowtorch would just keep it melted, right? This is like- Like, road lava. We want to cool it down. And the water in the hoses is pretty cold.”

“Harden too quickly, it’ll probably be pretty brittle, too,” Eddie commented, glancing at Buck. “Easier to chip away.”

“We could maybe use it to power wash some of the asphalt off,” Buck added, tilting his head as he thought about it. “But then that risks drowning Felisa if we break her window.”

“And if we break her window, the asphalt gets in… Alright.” Bobby nodded, coming to a decision. “Go hook up the hose, keep it on the narrow fog. Don’t worry about washing it off, just focus on getting it hardened enough to break it off.”

The three of them nodded in confirmation before dashing back to the engine, dropping the hooks and the shovel to the ground so they could drag out the hose to hook it up. They were quick about it, a skill learned by necessity, and soon they were spraying down the asphalt on the driver’s side. They kept the water away from where Hen and Chimney were crowded around the busted tail light, pumping clean air into the car.

The hot asphalt steamed as the cold water hit it.

They stopped every few minutes, so Bobby could try and hack away at it with his halligan. Every time he would shake his head, and they would hit it with the water once more. They kept it up until, finally, the captain managed to get a large chunk to crack and drop off. And no new asphalt oozed in to fill the gap.

Deeming it hardened and brittle enough, they shut the hose off and dropped it, rushing to get back with the hooks and the shovel. Now, unlike before, they were getting large pieces to fall away. With the hooks and the shovel and the halligan, they got large chunks to peel off the car to reveal a cracked window, and then a handle, and then hinges and edges of the door.

“Chim, Hen! Get the gurney!” Bobby called, before driving the halligan under the edge of the door. Buck grabbed the handle and together, they pulled and they shoved until the metal squealed in protest and the door popped open.

Buck and Bobby both stepped out of the way, letting Eddie drag out a coughing and lethargic Felisa. They got her strapped onto the gurney and away from the car. When they got to the ambulance, they crossed paths with Athena and a few other uniformed officers escorting a man away in cuffs.

“How is she?” the sergeant asked.

“Lucky to be alive,” Eddie answered.

“Who’s that?” Buck asked, pointing to the cuffed man that the cops were escorting.

“Oh, he chased her,” Athena explained. “Looks a lot like the man from the security camera from the crystal shop and the spa.”

That bit of exposition was accompanied by a pointed look at Felisa.

“I’m sorry,” the man said, an officer pausing in the middle of shoving him into the back of a cruiser. “I didn’t mean to harm anyone.”

Felisa sat up as best she could to frown at him.

“You mean it wasn’t a curse?” she asked.

There was a flabbergasted silence from the man, before he spoke again.

“Again, sorry. Could you tell me where the bracelet is?”

This had Felisa glaring at the man. She yanked the bracelet off her wrist, and hurled it at the man. It struck him in the face, leaving a gash on his cheek, before he was put into the cruiser.

“You’re going to explain what that was all about, right, Ma?” Buck asked Athena, once Felisa was safely loaded up. Eddie thumped a fist on the ambulance doors before joining him in looking expectantly at the woman.

“Mm, maybe,” the sergeant said, turning her back on him and heading for her cruiser. “If you behave.”

“I always behave!”

This explanation was given to them by Athena the next morning. They’d gathered around the dining table at the station, like little kids being told a story. Chimney had even made popcorn, though the bowls always seemed to be just out of Buck’s reach. Thankfully, Eddie had no problem tugging one of the bowls closer to him every time it happened.

Anyways, this was the convoluted mess:

Nico Perelli, which Buck was still convinced was a made up name, ran an online business that made trinkets in China to be passed off as ancient artifacts. It was a scam, an obvious one if someone that actually knew anything started looking too closely. Which was why he made all buyers agree to not showcase the “artifacts.”

Unfortunately for him, Alexis was one of the many, many people who didn’t read the Terms and Conditions before agreeing to them. And Felisa didn’t see them at all. So, when she talked about doing a live unboxing for her fans, the man panicked.

The first attempt was to intercept the package before it was delivered, which was foiled by Alexis waiting outside for it.

The second attempt was to steal it while Felisa was at the spa, which he bungled so badly he had to run away empty handed while the actress screamed bloody murder.

The third attempt was at the crystal shop. Nico confronted Alexis, demanding the bracelet to be returned. The two got into a struggle, Alexis got stabbed in the stomach, and he had to flee again.

A car chase through Los Angeles, resulting in Felisa being buried under asphalt and Nico being arrested, was the fourth and final attempt.

“I’m starting to think this Nico guy’s the one who’s actually cursed,” Buck said, reaching for the popcorn only to find that it had, once again, been moved just out of his reach. He gave up on it, leaning against Eddie instead and deciding they could get something to actually eat later.

“No more talking about curses,” Bobby warned, fixing him with a stern look.

“Either way, he can’t terrorize Felisa or us, ever again,” Eddie concluded.

“And on that happy note, shift’s over,” Hen declared, slapping the table top as she stood. “Let’s go and get real food.”

“Eddie and I can’t,” Buck told her as he stood up as well, which was apparently the wrong thing to say. It had the paramedic glaring at him.

“Oh?”

“We- We have an appointment…” he said, feeling like he was defending himself. He didn’t even know what he was defending himself against!

“Copeland,” Eddie added, standing up as well and not quite scowling at her. “Our twice monthly tune up.”

“Alright, then,” Hen said, turning and stomping away from them. Chimney followed after her with a quick look back at them.

“She’s still in a mood?” Albert asked, staring after the two.

“Buck.” The captain saying his name had Buck turning to look at him. “Do I need to be concerned about that?”

“I… Don’t know?” He shrugged and shared an equally confused look with Eddie. “We need to get going though. We’ll be cutting it pretty close this time.”

They said their goodbyes to Bobby and Athena and headed to the locker room with Albert to change into their civvies. It was awkward, sharing space with Hen and Chimney and feeling like they were silently judging him for something.

At least it was a therapy appointment they were on their way to.

“We’ll go grocery shopping after we get out,” Buck said, pulling out of the station parking lot. “Abuela saw a new recipe for enchilada meatballs that she was wanting to try. And we also need to get stuff for Sunday.”

“Carlos finally got me that chili recipe from Paul,” Eddie said, scrolling through his phone as he leaned back in his seat. “We could get the stuff for that, too.”

“You’re going to put corn chips in it again, aren’t you?” Buck guessed, rolling his eyes fondly. “You’re so weird.”

“It’s the only way to eat chili.”

“So weird!”

He caught Eddie looking at him from the corner of his eye, and when he glanced over, his husband was frowning.

“What?” Buck asked.

“Are you okay?” Eddie asked, which was a total non sequitur from the chili talk.

“Yeah? Why- Hold on, which thing is this about?” Buck asked in turn. “Because if it’s about me not eating chili with corn chips, I think that means I’m perfectly fine and you’re the not okay one.”

“It’s not about that.”

“Then is it about the, uh. What happened on Friday? Because-”

“I know you’re not okay about that,” Eddie interrupted him before he had the chance to lie. “If you were, you wouldn’t have sent me a picture of yourself at the PTO meeting like you were showing proof of life.”

“I just wanted to make sure you knew I was where I said I’d be.” Buck hunched his shoulders a little.

“Yeah, I know, and I appreciate it, but I’m also going to be bringing it up with Copeland today,” Eddie assured him. “No, I meant… Whatever’s going on with Hen.”

“Oh, that. Yeah, I’m just not focusing on that.”

“Really, mi sol?”

“Listen, whatever it is, it’s her problem,” Buck told him. “I asked what was wrong and didn’t actually get an answer. I have no clue what I did to piss her off, and she won’t tell me, because apparently she expects me to just know, so… It’s whatever. She’ll either get over it or she’ll actually talk to me or-”

He was interrupted by the jeep’s engine making some truly concerning noises.

“That’s not good,” Eddie said, sitting up.

“Nope,” Buck agreed, hitting the hazard lights and pulling over onto the curb. It was a haphazard parking job, because the engine completely gave up just as they cleared traffic and they coasted to a stop.

He tried the ignition, and then tried it again, only for nothing to come of it.

“Stupid, stupid bracelet curse,” he said, thumping his head against the steering wheel until Eddie’s hand caught him.

“There isn’t a curse,” his husband said. “But we’re going to miss our appointment.”

“No we’re not,” Buck declared, sitting back and tapping at his phone, which was hooked onto the dashboard. “We’re just having it here.”

Eddie stared at him, before shrugging.

“Do you want to call for a tow before or after?”

“Might as well call them now,” Buck said, the sound of ringing filling the air. “You know how long they take.”

“That’s true,” he said, stepping out onto the sidewalk with his phone.

“Hello, Evan,” Copeland greeted when she answered. “Has something come up?”

“The Jeep died on the way there, but it’s fine. Eddie’s calling for a tow and we’ll just talk to you like this.”

“Alright.” That’s what Buck liked most about the good doctor, the way she just rolled with it. “Is there anything you want to talk about today?”

“Well, er.” Buck glanced at Eddie through the open passenger door, who nodded back in encouragement. “Eddie thought I was cheating on him.”

There was a brief bout of silence on the other end of the line, broken by the sound of someone settling into their chair and then,

“Okay then. Let’s start from the top of that, shall we? Tell me what happened…”

Maddie knew something was up the moment Howie got home. He had breakfast with Hen after his shift which didn’t bother her. She’d done it before with Josh and Linda. It just meant he got home a bit later than he was supposed to, which was honestly more the norm than actually getting home on time, considering the way calls usually went. But the moment he got home, the moment Maddie laid eyes on him, she knew something was going on.

“I assume no one’s dead,” she guessed, pausing in the middle of playing with Jee-Yun. “Or else you wouldn’t have been out eating with Hen.”

“No, uh. No one’s dead,” Howie confirmed, eyes flittering around the apartment.

“Did something happen at work last night?”

Maddie had gone home shortly after the call with the Felisa Valdez woman, whom Josh nearly had a conniption about when she admitted to not recognizing the name. She’d picked up Jee from the Lees and crashed into bed, so if the 118 had had any crazy calls over night, she wouldn’t have heard about it.

“No, no, nothing happened at uh. At work.” Howie still wasn’t quite looking at her, and she could tell he was anxious about something.

“Okay, come on, just tell me,” Maddie said. “Or- No, please don’t tell me if you punched my brother again. Because I managed to forgive the once, but…”

“He’d definitely deserve it this time,” the man muttered, and then sent her a startled, wide-eyed look. “I- I mean…”

“Howie,” Maddie started, getting to her feet and fixing him with her best glare. “What’s going on with my brother?”

“He cheated on Eddie!” Howie finally blurted out.

She stared at him for one solid moment, and then threw her head back, laughing. Jee-Yun, not wanting to be left out, soon joined in with giggles of her own.

“You- He- What?” she managed to get out. “W- What?”

“Hen told me,” Howie said, crossing his arms and shifting his feet. “He- He apparently lied to Eddie about hanging out with you so he could go on a date with some woman.”

That would almost explain that odd phone call from Eddie on Friday, she thought, but…

“Okay, seriously now, what’s actually going on?” she asked him. “Because I know that’s not it. My brother might have spent some time as a manwhore, but he is not the type to cheat on someone.”

“Maddie. I’m- That’s what happened,” he said. “Or, that’s what Hen told me…”

“Then Hen is wrong.” It was a simple declaration. She grabbed her shoes and her keys. “Get Jee’s shoes.”

“Uh, okay.” Howie scrambled to find them and slip them on their daughter’s feet, who decided to make a game of kicking them away. “What’re we doing?”

“We are going to see Buck and Eddie,” she told him. “Because clearly someone got something wrong, somewhere.”

“Well, okay, but-” Howie picked Jee up, bouncing her on his hip as he joined her at the door. “According to Hen-”

“According to Hen,” Maddie repeated, rolling her eyes.

“According to Hen, Eddie is pretending it isn’t a thing, so…”

“Well.” She flung the door open, and gestured for him to go ahead. “We’ll see, won’t we?”

After the session with Copeland, where the word of the day was apparently “Communication,” which Buck had once assumed he and Eddie were excelling at, they had to wait a bit longer on the side of the road for the tow truck to get to them. Buck suggested they tow it straight to a repair place and get them to look at it, but Eddie insisted he could fix whatever was wrong.

Buck could probably fix it, too, given his experiences keeping things running while driving up and down the continental United States. He just didn’t see the point of fumbling his way through it when he could pay someone else to do it for him, and do it better. But if Eddie wanted to flex and show off his auto repair skills, then who was Buck to stop him?

After they got the jeep home, they took the truck to the store for groceries. Somehow Buck still ended up driving them there and back, because his husband not-so-secretly hated driving when he could avoid it. Besides the things for keeping people fed through the rest of the week, they also tried surprising each other with flowers. It didn’t work, because they both made excuses to split up and then ran into each other in the floral section.

They just ended up choosing flowers together, which would definitely get them made fun of if TK heard about it.

They got home, got the groceries put away and the new flowers in a vase, placing them in the living room. They greeted Abuela and Hoover again, and debated whether or not to risk bloodshed trying to clip the dog’s nails or if Eddie should get started on finding what was wrong with the jeep, when someone started knocking on their door. When Eddie answered it, an irate Maddie marched in followed by an anxious looking Chimney, who set down Jee-Yun and allowed her to run giggling towards Hoover, who’d abandoned Abuela in the backyard to greet the visitors.

“Hoof!” she cried, immediately wrapping her arms around the dog’s neck.

“And a hello to you, too,” Eddie said, closing the door behind them.

“Maddie?” Buck walked into the living room, patting both Hoover and Jee-Yun on the head as he passed them. “What’s up?”

“This one,” Maddie pointed at Chimney, “came home with the most ridiculous of rumors today.”

“Maddie, I don’t think-” Chimney tried, only to wilt when she turned to glare at him.

“And what rumor would that be?” Eddie asked.

“Well?” Maddie continued glaring at Chimney. “Do you want to tell them?”

“Hen said…” The paramedic glanced between Buck and Eddie, obviously reluctant to finish the sentence. “She said that, uh. That Buck was…”

“That I was what?” Buck prompted.

“That you were cheating on Eddie!” the man finally snapped. “That you lied to Eddie about where you were so you could go on a date with someone else!”

Buck turned to look at Eddie, who had closed his eyes and was running a hand down his face.

“God damn it,” his husband whispered, sinking into the nearest comfy chair. “Shit. I can’t believe that I… I forgot. I went to talk to Karen, and then I- I completely forgot about it.”

“To be fair,” Buck said, walking over and rubbing at his shoulders, “you were kinda distracted afterwards. I think I can forgive you for it slipping your mind.”

“Still, fuck. I am so sorry…”

“At least it explains how she’s been acting.”

“So it’s true?” Chimney demanded. “Buck, how could-”

“Oh, shut up!” Eddie snapped, glaring at the other man. “Buck didn’t cheat on me. I was just a giant fucking idiot and accused him of it when all he was doing was trying to be romantic and surprise me.”

“We were both idiots, babe. Remember? We agreed.”

“Yeah, yeah…”

“I knew something got twisted somewhere. And you are an idiot, Buck. The next time you use me in a lie to surprise your husband, let me know!” Maddie said, before elbowing Chimney. “Well?”

“I’m sorry,” Chimney said, letting out a heavy sigh. “I shouldn’t have just immediately believed it.”

“At least we know that you and Hen are Team Eddie if anything happens,” Buck pointed out, focusing on the bright side.

“There is no Team Eddie,” his husband said, scowling up at him. “There’s only Team Us.”

“Why the hell would I ever cheat on that? ” Buck asked Chimney, gesturing at his husband. He crossed the room to grab his keys out of the bowl by the door. “I’m taking the truck.”

“And going where?” Maddie asked him. “Wait, what’s wrong with the jeep?”

“Don’t know yet, Eddie hasn’t looked,” Buck explained, opening the door and stepping out. “But I’m off to yell at Hen. One of you yell at Chimney.”

“Will do,” Maddie promised, and Buck did not feel any guilt for leaving the man to his fate.

It was a twenty minute drive with nothing but the radio and his thoughts, which meant it was a twenty minute drive spent getting angrier the more he thought about it. He wasn’t angry at Eddie, maybe a little annoyed, but he wasn’t angry. Buck couldn’t blame the man for going to one of their friends for support in a moment of crisis. Hell, he was happy that his husband felt comfortable enough to do so.

He was not happy that the puck had not stopped there, or at least with Hen.

There was a reason that both of them had the unspoken agreement to not bring this mess into the station. Because it was a mess, and they both figured it would only get messier if others at the station were involved. A fact that was so readily proven with Hen’s passive aggressiveness the past few days. Sure, Buck had been mostly concerned that Bobby would find out that Eddie had lobbed the accusation and be pissed at him on Buck’s behalf. But they hadn’t wanted the others involved in this mess. Eddie hadn’t wanted the others involved in this mess. It was their mess.

Before long, he was pulling up to the Wilsons’ house and bounding up the sidewalk and the porch steps. Before long, he was pounding at the door until Hen answered it. She opened her mouth to say something, but he was pushing his way into the house before she could speak. Karen was, thankfully, also present: sitting at the table where he did the vodka shots with Hen, looking much better than the last time he saw her.

“I didn’t cheat on Eddie,” Buck told them, twisting around to glare at the both of them in turn.

“Buck.” Karen glared at him. “Eddie came here and-”

“Eddie was wrong!” he snapped. “Yeah, I lied to him about where I was, but not because I was cheating on him. There are other reasons for people to lie about where they are, like, I don’t know. Planning a surprise!”

“Where were you then?” Hen asked him. “Who’s Myra?”

“Literally not your business,” Buck told her, and didn’t feel any guilt about the bluntness of it. “All you need to know is this: I didn’t cheat on Eddie. I love him and I would sooner set myself on fire than hurt him like that. I would rather burn than hurt him. And it has been killing me, knowing that I fucked up so badly that he honestly thought I would do that to him. But I didn’t.”

He was breathing heavily and doing his best not to scream himself blue in the face. The problem, he was finding, was that he was just as hurt as he was angry. Or, rather, the anger was because of how hurt he was.

“Seriously, nearly five and a half years, and you had no problem thinking I would do that?” he asked Hen. “Eddie, I understood. I know exactly all the ways I screwed up there. It hurt, but I understood. We talked like adults, and we’re both trying to do better. And Karen! I think we can both understand why Karen would immediately jump to that conclusion.”

He watched Hen do a full body flinch at that, and did not feel very satisfied.

“Hey!” Karen snapped at him, getting to her feet.

“But there was no excuse for you and Chimney to not give me the benefit of the doubt,” Buck said, ignoring Karen’s indignation behind him. “This was the catfishing all over again. I’m just the station manwhore, so of course I’d cheat on my partner. Right? Five years later and I’m still making up for those first few months. Right?”

“No,” Hen tried, shaking her head. “No, Buck…”

“Eddie and I, we didn’t want to bring this mess into the station,” Buck said, talking over her denial. “Because it wasn’t anyone else’s business. But you brought it into the station. You had Cap asking if he needed to be concerned.”

“I’m sorry.”

“I accept your apology,” he said. “But you owe Eddie one, too.”

He glanced behind him, at Karen who was looking somewhat abashed and guilty where she stood.

“I’m happy to see you up and about,” Buck told her, before stepping around Hen and out the door.

It slammed shut behind him.

Chapter 22: Bye, Felisa

Chapter Summary

In which Eddie tries, and Buck gets to be the cool dad.

“Maldita sea!” Eddie snapped, rubbing at the aching spot on his head. “Estupido de mierda…”

It was Friday afternoon, and he was trying to fix the jeep. It had taken him all day just to figure out what was wrong with it, followed by a trip to the store for a new part that was undoubtedly marked up. Unfortunately, though, fixing it meant having to be under the Jeep. And while it was probably less of a problem for him than for Buck - who, understandably, got a bit anxious at the prospect of being under a vehicle - that didn’t mean it wasn’t a problem at all.

“Hit your head again?” Buck’s voice was accompanied by his feet appearing within Eddie’s sight.

“Yes,” he grumbled, twisting the wrench with perhaps a bit too much force. “I can do this! I worked at a garage before we left Texas, I know what I’m doing.”

“Never said you didn’t or couldn’t.” Buck crouched down beside him and placed a warm hand on his bent knee. “Just that it would have been easier to have someone else do it.”

“And more expensive.” Eddie grunted, and then let out a cry of success. “Ha! Chúpamela.”

“Gladly,” the other man said, and Eddie didn’t need to see his face to know that Buck was smirking. “Probably shouldn’t out here, though. Does Abuela know you talk like this?”

“Like you haven’t heard worse from her when she cuts or burns herself cooking,” Eddie pointed out, doing his best to slide out from under the jeep without banging his head against anything. “Okay, the new fuel filter is in. Try and start it.”

“And if this doesn’t work?” Buck asked, obediently opening the car door and hopping into the driver’s seat.

“I go back under and cuss some more,” he answered. “And if I still can’t figure it out, then we give it a Viking funeral.”

“Seriously, babe?” His husband shook his head and turned the ignition. The jeep rumbled to life.

“Ha! I told you I could fix it. And for cheaper than what they’d have charged us.”

“It took you all day just to figure out what was wrong,” Buck pointed out, and Eddie swatted at his shoulder. “Go get washed up.”

“Only if you’ll join me,” he said, leaning into his husband’s personal space. Buck rolled his eyes and shoved at him, but he was grinning as he did it.

“We don’t have time. Chris has been invited to the beach. He’s already getting ready to go.”

“And you want to go to the beach, too, I’m guessing.”

“I mean.” Buck shrugged. “Yeah? A day lounging in the sun sounds nice, after, y’know. Everything.”

“Yeah, it does,” Eddie agreed, gathering up his tools. “Okay, I’ll go hop in the shower and get my shorts on.”

“Oh, just your shorts?” the other man asked, jumping out of the jeep and closing the door. “Or are you going to put on one of your slutty tanks?”

“One of my- What?”

“You heard me,” Buck said, shrugging. “The ones that scream, ‘oh please ravish me.’ Or maybe, ‘let me ravish you.’”

“Ravish?” Eddie shook his head, chuckling. “Have you been reading one of Pepa’s books?”

“Maybe.” Buck shrugged again, leading the way into the house. “Is it bad that I kinda enjoyed it?”

“I’m going to make fun of you for it,” Eddie warned him.

“That’s fair.”

Getting ready to go didn’t take very long. A quick shower for Eddie, a quick change of clothes for Buck, and a moment to grab the chairs and towels. Christopher was ready before either of them, and was waiting impatiently by the door while Buck and Abuela packed a basket of snacks and drinks.

When they got to the beach, the pre-teen pretty much abandoned them the moment his feet touched sand and he spotted his friends.

“Are these friends from school?” Eddie asked, squinting at the two kids waiting for his son.

“Yeah, but not the ones he skipped Science Club with,” Buck answered, setting down his towel and unfolding a chair. He sat down and dug his toes into the sand. “Y’know, in some places, there are worms in the sand that can burrow into your skin.”

“What a lovely fact,” Eddie sniped. “Totally not something that’s going to haunt me for the rest of the day.”

“Well obviously Santa Monica isn’t one of those places,” his husband said, poking at his side. “But Peru was.”

“Buck, mi sol.” He did his best to keep the smile off his face. “Do we need to take you to the vet for worms?”

Any smart ass reply Buck had for him was interrupted by Chris shouting,

“Pops!”

“I better go see what that’s about,” Buck told him, getting up with a groan. “If they drown me, will you give me mouth to mouth?”

“You might have worms,” Eddie replied, and laughed when his husband kicked sand at him.

Eddie settled into his own chair and watched his husband jog across the beach to where Chris and his friends were gathered at the tide’s edge. It was a lovely afternoon for this, he decided, and it was nice to just bask in the sun after the week they’d had.

Speaking of the week, though, there was one thing still bothering him. He felt obligated to try, and so he pulled out his phone to make a call.

She didn’t arrive until the sun was sinking into the ocean, everything bathed in soft gold and the ocean lit up like fire. Buck crashed into his chair after the kids were done with him, ate a sandwich, and then passed out with his sunglasses on his face. He’d been quietly snoring for the past half hour.

“Hey Eddie.” Felisa’s voice came from behind him, and he turned his head to greet her with a smile.

“Hey, I didn’t think you were gonna show.”

“I wasn’t going to,” she admitted. “But you saved my life once or twice, so I figured I owed you.”

“How’s Alexis?” he asked, gesturing at Christopher’s unused beach chair.

“They’re releasing her tomorrow,” Felisa told him, sitting down. “She’s gonna come stay with me.”

“Did you talk to her about the money?”

She looked away from him, and he already knew what the answer was going to be before she said it.

“I don’t care about that.”

“She’s stealing from you,” Eddie pointed out.

“Maybe she’s earned it.” Something about his expression must have shouted his opinions out loud and clear, because she was quick to defend herself. If it could be called defending. “You know who I am, I’m sure you’ve heard the stories. The trouble I’d get in, the DUIs, the arrests and the friends who OD’d in my bathroom. That kind of loss… I don’t know what else you’d call it but a curse.”

The consequences of her own actions, is what Athena would call it. But he wasn’t about to rub someone’s face in their past mistakes, especially not after defending Buck from the exact same.

“My wife died a bit over three years ago,” he told her. “A few months later, my son was on the pier when the tsunami hit.”

Her eyes followed his finger when he pointed at the newly rebuilt one.

“Oh, God,” she breathed. “I’m so sorry.”

“Oh, he’s fine,” Eddie assured. “He’s right over there. Christopher!” At the sound of his name, his son turned. They waved to each other. “He would have died in this ocean, except my husband was there with him. Well, he wasn’t my husband yet, but… I almost lost my entire world that day.”

“Must have been terrifying.”

“It was,” he said. “We were all scared for a long time afterwards.”

“You wouldn’t know it now,” Felisa said, gazing over at Chris and his friends, happily chatting away while the tide washed over their feet.

“The day the beach reopened, we all came out here,” Eddie told her. It had actually been just a couple weeks before the Christmas he pulled his head out of his ass. “Buck and I, we both figured that the longer we stayed away from the water, the more the fear would grow. And we didn’t want the ocean to have that kind of power over him.”

Maybe the metaphor was a bit too heavy-handed, because Felisa was smiling like she was in on a joke.

“You know,” she said, “sometimes it’s easier to believe that you’re cursed than to believe you’re wrong?”

“Or to know you’re right?” he countered. She shook her head at him. “Felisa.”

“I’m gonna get going,” she said, getting up. “I’m getting the house blessed before Alexis gets home. I know, it sounds silly, but…”

“If it works for you.”

“Take care of yourself, okay?” she said. “The three of you.”

“You too.”

Eddie watched her go, feeling very disappointed in how things went. But at this point, what more could he do? Felisa had pretty much admitted to not only knowing what Alexis was doing, but that she felt like it was payment for old sins.

“You tried.”

Buck’s voice didn’t startle him, but only because he’d noticed the lack of snoring sometime during the tsunami story.

“Did you have a nice nap?” he asked.

“I did,” Buck said. “I had a lovely dream. I was on a beach with my beautiful husband and he was hand feeding me grapes.”

“Chris ate all the grapes,” Eddie told him, and grinned at the whine Buck let out. “There’s still those cherry tomatoes you like. Will those do?”

Buck waggled his head back and forth.

“I guess,” he finally said. “What would Abuela do, if she knew you invited a celebrity to the beach with us and then didn’t even get a selfie to show her church friends?”

Eddie paused in the middle of digging through the picnic basket to contemplate the question. The answer he came up with was frightening.

“Don’t worry,” Buck said, reaching up to pat his cheek. His own grin was the epitome of shit eating. “Your secret’s safe with me.”

“Hey Pops?”

Chris’ voice, timid and anxious, had Buck freezing in his steps. He’d been on the way to the bathroom, after helping clean up after dinner, but that could wait. Instead, he carefully made his way to his kid’s room, where said kid was peeking his head out.

“What’s up?”

“Could I… Could I talk to you about something?”

Okay, whatever it was, Chris was very anxious about it.

“Of course,” he said. “Do you want me to get Dad, too?”

“No!” Chris covered his mouth, clearly taken aback by his own outburst. “I- I mean no. Just you. Please?”

“Then it’ll just be me.”

Chris’ room at the new house was set up quite similar to his old room. It was slightly bigger, Buck thought, and the walls were painted a light blue. The ceiling was covered in glow in the dark stars, which Buck had tried his best to form into the proper constellations.

He took a seat on the desk chair, while the boy himself perched on the edge of his bed.

“Alright,” Buck said, “what’s going on?”

“There’s a party at school,” Chris told him, and he nodded.

“I know, I’m gonna be one of the chaperones.”

For some reason, this had the kid looking panicked.

“You will!?”

“Is that a problem…?”

“No.” Even though Chris was quick to shake his head, Buck got the impression that his presence at the function was, in actual fact, going to be a problem.

“Hey, come on. Whatever you need to talk to me about, go ahead,” he urged. “We got through the Talk together, we can get through this.”

Chris, impossibly, looked even more embarrassed by the mention of the Talk. Which Buck didn’t blame him for. That experience was harrowing for everyone involved, and he had so much more respect for Maddie, considering she was the one to give it to him. And she’d done it alone.

“How do you know you like someone?”

“Like, as a friend?” he asked, somewhat confused.

“No, like… Like more. Than a friend.”

Oh. Ohhhh. 

Oh shit.

“Well, uh.” Buck rubbed at the back of his neck, trying to figure out what to say. It had been easier with Ravi and Albert, they’d just been asking how he knew he liked guys. This was something else entirely. “It’s, um. It’s different for different people.”

“Pops,” Chris whined, rolling his eyes.

“It’s true,” he said, defending himself. “Sometimes it’s obvious, and sometimes it just sneaks up on you.”

“Did it sneak up on you with Dad?”

“Your dad hit me like a train,” was his dry response, which at least got Chris to laugh. “But c’mon, this person. Do you smile when you think about them?”

“Yeah,” Chris admitted, and it was indeed accompanied by a small and bashful smile.

“Well that’s usually a sure sign,” Buck told him. “And uh, do you get a fluttery feeling in your stomach? The ol’ butterflies? Like when you’re just about to fall?”

This time, Chris just bobbed his head in a nod.

“I think you have a crush,” he stage-whispered. “I also think you already knew that.”

“Yeah,” the kid shyly admitted.

“C’mon, tell me about them,” Buck urged, poking at Chris’ knee. “Are they pretty? Are they nice?”

Another embarrassed nod.

“They’re gonna be there, aren’t they?” Buck guessed. “At the dance, party, whatever.”

“Yeah.” The word seemed to have become his entire vocabulary at the moment.

Buck could not remember being this embarrassed about his crushes, but then again, he’d only really had Maddie to talk about them with. Until he didn’t.

“I’m guessing this is why you don’t want me there,” Buck told him. “Kinda awkward, trying to have your first kiss while your step-dad’s watching.”

Chris blushed harder and looked away. And then he shook his head.

“It’s not awkward?” he guessed. “Or- Oh. Ohhhh.”

He supposed “that’s my boy” was probably not the appropriate response. Neither would be Eddie’s if he heard about it, which would probably be to seal Chris inside a tower like Rapunzel.

“Was it…?” Buck trailed off, uncertain what he should even ask. He probably shouldn’t start demanding names. “Was it with your crush, or someone else?”

“Someone else,” Chris told him, still not quite meeting his eye. “She tried to stick her tongue in my mouth. It was weird.”

“Not everyone likes it,” Buck assured him, feeling so very out of his depth. “But uh, okay. So your crush is going to be at the dance-party-thing. And you wanna impress them?”

“I need a suit,” Chris declared. “I need to look nice.”

“I mean, you don’t need a suit to look nice, but… Your dad and I both need new suits too, might as well make it a family trip,” he said, standing up, but the mention of Eddie had Chris panicking again.

“Don’t tell Dad. Please.”

“Your secret is safe with me,” Buck promised, drawing his finger across his breast. “Cross my heart.”

Chris furrowed his brows at him, before raising his hand. He was holding his pinky out.

“Promise?”

“You’re killing me, kid,” Buck told him, wrapping his own pinky around Chris’. “Killing me.”

There was a six pack in the fridge with his name on it, after this.

Chapter 23: That's Gonna Sting

Chapter Summary

In which a queen is rescued from a knight.

Work after yelling at Hen was awkward. Not in the, “everyone’s pissed off and things are about to blow” kind of way. Awkward in the way that Hen and Chimney were both clearly feeling guilty about things but weren’t quite sure how to make amends. They’d ambushed the Diazes at the start of their next shift, a half day on Saturday, with apologies. Apologies for immediately believing the worst of Buck; apologies for bringing it into the station; apologies for spreading rumors. The latter two were mostly from Hen.

Buck accepted the apologies. Accepted, but wasn’t quite yet to the point of forgiving. He had always known that everyone at the station looked at him and still saw the asshole probie he’d been. This mess was just confirmation.

Yes, it sucked that Hen and Chimney believed it without question. Eddie needed more than a month of festering insecurities and then a host of evidence confirming his worst fears before confronting Buck about it. And Karen’s first instinct was always going to be to assume infidelity, because that’s the consequence of having your trust betrayed by someone you loved.

But Hen just believed it, without even asking Eddie about whether or not it was true. Hell, she didn’t even ask if he was okay. And then Chimney went and did the same. It sucked and it hurt and none of it was a surprise, because even Bobby still liked to joke about Buck’s five year old mistakes.

So Buck accepted the apologies, but he didn’t forgive them. Not yet. He would eventually, inevitably, because he was physically incapable of holding grudges. Unless someone tried killing him or Eddie, then he was quite capable of it.

(Fuck the Costas kid, and fuck Jonah Greenway, and fuck the sniper guy.)

Buck was an adult, however much he didn’t act it. So even though his feelings were bruised, he just tucked that away and continued on. The mess was behind them, and he was moving on forward. Nothing had changed, not really. He just knew for sure now what their friends thought of him, which apparently also hadn’t changed. It was just a matter of moving forward until the bruises faded.

Eddie was very obviously unhappy with that line of thinking, but what else could they do? Cause problems at the station? That was exactly the kind of thing they’d been trying to avoid the entire time. So the high road it was, ignoring the awkwardness and acting like everything was fine. Because it was, or would be.

Poor Albert was stuck in the middle of it all. Literally, because whenever they rode in the truck, he usually ended up sitting between the paramedics and the Diazes.

Bobby noticed, of course.

Towards the start of their shift on Monday, Eddie finally forced Buck into talking to the captain about the leadership courses. It was nearing the deadline for signing up, and apparently his husband wasn’t going to just let him “forget.” Which meant that he and Bobby ended up alone in the captain’s office, going over just what taking the classes would entail.

“It meets one week a month over four months,” the captain explained, tapping at the flier he pulled out and set on his desk. “Eight hour days, which means-”

“Which means I won’t be working those weeks.”

“Exactly,” Bobby said, doing something on his computer. A moment later, the nearby printer came to life. “Now, for you, it’ll be free as an employee of the Los Angeles Fire Department. You already know the general gist of what you have to look forward to?”

“Reading, journals, exams, and a term paper,” Buck recited, already feeling tired. “What happens if I flunk out of this class?”

“The class you’ve already done the reading for?” The captain smirked at him as he got up and grabbed whatever he printed out. When he brought it back to the desk, he plucked a pen from the holder and scribbled something on the bottom. “I think you’ll be fine. Here.”

“No, seriously, I am not good at classroom settings,” Buck said, taking the paper when Bobby held it out to him. When he looked at it, he saw that it was a letter of recommendation. “When did you type this up?”

“Months ago. You’ll need to send it in with your application.” The captain sat back down behind his desk and peered at him seriously, hands clasped in front of him. “Did you ever figure it out?”

“Figure what out?” Buck asked, a bit thrown by the question.

“If I needed to be concerned,” he answered. “About whatever was going on between you and Hen.”

“Oh.” He’d been dreading this. “Uh, yeah. I mean, no. It’s- It’s fine.”

“So I don’t need to be concerned?” That raised eyebrow was screaming doubt.

“Everything’s fine,” Buck tried to assure him. “There was just a, uh. Thing. Hen got half the story and…” He flapped his hand in the air, waving the conversation away. “It’s fine.”

Maybe if he chanted the Q-word in his head, something would happen to rescue him from this conversation.

Quiet. Quiet. Quiet.

“A thing?” Bobby asked him. “What sort of… Thing?”

Quiet. Quiet. Quiet.

“Just a misunderstanding,” he said. “Nothing you need to worry about, Cap. ”

The emphasis on his title had Bobby’s other eyebrow ascending.

Quiet. Quiet. Qui-

“So then-”

The alarm rang above them, and Buck leapt to his feet. He fled from the office and the conversation, knowing that it was probably suspicious as hell but not really caring. The others seemed a bit bemused by the way he was running as if it was the station itself on fire, but none of them asked any questions.

“So what do we have, Cap?” Albert asked the moment everyone was piled into the engine, squeezed between his brother and Eddie.

Bobby, sitting up front, frowned at the tablet in his hand.

“A knight is being swarmed by bees.”

Honestly, it was Los Angeles, so rescuing a knight wasn’t actually out of the realm of possibility. Not an actual knight, obviously, but someone dressed up as one. It was the bees that struck Buck as odd. Even the Africanized honey bees endemic to Peru only attacked to defend their colonies, they just happened to be a bit more aggressive about it and chased threats a bit farther.

Following Bobby’s directions, Jones drove them to the site of the call: a Renaissance Fair. Which admittedly made more sense than either of Buck’s guesses: a weirdo in a suit of armor who decided to attack a hive with a sword, or someone screwing up a film shoot.

“Bee nets on, everybody. Make them secure,” Bobby instructed as they all hopped out of the cab, already pulling his own over his head. “Buck, start running a hose line.”

“Class A, half-percent foam, coming up,” Buck recited

Class A foams were developed in the Eighties to originally fight wildfires. They lowered the surface tension of the water, helping with the saturation and allowing it to reach embers at depth. This aided in fire suppression and helped prevent reignition. Its use was eventually expanded to other class A, ordinary combustible fires, such as structure fires.

And in a pinch, it was also very useful to subdue swarming insects.

The droning of the bees was loud, when Buck dragged the hose within range. He could see them flying around the downed knight, as everyone else - faux courtiers and bee netted paramedics - gave them a wide berth.

“Alright, clear back, everybody!” Bobby called out, which wasn’t very necessary, before signaling for Buck to start spraying at low pressure. The droning quickly died down, and the captain motioned for him to stop. “Okay, guys, get in there.”

The paramedics themselves swarmed the victim, dropping to their knees by their side.

“Pulse is racing,” he heard Chimney say. “Got massive swelling.”

“The melittin from the venom probably caused her BP to bottom out,” Hen said.

“We need to push epi, but I can’t access her thigh or shoulder through all this armor,” Chimney said, grunting a little as he tried to pull off a bracer. “Come on.”

“Al!” Hen called out, motioning for the younger Han to come over. “Help me get this helmet off.”

“The swelling is keeping it in place,” Albert told her, tugging at the helmet before reaching into his bag. “Need to lube it up.”

“Inserting oral airway to keep her tongue and throat from swelling shut,” Hen said, explaining what she was doing while she did it. “It should hopefully buy us a little bit more time.”

“And what if it doesn’t?” a girl asked. She was about Chris’ age and dressed as a princess. Buck figured she was related to the vic, somehow.
