“Then your sister-” Ah, that made sense. “-is gonna need an intubation or a tracheotomy,” Hen explained to her while Al finally got the helmet off the knight, not that it seemed to calm the girl down any. Something Hen seemed to realize. “Cap?”

Bobby went over and gently tugged the girl away, kneeling down and talking to her in the calm and quiet way he was so good at.

“Do you think Chris would like this place?” Buck asked Eddie, who’d been hovering by his side, allowing the paramedics’ chatter to fall into background noise. “I think he’d like this place.”

“We’ll ask him when we get home, but for now-” Eddie raised the power tool he was holding, squeezing the trigger so it buzzed to life, “I have to go open a tin can.”

Buck huffed out a laugh and watched his husband step up to and kneel by the lady knight. The air was soon filled by the whirring of the saw and the screeching of metal as he cut through the armor. And then the whirring and the screeching was accompanied by very familiar droning.

“Cap!” Buck shouted, whirling around with the hose. He let loose with a short burst of foam, hitting the small cloud of incoming bees.

Why the hell did they hate this one girl so much?

“I got an opening,” he heard Chimney say. “Pushing epi.”

“There’s another swarm coming in,” Bobby told them, coming to stand anxiously beside Buck as yet more droning came towards them. “Hen, we’ve got to go.”

Buck turned his head when he heard the saw shut off, and caught Eddie pulling apart the breastplate. Hen reached down with a pair of forceps and plucked something off the girl’s chest, which she then immediately released into the air. As Buck watched, the tiny thing - the queen bee, he realized - flew off to join the incoming swarm, which changed directions almost immediately.

That explained things.

With the threat of death by killer bees no longer so imminent, they were free to focus on the victim. They got the stingers out of her, and the epi kicked in to take down some of the swelling. Enough so that Hen felt comfortable removing the oral airway, before they lifted her onto the gurney.

Albert stopped Buck and Eddie before they could climb into the engine, after rolling up and putting away the hose. Hen and Chimney were at the ambulance with the sisters.

“So uh, weird question,” he said, shifting nervously. “But do you think this would be a stupid place to take Ravi for a date?”

Buck and Eddie looked at each other, and then shrugged.

“Might want to bring an epi pen,” Eddie suggested, climbing into the engine cab. “In case there are any more bees.”

“Yeah, that kind of swelling is a real buzz kill.”

“Oh for fuck’s sake, Buck.”

The nanny- sorry, the household manager that Maddie hired to watch Jee-Yun hadn’t lasted past that first week. Apparently, Maddie didn’t like having someone come in and take over all the household chores while she got to hide in the bathtub with a glass of wine. Not that Buck was judging his sister or anything. Admittedly, the prospect of having a stranger just come in and take complete control of his home was a bit off putting. 

So maybe Maddie had a point there.

But firing the nanny/household manager meant that Maddie and Chimney were back to the “Friends and Family” babysitting plan. That was extremely inconvenient for them since half their friends and family worked the same hours, but like so many things, that was not Buck’s problem. It mostly just meant that the Lees had become the go-to babysitters since Chimney was moved back to A-shift, and occasionally Ravi.

Today, though, it was once again Buck and Eddie’s time to shine as uncles.

Maddie and Chimney arrived early in the day to drop her off, while Eddie was still out dropping Chris off at school. Hoover heard them coming before they got to the door, and ambled along with Buck to answer it.

“There’s my favorite girl!” he cried, sweeping Jee-Yun out of his sister’s arms.

“Buck!” she said and then when the dog barked his own greeting, “Hoof!”

“And we’re here, too,” Maddie said, sounding very unimpressed as she and Chimney came inside, both of them carrying bags. “She’d had breakfast and been changed, so you should be good until lunch.”

“We’ve got turkey sandwich squares, or organic chicken nuggets,” Chimney told him, moving into the dining room and setting his bag on the table. It was an insulated lunch bag that he unzipped to pull out pre-made meals. “If she goes for the nuggets, no sharing. You’re on your own.”

“He does remember that I can cook, right?” Buck asked his sister, setting Jee-Yun down on the floor. The toddler immediately latched herself onto Hoover. “Did you forget that I can cook? She isn’t going to starve.”

“She’s getting picky again,” Maddie explained, setting down the diaper bag she was carrying. “It’s just easier.”

Buck knew that, had noticed that at the last couple of Sunday dinners. But still. It was a point of pride, damn it! It probably would have stung less a couple weeks ago, but he was ignoring that. Like an adult.

“Excited to go house hunting?” he asked them.

“I think so,” she said. “Our apartment has so many memories and firsts…”

“We’re ready for seconds,” Chimney chimed in, coming back into the living room. “As in a second bedroom.”

“Sick of having the world’s most adorable roommate?” Buck reached knelt down so he was close to eye level with his niece, only to get a face full of dog tongue. “Okay, okay, you’re an adorable roommate, too.”

“The world’s most adorable roommate has been very active lately,” the paramedic said. He added in a quieter voice, like he was trying to keep Jee from hearing, “The terrible twos are no joke.”

“She’s not two for another few months.”

“Try telling her that,” Maddie countered.

“We’d better get moving,” Chimney interrupted, heading for the door. “We’ve got four drive-bys, three showings, two open houses, and hopefully a pear tree to fit our partridge in.”

Oh, there was so much hope in the man’s voice. He remembered when he had hope about house hunting. It was murdered by psychedelic flooring, and didn’t return until they found their current home.

“We actually have a pear tree in the front yard,” Buck pointed out. “If someone wearing an ugly brooch is the one showing you a house, don’t be surprised by the casual racism.”

“We’ll be sure to tell her you said hi,” Maddie said, heading out the door with Chimney. “Bye Jee!”

“Bye-bye!” Jee replied, waving.

“Good luck!” Buck called after them, just before the door closed. “Oh, princess, they’re gonna come back crying.”

“Is that mi princessa?” Abuela asked, shuffling into the living room. Jee-Yun lit up and ran up to hug her leg, Hoover following along.

“We have her for the day,” Buck told her. “Maddie and Chim are house hunting.”

“God help them,” the old woman muttered, and he barked out a laugh.

The day went very, very quickly. Buck sat Jee-Yun at the coffee table to color for a bit, but that quickly turned into a game of crayon throwing. By the time Eddie got home, Buck had just given up on picking the crayons back up, and instead was just leaving them on the floor until his niece got bored. Or ran out of crayons, either/or.

Honestly, though, Buck didn’t understand what Chimney and Maddie were complaining about. Sure, Jee was a rampaging little terror, but there was an easy solution to that: they stuck her outside with Hoover, and let them tire each other out. The dog needed the exercise, and it kept his niece from trailing toilet paper all through the house. Again.

They should totally do something nice for Bobby, for kidnapping the pooch.

She was taking a much deserved nap on the couch, completely wiped from a day of play, when her parents got back.

“Oh you are a miracle worker,” Maddie gushed, swooping in to check on her daughter. 

“Thank the dog,” Eddie told her. Said dog was snoozing at the foot of Abuela’s bed, last they checked. “So, how did it go?”

“Yeah, when’s the move-in date?” Buck asked, already knowing the answer.

“Never,” Chimney grumped, also checking on his daughter.

“Everything in our price range was either the size of our apartment, needed to be torn down, or both,” Maddie complained.

“There was that one house. It was already renovated.”

“You had to drive through a parking lot to get into the garage.” She let out a huff. “How the hell did you two get this house?”

“I sold my body to the US government for a few years,” Eddie said, and got blank stares in response. “Mortgage through the VA.”

“And Abuela sold her house,” Buck added. “So, what are you guys gonna do?”

“Buy lottery tickets,” was Chimney’s answer. “Oh, we saw Marcy. She says hello.”

“Did she really?”

“No, her face did this thing when we mentioned you both and then she said something about fruits,” Maddie said. “We weren’t sure if it was a homophobic thing, and we were too distracted by the flooring to really notice until after the fact.”

“She was a captive audience to Chris’ fruit tree facts,” Eddie told her. “Flooring?”

“It was linoleum,” Maddie explained. “It was peeling and had this god awful geometric pattern. And it was the color of-”

“Jee’s worst diapers?” Buck guessed.

“Yes!”

“That was the first house we looked at,” Eddie said. “It was so awful I forgot to be upset over all my dead army buddies.”

“Holy shit , babe.”

Chapter 24: Of Fittings and Other Things

Chapter Summary

In which the Diazes go suit shopping, and Albert has a couple questions.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

“Oh, I like this one,” Buck said, draping himself over Eddie’s shoulders as they stood in front of the mirror.

It had taken some effort on his behalf to convince Eddie to go suit shopping. The man just hadn’t really seen a need for it, considering Chris had plenty of button downs and slacks in his closet. Hell, the clothes they bought for the wedding last year still fit just fine. But the man was terminally weak to The Eyes, so he eventually faltered and gave in.

“I’m not the one who needs a suit,” Eddie insisted, rolling his eyes at their reflection despite the fact that he’d been quite happy to try a suit on for himself. “For a school party. Why are we getting him a suit for a school party?”

“Because he wants to look fancy, ” Buck answered, and couldn’t be more specific without breaking his promise. “And you never know when he’ll need a suit again. It’s an investment, babe.”

“An investment he’ll outgrow in a year. Why are you getting a new suit? What happened to the one you wore to the vow renewal?”

“I, uh. Tore the seams,” he admitted. “Turns out that probie Buck had smaller arms. And legs. And… Okay, it was basically a miracle that I didn’t have a wardrobe malfunction on the dance floor.”

“And I’m sure everyone was thankful for it.” Carla was smirking at them as she came up. She had leaped at the invite to come, telling them they’d need a woman’s opinion. “Well. Almost everyone.”

“I am the only person that gets to see his wardrobe malfunctions,” Eddie declared before peeling Buck off of him and moseying over to the nearby dressing room that Chris was in. He leaned in close to the curtains and asked, “How are we doing in there, pal?”

“Good,” was the bland answer.

“Do you need any help?”

“I got it!”

“Come on out and let us see it, Superman,” Buck said, going over to stand beside his husband. He reached over and parted the curtains a bit, giving Chris an easier time to come out and show off.

Eddie wolf whistled.

“Looking good,” he gushed, and Buck nodded in agreement. “Like Tia Pepa would say, te queda pintado.”

“Are you sure it looks okay? It isn’t too small?” Chris asked, ducking his head. “I think I look funny.”

“You look wonderful,” Carla assured him. “It fits you like a glove, any bigger and people might think you borrowed it from your father.”

“They say you should only be able to see a quarter inch of your shirt sleeve,” the boy recited, almost like he’d memorized a list of fashion tips. He raised his arms a bit, so they could see the ends of his sleeves better. “This isn’t too short?”

“Who’s they?” Eddie asked him.

“The internet.”

Chris’ answer had both Eddie and Carla giving Buck a look, as if it was entirely his fault. Their kid was fully capable of being drawn into research spirals on his own, thanks much.

“Well, any more suavecito,” Eddie said, turning back to their son, “and you’ll be giving James Bond a run for his money.”

“Okay,” Chris said, looking somewhat mollified. “Can I get a haircut this week?”

“Now a haircut?” Eddie shook his head in amusement. “You got some kind of hot date?.”

“No!” was the sudden and kinda loud answer. “Geeze.”

“Okay, okay,” Buck said, stepping in and rescuing the kid. “Why don’t you go back and change, so we can ring this up?”

Chris nodded frantically before ducking back into the changing room. Carla, being the wise and wonderful woman that she was, took the cue and grabbed Eddie by his sleeve, dragging him away to give the boy space.

“What was that all about?” Eddie asked, laughing.

“He’s excited about the school party, and he wants to look good,” Carla told him. “But I’m starting to think there might be a first crush situation going on here.”

“First crush?” Eddie wasn’t facing Buck’s way, which he was very thankful for, because he knew his face was not a subtle one. “That’s great, but why hasn’t he said anything to me about it?”

Carla, who was facing Buck’s way, smirked even harder. Because she was almost always wise, but wasn’t always wonderful.

“I- I mean, I never talked to my parents about any of my crushes,” he said. “Who wants to talk to their parents about things like that?”

“Please never compare your parents to us ever again,” Eddie told him, turning his way, “and I thought we were past the keeping secrets-” He stopped mid-sentence upon taking in Buck’s face, his whole expression going flat. “He told you about it.”

“C’mon, Eds,” Buck whined. “Don’t make me tell you. He made me pinky promise not to say anything.”

Actually, Buck was pretty sure that pinky promise was mostly about the kissing, but he wanted to stay on the safe side.

“Okay, mi sol,” Eddie sighed. “I’ll just be over here, totally ignorant of anything going on in my baby boy’s life.”

Buck wished he could be ignorant, but he knew better than to voice that thought aloud. That would be dangerously close to violating the sanctity of the pinky promise, and he did not want to bring that kind of karma down upon himself.

“How about, instead of focusing on that, we get you out of that suit-” Carla cleared her throat, interrupting him. “You get yourself out of that suit, and then you can help pick out one for me.”

“Fine,” Eddie huffed, heading for the dressing room that held his own clothes. “I don’t know why I needed to try one on, anyway.”

“Because I like seeing you dressed up,” Buck called after him, eyes fixed on a particular asset of his husband’s. He followed it up with a quieter, “I mean, I also like seeing you dressed down, but…”

“Boys,” Carla tutted, sounding fond. “And how long is it going to take you to pick one out?”

“As long as it takes,” he told her, sticking his nose in the air. “This is an investment, Carla.”

“Mhmm. Go grab Chris’ investment so we can take it to the counter.”

“Yes, ma’am.”

“Not that I mind helping out.” That was a lie: Albert minded. Albert minded a lot. “But you never said why I’m the one helping you.”

“Because Buck abandoned us in our time of need,” Howie told him. “Now lift with your knees.”

Albert rolled his eyes but did as his older brother bid, picking up the toddler sized bed on the man’s count of three. They carried it into the middle of what had once been the living room. Once upon a time, it had been filled with a couch and tables and seats. Now, there was a light pink dresser and a small bookcase for child-sized books, along with toy chests and toy sets and stuffed animals lining the walls.

“Perfect,” Howie declared, once they set the bed down. “It’s like her own little goshiwon.”

“How come you never did anything like this when I was crashing here?” Albert asked.

“Because I wanted you to leave.”

Rude and hurtful. Buck had been a much better roommate. At least until he moved in with Eddie, and then suddenly Albert was being told he had to find somewhere else to stay. Not that he got to linger on that problem for long, since the accident followed soon after.

“I think it’s adorable,” Maddie said, coming in with Jee-Yun. “But let’s ask the expert. Jee, what do you think?”

The little girl babbled as she went for the bed, wasting no time tucking in the bear she was carrying.

“It’s all yours, kid,” Howie told her.

“This is all new?” Maddie asked, looking around the place. “How many new toys did you buy her?”

A lot, Albert knew. He’d been the one to help carry them up.

“This is a big change for her,” his brother defended himself. “I thought we could smooth it over.”

“With bribery?” Maddie scoffed. “Why didn’t I think of that?”

“It works for Buck,” Albert pointed out. Because that was the only conceivable reason that Buck was beating him in their contest for their niece’s affections.  “Now, unless you need help moving anything else, I’m headed out. Actually, if you do need help, I’m still heading out.”

“Worst brother!” Howie called after him.

“Yeah, yeah.”

The apartment that Albert had ended up settling with unfortunately put him much farther from the station than he wanted to be. In fact, he was on the other side of everyone else from it. But the rent didn’t eat up an entire paycheck (only most of it) and it came with washer and dryer units. And! There was a pool.

Sure, it was kinda cramped and it wasn’t the best place to live, but his grandfather hadn’t left him multiple shares in Samsung. And he had next to no idea what Buck did that allowed him to afford that loft. He had theories, but at least one of them was countered by the way that Buck had no coordination whatsoever outside of life or death situations. Which meant a secret double life pole dancing was out. Probably.

Albert could have done what Ravi was doing, split the rent with a bunch of other people, but any desire to do that died the first time he went over to Ravi’s place. Less privacy than when he was sleeping on Buck or Howie’s couches, more people sharing the bathroom. No, just… No.

And apparently his boyfriend agreed, considering how often he came home to find him waiting on the couch. Like now.

“Why did I let you talk me into this academy job?” was how Ravi greeted him, head hanging off the arm of the couch.

“You didn’t,” Albert pointed out, slipping off his shoes and throwing them into the corner somewhere. “You let Eddie and Buck convince you in a single afternoon, after I spent days trying.”

“Well all three of you suck, Alley Cat,” his boyfriend whined.

Albert rolled his eyes and rounded the couch, lifting the other man’s feet up so he could sit down.

“The douchebag again?”

“The douchebag again,” Ravi confirmed. “I don’t know how he’s made it this long, I know the instructor’s complained about him. I think he’s someone’s kid.”

“Lovely,” he said, pressing his thumbs into the bottom of one of Ravi’s feet. The sound he got in response was not unfamiliar to him, though not in this context. “Feel good?”

“Real good,” his boyfriend responded with another delighted groan. “I knew I kept you for a reason.”

“Just for my hands?”

“And other things.”

“Uh-huh.” Albert rolled his eyes, but still grinned. “Hey, uh. We had a call the other day-”

“A bad call?” Ravi interrupted, lifting his head up to squint at him.

“No, no, it was fine,” he assured. “No, uh. It gave me an idea, though?”

“Yeah, sorry, kitten,” his boyfriend said, letting his head fall back. “I really don’t want to try anything that gets you guys called out. I don’t think I could look Cap in the eye ever again.”

“What? No! Not- Not that kind of call,” Albert said, shaking his head. “And not that kind of idea. What do you think about ren fairs?”

“I don’t,” Ravi said. “Why?”

“Oh.” Now he was feeling stupid. “Just. It kinda looked neat? I dunno, I just thought…”

“We’ll go this weekend,” his boyfriend said, interrupting him again. “Wait, you’re off this weekend, right?”

“Yeah, Saturday.”

“Then we’ll go on Saturday,” Ravi told him, and then hit him in the leg with his heel. “Why did you stop massaging my feet?”

“Sorry, Rav.”

“You’re forgiven as long as you don’t do it again.”

“I’m gonna have to stop eventually,” Albert pointed out, even as he dug his thumbs into the bottom of Ravi’s other foot. “Even if you spend the night, we both work tomorrow.”

“Ugh, I can’t spend the night, I didn’t think to stop by the apartment for clothes.” Ravi let out a mournful sigh. “I don’t want to go back.”

“What if you didn’t have to go back?” Albert offered, keeping his eyes fixed on what he was doing. “I- I mean, what if you just… Stayed here?”

“I still don’t have clothes for tomorrow,” Ravi said, and then suddenly he was yanking his feet out of Albert’s lap as he scrambled to sit up. “Wait, are you-?”

“Yes?” Albert said, chancing a look at the other man. “Maybe?”

“I thought…” Ravi shook his head, like he was shaking away the thought. “You never said anything while you were looking for an apartment.”

“I wasn’t sure if you’d want to,” Albert admitted. “I mean, we haven’t… It hasn’t even been a year, Rav. I wasn’t sure if it was too fast, y’know? But you’re here all the time anyways. I like it better when you’re here, and I know you hate the place you’re at now.”

“Then Saturday,” Ravi said, crowding into his space. “We’ll go to that ren fair together, and then afterwards, we start bringing my things over.”

“Your roommates won’t be upset?”

“Fuck them. They ate the leftovers I got from Bobby.”

“And tonight?” Albert asked.

Ravi’s answer was to grab his shirt and pull him even closer.

“Okay, what do you do if someone doesn’t like you?”

The question had Buck pausing in the middle of putting away the freshly dried dishes.

“Uh, agonize over it but act like I wasn’t,” he answered, adjusting the phone against his ear. “But everyone tells me I have a pathological need to have everyone I meet like me. I thought you were going to stop coming to us for advice?”

“I really should, because you’re never helpful,” May said. “There was this guy, a friend of a- Well, a roommate of a friend. And he just… Didn’t like me. The moment he saw me, he was all hostile. He even left the house just to get away from me.”

“His loss, then.”

“Buck!”

“Sorry, sorry. Okay.” He tapped his fingers on the counter, trying to think of actual, useful advice. “Okay. A stranger’s opinions and attitude have no actual bearing on you. Whatever this guy’s problem is, it is not reflective of you in any way.”

“How often has Copeland told you that?”

“Almost every time my parents come up,” he admitted. “But it’s good advice! Maybe the guy just doesn’t like women.”

“Maybe,” May said. “He gave me the creeps, too.”

“Well then… Let’s make fun of him.”

“Seriously?” She did not sound impressed.

“It’ll make you feel better,” he said. “Taylor does it all the time.”

“Because what I want in life is to be like Taylor Kelly.”

“Taylor Kelly doesn’t let it bother her when people just hate her for no reason.”

“Because everyone has a reason for hating Taylor Kelly,” Eddie chimed in as he entered the kitchen. “What’s up?”

“Someone doesn’t like May,” Buck told him, putting his phone on speaker and setting it on the counter.

“His loss,” Eddie said with a shrug.

“Oh, you are both so helpful.”

“C’mon, make fun of him,” Buck urged. “It’ll make you feel better.”

There was a sigh on the other end of the line, followed by,

“He had this stupid mustache…”

Chapter End Notes

Fun fact: I don't actually remember why I had Albert and Ravi get together? But I did, and they are.

Chapter 25: It's Raining Men

Chapter Summary

In which Bobby is very, very unhappy with a couple's fantasy.

“So who’s this mystery crush of Christopher’s?” Hen asked as they exited the building.

They were finishing up at the sight of a minor - very, very minor - warehouse fire. They hadn’t even needed the hoses, just poke some holes and spray with the CO2. Eddie had been complaining about Chris’ newest stage of growing up off and on all day. Mostly to Bobby, who was eternally amused, but the others had been around to hear it, too.

“No idea. He’s not spilling.” Eddie slanted a look at Buck, who glanced away. “To me, anyway. And I don’t feel like pushing.”

“Isn’t he kind of young to start dating?” Albert asked.

“It’s not like he’s taking ‘em to a dinner and movie,” Eddie pointed out. “He’s just going to a school party.”

“There is nothing wrong with a little harmless adolescent infatuation,” Bobby added.

“How old were you when you had your first crush, Buckaroo?” Chimney asked.

“Uh.” Buck thought about it for a moment as he put the extinguisher away. He remembered telling Ravi and Albert about his first real crush, Shelby, when he was ten. But before her… “Kindergarten. Miss Garcia, RIP.”

That had mostly just been puppy love, but still. It counted, right?

(Older than him, Latina, dimples when she smiled, good with children. Maybe his crush on Miss Garcia left more of a mark than he thought.)

He jumped up a little to grab the compartment door and slam it closed, before climbing into the engine cab. Once again, Albert was sitting in the middle, Eddie and Buck sitting facing each other, with their legs tangled together.

They pulled on their headsets as Jones pulled the engine out of the parking lot, getting them on the road back to the station.

“Hen,” Chimney said, on the other side of Albert, “tell us about your first kiss.”

“Ricardo Sanchez,” Hen said, grinning. “Sixth grade, someone dared us to make out under the bleachers.”

“Salacious.”

“I was not aware there was gonna be tongue involved, so essentially he licked my lips for like. Five seconds,” she said. “And then I ran away.”

“That sounds traumatizing,” Buck laughed.

“Illuminating,” Hen corrected. “I realized I was kissing the wrong Suarez. His sister Martina was more my speed.”

“Ah, right.”

“We’ve drifted off topic,” Eddie said. “Christopher isn’t kissing anyone.”

Buck stared intently out the window, so he didn’t accidentally catch his husband’s gaze. That tree over there was very… Tree. The most tree-like tree he’d ever seen. And oh, that cloud…

“As far as you know,” Albert oh-so-helpfully supplied.

“He’s not,” Eddie insisted. “Right? Buck?”

Was that cloud shaped like a dog or-?

“Buck!”

“He made me pinky promise,” he whined, finally turning his head to look at his husband. The others started cackling; even Bobby and Jones chuckled a bit. “Don’t make me break the pinky promise, babe.”

“He-” Eddie was facing him, but his eyes weren’t seeing anything. “My baby boy.”

“Is growing up,” Bobby said from the front. Buck couldn’t see him, but he just knew the captain was grinning.

“At least he’s talking to one of you about it,” Hen said, trying her best to sound reassuring despite the fact that she was still laughing. 

“I wish he kept it to himself,” Buck grumbled.

“Now you know how we feel,” Chimney was apparently compelled to add.

“I know it’s normal,” Eddie said, finally coming back from that far away place. “I just didn’t think Christopher was old enough yet.”

“Maybe the problem is it makes you feel old,” Bobby suggested.

“Wait ‘til he starts shaving,” Chimney taunted, leaning forward some so he could smirk at them from around Albert. 

“I hate you both,” Eddie said.

“Oh! Ravi’s moving in with me,” Albert announced, as the radio up front came to life.

“Dispatch to One Eighteen, reports of a fire at DuPont RV Storage.”

“Copy that, Dispatch,” Bobby replied. “One Eighteen responding.”

The engine’s sirens wailed to life.

“That’s great, man,” Eddie said, tapping Albert’s knee with his fist. “Finally got tired of the roommates?”

“That and he’s over all the time, anyway.”

“I think that was the same logic Ed-” Buck was interrupted by an odd thump. It sounded like it came from above. “What was that?”

There was another succession of softer thumps, like something rolling, that had them all sharing confused looks.

“You guys secured all the gear and locked the compartment doors?” Bobby asked.

There was a chorus of yeses, even as the thumping continued. It didn’t sound like it was coming from any of the compartments, or Buck didn’t think so, at least. Unless there was a compartment right above the cab that he was unaware of.

“You better take Wilcox,” the captain told Jones. “There’s construction up ahead.”

Jones didn’t give an affirmative to the directions. He rarely ever did. Instead, he just took the next turn, jolting the engine a little.

And then Bobby was ordering Jones to pull over.

“What’s going on?” Buck asked, yanking off his headset and undoing his straps to hop out.

“There’s someone in the road,” was the captain’s answer.

And there was, indeed, someone in the middle of the road. He was impossible to miss, when they all hopped out of the cab to investigate. So impossible to miss, Buck wondered how Jones either didn’t see him or didn’t run him over. 

The paramedics snagged their med kits and rushed to the man. The shoulder of his shirt was torn open, and the exposed skin was one big bloody scrape. There were smaller scrapes and cuts elsewhere.

“Looks like we’ve got second-degree abrasion,” Chimney reported, looking him over. “Separated shoulder.”

“Irregular pupillary response,” Hen added, checking the guy’s eyes with a penlight. “Probable concussion.”

“Let’s get him- Thanks, Al.” Chimney took the C-collar his brother was handing to him. “Start irrigating these wounds.”

“Got it,” Al replied, grabbing the bottle of saline from his kit.

“Dispatch, this is Captain Nash, One Eighteen,” Bobby relayed into his radio as he watched them. “We’ve encountered a trauma victim in transit. Looks like we won’t be able to make the RV Storage fire.”

“There aren’t any tall buildings around here,” Eddie commented, looking around.

“And unless that song was based in truth,” Buck said, trying to figure out how a man just dropped into the middle of the road, “I don’t think it’s ever actually raining men.”

He smiled at the unimpressed look his husband shot him, before the other man shook his head and turned away.

And then froze.

“I don’t think he fell from the sky.”

Buck glanced at him and then followed his line of sight to the engine. Huh. That would explain the odd thumping noises they were hearing before.

Together, they went back to the engine and climbed up the side to peer over the roof. There, waiting for them, was not quite what Buck typically saw on top of a fire engine. It was a woman, bleeding from a wound on her forehead and wearing a dress that seemed to have been in the process of coming off.

“Uh,” Buck said.

“Huh,” Eddie said.

“Is he going to be okay?” the woman asked.

They stared at her. They stared at each other. They turned around to stare at Bobby.

A very, very unhappy looking Bobby.

“What the hell were you two thinking, hitching a ride up there?” Bobby demanded, once they’d gotten her down. A pair of ambulances had arrived for both her and her fallen lover, and now she was sitting in the back of one while Eddie tended to that cut on her head and the captain scolded her.

“I don’t know,” was her meek response.

“You don’t know,” the captain repeated, and Buck unconsciously shifted a bit away from him. He recognized that tone of voice. He’d been on the receiving end of that tone of voice. “What do you mean you don’t know?”

“It was just something we always fantasized about,” she said. “Having sex on top of a firetruck.”

“Like Backdraft!” Buck loved that movie. But sadly, the woman apparently didn’t; all he got was confusion.

Eddie glanced up from his work to smile at him and shake his head.

“Backdraft was a seminal influence in Buck’s life,” Chimney popped up to chime in. Bobby turned his glare upon him. “Sorry, poor choice of words.”

“This has to be the dumbest, most dangerous, and- And dumbest fantasy I’ve ever heard of,” the captain said. Buck was nearly tempted to point out that he said “dumbest” twice, but he promised Eddie he wouldn’t throw himself into danger like that anymore. “And it’s not a truck. It’s an engine.”

With that, the captain stomped away, glaring at everyone unfortunate enough to be in his path.

The station was still, this late at night. Everyone else was in the bunks, catching what sleep they could before they were called out for a late night emergency. Not Buck, though. Nor Eddie. They were up in the den of the loft, lounging on the couches. Mostly because Buck couldn’t sleep, and Eddie hadn’t wanted to leave him.

Buck was flipping through the channels, kinda hoping to find Backdraft playing somewhere, but knew that it was a fleeting dream. But speaking of the movie that had positively influenced his life choices…

“So,” he said, snaking out an arm to wrap around Eddie’s shoulders. “Everyone’s asleep-”

“No,” his husband immediately said, not even looking up from the book he was reading.

“You don’t even know what I was gonna say.”

“I don’t need to,” Eddie told him. “I just know that nothing good could ever follow you pointing out that everyone’s asleep.”

“I am hurt,” Buck said, throwing himself back against the arm of the couch, clutching at his chest. “Devastated. Betrayed by the love of my life…”

“Uh-huh, okay, play it up.” Eddie marked his spot and set his book down on the coffee table. “What were you going to say, then?”

“Well, y’know.” He sat up, smirking at him. It got him an eye roll in response. “Talking about Backdraft got me thinking…”

“Yeah, a definite no, mi sol,” Eddie said, reaching out to poke at his forehead. “We’re not having sex on or in or even near the engine. Or the ladder truck. Cap would murder us.”

“Only if he found out.”

“See, we’ve had this argument before. You said they would never find out. That it would be okay,” his husband pointed out. “We ended up with a cop knocking on the jeep window, who then went and called Athena.”

Buck groaned before reaching up and grabbing Eddie’s arm, tugging him closer. His husband laughed as he let himself fall sideways.

“I guess you’re right-”

“Guess?”

“The alarm would probably go off anyway,” Buck continued, wrapping his arms around the other man. “Probably just as we were getting to the good part.”

“If anything could get me to believe in jinxes and curses,” Eddie said, “it’s your luck.”

“My luck can’t be too bad,” he replied, running his fingers through Eddie’s hair. “I mean, I have you.”

The sound of Hen cooing had them both sitting up to blink at her over the back of the couch. She was standing in the kitchen, hands poised to open one of the cabinets.

“What are you doing awake?” Eddie asked her.

“Late night snack,” she explained, pulling out a large bowl before moving over to the pantry. “What are you doing awake?”

“Couldn’t sleep,” Buck told her.

The paramedic nodded. There wasn’t much noise, aside from the late night traffic of the nearby freeway; the television with its volume turned down; the sound of pretzels hitting the bowl as Hen poured them out. Neither he nor Eddie moved to break the near silence, even though Buck felt the urge to.

Hen did it for him.

“I have been an awful friend,” she said, carrying the bowl over to them. She set it down on the coffee table beside Eddie’s book, and took a seat on the nearest comfy chair. “To both of you, but mostly to you, Buck.”

“Hen,” Buck said as Eddie quietly observed “you’ve already apologized.”

“For rushing to conclusions, for not keeping drama out of work,” she said. “Not for being an awful friend. You were right, when you came and yelled at us. I should have known better. I should have known you better. You haven’t been that person since you were a probie. Hell, you weren’t that person then. Enjoying sex, having lots of sex, it doesn’t mean you’re going to cheat on your partner.”

“You didn’t believe me then, either.”

“The catfishing?” she asked, and Buck nodded. “Considering what I had done just before, I think… I think it was easier to believe the worst of everyone else, too.”

“And now?”

“I was just an awful friend,” Hen said. “And I am really, truly sorry about that. Because after five and a half years, after everything you’ve done for me and Karen, you deserve better.”

Buck let out a sigh and met Eddie’s gaze beside him. His husband shrugged.

Screw it.

“You’re forgiven,” Buck told her, and watched her relax in her seat.

“Thank you,” she said. “But uh, to go along with it… What would you say if I invited the two of you and Chris to the renaissance fair on Saturday? My treat?”

It would have to be after Chris’ equine therapy, and Eddie’s meeting with the VA group, but…

Buck and Eddie looked at each other again, before they both shrugged.

“Sure,” Eddie said. “Your treat.”

“So the guy May was telling me about the other day,” Buck said, tossing the squeaky hedgehog across the yard and watching Hoover chase after it. His phone was sitting on the patio table, set to speaker. “He was some sort of creepy incel, war on men, dude?”

“Seems like it,” Bobby told him. “May did a good job, keeping her cool and keeping everything from blowing up.”

Evidently, the day earlier, May had accidentally stumbled onto Incel Creep’s plan to do… Something, Buck wasn’t really clear on the details there. She found a book with detailed plans and immediately took it to her mother, but Incel Creep threw a (somewhat understandable) fit when he realized it was gone. A hostage situation ensued, but May and Athena worked together to keep it from getting any uglier.

“Of course she did,” Buck agreed, giving Hoover all the pats and pets he deserved when he brought the toy back. “Good boy!”

“Uh. Thank you?”

“Talking to Hoover, Pops,” Buck assured him, taking the hedgehog and tossing it again. “Now, this Darius, the guy Incel Creep-”

“We are not calling him that.”

“The guy Mr. Well-Adjusted took hostage. He’s the kid she took to prom with her, right? Her senior year?”

“That’s right,” Bobby confirmed. “They broke up when he left for college, but now he’s back in town.”

“Uh-huh. Interesting. And you and Ma are inviting him over for dinner?”

“Yes- Buck, no.”

“I promise to be on my best behavior,” Buck assured him, before ending the call with a swipe. He picked up the hedgehog toy when Hoover brought it back over, leading him inside. “Eddie! Get the ax! We’re going to Ma and Pops’ for dinner!”

Chapter 26: Little Terrors

Chapter Summary

In which Buck enjoys being an older brother, and then has to suffer as an adult.

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Fifteen minutes after hanging up, Buck was cheerfully knocking on the Grant-Nashes’ front door, Eddie and Chris standing behind him. Abuela had been invited to come with, but she turned it down to invite Pepa over, instead. Something about not wanting to be involved with whatever stupidity they were cooking up. Which was both insulting and fair.

“So this is why Bobby got out more food,” Athena said, when she opened the door.

“Pops mentioned dinner and I just couldn’t help myself,” Buck said, grinning at her as he slid past her.

“Uh-huh, right. And you, Eddie?”

“I go where he goes.”

“Wow.” Athena did not sound impressed as she closed the door behind them. “That won’t ever end poorly for you at all.”

“I just wanted Grandpops’ food,” Chris commented, following everyone else down the stairs. “Pops wanted to bring an ax.”

“Bring a- What?”

Buck scurried down the steps faster as he felt the heat of Athena’s stare on the back of his neck. He practically fled through the living room, aiming for the perceived safety of the kitchen, where he could hear Bobby moving around. Not that he thought the man would actually protect him for the sergeant, but still.

“So, when’s May supposed to be getting here?” he asked, stepping beside him to look at what he was making.

“A little while, yet,” Bobby said. The captain fixed him with a look. “I told you no.”

“And yet, Ma said you got more food out for us.”

“You will be on your best behavior.”

“When am I ever not?” Buck asked, grinning innocently at him.

“Do you really want us to answer that?” Athena asked, joining them in the kitchen. “Because we can. You won’t like it, but we can. One of you grab me a glass.”

“Wine?” Buck asked her, moving around Bobby to get to the right cabinet.

“Only if you want your son to be drinking it,” she said, opening the fridge and taking out the jug of orange juice she kept in it. Mainly for the occasional mimosa. “He said he was thirsty, and as he is the only one of you three with any sense…”

“Hey, I asked Eddie to bring an ax, but he turned me down,” Buck said, instinctively defending his husband as he handed Athena the glass. And then he realized what he said and went back to doing his best ‘aren’t I cute?’ impression.

“Why?” the sergeant asked. “Just. Why?”

“It’s apparently what they did with Albert and Ravi,” Bobby explained, sounding very put upon. “Buck threatened and Eddie menaced.”

“They are quite the double act,” Athena said, shaking her head. “I own a gun, Buckaroo. I am more than capable of giving the shovel talk to my little girl’s boyfriends.”

“Oh! Do you think Eddie could borrow it?” Buck asked. “He can clean it while we introduce ourselves to Darius.”

The woman gave him a flat look before turning and exiting the kitchen with the glass of juice. From the other room, Buck could hear her declare,

“Your husband is an idiot.”

“Pops,” Buck whined. “Ma’s being mean to me.”

“Cut these for me,” Bobby said, pointing at a stack of veggies, without an ounce of sympathy in his voice.

May didn’t show up with Darius until just before the food was done cooking. Her arrival was announced with the sound of the front door opening, followed by her shouting,

“Is that Buck’s jeep out front?”

Buck, of course, immediately abandoned Bobby to hurry into the living room. Eddie, seeing where this was heading, faithfully pulled himself off the couch with a groan to stand beside him.

“Hi, May,” Buck said, smiling as innocently as possible when they made it to the bottom of the stairs.

“Hi, Buck.” She was very obviously suspicious about whatever this was about. “And Eddie. And Chris! I didn’t know you three were coming today.”

“They invited themselves,” Athena told her. She hadn’t gotten up, apparently quite content simply watching the trainwreck from a place of comfort.

“And you’re Darius, right?” Buck cut to the chase, smiling as innocently and welcoming as possible. He held his hand out for the younger man.

“Oh, no,” May whispered, shaking her head. “No, no, we are not doing this.”

“Yeah, and you’re…” Darius trailed off, obviously trying to think of just who the large white man in front of him was and what he was doing there. And also, probably, why he was trying to break his hand. “You were at that graduation party.”

“He’s Bobby’s,” May said, though Buck didn’t think it helped Darius’ confusion any. “You know what, why don’t Darius and I go sit outside until dinner’s done? You all can just… Keep talking about whatever it was you were talking about before we got here.”

“What a wonderful idea!” Buck said. “We’ll sit out there with you.”

“On second thought.” May grabbed Darius’ arm and tried leading him back to the stairs, a task made difficult by the fact that Buck still had a grip on his hand. “We can go get take-out. In-N-Out sound good to you, Darius?”

“Just let it happen, May,” Eddie sighed. “He’ll just ambush the both of you elsewhere.”

“Rude,” Buck complained, even if it was true. He finally let go of Darius’ hand, who shook it to get the feeling back into his fingers.

“What’s happening?” the young man asked, whispering the question to May.

“Insanity,” she whispered back. “I am so sorry. I wish I could say he wasn’t normally like this.”

“You love me,” Buck said, because he wasn’t deaf and they were right there in front of him. He clapped his hands together. “So. Darius. What’s your intentions with my baby sister?”

Darius sputtered for an answer while May stomped into the kitchen and shouted for Bobby to,

“Control your son!”

Buck had never been to a ren faire before, but he was enjoying it so far. The three of them - Buck and Eddie and Chris - met Hen and Denny at the entrance shortly before noon, after everyone’s therapy related activities were done for the day. Karen wasn’t there, but Buck wasn’t too terribly surprised by that. It had only been just over a month since the lab explosion, after all, so she probably still wasn’t feeling up to meandering around for hours at a time.

There were just as many people dressed up as there was when they arrived earlier in the week, for the bee attack call. Buck wasn’t sure how he felt about dressing up in renaissance garb, but he was tempted to come back with Jee-Yun just to have an excuse to dress her up as an actual princess.

“You know, I thought this place was gonna be lame,” Denny commented as they walked through the place. “But it’s actually kind of cool?”

“Since when do I take you to lame places?” Hen demanded.

“Dentist,” her son replied, “the doctor. School.”

“You are an ungrateful child.”

“Can I get a turkey leg?” Denny asked, entirely unbothered with the appellation.

“Oh, can I?” Chris asked as well. Buck nodded and started to pull out his wallet, but Hen waved him off.

“Get five,” she said, handing Denny money. “Bring three back.”

The two boys let out quiet cheers before rushing for the nearest food booth.

“You didn’t have to do that,” Eddie said.

“I told you,” she said. “Today is my treat.”

“Well, thank you,” Buck told her, and was then distracted because, “Look, swords!”

He bounded for the small table that was selling them. There were tiny swords and tiny shields, along with child-sized pieces of armor. Eddie and Hen followed at a more sedate pace, shaking their heads at his perfectly reasonable excitement.

“What do you think?” he asked them, picking up one of the little plastic weapons and giving it a swing.

“Little small for you,” Hen answered.

“It’s for Jee-Yun.”

“Then I think Maddie’ll kill you,” Eddie said, reaching over to pick up one of the shields. “And that we should definitely get her one of these, too.”

“If we get Hoover a little saddle, do you think we could train him to be her little steed?”

“Is she your princess or a knight?” Hen asked, obviously trying to hold in laughter.

“Clearly,” Buck said, poking her with the tip of the plastic sword, “she is a princess and a knight.”

“Oh, obviously, my bad.” She reached over and grabbed one of the plastic helmets. “Then she definitely needs armor if she’s going to be the next Warrior Princess, right?”

“We are not getting her a frisbee of death,” Eddie said. “Hold on, though. Do we get her a helmet or a crown?”

Eddie and Buck looked at each other, and then they both nodded.

“Both,” Eddie said.

“Both,” Buck agreed.

“Both is good.”

“Do I get a sword?” Chris asked when he and Denny rejoined them. The other boy was carrying a small pile of drumsticks, which his mother helped distribute to everyone. 

“Only if I get one, too,” Buck countered as a joke. The kid had the audacity to scrunch his face up at that.

“Never mind,” he said. “You’d break something if you got a sword.”

Buck scowled down at him, and then glared at Hen and Eddie when they didn’t even bother trying to smother their laughter. He grabbed a pair of swords and a pair of shields, before stomping off to find the person he needed to pay.

And then froze in his steps.

There, at the other end of the lane, were Ravi and Albert. They were laughing as they poked around the goods at one of the stalls. Laughing and very clearly on a date, with their hands tucked into each other’s back pockets and generally hanging off each other. It was sweet and sappy, but absolutely caused a problem. Because he highly doubted the two of them wanted their date to be crashed by their coworkers.

They definitely wouldn’t want their date to be crashed by their coworkers’ children.

“How about,” he said, setting the swords and shields back down, and then set to work trying to herd everyone in the opposite direction, “we wait until we see if there’s uh. Better weapons? Yeah. Over there.”

“What? Weren't you-” Eddie must have finally noticed the two men over Buck’s shoulder, because his eyes went wide and he nodded. “Oh. Yeah, okay. We’ll do a circuit of the place.”

The two boys shrugged, going along with it easy enough. Hen looked at them funny, until Buck discreetly - or as discreet as he ever managed to be - pointed Ravi and Albert out. Then she nodded in understanding.

They could tease Albert about it tomorrow, and Ravi the next time they saw him.

“How long are you planning on standing guard out here?” Carla asked.

She and Eddie were standing in the school parking lot, watching Chris chat with some of his friends. His kid was definitely the sharpest dressed among them, looking good in his new suit and his curls freshly trimmed. And he was enjoying himself, laughing at something that one of the other kids said.

The entrance to the school was decked out for the party. Balloons formed an arch over the doors, which had handmade posters inviting people to come in and “dance the night away.” Eddie could recognize his husband’s sloppy handwriting on one of them.

“Just until they get inside,” Eddie answered her. “And then we can hang out in the car after.”

“It’s a chaperoned school function,” Carla laughed. “Buck is just inside. Chris isn’t shipping out for a year-long tour.”

“If Buck is one of the chaperones,” he said, not taking his eyes off Chris and his friends, “how chaperoned can it really be?”

“I’m telling him you said that.”

They both laughed, but Eddie sobered up quickly.

“When you know you’re having kids, you have all these expectations of what life’s gonna be like, you know?” he said. “The milestones. Their first words, first steps-”

“First crush,” Carla helpfully added.

Which of the kids with Chris was his crush? he wondered. Or was his crush inside, waiting for him?

“Then they tell you your kid’s gonna be different, that he’s gonna have challenges that other kids don’t. They warn you to manage expectations.” The words tasted like bile on his tongue. “And all those milestones start looking like a stupid fantasy.”

“Looks like that fantasy’s turning into reality,” Carla told him, as they watched Chris and his friends start heading inside. His friends walked beside him, patiently keeping pace with him, and Eddie could cry just from watching it. Buck, standing at the door to hold it open, reached out to brush his hand through Chris’ hair as he went by and the boy ducked his head to get away. “How’s that feel?”

“Pretty damn good,” Eddie admitted.

“Well, the school said this was going to last three hours,” Carla told him, pulling her phone out of her purse.

“Are you calling for a ride home?” he asked, watching her fingers play across her phone screen. “Cause-”

“And miss this milestone? No,” she scoffed. “I’m ordering takeout. How do you feel about dim sum?”

“I think it sounds delicious,” he said, pulling out his keys and hitting the button on his fob. The truck beeped behind him, and he heard the doors unlock. “And that Buck’ll be upset if he finds out we ate without him.”

“Don’t worry your pretty little head,” Carla said. “I’ll order enough for him, too.”

They hung out in the truck, waiting. The delivery guy, when he showed up with their food some twenty minutes later, was very obviously confused as to what he was doing there, of all places. After briefly looking around for where he was supposed to take it, he started heading for the school entrance before Eddie flashed his lights and waved him over.

Ten minutes after that, Buck came jogging out.

“The next time I get volunteered to do something like this,” he said when he got to Eddie’s window, “I’m going to break my leg again to get out of it.”

“That bad?” Eddie asked him.

“So bad- Did you guys get food without me?” He pouted, until Carla pulled out the extra container of food, which he happily took from her. “Love you, Carla! Ooo, dim sum.”

“You’re welcome, Buckaroo.”

“How bad can it be?” Eddie asked. “You just have to, what? Watch a bunch of kids and their bad dancing?”

“Oh, it’s so much worse than that,” his husband said, between bites of dumpling. “These are pre-teens, babe. I keep having to tell couples to leave room for Jesus, but Eddie? There is no Jesus in there.”

“They’re kids,” he repeated.

“They’re little terrors,” Buck retorted.

“Well, how’s Chris?”

“Uh.” Buck leaned back, glancing at the school doors like he was afraid their kid was liable to pop out and catch him gossiping. “Kid’s got game.”

“What.” Eddie blinked at him. Carla started laughing in the passenger seat. “What does that mean?”

“I mean our kid has game, Eds,” Buck said. “He obviously gets it from me.”

“That’s bullshit, I kissed you first.” For the briefest of moments, Eddie had the burning urge to storm in there and sweep his baby boy up. To take him far, far away from any and all corrupting influences, including time itself. But he resisted that urge, crushed it down and sealed it over like a pothole. “Please tell me he-”

“Oh, no, he’s fine,” Buck assured him. “Flailing more than dancing, but he’s got her laughing, so.”

“Well, good for him,” Carla said, still laughing.

“Yup.” He closed the container and passed it back, wiping his mouth. “And now I have to go back inside.”

Eddie leaned up to kiss him, thumb rubbing against his cheek.

“Good luck,” he said.

“I’m gonna need it,” Buck replied. Then he let out a sigh, adjusted his shirt, and walked back into the school like a man marching to his own execution.

“He is such a drama queen,” Carla said.

“He is,” Eddie agreed, and couldn't help the way his voice betrayed how in love with the man he was.

Ten minutes later, his phone chimed repeatedly with texts from Buck.

Terrors!!! 🤬

Some1 spiked the punch

Broke up a fight

No Jesus!!!!!

On second thought, maybe Chris was too young for any and all school functions after this.

Chapter End Notes

My mother's been a middle school teacher for more than a decade and a half now. The school dances are Godless events.

Chapter 27: The Devil Winds

Chapter Summary

In which people start losing their minds because of the weather.

The Santa Ana winds came down from the Great Basin just after Thanksgiving. Strong and hot and dry, they hit Southern California without mercy, taking down tree limbs and power lines alike. The winds brought with them the same sort of insanity that was typically found on the worst Full Moon shifts, but drawn out. More drunks, more disorderlies, more discord.

On Monday evening, the winds brought a severed hand. Well, a dog got out and he brought the severed hand to his owners, but that was semantics.

“Every year, I think, that’s it. We’ve seen everything now. And then the Santa Anas blow in,” Buck complained as he and Bobby and Eddie walked up to the house that called 9-1-1. Said winds were blowing strong even now. “And it’s like, oh look! Something weirder.”

“It’s a severed hand,” Eddie retorted. “We’ve seen those before.”

“Yeah, but usually in the general vicinity of its owner.”

“Hen, Chim, what do we think?” Bobby asked when they reached the two paramedics. A man and woman, the owners of the dog, hovered just a couple feet away. Albert wasn’t there because, much like on the full moon, sometimes they had to divide and conquer when the winds were strong. So he was out on another, undoubtedly just as weird, call. Probably another parrot stabbing.

(Buck had been very disappointed when he learned that someone had been stabbed with a parrot statue, and not - as he originally thought upon first hearing about it from his sister - stabbed by a bird.)

“It doesn’t look like it was cut off,” Hen told them.

“Wound’s pretty jagged,” Chimney commented, holding up the hand as he and Hen looked at it. It was in a ziplock baggie. “Maybe blown off?”

“The fireworks,” the woman said. “Before my husband got home, someone was setting off fireworks.”

“I’m not hearing fireworks now,” Eddie said. Which was a good thing: the Diazes didn’t particularly do well with fireworks.

“You know which house?” Bobby asked her.

“I think that way,” she said, pointing.

“Okay, everybody,” the captain said, turning to face them. Buck had a feeling he would not like where this was going. “Time to go door-to-door.” Fuck. He knew it. “We need to find the person who belongs to this hand.”

The night had just barely started and Buck was already feeling exhausted. He shared a commiserating look with Eddie, who seemed to be in the same boat as him, before they both headed for the first houses. On the bright side, if there was one thing they could depend on, it was people being drawn to gawk whenever an emergency vehicle arrived on their street with lights and sirens going. So he didn’t have to wait long between knocking and being answered.

Not that he knew why they were bothering to knock. He kinda figured that if someone was missing a hand, they’d have already called them for help. And if they hadn’t, then it was because they were unable or unwilling, and so probably wouldn’t answer.

Mostly, it became a rota of asking the same questions again and again. Was anyone in the house injured? Had they seen or heard anything suspicious? Were they the ones setting off the fireworks, and if not, did they know who it was? Did they need a hand? Buck couldn’t help himself with that last question, and was not taking criticisms at this time.

They met back up at the trucks, each of them empty handed.

“Any luck?” Bobby asked.

“No.” Buck shook his head, hunching up his shoulders against the wind, hands tucked into his pockets. “Searched the whole block. But it could’ve come from anywhere, right? Dog could’ve picked it up farther away.”

“Excuse me.” The voice belonged to a young man, anxious and sweaty looking, with one of his hands tucked out of sight. “Can I give you guys a hand with anything?”

Buck made a valiant effort to cover up the snort of laughter.

“Thanks,” Eddie said, shooting Buck a warning look. “We’ve got all the help we need.”

“Oh, okay,” the guy said, nodding. “Cool.”

They were barely turned back around before the guy was literally running off. With the cooler that held the severed hand.

Buck looked at Eddie; Eddie looked at Buck.

“Not it,” Buck declared, and his husband rolled his eyes with a groan before taking off at a sprint. The rest of them followed at a more sedate pace, Buck pulling on a pair of medical gloves as he went. “I wonder why he pulled the five-fingered discount?”

“Five fingered- Buck.” Hen shook her head at him. “I expect that kind of humor from Chimney.”

“Thank you, Hen,” Chimney said, not looking the least bit offended. “I am hands down the funniest person here.”

“That one was a bit heavy-handed,” Bobby said.

“These puns are getting out of hand,” Hen told them, shaking her head again.

“Two hands, nine fingers!” Eddie shouted to them. The guy who’d taken off with the cooler was laying on his back, having tripped and fallen while he was running. “Who’s missing the hand?”

“My buddy, he’s in the car.” The guy was more of a kid, really, now that Buck got a closer look at him. “You’ve gotta help him, I was gonna take him to the hospital.”

Chimney and Hen made for the car he gestured at. Bobby grabbed his radio, asking for another ambulance and reporting the “traumatic amputations.”

“Do you know where your finger is?” Eddie asked, looking at the kid’s hand. A bloody stump was in the place of his left pointer.

“No, I didn’t try looking for it,” the kid told him. “I was too worried about my friend, Manolo.”

“How did you both manage to lose appendages?” Bobby demanded.

“Fireworks fight.”

And there were people that called Buck stupid.

“Why didn’t you call Nine One One?” Buck asked him.

“My mom’s on the neighborhood council,” the kid said. “She’s responsible for cracking down on illegal fireworks.”

“Awkward,” Eddie muttered.

“What were you thinking of doing with the hand?” Bobby demanded. The poor kid didn’t seem to have an answer, probably not used to thinking about things beyond the next immediate step. 

Buck was definitely going to save this story for the next time someone accused him of not thinking things through. Because this? This was not thinking things through.

“Got the finger!” Chimney called from the car, and Buck hurried over to take it from him. He didn’t look at it too closely, because what were the chances of it not belonging to the person missing a finger?

“How are you gonna explain this to your mom?” Eddie was asking when he got back, because the kid was stupid and they all wanted to bully him for it, apparently.

“Um.” The kid floundered for a moment. “I don’t know.”

“Okay,” Buck said, kneeling down and reaching for the kid’s hand, ready to match finger to hand. Except, he noticed a small problem when he actually looked at what he was holding, because, “This is a thumb.”

So the chances had been higher than he thought.

“He’s not missing a thumb,” Bobby said. The look Buck gave him must have perfectly conveyed his thoughts on that statement, because he got a scowl in return.

“There’s another victim.” Eddie grabbed the open cooler and held it out for Buck to drop the thumb in.

“The Santa Anas suck,” Buck said as Bobby radioed Dispatch about the third traumatic amputation. “Two thumbs down.”

Eddie smacked him for it.

“What is she doing here?”

Buck looked up from the scrapbook at the sound of the door opening and then Eddie’s voice, and quickly put on his best smile.

“Uh. Visiting?”

The night before had been spent hunting down the owner of the severed thumb, and then running around putting out even more (metaphorical) fires stirred up by the winds. It was the sort of exhausting that left Buck feeling restless, not helped by the weather. Something about the Santa Anas just had his skin feeling all.. Itchy.

 He and Eddie got home after Carla had already taken Chris to school, and they had grumpily piled into bed to steal a couple hours of sleep. Only a couple hours, because Abuela had a doctor’s appointment and Eddie was the one taking her. Which meant Buck was home alone with the dog, getting more and more restless as he tried to find something to do. He even contemplated cleaning the bathroom, even though they’d done that just the other day.

Which was probably why, when Taylor Kelly showed up at their door, he had been more than happy to let her in.

“Aw, Eddie.” The reporter’s smile had too many teeth to be friendly. She was sitting in one of the chairs that flanked the couch; not Abuela’s. “Aren’t you happy to see me?”

“No,” Eddie said. Abuela, walking past him into the house, tutted and shook her head, but didn’t chide him for his rudeness. She didn’t like Taylor, either. He pointed a finger at Hoover, who had greeted them at the door. “You’re supposed to keep intruders out.”

“Funny,” Taylor huffed. “He got fur all over my pants.”

Hoover, as it turns out, did like the reporter. Then again, Hoover hadn’t met a living creature that he didn’t like. The affection was not returned, however much he tried to beg for pets and scratches from her. The scrunched up look of distaste on her face had been hilarious, and Buck wasted no time taking pictures of it.

“How’d the appointment go?” Buck asked.

“Fine,” Abuela answered. “Same as last time.”

“She will outlive us all,” Eddie declared. “Her arthritis meds are getting switched up, so we’ll need to let Carla know.”

Buck wasn’t surprised, they’d both noticed the way her joints were bothering her again even if she refused to complain about it. Stoicness in the face of discomfort was a trait that must have come from her side of the family.

The old woman rolled her eyes and left the room, Hoover trotting along behind her.

“I didn’t realize you had such an artistic side, Eddie,” Taylor said, her smile acid sweet. That was when Eddie apparently realized what they’d been hunched over the coffee table, looking at: the scrapbook he surprised Buck with. He scowled at her. “Though I noticed I’m not in here anywhere. Not even any of the group photos from the wedding.”

Buck had not noticed this fact, not until that moment. That was interesting. His husband was so petty.

“The book’s for the parts we want to remember,” Eddie said, stepping closer so he could tug the scrapbook away from her. He flipped it closed. “So, no. You’re not in there.”

“Oh, that’s hurtful.”

“You two get along so well,” Buck told them, leaning back into the couch. “I’m beginning to think someone’s taking my spot as Eddie’s best friend.”

“Please,” Taylor scoffed. “Your spot’s safe.”

“Hey, since you're here,” Eddie said, smiling pleasantly at her. It looked as fake as Lyndsay-with-a-Y’s dye job. “Could you call your boss downstairs and ask him to shut off his hair dryer?”

“Oh, I get it.” Taylor rolled her eyes. “Funny, funny.”

“I don’t think she’s a demon, babe,” Buck told his husband. “I had to invite her inside. That makes her a vampire, right?”

He grinned at the venomous glare she shot him as Eddie shouted for Abuela to fetch him a crucifix.

The second night under the auspices of the Santa Ana winds was proving to be much like the one before: weird and restless.

Albert was sent along with Howie to the creepiest house he had ever seen. It was clearly abandoned, its lawn left untended for who knew how long. There was a No Trespassing sign that he and his brother ignored as they stepped inside, flashlights illuminating the way.

They found the reason they were there sitting in the middle of a living room filled with dust and old furniture and ancient Christmas decorations. Two officers, one tending to her partner’s head wound. Albert vaguely recognized Williams, the injured one, from previous calls.

“This is so embarrassing,” Williams complained. “I’m sorry you guys had to come all the way out here.”

“He thought I was a ghost,” his partner explained.

“There was a white, floating…”

“It was a trash bag.” She pointed to where it lay on the floor, not far away. “It was blowing around outside.”

“It’s fine,” Albert assured him, kneeling down to get a closer look at the wound. “I’ll just take a look at your head, here.”

“Everyone knows this house is haunted,” Williams told them, before demanding of his partner, “Why were you skulking around out there?”

“It’s my job,” she retorted.

“I don’t know anything about this house,” Albert told them, getting out some saline and gauze. “Got any stories?”

“It’s- It’s a haunted house,” Williams said, hissing a bit as Albert started cleaning the wound. “Teens come in on dares or whatever, and they run out screaming.”

“A haunted house with really good bones,” Howie commented, and Albert could see his focus was more on their surroundings than their patients.

“They’re kids,” the other cop said. “You’re forty years old.”

“Looks like you just bumped your head,” Albert said. “Dispatch said you were knocked out?”

“You told them that?” Williams complained to his partner. “Great. Now everyone’s gonna know.”

Albert figured he was probably right, since it had been Josh that took the call. Everyone knew Josh was just as big of a gossip as Howie. Speaking of the dispatcher, he radioed them again, saying,

“Dispatch to unit One Eighteen. Could you please have the house stop calling me?”

The statement had Williams donning an expression of vindication.

“The house is calling you?” Howie replied.

“Somehow the house keeps dialing Nine-One-One. I’ve had six more calls since the police got there,” Josh said. “Make it stop. Please.”

“Alright, uh.” Howie glanced at Albert from across the room. “Checking it out, Dispatch.”

Albert couldn’t see what his brother did, because he disappeared around a corner. But after a minute or two, during which he got William’s wound bandaged up, he heard Howie radio back to Josh.

“Dispatch, I think I found the source of the problem. You shouldn’t be getting any more calls tonight.”

“Copy that, One Eighteen. Thank you.”

“I think you might have a slight concussion,” Albert told Williams, flicking his light in the officer’s eyes, who did his best to jerk away. “So we’re gonna take you in to get looked at.”

“Do we really?” Williams complained.

“I mean, maybe not,” he replied, zipping his bag back up. “I’m just a trained medical professional, what do I know?”

“Fine, fine,” the officer huffed, his partner helping him up. “I’m never living this down. Stupid Murder House.”

“Hey, cheer up,” Albert said. “This is only going to haunt you for the rest of your career. Probably.”

Williams did not laugh, though his partner did.

Chapter 28: Our Whole Lives Laid Out (Right in Front of Us)

Chapter Summary

In which the Santa Anas continue to blow, and a thing comes full circle.

It was late into Tuesday night, so late that it was actually Wednesday, and Eddie could not sleep. He had tried, for a bit, but he eventually gave it up as a loss and retreated to the station’s loft. Bobby apparently had the same idea, and so did Hen, the latter looking morose about something. It didn’t take much effort, while they sat around the kitchen island, to get her to open up about Denny’s questions from earlier that day. Questions about his birth mother, Eva.

Eddie supposed that telling the kid the blunt and honest truth wasn’t an option at the moment.

“It sounds like you handled it pretty well,” he told her. “You answered his question. When there’s more, you’ll answer those, too.”

“I guess so,” Hen said, not sounding very comforted. Beyond her, Bobby was scowling down at his phone. “I just wish I knew where this was coming from.”

“Karen nearly died,” Eddie pointed out. “It’s natural that it’s got him thinking about things. And maybe it’s better he knows. We’ve seen what happens when secrets like this are left buried for too long.”

If anything positive could be taken from the Great Buckley Fuck-Up of 2021, it was a lesson on how not to handle life-altering family secrets. Especially when it was almost inevitable for them to be uncovered.

“Maybe you’re right,” she agreed, though she didn’t look very happy about it. “But him asking now is unsettling, and this damn wind doesn’t help. Always puts me out of sorts.”

“That’s because we know what these winds can do,” Bobby said, letting out a sigh as he sat down in the stool beside her. “Where there’s wind, there’s fire.”

That wasn’t how the phrase went, but Eddie wasn’t going to bring it up.

“You guys couldn’t sleep either?” Chimney asked, cresting the loft stairs. He was carrying a tablet.

“Starting to feel like it’s contagious,” Eddie said. “Buck still sleeping?”

“Like a baby.” Chimney set the tablet down on the island, before moving for the coffee maker. “In the world’s emptiest bunkroom.”

“Where’s Al?” Hen asked.

“Downstairs, on the phone with Ravi. Apparently he couldn’t sleep either.”

“What’s got you up?” Bobby asked him.

“I was looking into that house Al and I were called to,” Chimney said. “The one Williams thought he saw the ghost.”

“The Murder House?’ Hen asked, and Eddie barely kept from groaning aloud. Jinxes and curses and devil winds, and now a haunted murder house. Delightful.

“I wanted to know more about it.” The paramedic shrugged. “Seemed like it hadn’t been lived in for a while.”

“It is called the Murder House,” Bobby pointed out. “Maybe that’s why you can’t sleep.”

“What kind of murder?” Eddie asked, because he was genuinely curious. And also because Buck would pout if he didn’t get all the details for him.

“Triple homicide in the Sixties,” Chimney answered, scrolling on the tablet. “Dad came home right before Christmas, killed his three daughters and then himself. Mom got away, don’t know where to. House has been vacant ever since.”

“Can’t imagine why.”

And then a car came screeching into the bay.

Buck was used to being gracelessly yanked out of sleep. Especially during an overnight shift at the station. The thing was, he was used to being woken up by the shrill screams of the alarm rousing them for an emergency. He was not used to being woken up by the sound of brakes screeching and someone shouting.

It was an effective wake-up call just the same, though.

He slid on the pair of sandals he kept by the bed and hurried out of the bunkroom, eager to find out just what was going on. He was not expecting to find a car sitting in the middle of the bay, nor to find Bobby and Eddie and the Hans surrounding a naked woman.

“Okay!” he cried, raising a hand to cover his eyes. “This is not a dream I’ve had before.”

Normally, when he had these dreams, he was the one naked in the middle of the station. 

“Is she…?” Eddie let the question trail off. He was also covering his eyes, and had his face turned away.

“Yeah, she’s naked,” Albert confirmed, eyes fixed on the ceiling.

“And asleep,” Chimney said, and when Buck risked a peek, he saw that he was snapping his fingers in her face. “She is out cold. Maybe she’s sleepwalking.”

“You mean sleep-driving,” Eddie helpfully corrected.

“Uh, what should we do?” Buck asked. He lowered his hand, but couldn’t bring himself to look in the direction of the woman for long. “Isn’t it dangerous to wake up a sleepwalker?”

He seemed to remember hearing that somewhere.

“Probably not as dangerous as driving while sleeping,” Chimney pointed out.

“Here’s what we’ll do. Buck, go get a blanket,” Bobby ordered, and Buck hurried to go find one as the captain kept giving orders. Voices carried very well in the station bay, especially when everyone was silently gawking at something. “Chim, Albert, check her out to be safe. And maybe she left her ID in the car. Eddie, can you check the front seat?”

“She’s naked,” Eddie retorted, and Buck could easily imagine the sassy way his eyebrows were raised. “You really think she thought to grab her purse on the way out the door?”

“I don’t know,” Bobby said, exasperation coating his words. “Maybe it’s in the cup holder or the glove compartment.”

In the meanwhile, Buck had found and was running back with one of the spare blankets they kept on hand.

“Pops,” he said, holding it out. “Here.”

“Dispatch is sending officers!” Hen came running to them, only to skid to a stop when she saw what was going on. “Oh. Uh, okay.”

“Thank you,” Bobby said, taking the blanket from Buck. He started wrapping it around the woman, who woke up and started screaming the moment he got the blanket on her shoulders. The captain leaped back like he was burned and Hen jumped in to take over.

“Hey, hey, you’re okay,” she said, helping get the blanket to cover the woman better. “You’re in a safe place, okay? My name is Hen, I’m a firefighter.”

“O- Okay.” The woman wasn’t screaming any longer, but she didn’t look any less freaked out.

“We just want to help you,” Hen said, guiding her to the ambulance, where Albert and Chimney were waiting. “Can you tell me your name?”

“Lizzy,” the woman said. “Lizzy Sampson.”

“Well, Lizzy, if it’s alright with you,” Chimney said, directing her to sit down on the back of the ambulance, “we’re going to check you out, make sure you’re okay.”

“Uh, yeah, okay.”

“So, Lizzy,” Bobby started as Chimney did the basic concussion checks. “You got behind the wheel while you were sleeping. You have a history of this kind of thing?”

“Sleepwalking,” Lizzy confirmed. “But this is new.”

“Pupils are equal and reactive,” Chimney reported. “No sign of head trauma or concussion.”

“Pretty lucky,” Eddie told her. “The way you came in here, you could’ve really hurt yourself. Or us.”

“I don’t know if ‘lucky’ is how I would describe how I feel,” Lizzy protested. “Maybe ‘mortified.’”

She burrowed deeper into the blanket, pulling it up to her chin.

“Do you remember anything?” Hen asked her.

“I went to bed around eleven, like always,” Lizzy told them. “And then I woke up here. In a firehouse, oh my god.”

“And do you always sleep in the nude?” Buck asked, and got several judgmental looks in response. “Hey! Don’t look at me like that. She could have-” He lowered his voice to a hissed whisper, though it was probably futile. “Something could have happened.”

There were lots of medically relevant reasons that someone could fall asleep clothed and wake up naked. Most of them awful. It was not a stupid question!

“I like to sleep with the window open,” she confessed. “And feel a… Breeze.”

“Did you take anything to help you sleep?” Albert asked, and then glanced at the others. “People on Ambien drive in their sleep. It’s a thing.”

Buck did not know that. Huh.

“Uh, no.”

“You seem to be fine,” Chimney said, getting the woman’s attention. “But if it’s okay, we’re gonna take you to the hospital. Get you checked out, just to be safe.”

“I guess, sure.”

“Okay, Buck?” Bobby turned to face him. “Go check the locker room, see if there’s any spare clothes?”

“Right, but uh… What about her car?” Buck asked. It was kind of blocking the ambulance's way out of the station, after all.

“My car?” Lizzy seemed confused, but then again, she’d been in a state of confusion ever since she woke up.

“The car you drove here,” Albert said, pointing over in its direction.

“I don’t own a car.” And then it seemed the entire conversation they’d been having finally dawned on her. “Oh my god, I don’t own a car. I stole someone’s car. In my sleep. Naked. Oh my god.”

Buck patted Bobby’s shoulder before leaving him and the others to deal with all of that.  

“Murder’s too obvious,” Chimney complained. “There’s no way that’s what it was.”

They had migrated to the station roof, watching the sun begin to peek over the horizon, after Lizzy and the car that wasn’t hers had been taken care of. Or, most of them had. Buck went back to the bunkroom, declaring his intent of getting more sleep in before they were called out again. The rest of them were sitting around in lawn chairs, sipping at the hot chocolate Bobby made for them.

“Then why do they call it Murder House?” Eddie asked, feeling the lack of sleep.

“Please don’t encourage him,” Hen begged. Understandably so, considering the man had been going on about the house for some time now.

“Because Mishap House,” Chimney sniped, “doesn’t have the same ring to it.”

“Is that what you think happened to this family?” Bobby asked him. “A mishap?”

“Nobody seems to know,” the paramedic said. “I’ve looked up three blogs and a podcast, and got four different things.”

“Didn’t seem like an old murder scene when we were there,” Albert pointed out. “Everything was just dusty and forgotten.”

“What are we talking about?” a groggy sounding Buck asked, shuffling into their circle.

“Couldn’t sleep, either?” Hen asked him.

“Who could sleep when there’s cars driving unannounced into the building?” he asked, dragging one of the empty chairs as close to Eddie as he could manage, before leaning into his space to rest his head on Eddie’s shoulder.

“Would you prefer an announcement?” Eddie asked, leaning down to press a kiss into his curls and ignoring Chimney’s fake gagging.

“I would.”

“Have some hot chocolate,” Bobby said, and Buck perked up when Hen poured him a cup from the thermos.

“Thanks,” he said, taking it from her. “What were you talking about?”

“The Murder House,” Albert answered, sipping at his own hot chocolate.

“Triple homicide in the Sixties,” Chimney explained, when Buck just squinted in confusion. “Dad came home just before Christmas-”

“Oh, that Murder House,” Buck said, nodding. “It’s on Taylor’s list.”

“Taylor’s got a list of Murder Houses?” Eddie asked him.

“No, a list of mysteries in the city,” his husband answered. “The wind’s making my skin itch.”

“It’s the ions in the air,” Hen told him.
